Introduction to Linear Response Theory — Contents Appendices / Appendix D

Appendix DSolutions to the Exercises — Complete Solutions to All 94 Problems

This appendix collects the solutions to all 94 exercises placed in Chapters 1–14 and Appendices B and C of this book. In keeping with the policy of this book, we do not skip intermediate steps. Each solution begins with a one-line statement of “what is being asked,” followed by the strategy, every step of the calculation, and the result, with a “Check” and a “Physical meaning” added where appropriate.

Tips for using this appendix
  • First try to solve it on your own. If you get stuck, do not open this appendix right away; go back to the hint at the end of the exercise in the main text. In many cases the hint gives the final value or the formula to be used. Only when you are still stuck should you open this appendix.
  • When you read a solution, too, follow along with pencil and paper. As we have said many times in the main text, there is a deep gap between “I understood it when I read it” and “I can actually do it by hand.”
  • Watch the sign convention. The solutions to Chapters 1–3 are written in the “force” convention ($\omega>0$ with $\Im\chi>0$ being absorption), and the solutions from Chapter 7 onward in the $\Ham_{\text{ext}}=+f\hat B$ convention ($\chi''<0$ being absorption). This is exactly the convention of the corresponding chapters of the main text (see the correspondence table in Chapter 7, Section 7.6.5).
  • Answers to numerical problems are given to three or four significant figures. The physical constants used are standard values (CODATA).

Chapter 1: What Is Linear Response?

Exercise 1.1: Response to a step-like force

Solution

(1) For a general $\chi(\tau)$, we write the response to the step-like force $f(t)=f_0\theta(t)$ in integral form. The starting point is the convolution integral that we derived in this chapter from the three postulates,

$$ \delta A(t) = \int_{0}^{\infty} \chi(\tau)\, f(t-\tau)\, \dd\tau $$

into which we substitute $f(t-\tau)=f_0\,\theta(t-\tau)$. Since the step function $\theta(t-\tau)$ equals $1$ for $\tau < t$ and $0$ for $\tau > t$, the upper limit of the integral is cut off automatically at $t$, and we obtain

$$ \delta A(t) = f_0\int_{0}^{\infty} \chi(\tau)\,\theta(t-\tau)\, \dd\tau = f_0\int_{0}^{t} \chi(\tau)\, \dd\tau \qquad (t > 0) $$

This is the formula given in the hint. For $t < 0$ the integration interval is empty, so $\delta A(t)=0$; that is, there is no response before the force is applied. Causality is correctly reflected.

(2) We carry out the integral of (1) for Debye relaxation, $\chi(\tau)=(\chi_0/\tau_0)\ee^{-\tau/\tau_0}\theta(\tau)$. For $t > 0$,

$$ \delta A(t) = f_0\int_{0}^{t} \frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-\tau/\tau_0}\, \dd\tau = \frac{\chi_0 f_0}{\tau_0}\Bigl[-\tau_0\,\ee^{-\tau/\tau_0}\Bigr]_{0}^{t} = \frac{\chi_0 f_0}{\tau_0}\cdot\tau_0\left(1-\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\right) $$

that is,

$$ \delta A(t) = \chi_0 f_0\left(1-\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\right) $$

which proves the stated formula. The response starts from zero, $\delta A(0)=0$ (a system with memory cannot move instantaneously), and approaches its saturation value with time constant $\tau_0$.

Check. We verify the statement “impulse response = derivative of the step response” made in the sidebar of Section 1.5.1. Differentiating the step response just obtained (setting $f_0=1$) with respect to $t$ gives

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left[\chi_0\left(1-\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\right)\right] = \frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0} = \chi(t) \qquad (t > 0) $$

and indeed the original response function $\chi(t)$ is recovered.

(3) We verify that the static response is reached as $t\to\infty$. Letting $\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\to 0$ in the result of (2),

$$ \delta A(t) \xrightarrow{\ t\to\infty\ } \chi_0 f_0 $$

On the other hand, as we saw in Section 1.5.2, the static susceptibility is $\chi(\omega=0)=\int_0^\infty\chi(\tau)\dd\tau$. For Debye relaxation,

$$ \chi(\omega=0) = \int_{0}^{\infty} \frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-\tau/\tau_0}\, \dd\tau = \frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\cdot\tau_0 = \chi_0 $$

so the static response to a constant external force $f_0$ is $\chi(0)\,f_0=\chi_0 f_0$. The two agree. Substituting $\omega=0$ into $\chi(\omega)=\chi_0/(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)$, obtained in Example 1.1, likewise gives $\chi_0$.

Physical meaning. When the external force is switched on as a step, the system struggles transiently only for the memory time $\tau_0$; after that it settles into the state in which “a constant force has been applied all along,” that is, the static (thermodynamic) response. This problem is the simplest bridge connecting the dynamic response and the static response.

Exercise 1.2: Symmetry of the real and imaginary parts of a response function

Solution

First half: From the fact that $\chi(\tau)$ is a real function, we show $\chi(-\omega)=\chi(\omega)^*$. Following the hint, we take the complex conjugate of both sides of the formula derived in Section 1.4,

$$ \chi(\omega) = \int_{0}^{\infty} \chi(\tau)\, \ee^{\ii\omega\tau}\, \dd\tau $$

(with $\omega$ taken to be real). Since $\chi(\tau)$ is real, $\chi(\tau)^*=\chi(\tau)$, and the complex conjugation acts only on the exponential function, giving $(\ee^{\ii\omega\tau})^*=\ee^{-\ii\omega\tau}$. Hence

$$ \chi(\omega)^* = \int_{0}^{\infty} \chi(\tau)\, \ee^{-\ii\omega\tau}\, \dd\tau $$

Looking at the right-hand side, we see that it is nothing other than the defining expression with $\omega$ replaced by $-\omega$. Therefore

$$ \chi(\omega)^* = \chi(-\omega) $$

holds. Incidentally, the reason why $\chi(\tau)$ must be real in the first place is that, for a real external force $f$, the response $\delta A$ (a measured real physical quantity) must be real, and for the convolution $\delta A(t)=\int_0^\infty\chi(\tau)f(t-\tau)\dd\tau$ to return a real value for every real function $f$, the kernel $\chi(\tau)$ itself has no choice but to be real.

Second half: From this relation we derive the even–odd symmetry of the real and imaginary parts. We split $\chi(\omega)$ into its real and imaginary parts and write $\chi(\omega)=\chi'(\omega)+\ii\chi''(\omega)$ ($\chi'$ and $\chi''$ are real functions). Writing out the left- and right-hand sides of the relation just shown, we have

$$ \chi(-\omega) = \chi'(-\omega) + \ii\chi''(-\omega), \qquad \chi(\omega)^* = \chi'(\omega) - \ii\chi''(\omega) $$

Since the two are equal, comparing real parts with real parts and imaginary parts with imaginary parts gives

$$ \chi'(-\omega) = \chi'(\omega), \qquad \chi''(-\omega) = -\chi''(\omega) $$

That is, $\mathrm{Re}\,\chi(\omega)$ is an even function of $\omega$, and $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi(\omega)$ is an odd function.

Check. We verify this with the Debye relaxation of Example 1.1.

$$ \mathrm{Re}\,\chi(\omega) = \frac{\chi_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} \quad(\text{even}), \qquad \mathrm{Im}\,\chi(\omega) = \frac{\chi_0\,\omega\tau_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} \quad(\text{odd}) $$

and these indeed agree with the general rule.

Physical meaning. Thanks to this symmetry, the information for $\omega > 0$ alone determines the whole of $\chi(\omega)$. Since experiments measure only positive frequencies, this is also important in practice; in Chapter 4 we use this even–odd symmetry when rewriting the Kramers–Kronig relations as an “integral over positive $\omega$ only.”

Exercise 1.3: Response function of an RC circuit

Solution

(1) We differentiate the circuit equation to obtain a differential equation for $I$. In a series RC circuit, the sum of the voltage drop $RI$ across the resistor and the voltage $Q/C=\frac{1}{C}\int^t I\,\dd t'$ across the capacitor equals the external voltage:

$$ RI(t) + \frac{1}{C}\int^{t} I(t')\,\dd t' = V(t) $$

Differentiating both sides with respect to $t$, the integral term reverts to its integrand:

$$ R\,\frac{\dd I}{\dd t} + \frac{1}{C}\,I = \frac{\dd V}{\dd t} $$

This is the required differential equation. Note that, in exchange for eliminating the integral, the right-hand side is now $\dd V/\dd t$ rather than $V$.

(2) We obtain the admittance from the steady-state response to a monochromatic force $V(t)=V_0\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$. In the convention of this book (Section 1.4), $\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$ is the monochromatic external force of frequency $\omega$. Since the equation is linear, the response also oscillates at the same frequency, so we may set $I(t)=I_0\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$. Substituting into the differential equation of (1), with $\dd/\dd t \to -\ii\omega$, we obtain

$$ -\ii\omega R\, I_0 + \frac{1}{C}\, I_0 = -\ii\omega V_0 $$

We factor $I_0$ out of the left-hand side and solve for $I_0$:

$$ I_0\left(\frac{1}{C} - \ii\omega R\right) = -\ii\omega V_0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad I_0 = \frac{-\ii\omega V_0}{\dfrac{1}{C} - \ii\omega R} = \frac{-\ii\omega C}{1 - \ii\omega RC}\, V_0 $$

Therefore the admittance is

$$ Y(\omega) = \frac{I(\omega)}{V(\omega)} = \frac{-\ii\omega C}{1-\ii\omega RC} $$

which agrees with the formula in the hint.

Check. We look at both limits. For $\omega\to0$, $Y\to -\ii\omega C\to 0$: direct current is blocked by the capacitor and does not flow. For $\omega\to\infty$, $Y\to (-\ii\omega C)/(-\ii\omega RC)=1/R$: at high frequencies the capacitor is effectively a short circuit, and only the resistance remains. Both agree with common knowledge about series RC circuits.

(3) We find the position of the pole of $Y(\omega)$ in the complex $\omega$-plane and confirm that it is not in the upper half-plane. $Y(\omega)$ diverges at the point where the denominator vanishes, that is,

$$ 1 - \ii\omega RC = 0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \omega = \frac{1}{\ii RC} = -\frac{\ii}{RC} $$

(we used $1/\ii=-\ii$). The pole is the single purely imaginary point $\omega=-\ii/RC$, and since $\mathrm{Im}\,\omega = -1/(RC) < 0$ it lies in the lower half-plane of the complex plane. There is no pole in the upper half-plane, which is consistent with “causality $\Longleftrightarrow$ analytic in the upper half-plane” stated in Section 1.4. This is as stated in the hint. Note also that the numerator $-\ii\omega C$ is a polynomial and therefore produces no pole.

Physical meaning. The magnitude $1/(RC)$ of the imaginary part of the pole is the damping rate of the transient current of this circuit. Indeed, if the voltage is raised to $V_0$ in a step at $t=0$, the familiar charging current $I(t)=(V_0/R)\,\ee^{-t/RC}$ flows. At first the capacitor is empty and acts like a plain wire, so $V_0/R$ flows; as charging proceeds, the current dies away with time constant $RC$. The correspondence “imaginary part of the pole = relaxation rate” appears repeatedly, both for the damped oscillator of Chapter 2 and for the Lehmann representation of Chapter 8.

Common Mistake: The $j$ of electrical engineering and the $\ii$ of this book

In electrical-circuit textbooks the time factor is usually taken to be $\ee^{+j\omega t}$, so the impedance of a capacitor is written $1/(j\omega C)$ and the admittance $Y=j\omega C/(1+j\omega RC)$. The convention of this book (monochromatic external force $\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$) has the opposite sign, and if one applies the replacement $j \leftrightarrow -\ii$, the two agree. When importing a formula from the literature, make it a habit to first check the sign of the time factor in that source. If you get the sign wrong, the poles seem to be “in the upper half-plane,” and you will mistakenly think that causality has been violated.

Exercise 1.4: Estimating where linearity breaks down

Solution

(1) From the linear-response condition $pE\ll k_BT$, we estimate the upper limit of the electric field $E$ for water. Following the idea of Section 1.1.2, we only need to compare “the energy that the external force gives the system” (the orientation energy $pE$ of the dipole) with “the characteristic energy of the system” (the thermal energy $k_BT$ that disturbs the orientation). Solving the condition for $E$, we obtain

$$ E \ll \frac{k_BT}{p} = \frac{4.1\times10^{-21}\ \mathrm{J}}{6.2\times10^{-30}\ \mathrm{C\,m}} \approx 6.6\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{V/m} $$

which agrees with the value given in the hint. That is, the electric field that serves as a guide for the orientational polarization of water to respond linearly lies in the upper part of the $10^{8}\ \mathrm{V/m}$ range.

(2) We compare this value with the dielectric breakdown field of water, $\sim10^{7}\ \mathrm{V/m}$. Taking the ratio gives

$$ \frac{6.6\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{V/m}}{10^{7}\ \mathrm{V/m}} \approx 66 $$

In other words, water itself undergoes dielectric breakdown long before the nonlinearity of the orientational polarization shows up (at about 1/66 of that field). Since $pE/k_BT \ll 1$ is maintained over the entire range of fields reachable in electrostatic experiments, the dielectric response of water remains linear all the way until the sample breaks down. This is why we may treat the dielectric constant $\epsilon$ as a constant independent of the field.

Check. The parameter at the breakdown field $E=10^{7}\ \mathrm{V/m}$ is

$$ x \equiv \frac{pE}{k_BT} = \frac{6.2\times10^{-30}\times10^{7}}{4.1\times10^{-21}} \approx 1.5\times10^{-2} $$

which is indeed sufficiently smaller than $1$.

Physical Meaning: The size of the nonlinear correction

If we calculate the orientational polarization properly with statistical mechanics (the Langevin function $L(x)=\coth x - 1/x$ appears; its expansion is $L(x)= x/3 - x^3/45 + \cdots$), the relative size of the lowest-order nonlinear correction to the linear term is about $x^2/15$. Even if we insert the breakdown field, $x\approx1.5\times10^{-2}$, the correction is only $\sim1.5\times10^{-5}$, that is, only about one hundred-thousandth ($1$ part in $10$ ten-thousands). As in the metal example of Section 1.1.2, although we say that “linear response is an approximation for weak external forces,” in most laboratory situations there is not even a hint that this approximation is breaking down.

Exercise 1.5: What happens if causality is violated

Solution

(1) We compute the Fourier transform $\chi(\omega)$ of the response function $\chi(\tau)=\dfrac{\chi_0}{2\tau_0}\,\ee^{-\abs{\tau}/\tau_0}$, which violates causality. This time $\chi(\tau)\neq0$ even for $\tau < 0$, so the formula with the lower limit set to $0$ cannot be used; we return to the original definition

$$ \chi(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \chi(\tau)\, \ee^{\ii\omega\tau}\, \dd\tau $$

and integrate over the entire interval. To remove the absolute value, we split the integral into $\tau > 0$ and $\tau < 0$. First, for the part $\tau > 0$ we have $\abs{\tau}=\tau$, so by the same calculation as in Example 1.1,

$$ \int_{0}^{\infty} \ee^{-\tau/\tau_0}\,\ee^{\ii\omega\tau}\, \dd\tau = \int_{0}^{\infty} \ee^{-(1/\tau_0-\ii\omega)\tau}\, \dd\tau = \frac{1}{1/\tau_0 - \ii\omega} = \frac{\tau_0}{1-\ii\omega\tau_0} $$

Next, for the part $\tau < 0$ we have $\abs{\tau}=-\tau$. Substituting $\tau\to-\tau$ (the sign change of $\dd\tau$ and the reversal of the integration interval cancel each other), we get

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{0} \ee^{\tau/\tau_0}\,\ee^{\ii\omega\tau}\, \dd\tau = \int_{0}^{\infty} \ee^{-\tau/\tau_0}\,\ee^{-\ii\omega\tau}\, \dd\tau = \frac{1}{1/\tau_0 + \ii\omega} = \frac{\tau_0}{1+\ii\omega\tau_0} $$

Adding the two and multiplying by the coefficient $\chi_0/(2\tau_0)$, we obtain, by bringing to a common denominator,

$$ \chi(\omega) = \frac{\chi_0}{2}\left[\frac{1}{1-\ii\omega\tau_0} + \frac{1}{1+\ii\omega\tau_0}\right] = \frac{\chi_0}{2}\cdot\frac{(1+\ii\omega\tau_0)+(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)}{(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)(1+\ii\omega\tau_0)} = \frac{\chi_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} $$

which agrees with the formula in the hint. Remarkably, this $\chi(\omega)$ is purely real for real $\omega$ ($\mathrm{Im}\,\chi(\omega)=0$). Because $\chi(\tau)$ is an even function, the imaginary part of the Fourier transform (which corresponds to the odd-function component) has vanished. Since the imaginary part represents energy dissipation (see the explanation of Figure 1.1), this “system” absorbs no energy at any frequency—the violation of causality shows up also in the form of the disappearance of dissipation. Note also that the relation $\chi(-\omega)=\chi(\omega)^*$ of Exercise 1.2 does hold. That symmetry holds as long as $\chi(\tau)$ is real, and it is a property independent of causality.

(2) We find the positions of the poles of $\chi(\omega)$. The condition for the denominator to vanish is

$$ 1 + \omega^2\tau_0^2 = 0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \omega^2 = -\frac{1}{\tau_0^2} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \omega = \pm\frac{\ii}{\tau_0} $$

There are two poles on the imaginary axis: $\omega=-\ii/\tau_0$ lies in the lower half-plane, and $\omega=+\ii/\tau_0$ lies in the upper half-plane. A pole in the upper half-plane, which a causal response function can never have, has indeed appeared. Which of the two halves is the culprit can be identified from the calculation in (1). The pole of the contribution of the causal part ($\tau > 0$), $\tau_0/(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)$, is at $\omega=-\ii/\tau_0$ (lower half-plane), which is the same position as the pole of Debye relaxation (Example 1.1). On the other hand, the pole of the contribution of the anticausal part ($\tau < 0$), $\tau_0/(1+\ii\omega\tau_0)$, is at $\omega=+\ii/\tau_0$ (upper half-plane). The pole in the upper half-plane was created by the “tail” at $\tau < 0$.

(3) We explain why a response appears at $t < 0$ when a pulse is applied at $t=0$. Taking the external force to be $f(t)=f_0\,\delta(t)$ and inserting it into the convolution (the whole-interval form, which does not assume causality), we find that, by the property of the delta function, the integral is evaluated at a single point:

$$ \delta A(t) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \chi(t-t')\, f_0\,\delta(t')\, \dd t' = f_0\,\chi(t) = \frac{\chi_0 f_0}{2\tau_0}\,\ee^{-\abs{t}/\tau_0} $$

Since the impulse response is $\chi(t)$ itself (the definition in Section 1.3) and $\chi(t)$ is nonzero for $t < 0$, the response appears at $t < 0$. Concretely, at a time $\tau_0$ before the time of the kick, the response has already reached $\ee^{-1}\approx0.37$ times its peak value. The system senses that it is “about to be kicked” and starts to move—the effect precedes the cause, which is physically impossible. As we will see in Chapter 4, the Kramers–Kronig relations fail for a response function with such a pole in the upper half-plane, so by comparing the measured data for the real and imaginary parts, one can detect experimentally that “this response function is wrong.”

Physical Meaning: The correspondence this exercise teaches

Exercise 1.5 confirms this chapter's claim, “causality $\Longleftrightarrow$ analyticity in the upper half-plane,” from the side of a counterexample. Merely adding one $\tau < 0$ tail in the time domain made one pole sprout in the upper half-plane in the frequency domain. The correspondence is concrete: a causal decay of the form $\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\theta(t)$ produces the pole $-\ii/\tau_0$ in the lower half-plane, and an anticausal tail of the form $\ee^{+t/\tau_0}\theta(-t)$ produces the pole $+\ii/\tau_0$ in the upper half-plane. Once we learn complex integration in Chapter 5, this correspondence can be understood completely in the following form: “for $t < 0$ we close the contour in the upper half-plane, so if there is no pole in the upper half-plane, the response is zero.”

Chapter 2: Solving the Damped Oscillator with Green's Functions

Exercise 2.1: Green's function of a first-order equation (warm-up)

Solution

(1) Integrate the defining equation $\hat{L}G^R=\tau_0\dot{G}^R+G^R=\delta(t)$ across $t=0$ to derive the matching condition $G^R(0^+)-G^R(0^-)=1/\tau_0$.

As a preliminary, let us check that $G^R$ itself contains no delta-function-type singularity. If $G^R$ contained a term $a\,\delta(t)$, then $\tau_0\dot{G}^R$ would contain $\tau_0a\,\delta'(t)$. But the right-hand side $\delta(t)$ has no $\delta'$, and no other term on the left-hand side can produce a $\delta'$. Therefore $a=0$, and $G^R$ is a bounded function with at most a finite jump (the singularity of highest order is the $\delta$ inside $\dot{G}^R$).

We then integrate both sides of the defining equation from $-\epsilon$ to $+\epsilon$ ($\epsilon>0$ is infinitesimal):

$$ \int_{-\epsilon}^{+\epsilon}\left(\tau_0\dot{G}^R+G^R\right)\dd t =\int_{-\epsilon}^{+\epsilon}\delta(t)\,\dd t=1 $$

The first term can be integrated immediately by the fundamental theorem of calculus, giving $\tau_0\left[G^R(\epsilon)-G^R(-\epsilon)\right]$. The second term vanishes because $G^R$ is bounded:

$$ \abs{\int_{-\epsilon}^{+\epsilon}G^R\dd t}\le 2\epsilon\max_{\abs{t}\le\epsilon}\abs{G^R} \xrightarrow{\ \epsilon\to0^+\ }0 $$

Letting $\epsilon\to0^+$, we obtain

$$ \tau_0\left[G^R(0^+)-G^R(0^-)\right]=1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad G^R(0^+)-G^R(0^-)=\frac{1}{\tau_0} $$

In the second-order equation of the main text, “the position is continuous and the velocity jumps by $1/m$”; in the first-order equation the highest derivative is of first order, so everything shifts down by one, and the value itself jumps by $1/\tau_0$. In general, for an equation of order $n$ only the $(n-1)$th derivative jumps, and all derivatives of lower order are continuous.

(2) We impose causality to determine $G^R(t)$.

Substituting the causality condition $G^R(0^-)=0$ into the matching condition of (1) gives $G^R(0^+)=1/\tau_0$. For $t>0$ the delta function vanishes and we obtain the homogeneous equation $\tau_0\dot{G}^R+G^R=0$, whose solution is $C\ee^{-t/\tau_0}$. Letting $t\to0^+$ fixes $C=1/\tau_0$, so that

$$ G^R(t)=\frac{1}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\,\theta(t) $$

which agrees with the expression given in the hint.

Check. Substitute back into the defining equation. Differentiating with $\dot{\theta}(t)=\delta(t)$ and $\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\delta(t)=\ee^{0}\delta(t)=\delta(t)$ in mind, we obtain

$$ \dot{G}^R=-\frac{1}{\tau_0^2}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\theta(t)+\frac{1}{\tau_0}\,\delta(t) $$

and hence

$$ \tau_0\dot{G}^R+G^R =-\frac{1}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\theta(t)+\delta(t)+\frac{1}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\theta(t) =\delta(t) $$

so the equation is indeed satisfied.

(3) We find $G(\omega)$ by the Fourier method, compare it with Debye relaxation, and examine the pole position.

We follow the same procedure as in Section 2.5.2 of the main text. Writing $G(t)=\int\frac{\dd\omega}{2\pi}\,G(\omega)\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$ and noting that $\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\ee^{-\ii\omega t}=-\ii\omega\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$, we see that $\hat{L}=\tau_0\frac{\dd}{\dd t}+1$ becomes, in $\omega$ space, a mere multiplication by $(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)$. Comparing with $\delta(t)=\int\frac{\dd\omega}{2\pi}\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$, we obtain

$$ \left(1-\ii\omega\tau_0\right)G(\omega)=1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad G(\omega)=\frac{1}{1-\ii\omega\tau_0} $$

This is exactly the Debye relaxation $\chi(\omega)=\chi_0/(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)$ of Chapter 1, Section 1.5.1 with $\chi_0=1$.

Consistency with the real-time answer of (2) can also be checked directly:

$$ \int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\dd t =\frac{1}{\tau_0}\cdot\frac{1}{1/\tau_0-\ii\omega} =\frac{1}{1-\ii\omega\tau_0} $$

Pole positions. The denominator vanishes when

$$ 1-\ii\omega\tau_0=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \omega=\frac{1}{\ii\tau_0}=-\frac{\ii}{\tau_0} $$

so the pole lies on the imaginary axis, in the lower half-plane, with imaginary part $-1/\tau_0<0$. This is consistent with the rule of this chapter (the frequency-representation form of causality) that “the poles of the retarded Green's function always lie in the lower half-plane,” as the hint says. Whereas the second-order oscillator had two poles at $\pm\omega_1-\ii\Gamma$ with a real part (oscillation), the first-order relaxation has only one pole, and it lies on the imaginary axis—the “face” of a system that simply relaxes without oscillating.

Physical Meaning: The value jumps because there is no inertia

In the second-order oscillator it was the velocity that jumped, while the position was continuous. This is because an impulse changes the momentum $m\dot{x}$ but cannot move the position instantaneously. The first-order relaxation equation has no inertia term $m\ddot{x}$, so an impulsive force pushes $x$ itself up by $1/\tau_0$ all at once. In terms of Debye relaxation, the polarization rises the instant a delta-function electric-field pulse hits, and thereafter it forgets exponentially with time constant $\tau_0$—the simplest form of the “memory” discussed in Chapter 1, Section 1.3.

Exercise 2.2: Green's functions for overdamping and critical damping

Solution

The tool is the same in every case. Combining the matching condition derived in the main text ($G^R$ is continuous at $t=0$, and $\dot{G}^R$ jumps by $1/m$) with causality $G^R(t)=0\ (t<0)$, the conditions that the solution for $t>0$ must satisfy reduce to the pair

$$ G^R(0^+)=0,\qquad \dot{G}^R(0^+)=\frac{1}{m} $$

—two conditions in all.

(1) We find $G^R$ for overdamping ($\Gamma>\omega_0$) from the matching conditions. Write the general solution for $t>0$ as $G^R(t)=\ee^{-\Gamma t}(A\cosh\kappa t+B\sinh\kappa t)$ with $\kappa=\sqrt{\Gamma^2-\omega_0^2}$.

First condition. As $t\to0^+$ we have $\ee^0=1$, $\cosh0=1$, $\sinh0=0$, so

$$ G^R(0^+)=A=0 $$

Hence $G^R(t)=B\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sinh\kappa t$. Second condition. Carrying out the differentiation of the product gives

$$ \dot{G}^R(t)=B\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left(\kappa\cosh\kappa t-\Gamma\sinh\kappa t\right) $$

As $t\to0^+$, $\dot{G}^R(0^+)=B\kappa=1/m$, that is, $B=1/(m\kappa)$. Therefore

$$ G^R(t)=\frac{1}{m\kappa}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sinh(\kappa t)\,\theta(t) $$

This agrees with the hint.

(2) Critical damping ($\Gamma=\omega_0$). We impose the same two conditions on the general solution $G^R(t)=(A+Bt)\ee^{-\Gamma t}$. As $t\to0^+$,

$$ G^R(0^+)=A=0 $$

Differentiating gives

$$ \dot{G}^R(t)=B\ee^{-\Gamma t}-\Gamma(A+Bt)\ee^{-\Gamma t} \quad\xrightarrow{\ t\to0^+\ }\quad B-\Gamma A=B=\frac{1}{m} $$

Therefore

$$ G^R(t)=\frac{t}{m}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\,\theta(t) $$

This too agrees with the hint.

(3) Using the recipe of Section 2.1.5, we reproduce (1) and (2) from the weak-damping result of the main text. Let us write the principal result of the main text in terms of the combination $\sin(\omega_1t)/\omega_1$:

$$ G^R(t)=\frac{1}{m}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\,\frac{\sin\omega_1 t}{\omega_1}\,\theta(t) $$

Overdamping. Substitute $\omega_1=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2}=\ii\kappa$ (a purely imaginary number). Since $\sin(\ii u)=\ii\sinh u$,

$$ \frac{\sin(\ii\kappa t)}{\ii\kappa}=\frac{\ii\sinh\kappa t}{\ii\kappa}=\frac{\sinh\kappa t}{\kappa} $$

and the answer of (1) comes out as it stands. Critical damping. In the limit $\omega_1\to0$ we have $\sin(\omega_1t)/\omega_1\to t$, so the answer of (2) follows. The three cases are indeed gathered into a single formula written in the form $\sin(\omega_1t)/\omega_1$.

(4) We compare numerically for $m=1\ \mathrm{kg}$, $k=100\ \mathrm{N/m}$ ($\omega_0=\sqrt{k/m}=10\ \mathrm{rad/s}$).

Critical damping, $\gamma=20\ \mathrm{kg/s}$. Here $\Gamma=\gamma/2m=10\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}=\omega_0$, which is indeed critical. We look for the maximum of $G^R(t)=t\ee^{-\Gamma t}/m$:

$$ \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\left(\frac{t}{m}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\right) =\frac{1-\Gamma t}{m}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad t_{\max}=\frac{1}{\Gamma}=0.100\ \mathrm{s} $$ $$ G^R_{\max}=\frac{1}{m\Gamma}\,\ee^{-1}=\frac{1}{10\,\ee} =3.68\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m/(N\,s)} $$

This agrees with the value given in the hint.

Overdamping, $\gamma=40\ \mathrm{kg/s}$. Here $\Gamma=20\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and $\kappa=\sqrt{20^2-10^2}=\sqrt{300}=17.32\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Then

$$ G^R(t)=\frac{\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sinh\kappa t}{m\kappa} =\frac{1}{2m\kappa}\left(\ee^{-(\Gamma-\kappa)t}-\ee^{-(\Gamma+\kappa)t}\right) $$

which is a superposition of two exponentials, with the fast root $\Gamma+\kappa=37.3\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and the slow root $\Gamma-\kappa=2.68\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$. For reference, the maximum follows from $\kappa\cosh\kappa t=\Gamma\sinh\kappa t$, that is, $\tanh\kappa t_{\max}=\kappa/\Gamma=0.866$, which gives $t_{\max}=\frac{1}{\kappa}\,\mathrm{artanh}\,0.866=7.60\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{s}$ and $G^R_{\max}=2.19\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m/(N\,s)}$, only about 60% of the value for critical damping.

Which decays faster? The long-time decay is governed, for critical damping, by $t\ee^{-10t}$ (time constant $0.10\ \mathrm{s}$) and, for overdamping, by the slow root's $\ee^{-2.68t}$ (time constant $1/2.68=0.37\ \mathrm{s}$). Following the numbers, the two curves exchange order at $t\simeq0.33\ \mathrm{s}$, and at $t=1\ \mathrm{s}$ the critical-damping value $G^R=4.5\times10^{-5}$ compares with $2.0\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{m/(N\,s)}$ for overdamping, which is still more than 40 times larger. It is critical damping that decays faster. As the hint says, in overdamping the slow root $\Gamma-\kappa$ drags out a tail. The lesson of Figure 2.1—that too much friction actually prolongs the response—applies just as well to the Green's function itself.

Exercise 2.3: Response to a rectangular pulse — the power of superposition

Solution

(1) We show, from linearity and time-translation invariance, that the response to a rectangular pulse can be written as $x(t)=x_{\rm step}(t)-x_{\rm step}(t-T)$.

The external force is the difference of two steps, $f(t)=F_0\theta(t)-F_0\theta(t-T)$. The solution formula $x(t)=\int\dd t'\,G^R(t-t')f(t')$ is linear in $f$, so the response is the difference of the responses to the individual terms (linearity). The response to the first term $F_0\theta(t')$ is, by definition, the step response $x_{\rm step}(t)$. For the response to the second term $F_0\theta(t'-T)$, substituting the integration variable $u=t'-T$ gives

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd t'\,G^R(t-t')\,F_0\theta(t'-T) =\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd u\,G^R\big((t-T)-u\big)\,F_0\theta(u) =x_{\rm step}(t-T) $$

What is at work in the second equality is the fact that $G^R$ is a function of the difference of its arguments only (time-translation invariance)—delaying the input by $T$ merely delays the output, unchanged, by $T$. Hence

$$ x(t)=x_{\rm step}(t)-x_{\rm step}(t-T) $$

Noting that $x_{\rm step}(t)=0\ (t<0)$, we see that this single formula correctly represents all of $t<0$ (no response), $0<t<T$ (first term only), and $t>T$ (both terms) at once. We have not redone the integral at all—this is the power of superposition.

(2) We verify that for $t>T$ the motion is a free damped oscillation. The step response obtained in the main text is

$$ x_{\rm step}(t)=\frac{F_0}{k}\left[1-\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left(\cos\omega_1 t+\frac{\Gamma}{\omega_1}\sin\omega_1 t\right)\right]\theta(t) $$

For $t>T$ both $\theta$'s equal $1$, so the steady terms $F_0/k$ cancel exactly, and what remains is

$$ x(t)=\frac{F_0}{k}\left[\ee^{-\Gamma(t-T)}\left(\cos\omega_1(t-T)+\frac{\Gamma}{\omega_1}\sin\omega_1(t-T)\right) -\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left(\cos\omega_1 t+\frac{\Gamma}{\omega_1}\sin\omega_1 t\right)\right] $$

Here $\ee^{-\Gamma(t-T)}=\ee^{\Gamma T}\ee^{-\Gamma t}$, and by the addition theorem $\cos\omega_1(t-T)$ and $\sin\omega_1(t-T)$ are linear combinations of $\cos\omega_1t$ and $\sin\omega_1t$ with constant coefficients. Therefore the whole expression takes the form

$$ x(t)=\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left(C_1\cos\omega_1 t+C_2\sin\omega_1 t\right) \qquad(t>T,\ \ C_1,C_2\ \text{are constants with a}\ T\ \text{dependence}) $$

of the general solution of the homogeneous equation. That is, it is a free oscillation damped as $\ee^{-\Gamma t}$. This is also natural physically: for $t>T$ the external force has been switched off, so the system has no choice but to perform a free damped oscillation whose initial conditions are the position and velocity at the end of the pulse.

(3) We show that in the limit $T\ll1/\omega_0$ the result reduces to the response to an impulsive force. The time scales on which $x_{\rm step}$ varies are $1/\omega_1$ and $1/\Gamma$, and for weak damping both are comparable to $1/\omega_0$ or longer. If $T$ is sufficiently shorter than these, keeping only the leading term of the Taylor expansion gives

$$ x(t)=x_{\rm step}(t)-x_{\rm step}(t-T)\simeq T\,\dot{x}_{\rm step}(t) $$

Now we differentiate the relation $x_{\rm step}(t)=F_0\int_0^t G^R(s)\,\dd s$ of Step 2 of Section 2.7.2 in the main text; the fundamental theorem of calculus gives

$$ \dot{x}_{\rm step}(t)=F_0\,G^R(t) $$

that is, the derivative of the step response is the impulse response (the “reverse of Step 2 of Section 2.7.2” mentioned in the hint). To be safe, let us check this by differentiating the closed-form expression above directly ($t>0$):

$$ \dot{x}_{\rm step}(t) =\frac{F_0}{k}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left[\Gamma\cos\omega_1t+\frac{\Gamma^2}{\omega_1}\sin\omega_1t +\omega_1\sin\omega_1t-\Gamma\cos\omega_1t\right] =\frac{F_0}{k}\cdot\frac{\Gamma^2+\omega_1^2}{\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin\omega_1t $$

Using $\Gamma^2+\omega_1^2=\omega_0^2$ and $k=m\omega_0^2$, this equals $\frac{F_0}{m\omega_1}\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin\omega_1t=F_0G^R(t)$. Hence

$$ x(t)\simeq T\,F_0\,G^R(t)=\frac{F_0T}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin(\omega_1 t)\,\theta(t) $$

This is nothing other than the response to an impulsive force of impulse $\int f\,\dd t=F_0T$ (the response to an impulsive force of the main text, $x=F_0G^R(t)$, with $F_0\to F_0T$), as the hint says. The oscillator cannot resolve the internal structure of events faster than its own period, and feels only the impulse (the time integral).

Exercise 2.4: Stopping the oscillation with two impulsive kicks

Solution

(1) We write the response to the two impulsive forces by superposition. The external force is $f(t)=F_0\delta(t)+F_1\delta(t-t_1)$. By linearity the response is the sum of the responses to the individual impulsive forces: the response to $F_0\delta(t)$ is $F_0G^R(t)$, and, by time-translation invariance (the same argument as in Exercise 2.3(1)), the response to $F_1\delta(t-t_1)$ is $F_1G^R(t-t_1)$. For $t>t_1>0$ both step functions equal $1$, so

$$ x(t)=\frac{F_0}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin\omega_1 t +\frac{F_1}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma(t-t_1)}\sin\omega_1(t-t_1) $$

In the second term we write $\ee^{-\Gamma(t-t_1)}=\ee^{\Gamma t_1}\ee^{-\Gamma t}$, and factoring $\ee^{-\Gamma t}/(m\omega_1)$ out of the whole expression gives

$$ x(t)=\frac{\ee^{-\Gamma t}}{m\omega_1}\left[F_0\sin\omega_1t+F_1\ee^{\Gamma t_1}\sin\omega_1(t-t_1)\right] $$

and we have obtained the expression given in the problem.

(2) For $t_1=\pi/\omega_1$ we have $\omega_1(t-t_1)=\omega_1t-\pi$, and the addition theorem gives

$$ \sin(\omega_1t-\pi)=\sin\omega_1t\cos\pi-\cos\omega_1t\sin\pi=-\sin\omega_1t $$

Substituting this into the result of (1), we obtain

$$ x(t)=\frac{\ee^{-\Gamma t}}{m\omega_1}\left(F_0-F_1\ee^{\Gamma t_1}\right)\sin\omega_1t \qquad(t>t_1) $$

The condition for this to vanish identically for all $t>t_1$ is that the parenthesis vanish, that is,

$$ F_1=F_0\,\ee^{-\Gamma t_1}=F_0\,\ee^{-\pi\Gamma/\omega_1} $$

If $x(t)\equiv0$, then $\dot{x}(t)\equiv0$ as well, so the position and the velocity are both zero and the system is at complete rest.

Physical meaning. The motion due to the first kick alone, $x=(F_0/m\omega_1)\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin\omega_1t$, passes exactly through the origin $x=0$ half a period later, at $t_1=\pi/\omega_1$. The velocity at that moment is

$$ \dot{x}(t_1)=\frac{F_0}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t_1}\left(\omega_1\cos\pi-\Gamma\sin\pi\right) =-\frac{F_0}{m}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t_1} $$

which points in the negative direction. If we now give a positive impulse $F_1=F_0\ee^{-\Gamma t_1}$, the velocity change $+F_1/m$ exactly cancels it. Since the position was already zero, the system comes to rest on the spot. This is what the hint means by “we should kick more gently by just the amount lost to friction.”

(3) Substituting the values from Table 2.2, $\Gamma=1\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$ and $\omega_1=9.95\ \mathrm{rad/s}$, we get

$$ \frac{F_1}{F_0}=\ee^{-\pi\times1/9.95}=\ee^{-0.3157}=0.729 $$

This agrees with the value $0.729$ given in the hint. The second kick needs to be only about $73\ \%$ as strong as the first.

Exercise 2.5: Correspondence with the RLC circuit

Solution

(1) We compare the two equations term by term and write $\omega_0$, $\Gamma$, $\omega_1$ and $Q$ in terms of $L,R,C$.

$$ m\ddot{x}+\gamma\dot{x}+kx=f(t), \qquad L\ddot{q}+R\dot{q}+\frac{1}{C}q=V(t) $$

Matching the coefficients in order, we have $m\leftrightarrow L$ (inertia ↔ self-inductance: both oppose change), $\gamma\leftrightarrow R$ (friction ↔ resistance: both are responsible for dissipation), $k\leftrightarrow1/C$ (the restoring force of the spring ↔ the action of the capacitor that pushes the charge back), $x\leftrightarrow q$, and $f\leftrightarrow V$. Simply substituting into the mechanical defining formulas gives

$$ \omega_0=\sqrt{\frac{k}{m}}\ \to\ \sqrt{\frac{1/C}{L}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}, \qquad \Gamma=\frac{\gamma}{2m}\ \to\ \frac{R}{2L} $$ $$ \omega_1=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{LC}-\frac{R^2}{4L^2}}, \qquad Q=\frac{\omega_0}{2\Gamma}=\frac{L\omega_0}{R}=\frac{L}{R}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}=\frac{1}{R}\sqrt{\frac{L}{C}} $$

These agree with the expressions in the hint.

(2) Simply replacing the symbols in the principal result of the main text, $G^R(t)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{-\gamma t/(2m)}\sin(\omega_1t)\,\theta(t)$, by means of the dictionary in (1), we obtain

$$ G^R(t)=\frac{1}{L\omega_1}\,\ee^{-Rt/(2L)}\sin(\omega_1 t)\,\theta(t) $$

Its meaning is “the charge $q(t)$ when a voltage pulse of area $1\ \mathrm{V\,s}$ is applied at time $0$.” Units. From the expression for the solution, $q(t)=\int\dd t'\,G^R(t-t')\,V(t')$, we get

$$ [G]=\frac{[q]}{[V][t]}=\frac{\mathrm{C}}{\mathrm{V\,s}}=\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{V}}=\Omega^{-1} $$

(we used $\mathrm{C/s=A}$). That is, this is the unit of conductance, the siemens $\mathrm{S}=\Omega^{-1}$. It agrees with $\mathrm{C/(V\,s)}=\Omega^{-1}$ given in the hint. It can also be confirmed from the prefactor: $[L\omega_1]=\mathrm{H/s}=(\mathrm{V\,s/A})/\mathrm{s}=\mathrm{V/A}=\Omega$, and since the prefactor is its reciprocal, the unit is $\Omega^{-1}$. This is the faithful counterpart of the mechanical $[G]=\mathrm{m/(N\,s)}$ (displacement per unit impulse).

(3) We substitute $L=1\ \mathrm{mH}=10^{-3}\ \mathrm{H}$, $C=1\ \mathrm{\mu F}=10^{-6}\ \mathrm{F}$, and $R=10\ \Omega$.

$$ \omega_0=\frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{10^{-9}\ \mathrm{s^2}}}=3.16\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{rad/s} \qquad\left(\frac{\omega_0}{2\pi}=5.03\ \mathrm{kHz}\right) $$ $$ \Gamma=\frac{R}{2L}=\frac{10}{2\times10^{-3}}=5.00\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}, \qquad \omega_1=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2}=\sqrt{10^{9}-2.5\times10^{7}}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}=3.12\times10^{4}\ \mathrm{rad/s} $$ $$ Q=\frac{\omega_0}{2\Gamma}=\frac{3.16\times10^{4}}{1.00\times10^{4}}=3.16 $$

All of these agree with the values in the hint. The damping is weak ($\Gamma<\omega_0$), and $\omega_1$ is only about $1.3\ \%$ lower than $\omega_0$.

(4) If $L,C$ are fixed, then $Q=\frac{1}{R}\sqrt{L/C}$ is inversely proportional to $R$. Therefore, to increase $Q$ by a factor of $10$ we need only reduce $R$ to $1/10$, that is, set $R=1\ \Omega$. This agrees with the hint. In a real circuit the losses of the wiring and of the coil are all absorbed into $R$, so raising $Q$ means, in short, reducing the dissipation—which is exactly the meaning of the definition $Q=\omega_0/2\Gamma$.

Exercise 2.6: The advanced Green's function and time reversal

Solution

(1) We derive $\dot{G}^A(0^-)=-1/m$ from the advanced condition. The matching condition of the main text,

$$ G(0^+)=G(0^-), \qquad \dot{G}(0^+)-\dot{G}(0^-)=\frac{1}{m} $$

is merely a consequence of integrating the defining equation $\hat{L}G=\delta(t)$ across the delta function, so it applies to any solution of $\hat{L}G=\delta$, retarded or advanced. The advanced condition $G^A(t)=0\ (t>0)$ gives $G^A(0^+)=0$ and $\dot{G}^A(0^+)=0$. Putting these into the matching condition, we obtain

$$ 0-\dot{G}^A(0^-)=\frac{1}{m} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \dot{G}^A(0^-)=-\frac{1}{m} $$

Continuity also gives $G^A(0^-)=G^A(0^+)=0$. What this says is that, in order to “stop at the instant it is kicked,” the system must have been rushing toward the origin with velocity $-1/m$ just beforehand (the impulse $+1$ of the impulsive force exactly cancels the momentum $-1$—here too, impulse equals change in momentum).

(2) For $t<0$ there is no delta function, so the equation is homogeneous, and the general solution can be written $G^A(t)=\ee^{-\Gamma t}(A\cos\omega_1t+B\sin\omega_1t)$. We impose the conditions of (1) as $t\to0^-$. First,

$$ G^A(0^-)=A=0 $$

Next, differentiating, we have

$$ \dot{G}^A(t)=\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left[-\Gamma(A\cos\omega_1t+B\sin\omega_1t)+\omega_1(-A\sin\omega_1t+B\cos\omega_1t)\right] \quad\xrightarrow{\ t\to0^-\ }\quad -\Gamma A+\omega_1B=\omega_1B=-\frac{1}{m} $$

so that $B=-1/(m\omega_1)$. Therefore

$$ G^A(t)=-\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin(\omega_1 t)\,\theta(-t) $$

has been shown. As a check, just before $t\to0^-$ ($\abs{t}$ small) we have $G^A\simeq-t/m>0$ and $\dot{G}^A\simeq-1/m$, which is consistent with (1) as a motion that rushes in toward the origin from the positive side and stops.

(3) For $t<0$ the envelope is $\ee^{-\Gamma t}=\ee^{\Gamma\abs{t}}$, so as $t\to-\infty$ the amplitude diverges exponentially. What this solution describes is the following motion: “an oscillation that had an infinitely large amplitude (and hence infinite energy) in the infinite past shrinks its amplitude while losing energy to friction, and is brought to rest exactly by the impulsive force at $t=0$.” Since the system is moving before it is kicked, this violates causality, and the divergence in the distant past is also incompatible with the physical boundary condition that “before the external field is applied, the system was quiet.” Therefore, even though it is a mathematically legitimate solution of $\hat{L}G=\delta$, it cannot be adopted physically. It is also a manifestation of the fact that a system with friction becomes an amplifier when viewed backward in time (this and (4) are two sides of the same coin).

(4) It does not hold. Computing explicitly, we substitute $s=-t$ into $G^R(s)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{-\Gamma s}\sin(\omega_1s)\,\theta(s)$ of the main text and obtain

$$ G^R(-t)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{+\Gamma t}\sin(-\omega_1t)\,\theta(-t) =-\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{+\Gamma t}\sin(\omega_1t)\,\theta(-t) $$

Comparing this with $G^A$ of (2), the $\sin$ and $\theta$ parts are the same, but the exponential factors disagree, $\ee^{+\Gamma t}$ versus $\ee^{-\Gamma t}$ (this is what the hint means by “$\ee^{+\Gamma t}$ appears, so the signs do not match”). Only for $\gamma=0$ do the two coincide.

The reason can be seen at the level of the operator. Under time reversal $t\to-t$ we have $\dd/\dd t\to-\dd/\dd t$, so

$$ \hat{L}=m\frac{\dd^2}{\dd t^2}+\gamma\frac{\dd}{\dd t}+k \quad\xrightarrow{\ t\to-t\ }\quad m\frac{\dd^2}{\dd t^2}-\gamma\frac{\dd}{\dd t}+k $$

The inertia term and the restoring-force term are invariant (an even-order derivative and a zeroth-order term), but only the friction term changes sign. Therefore what $G^R(-t)$ satisfies is the Green's function equation of the equation with $\gamma\to-\gamma$, and the place that must be corrected is the sign of the friction:

$$ G^A_\gamma(t)=G^R_{-\gamma}(-t) $$

Let us verify this. Since $\omega_1=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2}$ is an even function of $\Gamma$, it is invariant under $\gamma\to-\gamma$, and the retarded Green's function of the anti-friction system ($-\gamma$) is $G^R_{-\gamma}(s)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{+\Gamma s}\sin(\omega_1s)\,\theta(s)$—a solution whose amplitude grows. Setting $s=-t$, we get

$$ G^R_{-\gamma}(-t)=-\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin(\omega_1t)\,\theta(-t)=G^A_\gamma(t) $$

so the relation indeed holds. Friction breaks time-reversal symmetry—as the hint says—and the “amplification when going backward in time” of (3) is also a restatement of this sign reversal.

(5) First we carry out the one-sided Fourier transform in the convergence region $\mathrm{Im}\,\omega<-\Gamma$. Since $G^A$ has support only on $t<0$,

$$ G^A(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{0}G^A(t)\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\dd t =-\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin(\omega_1t)\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\dd t $$

Rewriting $\sin\omega_1t=\dfrac{\ee^{\ii\omega_1t}-\ee^{-\ii\omega_1t}}{2\ii}$ in terms of exponential functions, the integrand becomes the difference of $\ee^{a_+t}$ and $\ee^{a_-t}$. Here

$$ a_\pm=-\Gamma+\ii(\omega\pm\omega_1) $$

The integral $\int_{-\infty}^{0}\ee^{at}\dd t=1/a$ converges when $\mathrm{Re}\,a>0$. Writing $\omega=\mathrm{Re}\,\omega+\ii\,\mathrm{Im}\,\omega$, we have $\mathrm{Re}\,a_\pm=-\Gamma-\mathrm{Im}\,\omega$, so the convergence condition is exactly $\mathrm{Im}\,\omega<-\Gamma$—the convergence region stated in the problem. Hence

$$ G^A(\omega)=-\frac{1}{2\ii m\omega_1}\left[\frac{1}{a_+}-\frac{1}{a_-}\right] =-\frac{1}{2\ii m\omega_1}\cdot\frac{a_--a_+}{a_+a_-} $$

The numerator is $a_--a_+=-2\ii\omega_1$. The denominator is

$$ a_+a_-=\left(-\Gamma+\ii\omega\right)^2+\omega_1^2 =\Gamma^2-2\ii\Gamma\omega-\omega^2+\omega_1^2 =\omega_0^2-\omega^2-2\ii\Gamma\omega $$

(we used $\Gamma^2+\omega_1^2=\omega_0^2$). Substituting, and noting that $2m\Gamma=\gamma$, we obtain

$$ G^A(\omega)=-\frac{1}{2\ii m\omega_1}\cdot\frac{-2\ii\omega_1}{\omega_0^2-\omega^2-2\ii\Gamma\omega} =\frac{1}{m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)-\ii\gamma\omega} $$

This is exactly the same rational function as the Fourier transform of the retarded Green's function obtained in the main text, $G(\omega)=\left[m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)-\ii\gamma\omega\right]^{-1}$, and therefore the poles are also the same, $\omega_\pm=\pm\omega_1-\ii\Gamma$ (both in the lower half-plane).

The difference lies in the contour. We compare the contours $C$ of the inverse transform $G(t)=\int_C\frac{\dd\omega}{2\pi}\,G(\omega)\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$.

  • Retarded: Take $C$ to be the real axis ($\mathrm{Im}\,\omega=0$, above the two poles). For $t<0$ we close the contour in the upper half-plane; there is no pole inside the contour, so the result is zero. For $t>0$ we close it in the lower half-plane and pick up the two poles, obtaining a damped oscillation—exactly the calculation of Section 2.5.2 of the main text.
  • Advanced: Take $C$ to be a horizontal line $\mathrm{Im}\,\omega=c<-\Gamma$ inside the convergence region (below the two poles). This time, for $t>0$ closing in the lower half-plane encloses no poles and gives zero, whereas for $t<0$ closing in the upper half-plane picks up the two poles (counterclockwise) and yields the oscillation of $G^A(t)$.

In other words, even for the same function and the same poles, depending on whether the contour of the inverse transform passes above or below the two poles, the time-domain function splits into “the retarded one, supported on $t>0$” and “the advanced one, supported on $t<0$.” In the language of the two-sided Laplace transform, a single rational function has two convergence strips (the $\mathrm{Im}\,\omega>-\Gamma$ side and the $\mathrm{Im}\,\omega<-\Gamma$ side), and which strip we choose determines the time function.

Let us also check the caution in the hint. If we inverse-transform on the real axis the function $\left[m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)+\ii\gamma\omega\right]^{-1}$, which has its poles in the upper half-plane at $\pm\omega_1+\ii\Gamma$, then for $t>0$ we close below, there are no poles, and the result is zero, while for $t<0$ we close above and pick up the two poles. What we obtain is a function supported on $t<0$, but it is the $G^R(-t)=-\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{+\Gamma t}\sin(\omega_1t)\,\theta(-t)$ found in (4), and not $G^A(t)$ (the exponential factor is different). Indeed, if we write the Fourier transform of a function $g(t)$ as $g(\omega)$, then the transform of $g(-t)$ is $g(-\omega)$, and replacing $\omega\to-\omega$ in $G^R(\omega)$ gives exactly $\left[m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)+\ii\gamma\omega\right]^{-1}$. The statement “the poles of the advanced Green's function are in the upper half-plane” holds literally in the case of the prescription in which the damping is put in by hand as an infinitesimal $\eta\to0^+$, and this appears in Chapter 8, Section 8.3.

Common Mistake: Do not memorize “advanced = poles in the upper half-plane” by rote

For the oscillator of this chapter, which has finite friction $\gamma$, as we saw in Exercise 2.6(5), $G^A(\omega)$ is the same rational function as $G^R(\omega)$, and the poles of both lie in the lower half-plane at $\pm\omega_1-\ii\Gamma$. What distinguishes retarded from advanced is not the location of the poles but whether the contour of the inverse transform passes above or below the poles. The slogan “retarded has its poles in the lower half-plane, advanced in the upper half-plane” holds literally only for the prescription (Chapter 8, Section 8.3) in which an infinitesimal damping $\eta\to0^+$ is put by hand into a dissipation-free system and sent to zero at the end; applying the slogan as it is to a system in which the dissipation is in the equation from the start causes confusion.

Chapter 3: Forced Oscillation and the Response Function

Exercise 3.1: Computing the static limit in two ways

Solution

(1) Put $\omega=0$ into the relation $\chi(\omega)=\int_0^\infty G^R(s)\,\ee^{\ii\omega s}\,\dd s$ established in Chapter 3 to get $\chi(0)=\int_0^\infty G^R(s)\,\dd s$. This problem asks us to integrate this explicitly with the $G^R(s)$ of Chapter 2 and show that it equals $1/k$.

Strategy. What we substitute is

$$ G^R(s) = \frac{1}{m\omega_1}\,\ee^{-\Gamma s}\sin(\omega_1 s)\qquad(s>0), \qquad \Gamma=\frac{\gamma}{2m},\quad \omega_1^2=\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2 $$

As in Section 3.2.2, we first rewrite the sine as exponential functions with Euler's formula and then integrate.

$$ \chi(0) = \int_0^\infty G^R(s)\,\dd s = \frac{1}{m\omega_1}\int_0^\infty \ee^{-\Gamma s}\sin(\omega_1 s)\,\dd s $$

Carrying out the integral. Inserting $\sin(\omega_1 s)=\dfrac{\ee^{\ii\omega_1 s}-\ee^{-\ii\omega_1 s}}{2\ii}$ gives

$$ \int_0^\infty \ee^{-\Gamma s}\sin(\omega_1 s)\,\dd s = \frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\int_0^\infty \ee^{-(\Gamma-\ii\omega_1)s}\,\dd s - \int_0^\infty \ee^{-(\Gamma+\ii\omega_1)s}\,\dd s\right] $$

The real part of both exponents is $\Gamma=\gamma/2m>0$, so both integrals converge, and we may use $\int_0^\infty\ee^{-as}\dd s=1/a$ ($\mathrm{Re}\,a>0$).

$$ = \frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\frac{1}{\Gamma-\ii\omega_1}-\frac{1}{\Gamma+\ii\omega_1}\right] = \frac{1}{2\ii}\cdot\frac{(\Gamma+\ii\omega_1)-(\Gamma-\ii\omega_1)}{(\Gamma-\ii\omega_1)(\Gamma+\ii\omega_1)} = \frac{1}{2\ii}\cdot\frac{2\ii\omega_1}{\Gamma^2+\omega_1^2} = \frac{\omega_1}{\Gamma^2+\omega_1^2} $$

This is exactly the formula given in the hint. Therefore

$$ \chi(0) = \frac{1}{m\omega_1}\cdot\frac{\omega_1}{\Gamma^2+\omega_1^2} = \frac{1}{m\left(\Gamma^2+\omega_1^2\right)} $$

The factors of $\omega_1$ in the numerator and the denominator cancel, and using $\omega_1^2=\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2$ at the end gives

$$ \Gamma^2+\omega_1^2 = \Gamma^2+\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2 = \omega_0^2 $$

so $\Gamma^2$ disappears once more, in exactly the same way as in Step 8 of Section 3.2.2. Hence

$$ \chi(0) = \frac{1}{m\omega_0^2} = \frac{1}{k} $$

has been shown. ∎

Check. Putting $\omega=0$ into $\chi(\omega)=\left[m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)-\ii\gamma\omega\right]^{-1}$, derived in Chapter 3, also gives $\chi(0)=1/(m\omega_0^2)=1/k$. The two paths merge in the static limit as well. The result also agrees with the final value given in the hint.

(2) This problem asks us to explain physically that the equality in (1) means “the time integral of the displacement after a single kick equals the displacement under a steady static push.”

We first make clear what the left-hand and right-hand sides are. $G^R(s)$ is the displacement, a time $s$ later, of an oscillator that received a single kick of unit impulse at time $0$. Hence $\int_0^\infty G^R(s)\dd s$ is the sum of a single reply over its entire history. On the other hand, $\chi(0)$ is the coefficient that gives the static displacement when a constant force $F_0$ keeps being applied: $x_{\mathrm{static}}=\chi(0)F_0$.

The bridge between the two is the convolution. Put a constant force $F(t')=F_0$, acting since the infinite past, into the convolution $x(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G^R(t-t')\,F(t')\,\dd t'$ of Chapter 3, and change variables to $s=t-t'$. Then

$$ x = F_0\int_0^\infty G^R(s)\,\dd s $$

(the $t$ dependence disappears, so it also follows automatically that the response is stationary). This equation can be read as follows. A constant force is nothing other than a train of infinitesimal impulses $F_0\,\dd t'$ delivered at every instant of the past. An impulse delivered a time $s$ ago contributes $F_0\,G^R(s)\,\dd s$ to the present displacement. Since the system is invariant under time translation, “summing over all past kick times” and “summing the reply to a single kick over all the time after it” are one and the same integral. Hence the displacement under a steady static push (divided by $F_0$) is equal to the time integral of the reply to a single kick.

Physical meaning. In static equilibrium $\dot{x}=\ddot{x}=0$, so only the spring supports the force, and $x=F_0/k$. Neither friction nor inertia appears in the final answer. That $\Gamma$ (friction) and $\omega_1$ (a combination of inertia and friction) canceled out cleanly in the calculation of (1), leaving only $1/k$, is a manifestation of this mechanical fact. $G^R(s)$ is a damped oscillation that swings between positive and negative values, but the oscillating part cancels within the integral, and exactly $1/k$ remains as the net area. Note also that it is precisely because $\Gamma>0$ (dissipation) that this “sum of the memory” converges. In the language of Chapter 1, the static susceptibility is the total area of the memory function, $\chi(0)=\int_0^\infty\chi(\tau)\dd\tau$—and this is the simplest actual example of that statement.

Exercise 3.2: Phase lag and the three resonance frequencies

Solution

First we fix the notation. From $Q=m\omega_0/\gamma=\omega_0/(2\Gamma)$, for $Q=10$ we have

$$ \Gamma = \frac{\omega_0}{2Q} = 0.05\,\omega_0, \qquad \frac{\gamma}{m}=2\Gamma=0.1\,\omega_0 $$

Below, all answers are stated in units of $\omega_0$.

(1) We find the frequency at which $\chi'=0$. The denominator of

$$ \chi'(\omega) = \frac{m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)}{m^2(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)^2+\gamma^2\omega^2} $$

derived in Chapter 3 is always positive, so $\chi'=0$ holds when the numerator vanishes, that is, when $m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)=0$. The solution with $\omega>0$ is

$$ \omega = \omega_0 = 1.0000\,\omega_0 $$

This result does not contain $\gamma$, and it is exact regardless of the value of $Q$ (it is also the point at which the phase lag becomes $\delta=\pi/2$).

(2) We find numerically the frequency at which $\abs{\chi}$ is maximal. Putting $y=\omega^2$, we have $\abs{\chi}^2=1/D(y)$ with $D(y)=m^2(\omega_0^2-y)^2+\gamma^2 y$, so the maximum of $\abs{\chi}$ is the minimum of $D$.

$$ \frac{\dd D}{\dd y} = -2m^2(\omega_0^2-y)+\gamma^2 = 0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad y = \omega_0^2-\frac{\gamma^2}{2m^2} = \omega_0^2-2\Gamma^2 $$

(Since $\dd^2D/\dd y^2=2m^2>0$, this is indeed a minimum. It requires $\omega_0^2>2\Gamma^2$, that is, $Q>1/\sqrt{2}$, but this is satisfied with plenty of room for $Q=10$.) Therefore

$$ \omega = \sqrt{\omega_0^2-2\Gamma^2} = \omega_0\sqrt{1-2\times(0.05)^2} = \omega_0\sqrt{0.995} = 0.99750\,\omega_0 $$

(3) We find numerically the frequency $\omega_1$ of free decay. By the definition in Chapter 2,

$$ \omega_1 = \sqrt{\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2} = \omega_0\sqrt{1-(0.05)^2} = \omega_0\sqrt{0.9975} = 0.99875\,\omega_0 $$

(4) We find the frequencies at which the phase lag is $\pi/4$ and $3\pi/4$. We use

$$ \tan\delta = \frac{\gamma\omega}{m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)} $$

derived in Chapter 3. The case $\delta=\pi/4$ means $\tan\delta=+1$ and the first quadrant ($\chi'>0$, that is, $\omega<\omega_0$), and the case $\delta=3\pi/4$ means $\tan\delta=-1$ and the second quadrant ($\chi'<0$, that is, $\omega>\omega_0$). In either case the condition is

$$ m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2) = \pm\gamma\omega \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad m\omega^2 \pm \gamma\omega - m\omega_0^2 = 0 $$

which is the same quadratic equation that fixed the half-maximum points of the absorbed power in Section 3.6.1. By the quadratic formula, the solution with $\omega>0$ is

$$ \omega = \frac{\mp\gamma+\sqrt{\gamma^2+4m^2\omega_0^2}}{2m} = \mp\Gamma+\sqrt{\Gamma^2+\omega_0^2} $$

In the double sign, the upper sign is for $\delta=\pi/4$ (the side of $+\gamma\omega$, $\omega<\omega_0$) and the lower sign is for $\delta=3\pi/4$. Inserting the numbers, $\sqrt{\Gamma^2+\omega_0^2}=\omega_0\sqrt{1.0025}=1.001249\,\omega_0$, so

$$ \omega_{\pi/4} = (-0.05+1.001249)\,\omega_0 = 0.9512\,\omega_0, \qquad \omega_{3\pi/4} = (+0.05+1.001249)\,\omega_0 = 1.0512\,\omega_0 $$

The difference between the two is $\omega_{3\pi/4}-\omega_{\pi/4}=2\Gamma=0.1\,\omega_0=\gamma/m$, which coincides exactly with the full width at half maximum $\Delta\omega$. The correspondence with Section 3.6.1—the half-maximum points are the points where the phase deviates by $45^\circ$ from $\pi/2$—has thus been confirmed numerically.

Summary. We line up the four answers.

Characteristic frequencies of an oscillator with $Q=10$ (in units of $\omega_0$)
QuantityFormulaValue
(1) $\chi'=0$ ($\delta=\pi/2$, maximum absorbed power)$\omega_0$$1.0000$
(2) Maximum of $\abs{\chi}$$\sqrt{\omega_0^2-2\Gamma^2}$$0.99750$
(3) Free decay$\omega_1=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-\Gamma^2}$$0.99875$
(4) $\delta=\pi/4$$-\Gamma+\sqrt{\Gamma^2+\omega_0^2}$$0.9512$
(4) $\delta=3\pi/4$$+\Gamma+\sqrt{\Gamma^2+\omega_0^2}$$1.0512$

Physical meaning. The values (1), (2) and (3) satisfy $0.99750<0.99875<1.0000$ and differ from one another by only $O(1/Q^2)$, so, as the warning in Section 3.4.2 says, even a single phrase like “resonance frequency” gives different values depending on the definition. On the other hand, the two points of (4) are $O(1/Q)$ away from the resonance, that is, an order of magnitude farther. The shift in the position of the resonance is a $1/Q^2$ detail, but the width of the resonance is a quantity of order $1/Q$—if you get a numerical feel for this hierarchy, you will not be confused when reading experimental spectra.

Exercise 3.3: The case of a $\sin$ force, and the $2\omega$ component of the instantaneous power

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to redo the calculation of Section 3.5.2 with the external force replaced by $F(t)=F_0\sin\omega t$, and to show that the average power comes out the same, $\frac{1}{2}\omega F_0^2\chi''$.

Step 1: Decomposing the sine into two exponentials. From $\sin\omega t=\dfrac{\ee^{\ii\omega t}-\ee^{-\ii\omega t}}{2\ii}$ and $\dfrac{1}{2\ii}=-\dfrac{\ii}{2}$ we obtain

$$ F(t) = F_0\sin\omega t = \frac{\ii F_0}{2}\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t} - \frac{\ii F_0}{2}\,\ee^{+\ii\omega t} $$

In the convention of this book, $\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$ is the component of frequency $+\omega$ (complex amplitude $\ii F_0/2$) and $\ee^{+\ii\omega t}$ is the component of frequency $-\omega$ (complex amplitude $-\ii F_0/2$). The only difference from the $\cos$ drive is that the amplitudes of the components are purely imaginary, $\pm\ii F_0/2$, instead of the real number $F_0/2$.

Step 2: Adding the responses by linearity. Multiplying each component by $\chi(\omega)$ and $\chi(-\omega)$, respectively, we get

$$ x(t) = \chi(\omega)\,\frac{\ii F_0}{2}\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t} + \chi(-\omega)\left(-\frac{\ii F_0}{2}\right)\ee^{+\ii\omega t} $$

Using the reality condition $\chi(-\omega)=\chi(\omega)^*$ that we verified in Chapter 3, the second term is the complex conjugate of the first (note also that $-\ii F_0/2=(\ii F_0/2)^*$ and $\ee^{+\ii\omega t}=(\ee^{-\ii\omega t})^*$). Since $z+z^*=2\,\mathrm{Re}\,z$,

$$ x(t) = 2\,\mathrm{Re}\left[\frac{\ii F_0}{2}\,\chi(\omega)\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}\right] = F_0\,\mathrm{Re}\left[\ii\chi(\omega)\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}\right] $$

Step 3: Expanding the real part. Since $\ii\chi=\ii(\chi'+\ii\chi'')=-\chi''+\ii\chi'$ and $\ee^{-\ii\omega t}=\cos\omega t-\ii\sin\omega t$,

$$ \ii\chi\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t} = (-\chi''+\ii\chi')(\cos\omega t-\ii\sin\omega t) = \left(\chi'\sin\omega t-\chi''\cos\omega t\right) + \ii\left(\chi'\cos\omega t+\chi''\sin\omega t\right) $$

Therefore the steady-state response is

$$ x(t) = F_0\left[\chi'(\omega)\sin\omega t - \chi''(\omega)\cos\omega t\right] $$

This agrees with the formula in the hint. Check: $\sin\omega t=\cos(\omega t-\pi/2)$ is the $\cos$ drive delayed by the time $\pi/(2\omega)$, so by time-translation invariance it suffices to replace $\omega t\to\omega t-\pi/2$ in the answer for the $\cos$ drive in Section 3.5.1, $x=F_0[\chi'\cos\omega t+\chi''\sin\omega t]$. Since $\cos(\omega t-\pi/2)=\sin\omega t$ and $\sin(\omega t-\pi/2)=-\cos\omega t$, the same expression results.

Step 4: Velocity and power. Differentiating, we obtain

$$ \dot{x}(t) = F_0\,\omega\left[\chi'\cos\omega t + \chi''\sin\omega t\right] $$ $$ \frac{\dd W}{\dd t} = F(t)\,\dot{x}(t) = F_0\sin\omega t\cdot F_0\omega\left[\chi'\cos\omega t+\chi''\sin\omega t\right] = F_0^2\,\omega\left[\chi'\sin\omega t\cos\omega t + \chi''\sin^2\omega t\right] $$

Step 5: Averaging over one period. As in Section 3.5.2, we average over $T=2\pi/\omega$. We have $\braket{\sin\omega t\cos\omega t}=\braket{\frac{1}{2}\sin2\omega t}=0$ (the same integral as in Section 3.5.2, (i)), and by the half-angle formula $\sin^2\theta=\frac{1-\cos2\theta}{2}$,

$$ \braket{\sin^2\omega t} = \frac{1}{T}\int_0^T\frac{1-\cos2\omega t}{2}\,\dd t = \frac{1}{2} $$

(the $\cos2\omega t$ term goes through exactly two cycles in one period and averages to zero). Therefore

$$ \left\langle\frac{\dd W}{\dd t}\right\rangle = F_0^2\,\omega\left[\chi'\cdot0+\chi''\cdot\frac{1}{2}\right] = \frac{1}{2}\,\omega F_0^2\,\chi''(\omega) $$

∎ This is exactly the same as for the $\cos$ drive. It is also what we should expect: the period average cannot depend on the phase of the drive (the choice of time origin), so the average power is the same whether the drive is $\cos$ or $\sin$.

(2) This problem asks us to write the instantaneous power $\dd W/\dd t$ as the sum of a constant term and a term of frequency $2\omega$. It suffices to insert the double-angle formulas $\sin\omega t\cos\omega t=\frac{1}{2}\sin2\omega t$ and $\sin^2\omega t=\frac{1}{2}(1-\cos2\omega t)$ into the expression of Step 4.

$$ \frac{\dd W}{\dd t} = F_0^2\,\omega\left[\frac{\chi'}{2}\sin2\omega t + \frac{\chi''}{2}\left(1-\cos2\omega t\right)\right] $$
$$ \frac{\dd W}{\dd t} = \underbrace{\frac{F_0^2\omega}{2}\,\chi''}_{\text{const. (mean dissipation)}} \;+\; \underbrace{\frac{F_0^2\omega}{2}\left[\chi'\sin2\omega t - \chi''\cos2\omega t\right]}_{\text{term at frequency }2\omega\text{ (mean zero)}} $$

This agrees with the formula in the hint. Check: At $t=0$ the whole expression, including the term outside the square brackets, becomes $\frac{F_0^2\omega}{2}[\chi''+0-\chi'']=0$. For the $\sin$ drive we have $F(0)=0$, so the instantaneous power should vanish, and it does. For the $\cos$ drive of Section 3.5.2, the same calculation gives

$$ \frac{\dd W}{\dd t} = \frac{F_0^2\omega}{2}\,\chi'' + \frac{F_0^2\omega}{2}\left[-\chi'\sin2\omega t + \chi''\cos2\omega t\right] $$

so the constant term is the same, and only the signs of both oscillating terms are reversed. This is because shifting the phase of the drive by $\pi/2$ shifts the phase of the term of doubled frequency $2\omega$ by $\pi$ (that is, it flips the sign).

(3) This problem asks for the condition under which $\dd W/\dd t$ becomes negative even for an instant (the system returns work to the force).

Combining the oscillating part into a single sine. The amplitude of the real expression $a\sin\theta+b\cos\theta$ is $\sqrt{a^2+b^2}$. Here $a=\chi'$ and $b=-\chi''$, so the amplitude of the oscillating part is

$$ \sqrt{\chi'^2+\chi''^2} = \abs{\chi(\omega)} $$

That is, using a suitable phase $\varphi$, we can write

$$ \frac{\dd W}{\dd t} = \frac{F_0^2\omega}{2}\left[\chi'' + \abs{\chi}\sin(2\omega t-\varphi)\right] $$

Since $\sin$ swings down to $-1$, the minimum of the instantaneous power is

$$ \left(\frac{\dd W}{\dd t}\right)_{\min} = \frac{F_0^2\omega}{2}\left(\chi''-\abs{\chi}\right) $$

The condition for this to be negative is $\abs{\chi}>\chi''$, that is,

$$ \chi'^2+\chi''^2 > \chi''^2 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \chi'(\omega)\ne0 $$
$$ \frac{\dd W}{\dd t}\ \text{becomes negative even for an instant} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \chi'(\omega)\ne0 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \omega\ne\omega_0 $$

Conversely, at resonance $\omega=\omega_0$ we have exactly $\chi'=0$, so

$$ \frac{\dd W}{\dd t} = \frac{F_0^2\omega_0}{2}\,\chi''\left(1-\cos2\omega_0 t\right) \ge 0 $$

and the instantaneous power is always nonnegative (it vanishes only at the instants when $\cos2\omega_0t=1$, that is, momentarily once every half period). ∎

Physical meaning. Away from resonance, the “store and return” response carried by $\chi'$—the exchange between the elastic energy of the spring and the kinetic energy—survives, and during part of each period the system returns work to the external force. This is the reactive power described in Section 3.5.2. Exactly at resonance, force and velocity are perfectly in phase ($\delta=\pi/2$), so $F\dot{x}$ takes a $\cos^2$ form and is never negative; energy flows into the system one way and all of it becomes heat. This is the picture “resonance = a drive that gives the system no time to return the energy.”

Exercise 3.4: A quartz oscillator (numerical)

Solution

The given data are $f_0=32768\ \mathrm{Hz}$ and $Q=1.0\times10^5$. Intermediate numbers are shown rounded, but the calculation itself is carried out without rounding.

(1) We find the full width at half maximum of the resonance in units of $\mathrm{Hz}$. As shown in Section 3.6.1, the full width at half maximum of the absorbed power is exactly $\Delta\omega=\gamma/m=\omega_0/Q$. To convert to a frequency (in $\mathrm{Hz}$) we divide both sides by $2\pi$, so

$$ \Delta f = \frac{\Delta\omega}{2\pi} = \frac{f_0}{Q} = \frac{32768}{1.0\times10^5} = 0.328\ \mathrm{Hz} \simeq 0.33\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

A resonance at $32768\ \mathrm{Hz}$ has a width of only $0.33\ \mathrm{Hz}$—an extremely sharp resonance with a relative width of $10^{-5}$.

(2) We find the time $\tau=2Q/\omega_0$ for the amplitude to fall to $1/\ee$ after the drive is switched off. First,

$$ \omega_0 = 2\pi f_0 = 2\pi\times32768 = 2.059\times10^5\ \mathrm{rad/s} $$

Therefore

$$ \tau = \frac{2Q}{\omega_0} = \frac{2\times1.0\times10^5}{2.059\times10^5} = 0.971\ \mathrm{s} \simeq 0.97\ \mathrm{s} $$

This is about one second. During this time the oscillator oscillates as many as $f_0\tau=Q/\pi\simeq3.2\times10^4$ times. We can also read this as follows: “the $Q$ factor tells us that the oscillator can oscillate about $Q/\pi$ times during the ringdown.” Note also that $\tau\Delta\omega=2$; that is, the lifetime in the time domain and the width in the frequency domain are reciprocals of each other, which is a concrete example of “narrow peak = long memory” in Section 3.6.1.

(3) We find the error of the clock per day when the frequency is off by a relative amount of $10^{-5}$ (of order $Q^{-1}$). The rate of a clock is proportional to the frequency, so a relative frequency error $\varepsilon=10^{-5}$ is directly a relative time error. One day is $86400\ \mathrm{s}$, so

$$ \delta t = \varepsilon \times 86400\ \mathrm{s} = 10^{-5}\times86400\ \mathrm{s} = 0.864\ \mathrm{s} \simeq 0.86\ \mathrm{s/day} $$

The error is about $0.9$ seconds per day—this is the error for “misreading the frequency by the width of the resonance curve.” The accuracy of an actual wristwatch (a monthly error of about $\pm15$ seconds, which is about $0.5$ seconds when converted to a daily error; one to two orders of magnitude better still with temperature compensation) is better than this. In other words, the oscillator circuit locks onto the resonance center with an accuracy far better than $Q^{-1}$, well inside the width $\Delta f$. What makes this possible is the phase condition described in the lower panel of Figure 3.2: the point where $\delta=\pi/2$ ($\chi'=0$) is exactly $\omega_0$ regardless of $Q$, and near resonance the phase changes rapidly by as much as $\pi/2$ over the width $\Delta\omega$, so watching the phase turns a slight deviation from the center into a large signal. Since the oscillator circuit keeps oscillating at the frequency that automatically satisfies the phase condition, what limits the accuracy of the clock is not the linewidth but the temperature drift and aging of $\omega_0$ itself.

Exercise 3.5: Proving the sum rule with the residue theorem (a preview of Chapter 5)

Solution

What we must show is the first of the sum-rule formulas stated in Chapter 3,

$$ \int_0^\infty \omega\,\chi''(\omega)\,\dd\omega = \frac{\pi}{2m} $$

We use $\chi''(\omega)=\dfrac{\gamma\omega}{m^2(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)^2+\gamma^2\omega^2}$.

(1) This problem asks us to use the even–odd symmetry of the integrand to extend the integral to the whole real axis. As we verified in Chapter 3, $\chi''(-\omega)=-\chi''(\omega)$ (an odd function), and $\omega$ is also an odd function, so the product $\omega\chi''(\omega)$ is an even function. In fact,

$$ (-\omega)\,\chi''(-\omega) = (-\omega)\left(-\chi''(\omega)\right) = \omega\,\chi''(\omega) $$

The integral of an even function over $[0,\infty)$ is half of its integral over the whole real axis, so

$$ I \equiv \int_0^\infty \omega\,\chi''(\omega)\,\dd\omega = \frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \omega\,\chi''(\omega)\,\dd\omega $$

(2) This problem asks us to find the locations of the poles of the integrand. Writing the denominator as $D(\omega)\equiv m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)-\ii\gamma\omega$, we have, for real $\omega$,

$$ \chi''(\omega) = \mathrm{Im}\,\frac{1}{D(\omega)} = \frac{\gamma\omega}{\abs{D(\omega)}^2}, \qquad \abs{D}^2 = D\,D^* $$

Now we check the given factorization. For $\omega_\pm=\pm\omega_1-\ii\Gamma$ we have

$$ \omega_++\omega_- = -2\ii\Gamma, \qquad \omega_+\omega_- = (\omega_1-\ii\Gamma)(-\omega_1-\ii\Gamma) = -(\omega_1^2+\Gamma^2) = -\omega_0^2 $$

(in the last step we used $\omega_1^2+\Gamma^2=\omega_0^2$). Hence

$$ -m(\omega-\omega_+)(\omega-\omega_-) = -m\left[\omega^2-(\omega_++\omega_-)\omega+\omega_+\omega_-\right] = -m\left[\omega^2+2\ii\Gamma\omega-\omega_0^2\right] = m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)-\ii\gamma\omega $$

Thus, using $2m\Gamma=\gamma$, we confirm that $D(\omega)=-m(\omega-\omega_+)(\omega-\omega_-)$. Likewise, taking the complex conjugate, for real $\omega$ we obtain $D^*(\omega)=-m(\omega-\omega_+^*)(\omega-\omega_-^*)$ with $\omega_\pm^*=\pm\omega_1+\ii\Gamma$. Therefore

$$ \omega\,\chi''(\omega) = \frac{\gamma\,\omega^2} {m^2(\omega-\omega_+)(\omega-\omega_-)(\omega-\omega_+^*)(\omega-\omega_-^*)} $$

If we read the right-hand side as a rational function $f(\omega)$ of the complex variable $\omega$, then $f$ coincides with $\omega\chi''$ on the real axis, and its poles are the zeros of the denominator, that is,

$$ \omega = \pm\omega_1-\ii\Gamma\ \ (\text{two in the lower half-plane}), \qquad \omega = \pm\omega_1+\ii\Gamma\ \ (\text{two in the upper half-plane}) $$

the four points $\pm\omega_1\pm\ii\Gamma$, all of which are simple poles. The two in the lower half-plane are poles of $\chi(\omega)$ itself (the natural oscillation of Chapter 2); the two in the upper half-plane are “mirror-image” poles that come from the factor $D^*$ that was multiplied in when forming $\abs{D}^2$. For later use, we also note the two symmetry relations

$$ \omega_-^* = -\omega_+,\qquad \omega_- = -\omega_+^* $$

(indeed, $\omega_-^*=-\omega_1+\ii\Gamma=-(\omega_1-\ii\Gamma)=-\omega_+$).

(3) This problem asks us to carry out the integral by using the residue theorem with a contour closed in the upper half-plane.

Step 1: Confirming that the contribution of the large semicircle vanishes. For $\abs{\omega}=R\to\infty$ the function decays as $f(\omega)\sim\gamma\omega^2/(m^2\omega^4)=\gamma/(m^2\omega^2)$, so the integral over the upper semicircle $C_R$ of radius $R$ satisfies

$$ \left|\int_{C_R} f\,\dd\omega\right| \le \pi R\cdot\frac{C}{R^2} \xrightarrow{\ R\to\infty\ } 0 $$

(where $C$ is a constant). Hence the integral along the real axis equals the closed-contour integral, and by the residue theorem

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} f(\omega)\,\dd\omega = 2\pi\ii\sum_{\text{poles in the upper half-plane}}\Res f $$

The poles in the upper half-plane are the two points $\omega_+^*=\omega_1+\ii\Gamma$ and $\omega_-^*=-\omega_1+\ii\Gamma$.

Step 2: The residue at $\omega=\omega_+^*$. Since this is a simple pole, it suffices to remove its factor and substitute the value of the pole into what remains. We write out all the differences that we need.

$$ \omega_+^*-\omega_+ = 2\ii\Gamma, \qquad \omega_+^*-\omega_- = \omega_+^*+\omega_+^* = 2\omega_+^*, \qquad \omega_+^*-\omega_-^* = 2\omega_1 $$

(in the second one we used $\omega_-=-\omega_+^*$). Therefore

$$ \Res_{\omega=\omega_+^*} f = \frac{\gamma\,(\omega_+^*)^2}{m^2\,(2\ii\Gamma)(2\omega_+^*)(2\omega_1)} = \frac{\gamma\,\omega_+^*}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1} $$

Step 3: The residue at $\omega=\omega_-^*$. Similarly, using $\omega_-^*=-\omega_+$,

$$ \omega_-^*-\omega_+ = -2\omega_+, \qquad \omega_-^*-\omega_- = -\omega_++\omega_+^* = 2\ii\Gamma, \qquad \omega_-^*-\omega_+^* = -2\omega_1 $$

Also, since $(\omega_-^*)^2=(-\omega_+)^2=\omega_+^2$,

$$ \Res_{\omega=\omega_-^*} f = \frac{\gamma\,\omega_+^2}{m^2\,(-2\omega_+)(2\ii\Gamma)(-2\omega_1)} = \frac{\gamma\,\omega_+}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1} $$

The two residues now differ only in whether the numerator is $\omega_+^*$ or $\omega_+$.

Step 4: Adding them. Since $\omega_+^*+\omega_+=(\omega_1+\ii\Gamma)+(\omega_1-\ii\Gamma)=2\omega_1$,

$$ \sum\Res f = \frac{\gamma\,(\omega_+^*+\omega_+)}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1} = \frac{\gamma\cdot2\omega_1}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1} = \frac{\gamma}{4\ii\,m^2\Gamma} = \frac{2m\Gamma}{4\ii\,m^2\Gamma} = \frac{1}{2\ii\,m} $$

The $\omega_1$ has canceled, and with $\gamma=2m\Gamma$ the $\Gamma$ has canceled as well. Here $\gamma$ and $\omega_0$ drop out completely—this is the very place, in the calculation, where we see that the sum rule depends neither on the damping nor on the natural frequency.

Step 5: Putting it together.

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\omega\,\chi''(\omega)\,\dd\omega = 2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{1}{2\ii m} = \frac{\pi}{m} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad I = \frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\pi}{m} = \frac{\pi}{2m} $$

∎ Just as the hint announced, both $\gamma$ and $\omega_0$ have disappeared, and only $\pi/2m$ remains.

Supplement: The second formula follows from the same contour. Let the integrand be $g(\omega)\equiv\chi''(\omega)/\omega=\gamma/\abs{D}^2$. This is an even function (at $\omega=0$ it is finite, equal to $\gamma/(m^2\omega_0^4)$, so there is no pole there), and it decays as $\omega^{-4}$ for large $\abs{\omega}$, so the same contour in the upper half-plane can be used. The poles of $g$ are the same four points as those of $f$, and the residues are built from the same products of differences as above, the only change being that the numerator $\omega^2$ is absent.

$$ \Res_{\omega=\omega_+^*} g = \frac{\gamma}{m^2\,(2\ii\Gamma)(2\omega_+^*)(2\omega_1)} = \frac{\gamma}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1\,\omega_+^*}, \qquad \Res_{\omega=\omega_-^*} g = \frac{\gamma}{m^2\,(-2\omega_+)(2\ii\Gamma)(-2\omega_1)} = \frac{\gamma}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1\,\omega_+} $$

Summing them, and using $\omega_+\omega_+^*=\abs{\omega_+}^2=\omega_1^2+\Gamma^2=\omega_0^2$, we obtain

$$ \sum\Res = \frac{\gamma}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1}\cdot\frac{\omega_++\omega_+^*}{\omega_+\omega_+^*} = \frac{\gamma\cdot2\omega_1}{8\ii\,m^2\Gamma\omega_1\,\omega_0^2} = \frac{1}{2\ii\,m\omega_0^2} $$

Hence $\displaystyle\int_0^\infty\frac{\chi''(\omega)}{\omega}\dd\omega=\frac{1}{2}\cdot2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{1}{2\ii m\omega_0^2}=\frac{\pi}{2m\omega_0^2}=\frac{\pi}{2}\chi(0)$, and the second formula stated in Chapter 3 is also confirmed. This time only $\gamma$ drops out, and $\omega_0$ remains. This formula is nothing other than the value at $\omega=0$ of the Kramers–Kronig relations that we derive in Chapter 4.

Physical meaning. The right-hand side $\pi/2m$ of the first formula is determined by the mass alone. At high frequencies $\chi\to-1/(m\omega^2)$; that is, however complicated the system, if we shake it fast enough it responds as a bare point mass, as we saw in Section 3.1.3(b). The quantity $\int_0^\infty\omega\chi''\dd\omega$ is tied to this universal high-frequency behavior, and that is precisely why it cannot depend on $\gamma$ or $\omega_0$—the “low-energy circumstances” of spring and friction. This is the prototype of the $f$-sum rule of Chapter 4 and of $\int_0^\infty\omega S(\qq,\omega)\dd\omega=\hbar q^2/2m$ of Chapter 12.

Exercise 3.6: Connecting the Drude and Lorentz models

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to take the limit $\omega_0\to0$ in the dielectric function of the Lorentz oscillator derived in Chapter 3 and so obtain the Drude form. The starting point is

$$ \epsilon(\omega) = 1 + \frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega_0^2-\omega^2-\ii\tilde{\gamma}\omega}, \qquad \omega_p^2=\frac{4\pi ne^2}{m},\quad \tilde\gamma=\frac{\gamma}{m} $$

If we remove the binding (spring constant $k\to0$, that is, $\omega_0\to0$), only the $\omega_0^2$ term in the denominator drops out, and we obtain

$$ \epsilon(\omega) = 1 + \frac{\omega_p^2}{-\omega^2-\ii\tilde\gamma\omega} = 1 - \frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2+\ii\tilde\gamma\omega} $$

This is the formula we were asked to show. ∎

Check: Consistency with the Drude conductivity. Let us substitute $\sigma(\omega)=\dfrac{ne^2/m}{\tilde\gamma-\ii\omega}$, derived in Section 3.7.2, into the Gaussian-units relation $\epsilon(\omega)=1+\dfrac{4\pi\ii\sigma(\omega)}{\omega}$. From the identity $\tilde\gamma-\ii\omega=-\ii(\omega+\ii\tilde\gamma)$ we have $\dfrac{\ii}{\tilde\gamma-\ii\omega}=-\dfrac{1}{\omega+\ii\tilde\gamma}$, so

$$ 1+\frac{4\pi\ii\sigma}{\omega} = 1 + \frac{4\pi ne^2}{m}\cdot\frac{\ii}{\omega(\tilde\gamma-\ii\omega)} = 1 - \frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega(\omega+\ii\tilde\gamma)} = 1 - \frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2+\ii\tilde\gamma\omega} $$

This agrees exactly with the result of (1). “Cutting the spring of a bound electron” and “the Drude response of a conduction electron” are two ways of restating the same physics.

(2) This problem asks us to find the frequency at which the real part $\epsilon'\simeq1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$ for $\tilde\gamma\ll\omega$ vanishes. First we obtain the real part. Rationalizing the denominator,

$$ \epsilon(\omega) = 1 - \frac{\omega_p^2\left(\omega^2-\ii\tilde\gamma\omega\right)}{\left(\omega^2\right)^2+\tilde\gamma^2\omega^2} = 1 - \frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2+\tilde\gamma^2} + \ii\,\frac{\omega_p^2\,\tilde\gamma}{\omega\left(\omega^2+\tilde\gamma^2\right)} $$

For $\tilde\gamma\ll\omega$ we drop $\tilde\gamma^2$ in the denominator and obtain $\epsilon'\simeq1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$ (the formula in the problem statement). This vanishes when

$$ 1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \omega = \omega_p $$

that is, at the plasma frequency itself. For $\omega<\omega_p$ we have $\epsilon'<0$ (the wave cannot penetrate and is totally reflected), and for $\omega>\omega_p$ we have $0<\epsilon'<1$ (transparent), so the optical response of a metal changes completely across $\omega_p$. Also, as stated in Section 3.7.2, $\epsilon=0$ is the condition for a collective longitudinal oscillation (a plasmon) to sustain itself, and it is the gateway to the main subject of Chapter 11.
