(3) This problem asks us to compute $\omega_p$ and the corresponding wavelength for aluminum ($n=1.8\times10^{29}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$), and to explain why it looks silvery. We substitute $e=1.602\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{C}$, $\epsilon_0=8.854\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{F/m}$, and $m=9.109\times10^{-31}\ \mathrm{kg}$ into the SI formula $\omega_p^2=ne^2/(\epsilon_0 m)$. Evaluating the numerator and the denominator separately,

$$ ne^2 = 1.8\times10^{29}\times\left(1.602\times10^{-19}\right)^2 = 4.620\times10^{-9}, \qquad \epsilon_0 m = 8.854\times10^{-12}\times9.109\times10^{-31} = 8.065\times10^{-42} $$

(SI units are omitted). Hence

$$ \omega_p^2 = \frac{4.620\times10^{-9}}{8.065\times10^{-42}} = 5.728\times10^{32}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}}, \qquad \omega_p = 2.393\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{rad/s} \simeq 2.4\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{rad/s} $$

The corresponding wavelength (in vacuum) is

$$ \lambda_p = \frac{2\pi c}{\omega_p} = \frac{2\pi\times2.998\times10^8}{2.393\times10^{16}} = 7.87\times10^{-8}\ \mathrm{m} = 78.7\ \mathrm{nm} \simeq 79\ \mathrm{nm} $$

This lies in the deeper part of the ultraviolet region (the vacuum ultraviolet). Converted to energy, $\hbar\omega_p\simeq15.8\ \mathrm{eV}$, which agrees well with the plasmon energy of Al measured by electron energy-loss spectroscopy (about $15$ $\mathrm{eV}$)—a number we will meet again in Chapters 11 and 12.

Why it looks silvery. Converting the visible wavelengths $400$–$700\ \mathrm{nm}$ to angular frequencies gives $\omega=2\pi c/\lambda=2.7\times10^{15}$–$4.7\times10^{15}\ \mathrm{rad/s}$, all of which are only $1/5$–$1/9$ of $\omega_p$. Therefore, throughout the visible range,

$$ \epsilon' \simeq 1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2} < 0 $$

This inequality holds (for example, at $\lambda=550\ \mathrm{nm}$ we have $\epsilon'\simeq1-(7.0)^2\simeq-48$, which is large and negative). The wave number in the medium is $k=\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\omega/c$, so if $\epsilon'<0$ (and $\epsilon''$ is small), $k$ is almost purely imaginary; the wave cannot propagate inside the metal and becomes an evanescent wave decaying as $\ee^{-\abs{k}z}$ (the penetration depth is of order $c/\left(\sqrt{\abs{\epsilon'}}\,\omega\right)\sim c/\omega_p\simeq13\ \mathrm{nm}$). Almost all of the energy is reflected before it can be absorbed. Because this total reflection occurs in the same way for every color in the visible range, the reflection spectrum of Al is nearly flat across the visible range, giving a high reflectance with no particular coloration—that is, the “silvery” metallic luster. Conversely, for vacuum-ultraviolet light with $\lambda<\lambda_p\simeq79\ \mathrm{nm}$, $\epsilon'>0$ and the metal becomes transparent (ultraviolet transmission). That a mirror reflects visible light without discrimination, and that the same metal becomes see-through in the deep ultraviolet, are both determined by a single thing: the sign of $\epsilon'=1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$. ∎

Physical meaning. Gold and copper look colored rather than silvery because, in addition to the Drude term, bound transitions from the $d$ band—precisely the Lorentz-oscillator term—come to lie in the visible range and lower the reflectance on the blue side. The “Drude + Lorentz” sum that we have connected in this exercise is exactly the standard description of the optics of real metals.

Chapter 4: Causality and the Kramers–Kronig Relations

Exercise 4.1: Verifying the KK relations with Debye relaxation

Solution

(1) We split $\chi(\omega)=\chi_0/(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)$ into its real and imaginary parts and check the even–odd symmetry. Multiplying the numerator and denominator by the complex conjugate of the denominator, $1+\ii\omega\tau_0$, rationalizes it. The denominator becomes

$$ (1-\ii\omega\tau_0)(1+\ii\omega\tau_0)=1-(\ii\omega\tau_0)^2=1+\omega^2\tau_0^2 $$

so that

$$ \chi(\omega)=\frac{\chi_0\,(1+\ii\omega\tau_0)}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \chi'(\omega)=\frac{\chi_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2}, \qquad \chi''(\omega)=\frac{\chi_0\,\omega\tau_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} $$

The denominator contains $\omega$ only in the form $\omega^2$, so it is invariant under $\omega\to-\omega$. As for the numerators, that of $\chi'$ is a constant (even) and that of $\chi''$ is linear in $\omega$ (odd). Therefore $\chi'(-\omega)=\chi'(\omega)$ (even function) and $\chi''(-\omega)=-\chi''(\omega)$ (odd function), which is exactly the even–odd symmetry derived in Section 4.5.1 from the symmetry $\chi(-\omega)=\chi(\omega)^*$ of a real response function. We can also see that if $\chi_0>0$ and $\tau_0>0$, then $\chi''>0$ for $\omega>0$; in other words, the absorption is positive at positive frequencies.

(2) We substitute this $\chi''$ into the right-hand side of the Kramers–Kronig (KK) relation $\chi'(\omega)=\dfrac{1}{\pi}\PV\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dfrac{\chi''(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega}\dd\omega'$, carry out the principal-value integral by elementary means, and show that the left-hand side is reproduced. Following the hint, we set $\Gamma_0\equiv1/\tau_0$. Dividing the numerator and denominator of $\chi''$ by $\tau_0^2$ gives

$$ \chi''(\omega')=\frac{\chi_0\,\omega'\tau_0}{1+\omega'^2\tau_0^2} =\frac{\chi_0\,\omega'/\tau_0}{1/\tau_0^2+\omega'^2} =\frac{\chi_0\,\Gamma_0\,\omega'}{\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2} $$

Therefore the quantity to be computed is

$$ \frac{1}{\pi}\PV\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\chi''(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega}\dd\omega' =\frac{\chi_0\Gamma_0}{\pi}\,J, \qquad J\equiv\PV\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\omega'\,\dd\omega'}{(\omega'-\omega)(\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2)} $$

Decompose into partial fractions. By the same procedure as in Section 4.6.2, we write

$$ \frac{\omega'}{(\omega'-\omega)(\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2)} =\frac{A}{\omega'-\omega}+\frac{B\omega'+C}{\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2} $$

and multiplying both sides by $(\omega'-\omega)(\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2)$ gives

$$ \omega'=A(\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2)+(B\omega'+C)(\omega'-\omega) $$

Substituting $\omega'=\omega$ gives $\omega=A(\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2)$, that is, $A=\dfrac{\omega}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2}$.
Comparing the coefficients of $\omega'^2$ gives $0=A+B$, that is, $B=-A$.
Comparing the constant terms gives $0=A\Gamma_0^2-C\omega$, that is, $C=\dfrac{A\Gamma_0^2}{\omega}$.

Carry out the three integrals. We compute on the symmetric interval $[-R,R]$ and let $R\to\infty$. All three are the same as those derived in Step 3 of Section 4.6.2 of the main text:

$$ \PV\int_{-R}^{R}\frac{\dd\omega'}{\omega'-\omega}\xrightarrow{\ R\to\infty\ }0, \qquad \int_{-R}^{R}\frac{\omega'\,\dd\omega'}{\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2}=0\ \ (\text{odd}), \qquad \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega'}{\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2}=\frac{\pi}{\Gamma_0} $$

Assemble the result.

$$ J=A\cdot0+B\cdot0+C\cdot\frac{\pi}{\Gamma_0} =\frac{A\Gamma_0^2}{\omega}\cdot\frac{\pi}{\Gamma_0} =\frac{\pi\Gamma_0}{\omega}\cdot\frac{\omega}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2} =\frac{\pi\Gamma_0}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2} $$

Therefore

$$ \frac{1}{\pi}\PV\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\chi''(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega}\dd\omega' =\frac{\chi_0\Gamma_0}{\pi}\cdot\frac{\pi\Gamma_0}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2} =\frac{\chi_0\,\Gamma_0^2}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2} =\frac{\chi_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} =\chi'(\omega) $$

(In the last equality we divided the numerator and denominator by $\Gamma_0^2=1/\tau_0^2$.) Indeed, the $\chi'$ of (1) has been reproduced.

Check. Setting $\omega=0$ makes the right-hand side $\chi_0$, which agrees with the static susceptibility $\chi'(0)=\chi_0$. As $\omega\to\infty$ it falls off as $\chi_0\Gamma_0^2/\omega^2$, which also agrees with the high-frequency tail of $\chi'$ in (1).

(3) For the reverse direction, that is, $\chi''(\omega)=-\dfrac{1}{\pi}\PV\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dfrac{\chi'(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega}\dd\omega'$, we substitute $\chi'$ from (1) and confirm that $\chi''$ comes out. $\chi'$ is a Lorentzian function with center $0$ and width $\Gamma_0$, and it can be written as

$$ \chi'(\omega')=\frac{\chi_0\,\Gamma_0^2}{\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2} =\chi_0\Gamma_0\cdot\frac{\Gamma_0}{\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2} $$

The Hilbert-transform formula for the Lorentzian function derived in Section 4.6.2,

$$ \PV\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\omega'-\omega}\cdot\frac{\Gamma\,\dd\omega'}{(\omega'-a)^2+\Gamma^2} =-\frac{\pi\,(\omega-a)}{(\omega-a)^2+\Gamma^2} $$

applied with $a=0$ and $\Gamma=\Gamma_0$, gives

$$ \chi''(\omega)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\cdot\chi_0\Gamma_0\cdot\left(-\frac{\pi\,\omega}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2}\right) =\frac{\chi_0\,\Gamma_0\,\omega}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2} =\frac{\chi_0\,\omega\tau_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} $$

(In the last equality we divided the numerator and denominator by $\Gamma_0^2$ and used $1/\Gamma_0=\tau_0$.) This agrees with $\chi''$ in (1), so the KK relation in the reverse direction holds as well. ∎

Physical Meaning: Debye relaxation is the minimal example of “one pole in the lower half-plane”

That the KK relations hold is no accident. In the time domain, Debye relaxation is the causal simple relaxation $\chi(t)=(\chi_0/\tau_0)\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\,\theta(t)$ (Example 1.1 in Chapter 1), and the pole of $\chi(\omega)=\chi_0/(1-\ii\omega\tau_0)$ is at $1-\ii\omega\tau_0=0$, that is, $\omega=-\ii/\tau_0$—only a single point, and moreover in the lower half-plane. Since the function is analytic in the upper half-plane, the KK relations hold exactly as in the scenario of Theorem 4.1. This is even simpler than the damped oscillator (two poles), and it is fair to call it “the minimal example in which the KK relations hold.” The next one, Exercise 4.2, is a contrasting example: its functional form is very similar, but the poles end up in the upper half-plane.

Exercise 4.2: If causality is violated, the KK relations fail too

Solution

(1) We find the Fourier transform of the two-sided exponential function $\chi(t)=\chi_0\,\ee^{-\abs{t}/\tau_0}$. We split the integral into two at $t=0$.

$$ \chi(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi_0\,\ee^{-\abs{t}/\tau_0}\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\,\dd t =\chi_0\int_{-\infty}^{0}\ee^{t/\tau_0}\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\,\dd t +\chi_0\int_{0}^{\infty}\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\,\dd t $$

In the first integral, combining the exponents gives $\ee^{(1/\tau_0+\ii\omega)t}$. Thanks to the real part $1/\tau_0>0$, the end $t\to-\infty$ drops to zero, and

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{0}\ee^{(1/\tau_0+\ii\omega)t}\,\dd t =\left[\frac{\ee^{(1/\tau_0+\ii\omega)t}}{1/\tau_0+\ii\omega}\right]_{-\infty}^{0} =\frac{1}{1/\tau_0+\ii\omega} $$

The second integral is similar (this time the end $t\to+\infty$ decays because of $\ee^{-t/\tau_0}$):

$$ \int_{0}^{\infty}\ee^{(-1/\tau_0+\ii\omega)t}\,\dd t=\frac{1}{1/\tau_0-\ii\omega} $$

Bringing the two to a common denominator and adding them, the $\pm\ii\omega$ in the numerator cancel, and

$$ \chi(\omega)=\chi_0\,\frac{(1/\tau_0-\ii\omega)+(1/\tau_0+\ii\omega)}{(1/\tau_0+\ii\omega)(1/\tau_0-\ii\omega)} =\frac{2\chi_0/\tau_0}{1/\tau_0^2+\omega^2} $$

The given expression has been obtained.

(2) We confirm that this $\chi(\omega)$ is purely real on the real axis and see the contradiction with the KK relations. For real $\omega$ the denominator $1/\tau_0^2+\omega^2$ is a positive real number and the numerator $2\chi_0/\tau_0$ is also real, so

$$ \chi'(\omega)=\frac{2\chi_0/\tau_0}{1/\tau_0^2+\omega^2}\ >\ 0\ \ (\text{for all real}\ \omega), \qquad \chi''(\omega)\equiv0 $$

If the KK relations held, then the formula that gives the real part from the imaginary part would yield

$$ \chi'(\omega)=\frac{1}{\pi}\PV\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{0}{\omega'-\omega}\,\dd\omega'=0 $$

But in fact $\chi'$ is positive for all $\omega$ and is not identically zero. This is a contradiction.

The reverse direction also fails. Setting $\Gamma_0\equiv1/\tau_0$, we have $\chi'(\omega')=2\chi_0\,\Gamma_0/(\omega'^2+\Gamma_0^2)$; putting this into the right-hand side of $\chi''(\omega)=-\frac1\pi\PV\int\chi'(\omega')/(\omega'-\omega)\,\dd\omega'$ and using the Hilbert-transform formula for the Lorentzian function (Exercise 4.1(3)), we obtain

$$ -\frac{1}{\pi}\cdot 2\chi_0\cdot\left(-\frac{\pi\,\omega}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2}\right) =\frac{2\chi_0\,\omega}{\omega^2+\Gamma_0^2}\ \neq\ 0 $$

which does not reproduce the true $\chi''\equiv0$. In neither direction do the KK relations hold.

(3) We explain the cause of the failure in terms of the positions of the poles. Factoring the denominator gives

$$ \frac{1}{\tau_0^2}+\omega^2=\left(\omega-\frac{\ii}{\tau_0}\right)\left(\omega+\frac{\ii}{\tau_0}\right) $$

so the poles of $\chi(\omega)$ are the two points $\omega=\pm\ii/\tau_0$, which are arranged symmetrically above and below the real axis, and $\omega=+\ii/\tau_0$ lies in the upper half-plane (Figure 4.1, right). The derivation of the KK relations in Section 4.4.1 used the fact that “there is not a single singularity inside the closed curve made of the real axis and a large circle in the upper half-plane” in order to apply Cauchy's integral theorem $\oint=0$. Now the pole $\omega'=+\ii/\tau_0$ falls inside the curve, so $\oint=0$ cannot be used. In terms of the time domain, because there is a response at $t<0$, “the exploding factor $\ee^{-\omega_I t}$ ($t<0$, $\omega_I>0$) that causality was supposed to cut off for us” (Section 4.2.1) survives, and analyticity in the upper half-plane itself is lost.

We can also say quantitatively how much the relations fail. When there is a simple pole inside the closed curve, we must use the residue theorem (Chapter 5) instead of Cauchy's integral theorem, and we get

$$ \oint_C\frac{\chi(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega}\,\dd\omega' =2\pi\ii\,\Res_{\omega'=\ii/\tau_0}\frac{\chi(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega} =2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{2\chi_0/\tau_0}{(2\ii/\tau_0)\,(\ii/\tau_0-\omega)} =-\frac{2\pi\ii\,\chi_0}{1/\tau_0+\ii\omega} $$

so a nonzero residue term remains on the right-hand side. (The residue was obtained by removing the factor $\omega'-\ii/\tau_0$ from the denominator at the pole $\omega'=\ii/\tau_0$ and substituting $\omega'=\ii/\tau_0$ into the remainder $\dfrac{2\chi_0/\tau_0}{(\omega'+\ii/\tau_0)(\omega'-\omega)}$. The last equality uses $\ii\,(\ii/\tau_0-\omega)=-1/\tau_0-\ii\omega$.) If we split the closed-contour integral into the principal value along the real axis, the small semicircle, and the large circle, as in Section 4.4.2, then the large circle gives zero and the small semicircle gives $-\ii\pi\chi(\omega)$, so that

$$ \PV\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\chi(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega}\,\dd\omega' =\ii\pi\,\chi(\omega)-\frac{2\pi\ii\,\chi_0}{1/\tau_0+\ii\omega} $$

Rationalizing the second term on the right-hand side, its imaginary part exactly cancels $\ii\pi\chi(\omega)$ (the left-hand side is the principal-value integral of a real function and must therefore be real, so this is consistent), and the real value $-2\pi\chi_0\,\omega/(1/\tau_0^2+\omega^2)$ remains. This is nothing other than the “spurious $\chi''$” found in (2) multiplied by $-\pi$. The residue of the pole in the upper half-plane is itself the failure of the KK relations. The KK relations do not hold without causality—this has been made visible in the minimal example. ∎

Exercise 4.3: The static susceptibility is a weighted sum of the absorption spectrum

Solution

(1) We set $\omega=0$ in the KK relation in positive-frequency form and derive the given expression. The starting point is

$$ \chi'(\omega)=\frac{2}{\pi}\,\PV\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\omega'\,\chi''(\omega')}{\omega'^2-\omega^2}\,\dd\omega' $$

derived in Section 4.5.2. Substituting $\omega=0$ makes the denominator $\omega'^2$, which cancels against the $\omega'$ in the numerator, so that

$$ \chi'(0)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\omega'\,\chi''(\omega')}{\omega'^2}\,\dd\omega' =\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\chi''(\omega')}{\omega'}\,\dd\omega' $$

There are two reasons why the principal-value symbol can be dropped. First, the denominator $\omega'^2$ never vanishes in the interior $\omega'>0$ of the integration domain, so there is no singularity inside the interval that has to be avoided symmetrically. Second, the endpoint $\omega'\to0^+$ is also harmless: since $\chi''$ is a smooth odd function, $\chi''(0)=0$ and $\chi''(\omega')=O(\omega')$, and therefore $\chi''(\omega')/\omega'$ approaches a finite value (the slope at the origin) as $\omega'\to0^+$. The integrand is finite everywhere, and the integral converges in the ordinary sense.

Note also that starting from the all-frequency form gives the same expression. The integrand of $\chi'(0)=\frac1\pi\PV\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi''(\omega')/\omega'\,\dd\omega'$ is a quotient of odd functions, that is, an even function, so it suffices to rewrite the integral as twice the positive side.

(2) We check the static susceptibility by substituting $\omega=0$ into $\chi'$ of the damped oscillator. In the expression from Chapter 3,

$$ \chi'(\omega)=\frac1m\cdot\frac{\omega_0^2-\omega^2}{(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)^2+4\Gamma^2\omega^2} $$

setting $\omega=0$ gives

$$ \chi'(0)=\frac1m\cdot\frac{\omega_0^2}{(\omega_0^2)^2}=\frac{1}{m\omega_0^2}=\frac1k $$

In the last equality we used $k=m\omega_0^2$. This is exactly the static susceptibility of Chapter 1, Section 1.5.2: applying a constant force $F$ and balancing gives $kx=F$, that is, the displacement per unit force is $1/k$. Note also that $\Gamma$ does not appear in the result. This is because friction does not affect “where the balance is reached.”

(3) We carry out the integral on the right-hand side in the limit $\Gamma\to0$ and see that it agrees with (2). From Example 4.3, as $\Gamma\to0^+$, on the positive-frequency side

$$ \chi''(\omega')\ \longrightarrow\ \frac{\pi}{2m\omega_0}\,\delta(\omega'-\omega_0) $$

We substitute this into the expression in (1). Since the integral of a delta function merely picks up the value at $\omega'=\omega_0$,

$$ \frac{2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\frac{1}{\omega'}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2m\omega_0}\,\delta(\omega'-\omega_0)\,\dd\omega' =\frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{\omega_0}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2m\omega_0} =\frac{1}{m\omega_0^2} $$

This agrees with $\chi'(0)=1/(m\omega_0^2)$ from (2).

Physical meaning. The weight in this sum rule is $1/\omega'$, so absorption at lower frequencies contributes more strongly to the static response. When the resonance moves toward the low-frequency side and the mode becomes soft ($\omega_0\to0$), $\chi'(0)=1/(m\omega_0^2)$ diverges. The starting point of soft-mode theory—that near a structural phase transition the dielectric constant grows divergently as the soft-mode frequency decreases—is contained in this single line. ∎

Exercise 4.4: The sum rule fails without inertia

Solution

(1) We check that the sum rule $\int_0^\infty\omega\chi''\,\dd\omega=\pi/2m$ for the $\chi''$ of the damped oscillator depends on neither $\omega_0$ nor $\Gamma$, by way of the partial-fraction form of Section 4.6.2:

$$ \chi''(\omega) = \frac{\Gamma}{2m\omega_1}\left[\frac{1}{(\omega-\omega_1)^2+\Gamma^2}-\frac{1}{(\omega+\omega_1)^2+\Gamma^2}\right] $$

First, we combine the two Lorentzian functions. The numerator is

$$ \left[(\omega+\omega_1)^2+\Gamma^2\right]-\left[(\omega-\omega_1)^2+\Gamma^2\right] = 4\omega\omega_1 $$

so, writing $D_\mp\equiv(\omega\mp\omega_1)^2+\Gamma^2$, we have

$$ \omega\,\chi''(\omega) = \frac{\Gamma}{2m\omega_1}\cdot\frac{4\omega^2\omega_1}{D_-D_+} = \frac{2\Gamma}{m}\,\frac{\omega^2}{D_-D_+} $$

The integrand is an even function of $\omega$ and decays as $\mathcal O(\omega^{-2})$ at large $\omega$, so we extend the integration interval to the whole real axis,

$$ \int_0^\infty\omega\chi''\,\dd\omega = \frac{\Gamma}{m}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\omega^2\,\dd\omega}{D_-D_+} $$

and evaluate it with the residue theorem (Chapter 5). Since $D_-=(\omega-\omega_1-\ii\Gamma)(\omega-\omega_1+\ii\Gamma)$ and $D_+=(\omega+\omega_1-\ii\Gamma)(\omega+\omega_1+\ii\Gamma)$, there are two poles in the upper half-plane, at $\omega=\pm\omega_1+\ii\Gamma$. Computing the residues gives

$$ \Res_{\omega=\omega_1+\ii\Gamma}\frac{\omega^2}{D_-D_+} =\frac{(\omega_1+\ii\Gamma)^2}{2\ii\Gamma\cdot4\omega_1(\omega_1+\ii\Gamma)} =\frac{\omega_1+\ii\Gamma}{8\ii\Gamma\omega_1}, \qquad \Res_{\omega=-\omega_1+\ii\Gamma}\frac{\omega^2}{D_-D_+} =\frac{\omega_1-\ii\Gamma}{8\ii\Gamma\omega_1} $$

(For the second residue, we replaced $\omega_1\to-\omega_1$ in the first calculation and used $(-\omega_1+\ii\Gamma)^2=(\omega_1-\ii\Gamma)^2$.) In the sum the imaginary parts cancel, giving

$$ \frac{(\omega_1+\ii\Gamma)+(\omega_1-\ii\Gamma)}{8\ii\Gamma\omega_1}=\frac{1}{4\ii\Gamma} $$

and therefore

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\omega^2\,\dd\omega}{D_-D_+}=2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{1}{4\ii\Gamma}=\frac{\pi}{2\Gamma}, \qquad\therefore\quad \int_0^\infty\omega\chi''\,\dd\omega=\frac{\Gamma}{m}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2\Gamma}=\frac{\pi}{2m} $$

The $\Gamma$ drops out by cancellation, and $\omega_1$ (that is, $\omega_0$) disappeared the moment we took the sum of the residues. The claim of Section 4.6.2 that the sum rule is determined by the inertia $m$ alone has thus also been confirmed from the side of the partial-fraction form.

(2) We show that for Debye relaxation, $\chi''_{\rm Debye}(\omega)=\dfrac{\chi_0\omega\tau_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2}$, the integral $\int_0^\infty\omega\chi''\dd\omega$ diverges. At high frequencies we have

$$ \omega\,\chi''_{\rm Debye}(\omega)=\frac{\chi_0\,\omega^2\tau_0}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} \;\xrightarrow{\ \omega\to\infty\ }\;\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\quad(\text{const}) $$

so the integral diverges linearly at the upper limit. The physical reason: Debye relaxation is a model without an inertia term. The equation of motion is a first-order equation of the type $\tau_0\dot x+x=\chi_0 f$, with no $m\ddot x$ term. The derivation of the sum rule (Section 4.6.2) presupposed the high-frequency limit $\chi\to-1/(m\omega^2)$—“no matter how fast we shake it, in the end the mass governs the response.” Without inertia this limit does not exist (in fact $\chi_{\rm Debye}\simeq\ii\chi_0/(\omega\tau_0)$ decays only as $1/\omega$), and the sum rule does not hold.

(3) What holds instead is the form of Exercise 4.3 (the sum rule for the static susceptibility):

$$ \frac{2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\frac{\chi''_{\rm Debye}(\omega)}{\omega}\,\dd\omega =\frac{2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\frac{\chi_0\tau_0\,\dd\omega}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} =\frac{2\chi_0\tau_0}{\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau_0}\Bigl[\arctan(\omega\tau_0)\Bigr]_0^\infty =\frac{2\chi_0\tau_0}{\pi}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2\tau_0}=\chi_0 $$

Indeed, $\chi(0)=\chi_0$ is reproduced. The sum rule weighted by $1/\omega$ does not require inertia, because the $\omega$ in the denominator kills the tail, whereas the $f$-sum rule, weighted by $\omega$, demands a fast decay of the tail—that is, inertia. Even though both are “sum rules,” their premises differ. ∎

Exercise 4.5: The high-frequency expansion and the second sum rule

Solution

(1) We differentiate $\chi(t)=\dfrac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{-\Gamma t}\sin\omega_1 t$ ($t>0$) twice and find their values as $t\to0^+$. First derivative:

$$ \dot\chi(t)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left(-\Gamma\sin\omega_1 t+\omega_1\cos\omega_1 t\right) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \dot\chi(0^+)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\cdot\omega_1=\frac1m $$

Second derivative: applying the product rule once more gives

$$ \ddot\chi(t)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{-\Gamma t}\left[(\Gamma^2-\omega_1^2)\sin\omega_1 t-2\Gamma\omega_1\cos\omega_1 t\right] \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \ddot\chi(0^+)=\frac{1}{m\omega_1}\,(-2\Gamma\omega_1)=-\frac{2\Gamma}{m} $$

(2) We substitute these into the expansion given in the problem statement:

$$ \chi(\omega)\simeq-\frac{\dot\chi(0^+)}{\omega^2}-\frac{\ii\,\ddot\chi(0^+)}{\omega^3} =-\frac{1}{m\omega^2}+\frac{2\ii\Gamma}{m\omega^3} $$

The real part is the first term (the high-frequency limit $-1/m\omega^2$ of Section 4.2.4 itself), and the imaginary part is

$$ \chi''(\omega)\simeq\frac{2\Gamma}{m\omega^3}=\frac{\gamma}{m^2\omega^3} $$

(with $\Gamma=\gamma/2m$). This is the very $\omega^{-3}$ law that we used for the tail of the absorption in Example 4.5. This expansion is to be read as saying that the rate at which the absorption tail decays is determined by the derivatives of $\chi(t)$ at $t=0^+$—that is, by how smoothly it rises.

(3) We expand the semi-infinite form of the KK relations

$$ \chi''(\omega) = -\frac{2\omega}{\pi}\,\PV\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\chi'(\omega')}{\omega'^2-\omega^2}\,\dd\omega' $$

for $\omega\to\infty$. For the values of $\omega'$ that matter in the integral (near the resonance, where $\chi'$ takes significant values) we may take $\omega'\ll\omega$, so we expand the kernel as $\dfrac{1}{\omega'^2-\omega^2}=-\dfrac{1}{\omega^2}\left(1+\dfrac{\omega'^2}{\omega^2}+\cdots\right)$, and keeping only the leading term gives

$$ \chi''(\omega)\simeq\frac{2}{\pi\omega}\int_0^\infty\chi'(\omega')\,\dd\omega' +\mathcal O(\omega^{-3}) $$

(The integral in the leading term converges thanks to the tail $\chi'\sim-1/(m\omega'^2)$. If we integrate the second and later terms of the kernel expansion term by term as they stand, divergent integrals such as $\int_0^\infty\omega'^2\chi'\,\dd\omega'$ appear, so the individual coefficients cannot be written down. It can still be confirmed, however, that the correction as a whole stays within $\mathcal O(\omega^{-3})$, by using the remainder $\omega'^2/[\omega^2(\omega'^2-\omega^2)]$ left after subtracting the leading term $-1/\omega^2$ from the kernel.) As we saw in (2), $\chi''$ of the damped oscillator is $\mathcal O(\omega^{-3})$, so the leading term of order $\mathcal O(\omega^{-1})$ must vanish. That is,

$$ \int_0^\infty\chi'(\omega)\,\dd\omega=0 \qquad(\text{second sum rule}) $$

This says that the positive and negative areas of the dispersion curve $\chi'(\omega)$ exactly balance (the purple curve in Figure 4.3 does just this).

The general form and its check. The general form of this sum rule is obtained by evaluating the inverse Fourier transform at $t=0$. Since $\chi(t)$ jumps at $t=0$ from $0$ ($t<0$) to $\chi(0^+)$, the inverse transform returns the midpoint of the jump:

$$ \frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi(\omega)\,\dd\omega=\frac{\chi(0^+)+\chi(0^-)}{2}=\frac{\chi(0^+)}{2} $$

On the left-hand side, $\ii\chi''$ is an odd function and drops out, while $\chi'$ is an even function, so $\dfrac1\pi\displaystyle\int_0^\infty\chi'\dd\omega$ remains. Therefore

$$ \int_0^\infty\chi'(\omega)\,\dd\omega=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\chi(t=0^+) $$

For the damped oscillator, $\chi(0^+)=0$ (the displacement is still zero immediately after the impulsive force), so the second sum rule holds. On the other hand, Debye relaxation has $\chi(0^+)=\chi_0/\tau_0\neq0$, so that

$$ \int_0^\infty\chi'_{\rm Debye}\,\dd\omega =\int_0^\infty\frac{\chi_0\,\dd\omega}{1+\omega^2\tau_0^2} =\frac{\pi\chi_0}{2\tau_0} =\frac{\pi}{2}\,\chi_{\rm Debye}(0^+) $$

and the direct calculation agrees with the general form as well. Here, too, the presence or absence of “inertia”—whether the onset is smooth or jumps—separates the two models. ∎

Exercise 4.6: Extrapolation error in KK analysis of reflectance

Solution

(1) We evaluate, for $\omega\ll\Omega$, the phase error produced when the region above the upper limit of the measurement, $\Omega$, is extrapolated with $R=R_\Omega$ (a constant). The given integral can be carried out in elementary terms:

$$ \int_\Omega^\infty\frac{\dd\omega'}{\omega'^2-\omega^2} =\frac{1}{2\omega}\left[\ln\frac{\omega'-\omega}{\omega'+\omega}\right]_\Omega^\infty =\frac{1}{2\omega}\ln\frac{\Omega+\omega}{\Omega-\omega} $$

For $\omega\ll\Omega$ we have $\ln\dfrac{1+\omega/\Omega}{1-\omega/\Omega}\simeq\dfrac{2\omega}{\Omega}$, so the integral is $\simeq1/\Omega$ and

$$ \Delta\theta(\omega)\simeq-\frac{\omega\ln R_\Omega}{\pi\,\Omega} $$

Since $R_\Omega<1$, we have $\ln R_\Omega<0$, and hence this is a systematic error with $\Delta\theta>0$.

(2) The error grows in proportion to $\omega$. Moreover, as $\omega$ approaches $\Omega$ the expansion for $\omega\ll\Omega$ breaks down, and the exact form $\dfrac{1}{2\omega}\ln\dfrac{\Omega+\omega}{\Omega-\omega}$ diverges logarithmically as $\omega\to\Omega$—at the upper end of the measurement range the extrapolation error grows sharply. The same argument holds for the extrapolation on the low-frequency side (the $\omega\to0$ side). In other words, the effect of the extrapolation is largest at the two ends of the measurement window and smallest in the middle. The practical guideline “place the spectral region of interest in the middle of the measurement range (measure with a sufficient margin on both sides)” is a direct consequence of the structure of this integral kernel $1/(\omega'^2-\omega^2)$—the farther $\omega'$ is from $\omega$, the smaller its contribution.

(3) The reason it is standard to take the high-frequency extrapolation as $R\propto\omega^{-4}$. By the universal high-frequency limit $\chi\to-1/(m\omega^2)$ derived in Chapter 4, for any material, as $\omega\to\infty$,

$$ \epsilon(\omega)-1\simeq-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2} $$

holds (the electrons all respond as free particles). Therefore the complex refractive index behaves as

$$ \tilde n=\sqrt{\epsilon}\simeq1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{2\omega^2} $$

approaching $1$, and the reflection amplitude at normal incidence is

$$ r=\frac{\tilde n-1}{\tilde n+1}\simeq\frac{\tilde n-1}{2}\simeq-\frac{\omega_p^2}{4\omega^2}\propto\omega^{-2} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad R=\abs{r}^2\propto\omega^{-4} $$

In other words, the $\omega^{-4}$ law is a consequence of inertia that does not depend on the material, and an extrapolation that follows it has the physically correct asymptotic form. A constant extrapolation assumes “a material that keeps reflecting even at infinitely high frequencies,” and produces the systematic error proportional to $\omega$ that we saw in (1). ∎

Chapter 5: Complex Analysis and the Residue Theorem

Exercise 5.1: Determining analyticity

Solution

(1) We decide whether $f(z)=\ee^z$ is analytic by means of the Cauchy–Riemann (CR) equations, and if it is analytic we find its derivative. Writing $z=x+\ii y$ and using the law of exponents together with Euler's formula $\ee^{\ii y}=\cos y+\ii\sin y$, we obtain

$$ \ee^z=\ee^{x+\ii y}=\ee^x\,\ee^{\ii y}=\ee^x\cos y+\ii\,\ee^x\sin y $$

so the real and imaginary parts are $u=\ee^x\cos y$ and $v=\ee^x\sin y$. We write out all four partial derivatives:

$$ \partial_x u=\ee^x\cos y,\qquad \partial_y u=-\ee^x\sin y,\qquad \partial_x v=\ee^x\sin y,\qquad \partial_y v=\ee^x\cos y $$

We check the CR equations $\partial_x u=\partial_y v$ and $\partial_y u=-\partial_x v$. The first reads $\ee^x\cos y=\ee^x\cos y$, which holds. The second reads $-\ee^x\sin y=-\ee^x\sin y$, which holds. Moreover, they hold not only at particular values of $(x,y)$ but on the entire plane, so $\ee^z$ is analytic on the whole complex plane (an entire function). For the derivative, we substitute into the formula $f'=\partial_x u+\ii\,\partial_x v$, which follows from the limit along the real-axis direction:

$$ f'(z)=\ee^x\cos y+\ii\,\ee^x\sin y=\ee^x\,\ee^{\ii y}=\ee^z $$

We obtain the same $(\ee^z)'=\ee^z$ as for the real function. Check. Differentiating the defining series $\ee^z=\sum_{n\ge0}z^n/n!$ term by term gives $\sum_{n\ge1}z^{n-1}/(n-1)!=\ee^z$, which agrees.

(2) We decide whether $f(z)=\mathrm{Re}\,z$ is analytic. Since $\mathrm{Re}\,z=x$, we have $u=x$ and $v=0$, so

$$ \partial_x u=1,\qquad \partial_y u=0,\qquad \partial_x v=0,\qquad \partial_y v=0 $$

The first CR equation becomes $1=0$, which fails at every point. Hence $\mathrm{Re}\,z$ is analytic nowhere. We can also check this directly by returning to the definition. If we approach along the real-axis direction $\Delta z=h$ (a real number), the difference quotient is

$$ \frac{\mathrm{Re}(z_0+h)-\mathrm{Re}\,z_0}{h}=\frac{h}{h}=1 $$

whereas if we approach along the imaginary-axis direction $\Delta z=\ii h$, the numerator is $\mathrm{Re}(z_0+\ii h)-\mathrm{Re}\,z_0=0$, so the difference quotient is $0/(\ii h)=0$. Since different values, $1$ and $0$, come out depending on the direction, the limit does not exist.

(3) We decide whether $f(z)=z\bar z$ is analytic. Since $z\bar z=\abs{z}^2=x^2+y^2$ is real-valued, we have $u=x^2+y^2$ and $v=0$, so

$$ \partial_x u=2x,\qquad \partial_y u=2y,\qquad \partial_x v=0,\qquad \partial_y v=0 $$

The first CR equation requires $2x=0$ and the second requires $2y=0$, so both hold at only one point, the origin. In fact, at the origin, directly from the definition,

$$ \frac{f(\Delta z)-f(0)}{\Delta z}=\frac{\Delta z\,\overline{\Delta z}}{\Delta z}=\overline{\Delta z}\ \xrightarrow{\ \Delta z\to0\ }\ 0 $$

so the function is differentiable there, with $f'(0)=0$. However, recall the definition in Section 5.2.1 of the main text: analyticity requires differentiability “at all points in some neighborhood of $z_0$.” Since there is not a single point other than the origin at which the function is differentiable, the condition cannot be met no matter how small a neighborhood we take. Therefore $z\bar z$ is analytic nowhere, the origin included.

To summarize, (1) is analytic on the whole plane with $f'=\ee^z$, whereas (2) and (3) are not analytic. Both (2) and (3) are functions that can be written in terms of $\bar z$ ($\mathrm{Re}\,z=(z+\bar z)/2$, and $z\bar z$ itself). We see that the practical test of the main text, “an expression containing $\bar z$, $\abs{z}$, or $\mathrm{Re}\,z$ is not analytic,” works exactly as stated. ∎

Common Mistake: “Differentiable at one point” does not mean analytic

The function $z\bar z$ in (3) is differentiable at the origin, but even so we cannot say that “there is a point where it is analytic.” This is because analyticity is a property of a point together with its whole neighborhood. From differentiability at a single point alone, nothing follows: neither the Taylor expansion nor Cauchy's integral theorem. Both on examinations and in practice, the correct answer for “a function that satisfies the CR equations only at an isolated point” is that it is not analytic.

Exercise 5.2: The answer depends on how the closed contour is chosen

Solution

We first do the preparation common to all three sub-questions. The integrand is

$$ f(z)=\frac{1}{z^2+1}=\frac{1}{(z-\ii)(z+\ii)} $$

so the singularities are the two points $z=\pm\ii$, each of which is a simple zero of the denominator, that is, a simple pole. By the $P/Q$-type formula $\Res=P(z_0)/Q'(z_0)$ (with $P=1$, $Q=z^2+1$, $Q'=2z$), the residues are

$$ \Res_{z=\ii}f=\frac{1}{2\ii}, \qquad \Res_{z=-\ii}f=\frac{1}{-2\ii}=-\frac{1}{2\ii} $$

All that remains is to determine which poles each contour encloses and to substitute into the residue theorem $\oint_C f\dd z=2\pi\ii\sum(\text{residues of the enclosed poles})$ (counterclockwise).

(1) We evaluate the integral along the circle $\abs{z}=2$. The circle is centered at the origin with radius $2$. The distance to each pole is $\abs{\pm\ii}=1<2$, so both poles lie inside. Summing the residues, we obtain

$$ \oint_{\abs{z}=2}\frac{\dd z}{z^2+1} =2\pi\ii\left(\frac{1}{2\ii}-\frac{1}{2\ii}\right)=2\pi\ii\cdot0=0 $$

(2) We evaluate the integral along the circle $\abs{z-\ii}=1$. The center is $z=\ii$ and the radius is $1$. The pole $z=\ii$ is the center itself (distance $0<1$), so it lies inside. The other pole, $z=-\ii$, is at distance $\abs{-\ii-\ii}=\abs{-2\ii}=2>1$, so it lies outside. Since the contour encloses only $z=\ii$,

$$ \oint_{\abs{z-\ii}=1}\frac{\dd z}{z^2+1} =2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{1}{2\ii}=\pi $$

(3) We evaluate the integral along the circle $\abs{z}=1/2$. The distance to each pole is $1>1/2$, so the contour encloses no pole at all. Since $f$ is analytic on the contour and in its interior, Cauchy's integral theorem gives

$$ \oint_{\abs{z}=1/2}\frac{\dd z}{z^2+1}=0 $$

We summarize the results in a table.

ContourEnclosed polesSum of residuesValue of the integral
$\abs{z}=2$$z=\ii$ and $z=-\ii$$\dfrac{1}{2\ii}-\dfrac{1}{2\ii}=0$$0$
$\abs{z-\ii}=1$$z=\ii$ only$\dfrac{1}{2\ii}$$\pi$
$\abs{z}=1/2$None—$0$

Discussion. (1) and (3) give the same value $0$, but for entirely different reasons. The zero in (3) arises because there is no enclosed pole at all (Cauchy's theorem), whereas the zero in (1) arises because the two residues cancel each other (the residue theorem). The zero in (1) can also be confirmed by another method. Far away, $\abs{f}\sim1/R^2$, so by the ML inequality the integral over the circle of radius $R$ satisfies $\abs{\oint}\le 2\pi R/(R^2-1)\to0$ ($R\to\infty$). On the other hand, since there is no singularity in between when the radius is enlarged from $2$ to $R$, the value of the integral is independent of the radius. Hence it must already be $0$ at radius $2$—this gives an alternative proof that the sum of all the residues is zero. Constructing the partial-fraction decomposition $\dfrac{1}{z^2+1}=\dfrac{1}{2\ii}\left(\dfrac{1}{z-\ii}-\dfrac{1}{z+\ii}\right)$ and applying to each term $\oint\dfrac{\dd z}{z-z_0}=2\pi\ii$ (when $z_0$ is enclosed) or $0$ (when it is not enclosed) also reproduces all three answers. ∎

Exercise 5.3: A real integral with a double pole

Solution

What we must show is $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{(x^2+a^2)^2}=\frac{\pi}{2a^3}$ ($a>0$). We follow the three steps of Section 5.10 of the main text (construct a closed contour → check that the large semicircle vanishes → pick up the residues).

Step 1: Constructing the closed contour and finding the locations and orders of the poles. The integrand, extended to the complex plane, is

$$ f(z)=\frac{1}{(z^2+a^2)^2}=\frac{1}{(z-\ii a)^2(z+\ii a)^2} $$

Both zeros $z=\pm\ii a$ of the denominator have multiplicity 2, so $f$ has a pole of order 2 at each of $z=\pm\ii a$. We take the closed contour $\mathcal{C}$ to be the real-axis segment $[-R,R]$ joined with the large semicircle $C_R$ in the upper half-plane (counterclockwise). The only pole in the upper half-plane is $z=+\ii a$.

Step 2: Checking that the large semicircle vanishes. On $C_R$ we have $\abs{z}=R$, so by the triangle inequality $\abs{z^2+a^2}\ge\abs{z}^2-a^2=R^2-a^2$, and therefore $\abs{f}\le1/(R^2-a^2)^2$. By the ML inequality ($L=\pi R$),

$$ \abs{\int_{C_R}f\,\dd z}\le\frac{\pi R}{(R^2-a^2)^2}\xrightarrow{\ R\to\infty\ }0 $$

The denominator is of degree 4 in $R$ and the numerator is of degree 1, so the rule of thumb in the main text, “if the degree of the denominator exceeds that of the numerator by 2 or more, the large semicircle vanishes,” is satisfied with room to spare. Since there is no exponential factor, Jordan's lemma is not needed; the ML inequality alone suffices.

Step 3: Calculating the residue at the pole of order 2. We use the formula for a pole of order $n$, $\Res=\dfrac{1}{(n-1)!}\lim\limits_{z\to z_0}\dfrac{\dd^{\,n-1}}{\dd z^{\,n-1}}\left[(z-z_0)^nf\right]$, with $n=2$. Noting that $(1!)=1$, we have

$$ \Res_{z=\ii a}f =\lim_{z\to\ii a}\frac{\dd}{\dd z}\left[(z-\ii a)^2\cdot\frac{1}{(z-\ii a)^2(z+\ii a)^2}\right] =\lim_{z\to\ii a}\frac{\dd}{\dd z}\frac{1}{(z+\ii a)^2} $$

Carrying out the differentiation gives $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd z}(z+\ii a)^{-2}=-2(z+\ii a)^{-3}$, so substituting $z=\ii a$ and using $(2\ii a)^3=8\ii^3a^3=-8\ii a^3$, we find

$$ \Res_{z=\ii a}f=\frac{-2}{(2\ii a)^3}=\frac{-2}{-8\ii a^3}=\frac{1}{4\ii a^3} $$

Step 4: Putting it together with the residue theorem. Since the contour is counterclockwise, we multiply by $+2\pi\ii$:

$$ \oint_{\mathcal{C}}f\,\dd z=2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{1}{4\ii a^3}=\frac{\pi}{2a^3} $$

Since the contribution of the large semicircle vanishes as $R\to\infty$, the remaining real-axis part is the answer itself:

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{(x^2+a^2)^2}=\frac{\pi}{2a^3} $$

Check (differentiation with respect to a parameter). First, the substitution $x=au$ gives $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{x^2+a^2}=\frac{1}{a}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd u}{u^2+1}=\frac{\pi}{a}$ (using the result of Example 5.9). We differentiate both sides with respect to $a$. The left side may be differentiated under the integral sign, so

$$ \frac{\partial}{\partial a}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{x^2+a^2} =\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{-2a\,\dd x}{(x^2+a^2)^2}, \qquad \frac{\dd}{\dd a}\frac{\pi}{a}=-\frac{\pi}{a^2} $$

Setting the two equal and dividing by $-2a$ gives $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{(x^2+a^2)^2}=\frac{\pi}{2a^3}$. This agrees exactly with the residue calculation. Since it is an independent derivation that does not use the residue theorem, it is a strong check.

Check (numerical). As in the hint, for $a=1$ we get $\pi/2\simeq1.571$, and for $a=2$ we get $\pi/16\simeq0.1963$. Numerical integration (python3) returns $1.5708$ and $0.19635$, respectively, agreeing to four digits. The dimensional count is also consistent: the integrand is $[x]^{-4}$, and with $\dd x$ it becomes $[x]^{-3}$, which matches the right-hand side $\propto a^{-3}$. ∎

Common Mistake: Do not use the $P/Q'$ formula for a pole of order 2

In this problem, if we set $Q(z)=(z^2+a^2)^2$, then $Q'(z)=4z(z^2+a^2)$ and $Q'(\ii a)=0$, so the formula $\Res=P(z_0)/Q'(z_0)$ becomes $1/0$ and breaks down. This formula is meant only for the case where $Q$ has a simple zero (the caution in Section 5.7 of the main text). If $Q'(z_0)=0$ comes out, recount the order and switch to the formula for a pole of order $n$ (do not forget the $(n-1)!$) or to the Laurent expansion.

Exercise 5.4: Using Jordan's lemma

Solution

We are to show that $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{x\sin x}{x^2+a^2}\,\dd x=\pi\,\ee^{-a}$ ($a>0$).

Strategy. If we extend $\sin z$ to the complex plane as it is, the second term of $\sin z=\dfrac{\ee^{\ii z}-\ee^{-\ii z}}{2\ii}$ blows up in the upper half-plane (the same situation as in Example 5.11). So we integrate

$$ g(z)=\frac{z\,\ee^{\ii z}}{z^2+a^2} $$

and take the imaginary part at the end. The exponential factor is $\ee^{\ii z}$, and the coefficient $+1$ in the exponent is positive, so by Jordan's lemma we close the contour in the upper half-plane (note that what corresponds to the $a$ of $\ee^{\ii az}$ in the lemma is this $+1$, not the width $a$ of the present problem).

Step 1: Constructing the closed contour. Take the closed contour (counterclockwise) consisting of $[-R,R]$ on the real axis and the large semicircle $C_R$ in the upper half-plane. The poles of $g$ are the zeros $z=\pm\ii a$ of $z^2+a^2=(z-\ii a)(z+\ii a)$, and both are simple poles. Only $z=+\ii a$ lies in the upper half-plane. There is no pole on the real axis ($x^2+a^2\ge a^2>0$), so the small-semicircle detour around a pole, as in Example 5.11, is unnecessary.

Step 2: The large semicircle—the ML inequality is not enough, and Jordan's lemma is needed. The magnitude of the part without the exponential, $f(z)=\dfrac{z}{z^2+a^2}$, on $C_R$ satisfies

$$ \abs{f}\le\frac{R}{R^2-a^2}\equiv M_R\xrightarrow{\ R\to\infty\ }0 $$

so it decreases only like $1/R$. With the ML inequality alone we get $M_R\cdot\pi R=\dfrac{\pi R^2}{R^2-a^2}\to\pi$, which does not vanish. Jordan's lemma, however, requires only $M_R\to0$, so the integral with the factor $\ee^{\ii z}$, $\displaystyle\int_{C_R}f\,\ee^{\ii z}\dd z$, goes to zero as $R\to\infty$. This is the point of the present problem.

Step 3: Calculating the residue. Since the pole is simple, we use the formula of the $P/Q$ type. With $P(z)=z\,\ee^{\ii z}$, $Q(z)=z^2+a^2$, and $Q'(z)=2z$,

$$ \Res_{z=\ii a}g=\frac{P(\ii a)}{Q'(\ii a)} =\frac{\ii a\,\ee^{\ii(\ii a)}}{2\ii a} =\frac{\ee^{-a}}{2} $$

Because $\ii\cdot\ii=-1$, the exponent has become $\ee^{-a}$, a real damping factor (the same mechanism as in Example 5.8(2)). The factors $\ii a$ in the numerator and denominator cancel, and the result is a real number.

Step 4: Putting it together with the residue theorem. Since the contour is counterclockwise, we multiply by $+2\pi\ii$, and dropping the large semicircle as $R\to\infty$ gives

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{x\,\ee^{\ii x}}{x^2+a^2}\,\dd x =2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{\ee^{-a}}{2}=\ii\pi\,\ee^{-a} $$

(The left-hand side means the symmetric limit $R\to\infty$ of $\int_{-R}^{R}$. At large distances the integrand behaves like $\sim\ee^{\ii x}/x$, so the integral does not converge absolutely, but this symmetric limit converges.)

Step 5: Separating the real and imaginary parts. Substituting $\ee^{\ii x}=\cos x+\ii\sin x$, we obtain

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{x\cos x}{x^2+a^2}\,\dd x +\ii\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{x\sin x}{x^2+a^2}\,\dd x =\ii\pi\,\ee^{-a} $$

The integrand of the real part, $\dfrac{x\cos x}{x^2+a^2}$, is an odd function ($x\cos x$) divided by an even function ($x^2+a^2$), hence odd, and its integral in the symmetric limit is zero. Comparing the imaginary parts,

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{x\sin x}{x^2+a^2}\,\dd x=\pi\,\ee^{-a} $$

Note also that $x\sin x$ is an even function, so the integrand is even, and for the one-sided integral we have $\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}\frac{x\sin x}{x^2+a^2}\dd x=\frac{\pi}{2}\ee^{-a}$.

Check. Look at the limit $a\to0^+$. The right-hand side is $\pi\ee^{0}=\pi$. The left-hand side becomes $\displaystyle\int\frac{x\sin x}{x^2}\dd x=\int\frac{\sin x}{x}\dd x=\pi$ (the value shown in Section 5.10(c) of the main text), which agrees. We also checked numerically: $\pi\ee^{-1}\simeq1.156$ for $a=1$ and $\pi\ee^{-2}\simeq0.4252$ for $a=2$, and numerical evaluation of the oscillatory integrals (python3) gave $1.1557$ and $0.42517$, respectively, in agreement to four digits.

Physical meaning. This is also an instance of the correspondence “Lorentzian in the denominator $\leftrightarrow$ exponential decay” (Section 5.10(b) of the main text). The width $a$ of the spectrum directly determines the decay $\ee^{-a}$. Even when a factor $x$ multiplies the numerator, all that changes is the value of the residue at the pole, and the structure “the pole $z=\ii a$ in the upper half-plane produces $\ee^{-a}$” is unchanged. ∎

Exercise 5.5: Recovering the Debye-relaxation response function with the residue theorem

Solution

We are to show that when the Debye-relaxation susceptibility of Chapter 1, $\chi(\omega)=\dfrac{\chi_0}{1-\ii\omega\tau_0}$, is put into the inverse Fourier transform $\chi(t)=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega}{2\pi}\chi(\omega)\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$, we obtain $\chi(t)=\dfrac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\theta(t)$, and in particular to explain from the location of the pole why the response vanishes for $t<0$. The procedure is the same as the derivation of $G^R(t)$ in Section 5.10(d) of the main text, and it is easier to the extent that the number of poles has decreased from two to one.

Step 1: Locating the pole. Rearrange the denominator in terms of $\omega$:

$$ 1-\ii\omega\tau_0=-\ii\tau_0\left(\omega+\frac{\ii}{\tau_0}\right) $$

(Check by expanding the right-hand side: $-\ii\tau_0\omega-\ii\tau_0\cdot\dfrac{\ii}{\tau_0}=-\ii\omega\tau_0-\ii^2=1-\ii\omega\tau_0$. It agrees.) Using $1/(-\ii)=\ii$, we can write

$$ \chi(\omega)=\frac{\chi_0}{-\ii\tau_0(\omega+\ii/\tau_0)}=\frac{\ii\chi_0/\tau_0}{\omega+\ii/\tau_0} $$

There is only one pole, at $\omega=-\ii/\tau_0$, and it is a simple pole. Its imaginary part is $-1/\tau_0<0$; that is, it lies in the lower half-plane (on the imaginary axis). The fact, familiar since Chapter 2, that the poles of a retarded response function lie in the lower half-plane holds here as well.

Step 2: Deciding which half-plane to close in. The exponential factor is $\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$, so by the iron rule of Section 5.9.1 of the main text we close the contour in the upper half-plane for $t<0$ and in the lower half-plane for $t>0$. On the large semicircle, $\abs{\chi(\omega)}\simeq\dfrac{\chi_0}{\tau_0\abs{\omega}}\to0$, so $M_R\to0$ holds, and by Jordan's lemma the contribution of the large semicircle vanishes. Note that the decay is only $1/R$, so the ML inequality is not enough and Jordan's lemma is essential.

Step 3: $t<0$—causality. We close in the upper half-plane, but there is no pole at all in the upper half-plane (the only pole lies in the lower half-plane). The right-hand side of the residue theorem is zero, so

$$ \chi(t)=0\qquad(t<0) $$

This is the answer to the second half of the problem. That the response vanishes for $t<0$ is neither an assumption nor a convention; it is a consequence of the pole $\omega=-\ii/\tau_0$ lying in the lower half-plane. Causality—the statement that the system does not respond before the external force is applied—comes out automatically from the mathematical fact of where the pole lies.

Step 4: $t>0$—picking up the residue. Closing in the lower half-plane makes the contour clockwise, so the residue theorem carries a factor $-2\pi\ii$:

$$ \chi(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,(-2\pi\ii)\,\Res_{\omega=-\ii/\tau_0}\left[\frac{\ii\chi_0/\tau_0}{\omega+\ii/\tau_0}\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}\right] $$

Since the pole is simple, we only need to multiply by $(\omega+\ii/\tau_0)$ and set $\omega\to-\ii/\tau_0$:

$$ \Res_{\omega=-\ii/\tau_0}=\frac{\ii\chi_0}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-\ii(-\ii/\tau_0)t} =\frac{\ii\chi_0}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0} $$

(Calculation of the exponent: $-\ii\cdot(-\ii/\tau_0)\,t=\ii^2 t/\tau_0=-t/\tau_0$. The imaginary part of the pole becomes the damping rate directly.) Substituting,

$$ \chi(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}(-2\pi\ii)\cdot\frac{\ii\chi_0}{\tau_0}\ee^{-t/\tau_0} =(-\ii)(\ii)\,\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\ee^{-t/\tau_0} =\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0} $$

($-\ii\cdot\ii=-\ii^2=1$.) Combining with the result of Step 3 and collecting everything with the step function,

$$ \chi(t)=\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\,\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\,\theta(t) $$

This is the formula we were to show. ∎

Check (transforming back with the forward transform). We check that Fourier-transforming the $\chi(t)$ we obtained returns the original function. Only $t>0$ contributes, so

$$ \int_0^{\infty}\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\,\dd t =\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\left[\frac{\ee^{(\ii\omega-1/\tau_0)t}}{\ii\omega-1/\tau_0}\right]_0^{\infty} =\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\cdot\frac{1}{1/\tau_0-\ii\omega} =\frac{\chi_0}{1-\ii\omega\tau_0} $$

(As $t\to\infty$, $\ee^{-t/\tau_0}\to0$ takes effect and the upper-limit term vanishes.) We indeed return to the original $\chi(\omega)$. As the hint says, this is the calculation of Example 1.1 in Chapter 1 run in the reverse direction.

Check (static limit). $\displaystyle\int_0^{\infty}\chi(t)\dd t=\frac{\chi_0}{\tau_0}\cdot\tau_0=\chi_0=\chi(\omega=0)$. The general rule that the time integral of the response equals the static susceptibility is also satisfied.

Physical Meaning: The location of the pole determines the form of the relaxation

The Debye-relaxation pole $\omega=-\ii/\tau_0$ lies on the imaginary axis (real part zero). Applying the reading of Section 5.10(d) of the main text—“the real part of the pole is the frequency, and the imaginary part is the damping rate”—we find frequency zero and damping rate $1/\tau_0$; that is, pure exponential relaxation without oscillation. The poles of the damped oscillator, on the other hand, $\omega_\pm=\pm\omega_1-\ii\Gamma$, have real parts $\pm\omega_1$, so the response oscillated while decaying. The lesson that the waveform of the response can be read off the moment the poles are plotted in the complex plane appears here in its simplest form. Dielectric relaxation, chemical reactions, heat conduction, and so on: every system that obeys a first-order relaxation equation $\dot P=-P/\tau_0+(\text{external force})$ has a pole of this type.

Exercise 5.6: Using the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to find $\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\to0^+}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{(x+\ii\eta)(x^2+1)}$. We apply the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula (upper sign)

$$ \lim_{\eta\to0^+}\frac{1}{x+\ii\eta}=\PV\frac{1}{x}-\ii\pi\,\delta(x) $$

to the test function $f(x)=\dfrac{1}{x^2+1}$:

$$ \lim_{\eta\to0^+}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f(x)}{x+\ii\eta}\,\dd x =\PV\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{x(x^2+1)} -\ii\pi\,f(0) $$

We evaluate the two terms in turn. For the principal-value term, note that the integrand $\dfrac{1}{x(x^2+1)}$ is an odd function (under $x\to-x$ the $x$ in the denominator changes sign, while $x^2+1$ does not). The definition of the principal value was an operation of cutting out the neighborhood of the origin symmetrically on the left and right, with the same width $\varepsilon$, so the contribution of an odd function cancels between left and right and is zero. The delta-function term is $-\ii\pi$ because $f(0)=\dfrac{1}{0+1}=1$. Altogether,

$$ \lim_{\eta\to0^+}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{(x+\ii\eta)(x^2+1)}=-\ii\pi $$

Check (direct calculation with residues). Keeping $\eta>0$ fixed and finite, let us compute the same integral with the residue theorem. The function $h(z)=\dfrac{1}{(z+\ii\eta)(z-\ii)(z+\ii)}$, obtained by extending the integrand to the complex plane, has three poles: $z=-\ii\eta$ (lower half-plane), $z=\ii$ (upper half-plane), and $z=-\ii$ (lower half-plane). Since $\abs{h}\sim1/R^3$ at large distances, the large semicircle in the upper half-plane vanishes by the ML inequality alone (there is no exponential factor, so Jordan's lemma is not needed). Closing in the upper half-plane, the only pole enclosed is $z=\ii$, and

$$ \Res_{z=\ii}h=\frac{1}{(\ii+\ii\eta)(\ii+\ii)}=\frac{1}{2\ii^2(1+\eta)}=-\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)} $$

Therefore

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd x}{(x+\ii\eta)(x^2+1)} =2\pi\ii\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{2(1+\eta)}\right)=-\frac{\ii\pi}{1+\eta} \ \xrightarrow{\ \eta\to0^+\ }\ -\ii\pi $$

This agrees with the answer from the S–P formula. Moreover, this method also tells us the value at finite $\eta$, namely $-\ii\pi/(1+\eta)$. Numerical integration (python3) also gives $-3.1385\,\ii$ for $\eta=10^{-3}$, consistent with $-\ii\pi/(1.001)\simeq-3.1385\,\ii$ and with the limiting value $-\ii\pi\simeq-3.1416\,\ii$.

(2) This problem asks us to find the imaginary part of the response function $\chi(\omega)=\dfrac{A}{\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta}$ in the limit $\eta\to0^+$ ($A$ is a real constant). If we read $x$ in the S–P formula as $\omega-\omega_0$, we obtain

$$ \lim_{\eta\to0^+}\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta} =\PV\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0}-\ii\pi\,\delta(\omega-\omega_0) $$

so the real and imaginary parts separate at one stroke:

$$ \mathrm{Re}\,\chi(\omega)=A\,\PV\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0}, \qquad \mathrm{Im}\,\chi(\omega)=-\pi A\,\delta(\omega-\omega_0) $$

The imaginary part we seek is $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi(\omega)=-\pi A\,\delta(\omega-\omega_0)$.

Let us also look at the intermediate result (finite $\eta$). Making the denominator real,

$$ \chi(\omega)=\frac{A\,(\omega-\omega_0)}{(\omega-\omega_0)^2+\eta^2} -\ii\,\frac{A\,\eta}{(\omega-\omega_0)^2+\eta^2} $$

the imaginary part is a Lorentzian (with a negative sign) of width $\eta$ and height $A/\eta$ centered at $\omega_0$, and its area is

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{Im}\,\chi(\omega)\,\dd\omega =-A\left[\arctan\frac{\omega-\omega_0}{\eta}\right]_{-\infty}^{\infty}=-\pi A $$

which is constant, independent of $\eta$. As $\eta\to0^+$ it becomes “infinitely narrow and tall, with area $-\pi A$,” and indeed converges to $-\pi A\,\delta(\omega-\omega_0)$.

Physical meaning. The imaginary part (the absorption spectrum) is a delta function standing only at the excitation level $\omega_0$; if a finite $\eta$ is retained, it becomes a Lorentzian of width $\eta$—a broadening due to the lifetime $1/\eta$ of the level. In the Lehmann representation of Chapter 8, the response function is written as a sum of terms of this type, $\sum_n\abs{M_n}^2/(\omega-\omega_n+\ii\eta)$, and the imaginary part becomes a train of delta functions standing at the positions of the excitation levels, $-\pi\sum_n\abs{M_n}^2\delta(\omega-\omega_n)$. The present problem is exactly the calculation for one such term. That the area $-\pi A$ is conserved independent of $\eta$ means that the total amount of absorption (the sum rule for the spectrum) does not depend on the degree of broadening. ∎

Chapter 6: The Three Pictures and the Time-Evolution Operator

Exercise 6.1: $\hat p_H(t)$ and the free particle

Solution

(1) By the same procedure by which we found $\hat x_H(t)$ in Section 6.3.3, we derive $\hat p_H(t)=\hat p\cos\omega t-m\omega\hat x\sin\omega t$ for the harmonic oscillator.

The starting point is the expression of the Schrödinger-picture operators in terms of the creation and annihilation operators

$$ \hat p=\ii\sqrt{\frac{m\hbar\omega}{2}}\left(\hat a^\dagger-\hat a\right), \qquad \hat x=\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2m\omega}}\left(\hat a+\hat a^\dagger\right) $$

together with the relations $\hat a_H(t)=\hat a\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$ and $\hat a^\dagger_H(t)=\hat a^\dagger\,\ee^{+\ii\omega t}$ derived in Section 6.3.2. Since $\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}(\cdots)\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}$ is a linear operation, it suffices to replace $\hat a$ and $\hat a^\dagger$ inside $\hat p$ by these, respectively:

$$ \hat p_H(t)=\ii\sqrt{\frac{m\hbar\omega}{2}}\left(\hat a^\dagger\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}-\hat a\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}\right) $$

Substituting Euler's formula $\ee^{\pm\ii\omega t}=\cos\omega t\pm\ii\sin\omega t$, we regroup the result into the part containing $\cos$ and the part containing $\sin$:

$$ \begin{aligned} \hat a^\dagger\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}-\hat a\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t} &=\hat a^\dagger(\cos\omega t+\ii\sin\omega t)-\hat a(\cos\omega t-\ii\sin\omega t)\\[2pt] &=\underbrace{(\hat a^\dagger-\hat a)}_{\text{proportional to $\hat p$}}\cos\omega t +\ii\underbrace{(\hat a+\hat a^\dagger)}_{\text{proportional to $\hat x$}}\sin\omega t \end{aligned} $$

The first term multiplied by the coefficient $\ii\sqrt{m\hbar\omega/2}$ is exactly $\hat p\cos\omega t$. The second term is

$$ \ii\sqrt{\frac{m\hbar\omega}{2}}\cdot\ii\,(\hat a+\hat a^\dagger)\sin\omega t =-\sqrt{\frac{m\hbar\omega}{2}}\,(\hat a+\hat a^\dagger)\sin\omega t $$

Since the expression for $\hat x$ gives $(\hat a+\hat a^\dagger)=\sqrt{2m\omega/\hbar}\,\hat x$, the coefficient combines into

$$ -\sqrt{\frac{m\hbar\omega}{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2m\omega}{\hbar}} =-\sqrt{m^2\omega^2}=-m\omega $$

Therefore

$$ \hat p_H(t)=\hat p\cos\omega t-m\omega\,\hat x\sin\omega t $$

is obtained.

Check. At $t=0$ we recover $\hat p_H(0)=\hat p$. Also, pairing this with $\hat x_H(t)=\hat x\cos\omega t+\dfrac{\hat p}{m\omega}\sin\omega t$ of Section 6.3.3 and differentiating, we find

$$ \frac{\dd\hat x_H}{\dd t}=-\omega\,\hat x\sin\omega t+\frac{\hat p}{m}\cos\omega t=\frac{\hat p_H(t)}{m}, \qquad \frac{\dd\hat p_H}{\dd t}=-\omega\,\hat p\sin\omega t-m\omega^2\hat x\cos\omega t=-m\omega^2\,\hat x_H(t) $$

so the simultaneous equations $\dot{\hat x}_H=\hat p_H/m$, $\dot{\hat p}_H=-m\omega^2\hat x_H$ that follow from the Heisenberg equation are indeed satisfied. They are identical in form to the classical canonical equations.

(2) We write down the Heisenberg equation for the free particle $\Ham=\hat p^2/2m$ and show that $\hat p_H(t)=\hat p$ and $\hat x_H(t)=\hat x+\dfrac{\hat p}{m}t$.

First, for $\hat p$. Since $\hat p$ commutes with its own power $\hat p^2$, we have $[\hat p,\Ham]=0$. This relation still holds when sandwiched between $\ee^{\ii\Ham t/\hbar}\cdots\ee^{-\ii\Ham t/\hbar}$, so the Heisenberg equation (the equation $\ii\hbar\,\dd\hat A_H/\dd t=[\hat A_H,\Ham]$ derived in Section 6.2.2) gives

$$ \ii\hbar\,\frac{\dd\hat p_H}{\dd t}=\left[\hat p_H(t),\Ham\right]=0 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \hat p_H(t)=\hat p_H(0)=\hat p $$

Momentum is a conserved quantity (translational symmetry, as in the Physical Meaning box of Section 6.2.2). Next, for $\hat x$, we first compute the commutator given in the hint. Using $[\hat A,\hat B\hat C]=[\hat A,\hat B]\hat C+\hat B[\hat A,\hat C]$, we obtain

$$ [\hat x,\hat p^2]=[\hat x,\hat p]\,\hat p+\hat p\,[\hat x,\hat p]=\ii\hbar\,\hat p+\hat p\cdot\ii\hbar=2\ii\hbar\,\hat p $$

Therefore $[\hat x,\Ham]=\dfrac{1}{2m}\cdot2\ii\hbar\,\hat p=\dfrac{\ii\hbar}{m}\hat p$. Sandwiching both sides between $\ee^{\ii\Ham t/\hbar}\cdots\ee^{-\ii\Ham t/\hbar}$ gives $[\hat x_H(t),\Ham]=\dfrac{\ii\hbar}{m}\hat p_H(t)=\dfrac{\ii\hbar}{m}\hat p$, so

$$ \ii\hbar\,\frac{\dd\hat x_H}{\dd t}=\frac{\ii\hbar}{m}\hat p \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \frac{\dd\hat x_H}{\dd t}=\frac{\hat p}{m} $$

Thanks to $\hat p_H(t)=\hat p$ just shown, the right-hand side is a time-independent constant operator. No product of operators appears either, so it can be integrated directly, just like an ordinary function, and the initial condition $\hat x_H(0)=\hat x$ gives

$$ \hat x_H(t)=\hat x+\frac{\hat p}{m}\,t $$

This too has the same form as the classical solution $x(t)=x_0+(p_0/m)\,t$ (uniform linear motion).

(3) Using the result of (2), we compute the commutator at different times, $[\hat x_H(t),\hat x_H(0)]$, and confirm that it is zero at equal times but not zero at different times.

By the bilinearity of the commutator,

$$ \left[\hat x_H(t),\hat x_H(0)\right] =\left[\hat x+\frac{\hat p}{m}t,\ \hat x\right] =\underbrace{[\hat x,\hat x]}_{=0}+\frac{t}{m}\,[\hat p,\hat x] =\frac{t}{m}\,(-\ii\hbar) =-\frac{\ii\hbar\,t}{m} $$

At $t=0$ it is indeed zero (the equal-time commutator, Section 6.2.3), but for $t\ne0$ it is not zero. Moreover, the result contains no operator: it is a c-number.

Check: The $\omega\to0$ limit of the harmonic oscillator. In $[\hat x_I(t_1),\hat x_I(t_2)]=-\dfrac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega}\sin[\omega(t_1-t_2)]$, derived in Section 6.7.2, set $t_1=t$ and $t_2=0$, and take $\omega\to0$. Since $\sin\omega t\simeq\omega t$,

$$ -\frac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega}\sin\omega t\ \xrightarrow{\ \omega\to0\ }\ -\frac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega}\cdot\omega t=-\frac{\ii\hbar\,t}{m} $$

which agrees with the result we have just obtained. The obvious fact that an oscillator that has lost its restoring force is a free particle holds even at the level of the commutator.

Physical Meaning: This commutator is the Green's function of the free particle

Inserting this commutator into the same definition as in Exercise 6.5, $\chi(t)=\dfrac{\ii}{\hbar}\theta(t)\braket{[\hat x_H(t),\hat x_H(0)]}$, we find that, because the commutator is a c-number, the value does not change whatever state the expectation value is taken in, and we obtain

$$ \chi(t)=\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\,\theta(t)\left(-\frac{\ii\hbar\,t}{m}\right)=\theta(t)\,\frac{t}{m} $$

This is the “retarded Green's function of the free particle $\theta(t)\,t/m$” mentioned in the hint. If we give an impulse $J$ at time $0$, the displacement grows in proportion to time as $\delta x(t)=Jt/m$—this is exactly the uniform motion of a free particle that has received an impulsive force. The structure in which the answer of classical mechanics emerges from the commutator of quantum theory is exactly the same as in the harmonic-oscillator case of Section 6.7.2.

Exercise 6.2: Alternative expressions for $\hat U$

Solution

(1) When $\Ham=\Ham_0+\Ham_1$ does not depend on time, we show that $\hat U(t,t_0)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}$.

We start from the definition of the interaction picture (Section 6.4.2), $\ket{\psi_I(t)}=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\ket{\psi_S(t)}$, and link three relations like a chain. First, since $\Ham$ is a constant, the time evolution in the Schrödinger picture can be written as $\ket{\psi_S(t)}=\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\ket{\psi_S(t_0)}$ (the exponential solution of Section 6.1.1 with $t\to t-t_0$). Substituting this into the definition gives

$$ \ket{\psi_I(t)}=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ket{\psi_S(t_0)} $$

Finally, we insert $\ket{\psi_S(t_0)}=\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}\ket{\psi_I(t_0)}$, which is the definition solved backwards at $t=t_0$:

$$ \ket{\psi_I(t)} =\underbrace{\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}}_{\text{this is }\hat U(t,t_0)}\,\ket{\psi_I(t_0)} $$

Since $\ket{\psi_I(t_0)}$ is arbitrary, the underbraced part is the operator that takes $\ket{\psi_I(t_0)}$ to $\ket{\psi_I(t)}$, that is, $\hat U(t,t_0)$:

$$ \hat U(t,t_0)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar} $$

Check. We verify directly that this expression satisfies the defining equation $\ii\hbar\,\partial_t\hat U=\Ham_{1I}(t)\hat U$, $\hat U(t_0,t_0)=\hat 1$ of Section 6.5.1. The initial condition is obvious from $\hat U(t_0,t_0)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}\,\ee^{0}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}=\hat 1$. For the derivative, since the factors containing $t$ are the left two,

$$ \ii\hbar\,\frac{\partial\hat U}{\partial t} =-\Ham_0\,\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar} +\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\Ham\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar} $$

In the second term, inserting $\hat 1=\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}$ to the right of $\Ham$ produces $\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\Ham\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}=\Ham_0+\Ham_{1I}(t)$ (the $\Ham_0$ part is unchanged by the sandwiching, and the $\Ham_1$ part becomes $\Ham_{1I}(t)$ by definition), and we obtain

$$ \ii\hbar\,\frac{\partial\hat U}{\partial t} =\left(-\Ham_0+\Ham_0+\Ham_{1I}(t)\right)\hat U(t,t_0)=\Ham_{1I}(t)\,\hat U(t,t_0) $$

so the equation is indeed satisfied. Since the solution of a first-order linear equation is unique once the initial condition is given (the same argument as in the derivation of the group property in Section 6.5.3), this expression is $\hat U$ itself.

Common Mistake: Do not combine the three exponentials into one

One is tempted to write $\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}$ as $\ee^{\ii[\Ham_0t-\Ham(t-t_0)]/\hbar}$, but this is wrong as long as $[\Ham_0,\Ham]=[\Ham_0,\Ham_1]\ne0$ (the “Common Error” box in Section 6.1.2). Only exponentials of the same operator, as used in (2) and (3), may be combined. The correct thing to do is to carry the three exponentials along as they are.

(2) We verify directly that $\hat U$ of (1) is unitary and satisfies the group property.

Unitarity. The Hermitian conjugate of a product reverses the order and takes the conjugate of each factor. Since $\Ham_0$ and $\Ham$ are Hermitian, the conjugate of each factor merely changes the sign of the exponent, so

$$ \hat U^\dagger(t,t_0)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}\,\ee^{+\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar} $$

Multiplying the two, the “exponentials of the same operator” cancel in pairs, one after another, starting from the middle:

$$ \hat U^\dagger\hat U =\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}\,\ee^{\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\, \underbrace{\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}}_{=\,\hat 1}\, \ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar} =\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}\, \underbrace{\ee^{\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}}_{=\,\hat 1}\, \ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar} =\hat 1 $$

$\hat U\hat U^\dagger=\hat 1$ can be shown by the same procedure. Hence it is unitary.

Group property. When we place two of them side by side, the factors at the junction $t_1$ cancel each other in the middle:

$$ \hat U(t_2,t_1)\,\hat U(t_1,t_0) =\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_2/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t_2-t_1)/\hbar}\, \underbrace{\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_1/\hbar}\,\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_1/\hbar}}_{=\,\hat 1}\, \ee^{-\ii\Ham(t_1-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar} $$

The remaining two in the middle are exponentials of the same $\Ham$, so they can be combined (as noted at the end of the box in Section 6.1.2):

$$ \ee^{-\ii\Ham(t_2-t_1)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t_1-t_0)/\hbar}=\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t_2-t_0)/\hbar} $$

Therefore

$$ \hat U(t_2,t_1)\,\hat U(t_1,t_0) =\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_2/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t_2-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar} =\hat U(t_2,t_0) $$

As for the other relation, if we write down the expression of (1) with the arguments interchanged, we find

$$ \hat U(t_0,t)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham(t_0-t)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar} =\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}\,\ee^{+\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar} =\hat U^\dagger(t,t_0) $$

which agrees with the $\hat U^\dagger$ obtained above. Combined with unitarity, $\hat U^\dagger=\hat U^{-1}$, all of the group property has now been verified.

(3) We show that $\hat U(t,t_0)=\ee^{-\ii\Ham_1(t-t_0)/\hbar}$ when $[\Ham_0,\Ham_1]=0$.

Only when $\Ham_0$ and $\Ham_1$ commute can the exponential be freely split and combined (because all the commutator terms of the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula vanish):

$$ \ee^{-\ii\Ham(t-t_0)/\hbar}=\ee^{-\ii(\Ham_0+\Ham_1)(t-t_0)/\hbar} =\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_1(t-t_0)/\hbar} $$

Moreover, $\ee^{-\ii\Ham_1(t-t_0)/\hbar}$ commutes with the exponentials of $\Ham_0$, so it can be moved freely within the expression of (1). Collecting only the factors that contain $\Ham_0$, we find

$$ \hat U(t,t_0) =\underbrace{\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0(t-t_0)/\hbar}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t_0/\hbar}}_{\text{sum of exponents}=\ \ii\Ham_0[t-(t-t_0)-t_0]/\hbar\ =\ 0}\; \ee^{-\ii\Ham_1(t-t_0)/\hbar} =\ee^{-\ii\Ham_1(t-t_0)/\hbar} $$

The underbraced part consists of exponentials of the same $\Ham_0$, so the exponents may be added; they add up to exactly zero, and the part collapses to $\hat 1$. ∎

Physical meaning. In this case the time dependence of the interaction-picture perturbation disappears: $\Ham_{1I}(t)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\Ham_1\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}=\Ham_1$. Since $\Ham_{1I}$ at different times trivially commute, the $T$-product is unnecessary, as stated in Section 6.7.1, and the $T$-exponential degenerates into the ordinary exponential $\exp[-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\Ham_1(t-t_0)]$. The result we have just obtained is precisely this form. It is the simplest verification of the claim of Section 6.7 that the $T$-product is truly needed only when $[\Ham_0,\Ham_1]\ne0$.

Exercise 6.3: Obtaining $1/3!$ from the third-order term

Solution

(1) We enumerate the $3!=6$ ways of ordering the three times $t_1,t_2,t_3$ relative to one another.

First there are three choices for “which is the latest,” and two ways to order the remaining two, for a total of $3\times2\times1=3!=6$ ways. Written out explicitly, they are

$$ t_1>t_2>t_3,\quad t_1>t_3>t_2,\quad t_2>t_1>t_3,\quad t_2>t_3>t_1,\quad t_3>t_1>t_2,\quad t_3>t_2>t_1 $$

These are the six orderings. The cases in which two or more times coincide belong to none of the above, but within the cube $[t_0,t]^3$ they form only planes (sets of zero volume), and since the integrand is bounded they do not contribute to the integral. We may ignore them from here on.

(2) We show, by renaming variables, that integrating over any of the six ordered regions with the $T$-product inserted gives the same value.

As a preparation, let us rewrite $I_3$ itself using the $T$-product. The nested range of integration of $I_3$ is the region $t\ge t_1\ge t_2\ge t_3\ge t_0$, that is, the first region above, $\{t_1>t_2>t_3\}$. In this region the operator ordering $\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)$ has “later times to the left,” which by definition is exactly the $T$-product in this region:

$$ I_3=\int_{\{t_1>t_2>t_3\}}\dd t_1\,\dd t_2\,\dd t_3\; T\!\left[\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)\right] $$

Next, let us carry out the cyclic permutation given in the hint. Since $I_3$ is a definite integral, its value does not change however we relabel the names of the integration variables. Rewriting the letters in the expression for $I_3$ all at once as $t_1\to t_2$, $t_2\to t_3$, $t_3\to t_1$, we obtain

$$ I_3=\int_{t_0}^{t}\dd t_2\int_{t_0}^{t_2}\dd t_3\int_{t_0}^{t_3}\dd t_1\; \hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)\hat h(t_1) $$

Reading off the ranges of integration, we see that the region has moved to $\{t_2>t_3>t_1\}$. And the ordering of the integrand, $\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)\hat h(t_1)$, is indeed “later to the left” in this region—in other words, it coincides with $T[\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)]$ in this region:

$$ I_3=\int_{\{t_2>t_3>t_1\}}\dd t_1\,\dd t_2\,\dd t_3\; T\!\left[\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)\right] $$

This is the key point. When we rename the variables, the integration region and the operator ordering move together, so “the correct time ordering for that region” is automatically preserved. The $T$-product is the instruction “in whatever region we are, put the later times to the left,” so in every ordered region the integrand is exactly the $T$-product.

The same holds for any renaming. Choose a permutation $\sigma$ of $(1,2,3)$ and rewrite the letters of $I_3$ as $t_1\to t_{\sigma(1)}$, $t_2\to t_{\sigma(2)}$, $t_3\to t_{\sigma(3)}$. The region then moves to $\{t_{\sigma(1)}>t_{\sigma(2)}>t_{\sigma(3)}\}$, and the integrand becomes $\hat h(t_{\sigma(1)})\hat h(t_{\sigma(2)})\hat h(t_{\sigma(3)})$—the product arranged from the left in order of decreasing time in that region, that is, the $T$-product. Since there are six choices of $\sigma$, we can summarize them in the following table. In every row the value is the same $I_3$.

The six ordered regions and the content of the $T$-product in each
Ordered region$T[\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)]$ in that regionValue of the integral
$t_1>t_2>t_3$$\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)$$I_3$
$t_1>t_3>t_2$$\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_3)\hat h(t_2)$$I_3$
$t_2>t_1>t_3$$\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_3)$$I_3$
$t_2>t_3>t_1$$\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)\hat h(t_1)$$I_3$
$t_3>t_1>t_2$$\hat h(t_3)\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)$$I_3$
$t_3>t_2>t_1$$\hat h(t_3)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_1)$$I_3$

Note also that $\Ham_1$, which we treat in this book, consists of an even number of fermion operators, so no sign arises when the $T$-product reorders them (the caution box in Section 6.6.3). There is therefore no need to worry about signs in this argument.

(3) From the above we conclude that $I_3=\dfrac{1}{3!}\displaystyle\int_{t_0}^{t}\dd t_1\int_{t_0}^{t}\dd t_2\int_{t_0}^{t}\dd t_3\;T\left[\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)\right]$.

As we saw in (1), the six ordered regions do not overlap one another (they overlap only on the zero-volume planes where times coincide), and together they cover the cube $[t_0,t]^3$ completely. Therefore the integral of the $T$-product over the whole cube decomposes into the sum of the integrals over the six regions, and by (2) all six are equal to $I_3$:

$$ \int_{t_0}^{t}\dd t_1\int_{t_0}^{t}\dd t_2\int_{t_0}^{t}\dd t_3\; T\!\left[\hat h(t_1)\hat h(t_2)\hat h(t_3)\right] =\sum_{\text{six ordered regions}}(\text{integral over each region}) =6\,I_3=3!\,I_3 $$

Dividing both sides by $3!$ gives the desired formula. ∎ In the second-order case (Figure 6.3), “the triangle is half of the square”; at third order this becomes “the triangular pyramid (simplex) is $1/6$ of the cube.” Repeating the same argument at $n$th order, $n!$ ordered regions fill the hypercube, and the factor $1/n!$ appears—this is the general theory of Section 6.6.3.

Exercise 6.4: Excitation by a rectangular pulse

Solution

(1) We compute the Fourier transform $F(\omega)$ of the rectangular pulse ($F_0$ for $0<t<\tau$, zero otherwise).

With the convention of Chapter 1, $F(\omega)=\int\dd t\,F(t)\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}$, and since $F(t)$ vanishes outside the interval $[0,\tau]$, we have

$$ F(\omega)=F_0\int_{0}^{\tau}\ee^{\ii\omega t}\,\dd t =F_0\left[\frac{\ee^{\ii\omega t}}{\ii\omega}\right]_{0}^{\tau} =F_0\,\frac{\ee^{\ii\omega\tau}-1}{\ii\omega} $$

In preparation for taking the absolute value, it is clearer to factor out half of the phase and tidy up:

$$ \ee^{\ii\omega\tau}-1 =\ee^{\ii\omega\tau/2}\left(\ee^{\ii\omega\tau/2}-\ee^{-\ii\omega\tau/2}\right) =2\ii\,\ee^{\ii\omega\tau/2}\sin\frac{\omega\tau}{2} $$

Therefore

$$ F(\omega)=\frac{2F_0}{\omega}\,\ee^{\ii\omega\tau/2}\,\sin\frac{\omega\tau}{2}, \qquad \abs{F(\omega)}^2=\frac{4F_0^2}{\omega^2}\,\sin^2\frac{\omega\tau}{2} $$

This agrees with the formula in the hint. The phase factor $\ee^{\ii\omega\tau/2}$ merely expresses that “the center of the pulse is at $t=\tau/2$,” and does not affect the absolute value.

(2) We substitute into the result of Example 6.3, $P_{0\to1}=\dfrac{\abs{F(\omega)}^2}{2m\hbar\omega}$, to show that $P_{0\to1}=\dfrac{2F_0^2}{m\hbar\omega^3}\sin^2\!\left(\dfrac{\omega\tau}{2}\right)$.

We simply substitute $\abs{F(\omega)}^2$ from (1), evaluated at the oscillator's natural frequency $\omega$:

$$ P_{0\to1}=\frac{1}{2m\hbar\omega}\cdot\frac{4F_0^2}{\omega^2}\sin^2\frac{\omega\tau}{2} =\frac{2F_0^2}{m\hbar\omega^3}\,\sin^2\!\left(\frac{\omega\tau}{2}\right) $$

∎ The desired formula has been obtained.

(3) We show that in the impulsive-force limit $\tau\to0$ (keeping the impulse $J=F_0\tau$ fixed), $P_{0\to1}\to\dfrac{J^2}{2m\hbar\omega}$.

For $\tau\to0$ we have $\omega\tau\ll1$, so we can expand $\sin(\omega\tau/2)\simeq\omega\tau/2$:

$$ P_{0\to1}\ \longrightarrow\ \frac{2F_0^2}{m\hbar\omega^3}\cdot\frac{\omega^2\tau^2}{4} =\frac{(F_0\tau)^2}{2m\hbar\omega} =\frac{J^2}{2m\hbar\omega} $$

∎ Even for $F_0\to\infty$, $\tau\to0$, the answer remains finite as long as the product $J=F_0\tau$ is finite.

Check: Independence of the waveform. In the impulsive-force limit, $F(t)\to J\,\delta(t)$, whose Fourier transform is $F(\omega)=J$ (white, containing all frequencies equally), so $P_{0\to1}=\abs{F(\omega)}^2/2m\hbar\omega=J^2/2m\hbar\omega$ follows immediately. For the Gaussian pulse of Example 6.4 as well, fixing the impulse $J=\int\dd t\,F(t)=F_0\sigma\sqrt{2\pi}$ and letting $\sigma\to0$ gives $P_{0\to1}=J^2\ee^{-\omega^2\sigma^2}/2m\hbar\omega\to J^2/2m\hbar\omega$, the same value. In the impulsive-force limit, the excitation probability does not depend on the details of the waveform; it is determined by the impulse alone. Note also that $J^2/2m$ is the kinetic energy that an impulsive force imparts in classical mechanics, so this probability can be read as “the classical energy imparted divided by the energy quantum $\hbar\omega$,” that is, the mean number of quanta excited ($P\ll1$ is the condition for the applicability of first-order perturbation theory).

(4) We explain physically why $P_{0\to1}=0$ when $\omega\tau=2\pi n$ ($n$ an integer).

Formally, this is because $\sin(\omega\tau/2)=\sin(n\pi)=0$; that is, because the spectrum $F(\omega)$ of the rectangular pulse has a zero exactly at the oscillator's natural frequency. As we saw in Example 6.3, the oscillator feels only the natural-frequency component $F(\omega)$ of the external force, so if that component is zero, nothing happens.

In the time domain the picture is even more concrete. While the constant force $F_0$ acts, the oscillator circles around the new equilibrium point $x_{\mathrm{eq}}=F_0/m\omega^2$ (classically, starting from rest, $x(t)=x_{\mathrm{eq}}(1-\cos\omega t)$ and $p(t)=m\omega\,x_{\mathrm{eq}}\sin\omega t$). If the pulse length $\tau$ is exactly an integer multiple of the period $2\pi/\omega$, then at the instant $t=\tau$ when the force is switched off we have $x=0$ and $p=0$—that is, it is the instant at which the oscillator has returned to its starting point. If the force is switched off there, the system stays at rest as though nothing had happened. The energy that the external force poured into the system during the first half period is given back by the system to the external force during the second half period. In the words of the hint, “the amount pushed in and the amount pushed back exactly cancel.” The transient oscillation set up when the switch is turned on and the one set up when it is turned off interfere completely in antiphase and cancel—this is the same physics of interference as in the resonance and antiresonance of Chapter 3.

Exercise 6.5: The commutator and the classical Green's function

Solution

(1) Confirm that $[\hat x_I(t_1),\hat x_I(t_2)]=-\dfrac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega}\sin[\omega(t_1-t_2)]$ is a c-number, and explain the reason in terms of the commutation relation of $\hat a,\hat a^\dagger$.

$\hat x_I(t)$ is a linear expression in the creation and annihilation operators:

$$ \hat x_I(t)=u(t)\,\hat a+u^*(t)\,\hat a^\dagger, \qquad u(t)\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2m\omega}}\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t} $$

In general, for complex coefficients $u_1,u_2$, expanding the commutator of two such linear expressions by bilinearity gives
