$$ \left[u_1\hat a+u_1^*\hat a^\dagger,\ u_2\hat a+u_2^*\hat a^\dagger\right] =u_1u_2^*\underbrace{[\hat a,\hat a^\dagger]}_{=1} +u_1^*u_2\underbrace{[\hat a^\dagger,\hat a]}_{=-1} =u_1u_2^*-u_1^*u_2 $$

(the terms with $[\hat a,\hat a]=[\hat a^\dagger,\hat a^\dagger]=0$ drop out). The right-hand side is a complex number and contains no operators. The reason is clear: the right-hand side of the fundamental commutation relation $[\hat a,\hat a^\dagger]=1$ is itself a number (a c-number). Since the commutator is bilinear, nothing but the right-hand side of the fundamental commutation relation can appear in the commutator of two linear expressions, so the result is necessarily a number. Substituting $u_i=u(t_i)$ explicitly, we obtain

$$ u_1u_2^*-u_1^*u_2 =\frac{\hbar}{2m\omega}\left(\ee^{-\ii\omega(t_1-t_2)}-\ee^{+\ii\omega(t_1-t_2)}\right) =-\frac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega}\sin[\omega(t_1-t_2)] $$

which reproduces the given expression (we used $\ee^{-\ii\theta}-\ee^{\ii\theta}=-2\ii\sin\theta$).

Physical meaning. This is a consequence of the fact that the harmonic oscillator (a Hamiltonian that is a quadratic form) is a linear system. Because the equation of motion is linear, $\hat x_I(t)$ remains a linear expression in $\hat a,\hat a^\dagger$ under time evolution, and the commutator cannot escape from being a number. In contrast, for the spin of Example 6.5, an operator remains on the right-hand side of the commutator, $[\hat S_x,\hat S_y]=\ii\hbar\hat S_z$, so $\hat S_z$ also remained in the commutator at different times. “Whether the commutator is a c-number” is the fingerprint of whether the system is linear.

(2) Verify that it vanishes at $t_1=t_2$ and is maximal at $t_1-t_2=\pi/2\omega$.

At $t_1=t_2$ we have $\sin0=0$, so the commutator vanishes. This is consistent with the trivial relation $[\hat x,\hat x]=0$ at equal times (Section 6.2.3). The magnitude $\abs{\sin[\omega(t_1-t_2)]}$ takes its maximum value $1$ when $\omega(t_1-t_2)=\pi/2+n\pi$ ($n$ an integer); the first such case is $t_1-t_2=\pi/2\omega$, that is, when the two times are separated by one quarter of the period $T=2\pi/\omega$. The value there is

$$ \left[\hat x_I(t_2+\tfrac{\pi}{2\omega}),\hat x_I(t_2)\right]=-\frac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega} $$

Physical meaning. Why a quarter period? Setting $\omega t=\pi/2$ in $\hat x_H(t)=\hat x\cos\omega t+\dfrac{\hat p}{m\omega}\sin\omega t$ of Section 6.3.3 gives $\hat x_H=\hat p/m\omega$—through the rotation in phase space (Figure 6.2), the “position” after a quarter period has turned into the initial “momentum.” The commutator at that point is $[\hat p/m\omega,\hat x]=-\ii\hbar/m\omega$, that is, the canonical commutation relation itself. The maximum of the commutator at different times is nothing other than the noncommutativity of $\hat x$ and $\hat p$ showing itself through the rotation in phase space.

(3) Compute the response function $\chi(t)=\dfrac{\ii}{\hbar}\theta(t)\braket{[\hat x_H(t),\hat x_H(0)]}$ and show that it agrees with the $\gamma\to0$ limit of the Green's function of Chapter 2.

From here on, following the instruction in (4), we write the natural frequency of the oscillator as $\omega_0$ (to distinguish it from the Fourier variable $\omega$). Since $\hat x_H$ here is the operator evolved by the unperturbed Hamiltonian $\Ham_0$ alone, it is the same as $\hat x_I$ (as noted in the box of Section 6.4.2), and the commutator of (1) can be used as it is. Since the commutator is a c-number, the expectation value is just the commutator itself, whichever state it is taken in:

$$ \braket{\left[\hat x_H(t),\hat x_H(0)\right]}=-\frac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega_0}\sin\omega_0t $$

Therefore

$$ \chi(t)=\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\,\theta(t)\left(-\frac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega_0}\right)\sin\omega_0t =\theta(t)\,\frac{\sin\omega_0t}{m\omega_0} $$

On the other hand, the Green's function of Chapter 2, $G(t)=\dfrac{1}{m\omega_1}\ee^{-\gamma t/(2m)}\sin(\omega_1t)\,\theta(t)$, had $\omega_1=\sqrt{\omega_0^2-(\gamma/2m)^2}$, so that as $\gamma\to0$ we have $\omega_1\to\omega_0$ and $\ee^{-\gamma t/(2m)}\to1$, giving

$$ G(t)\ \xrightarrow{\ \gamma\to0\ }\ \theta(t)\,\frac{\sin\omega_0t}{m\omega_0} $$

The two agree exactly. ∎ In the ground state, in a coherent state, or in a thermal equilibrium state at finite temperature, $\chi(t)$ is the same—the response function of a linear system returns the same answer as classical mechanics, independent of the state and of how $\hbar$ enters (this confirms the discussion of Section 6.7.2).

(4) Fourier-transform (3) with the convention of Chapter 1, and verify that $\chi(\omega)=\dfrac{1}{m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)}$ and that the poles lie in the lower half-plane.

We use the convention of Chapter 1, $\chi(\omega)=\int\dd t\,\chi(t)\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}$. Since $\chi(t)$ vanishes for $t<0$, the integral runs from $0$ to $\infty$, and since the oscillation does not die out as $t\to+\infty$, we insert a convergence factor $\ee^{-\eta t}$ ($\eta>0$, with $\eta\to0^+$ at the end):

$$ \chi(\omega)=\frac{1}{m\omega_0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(\omega_0t)\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\,\ee^{-\eta t}\,\dd t =\frac{1}{m\omega_0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(\omega_0t)\,\ee^{\ii zt}\,\dd t, \qquad z\equiv\omega+\ii\eta $$

As in the hint, we split $\sin$ into two exponential functions. If $\mathrm{Im}\,\alpha>0$, then $\abs{\ee^{\ii\alpha t}}=\ee^{-\mathrm{Im}\alpha\,t}\to0$ at the upper limit, so

$$ \int_{0}^{\infty}\ee^{\ii\alpha t}\dd t=\left[\frac{\ee^{\ii\alpha t}}{\ii\alpha}\right]_{0}^{\infty}=-\frac{1}{\ii\alpha}=\frac{\ii}{\alpha} $$

Using this for $\alpha=z\pm\omega_0$ (both have imaginary part $\eta>0$), we obtain

$$ \int_{0}^{\infty}\sin(\omega_0t)\,\ee^{\ii zt}\dd t =\frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}\ee^{\ii(z+\omega_0)t}\dd t-\int_{0}^{\infty}\ee^{\ii(z-\omega_0)t}\dd t\right] =\frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\frac{\ii}{z+\omega_0}-\frac{\ii}{z-\omega_0}\right] $$

Canceling $\ii$ and bringing to a common denominator:

$$ =\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{(z-\omega_0)-(z+\omega_0)}{z^2-\omega_0^2} =\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{-2\omega_0}{z^2-\omega_0^2} =\frac{\omega_0}{\omega_0^2-z^2} $$

Therefore

$$ \chi(\omega)=\frac{1}{m\omega_0}\cdot\frac{\omega_0}{\omega_0^2-z^2} =\frac{1}{m\left[\omega_0^2-(\omega+\ii\eta)^2\right]} \ \xrightarrow{\ \eta\to0^+\ }\ \frac{1}{m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)} $$

which gives the desired form. ∎ This also agrees with the $\gamma\to0$ limit of the response of the damped forced oscillation obtained in Chapter 3, $\chi(\omega)=1/[m(\omega_0^2-\omega^2)-\ii\gamma\omega]$.

Pole positions. Solving the denominator $\omega_0^2-(\omega+\ii\eta)^2=0$, with $\eta$ retained, gives $\omega+\ii\eta=\pm\omega_0$, that is,

$$ \omega=\pm\omega_0-\ii\eta $$

Both poles lie a distance $\eta$ below the real axis. The upper half-plane contains no poles and $\chi(\omega)$ is analytic there—this is the expression of causality (Section 6.8.2, Figure 6.4, and Chapter 4). In the limit $\eta\to0^+$ the poles reach $\pm\omega_0$ on the real axis, but they always approach “from below.” Viewed as the limit in which the lifetime of the excitation is infinite (spectral width zero), the undamped oscillator is a system whose poles sit right at the edge of the real axis.

Exercise 6.6: The adiabatic factor and convergence

Solution

(1) Explain why the lower limit of $\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}\ee^{\ii(\omega_{n0}-\omega)t'}\dd t'$ is not determined without the adiabatic factor.

We abbreviate $\alpha\equiv\omega_{n0}-\omega$. For $\alpha\ne0$ the antiderivative is $\ee^{\ii\alpha t'}/(\ii\alpha)$, so to determine the integral we need the limit at the lower limit,

$$ \lim_{t'\to-\infty}\ee^{\ii\alpha t'}=\lim_{t'\to-\infty}\left(\cos\alpha t'+\ii\sin\alpha t'\right) $$

However, $\abs{\ee^{\ii\alpha t'}}=1$ stays fixed while both $\cos$ and $\sin$ keep oscillating forever, so this limit does not exist. The value does not diverge; it keeps circling the unit circle and never settles anywhere. Hence the value as an improper integral is not determined. Note also that in the case $\alpha=0$ (exactly at resonance) the integrand is identically $1$, and $\int_{-\infty}^{t}\dd t'=\infty$ now truly diverges. Either way, without the adiabatic factor the expression has no meaning.

(2) Show, by estimating the absolute value of the integrand, that the integral converges when the adiabatic factor $\ee^{\eta t'}$ is inserted.

Since the absolute value of the phase factor is $1$,

$$ \abs{\ee^{\ii\alpha t'}\,\ee^{\eta t'}}=\ee^{\eta t'} $$

and this falls exponentially to zero as $t'\to-\infty$. The integral of the absolute value is

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{t}\abs{\ee^{\ii\alpha t'}\ee^{\eta t'}}\,\dd t' =\int_{-\infty}^{t}\ee^{\eta t'}\,\dd t' =\frac{\ee^{\eta t}}{\eta}\ <\ \infty $$

which is finite for any small $\eta>0$. That is, the integral converges absolutely. Writing out the value explicitly, the lower limit of the antiderivative vanishes thanks to $\ee^{\eta t'}\to0$, giving

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{t}\ee^{\ii\alpha t'}\ee^{\eta t'}\dd t' =\left[\frac{\ee^{(\ii\alpha+\eta)t'}}{\ii\alpha+\eta}\right]_{-\infty}^{t} =\frac{\ee^{\ii\alpha t}\,\ee^{\eta t}}{\ii(\alpha-\ii\eta)} $$

This is exactly the integral used in the derivation of Section 6.8.2. The combination $\alpha-\ii\eta=\omega_{n0}-\omega-\ii\eta$ appears in the denominator, and this is the origin of the combination $\omega+\ii\eta$.

(3) Verify that $c_n(0)=\dfrac{B_{n0}}{\hbar(\omega+\ii\eta-\omega_{n0})}$ remains finite even as $\omega\to\omega_{n0}$, and express the maximum value of $\abs{c_n}^2$ in terms of $\eta$.

We take the squared absolute value. Since the denominator is a complex number, it becomes the sum of the squares of its real and imaginary parts:

$$ \abs{c_n(0)}^2=\frac{\abs{B_{n0}}^2}{\hbar^2\abs{\omega-\omega_{n0}+\ii\eta}^2} =\frac{\abs{B_{n0}}^2}{\hbar^2\left[(\omega-\omega_{n0})^2+\eta^2\right]} $$

As a function of $\omega$, this is a Lorentzian function with center $\omega_{n0}$ and half width at half maximum $\eta$. Even as $\omega\to\omega_{n0}$, the denominator stops at $\hbar^2\eta^2\ne0$ and does not diverge—$\eta$ keeps the “core of the resonance” finite. The maximum is attained exactly at resonance, $\omega=\omega_{n0}$, and is

$$ \abs{c_n}^2_{\max}=\frac{\abs{B_{n0}}^2}{(\hbar\eta)^2} $$

in agreement with the value given in the hint. The reason this maximum diverges as $\eta\to0^+$ is that an external field at the resonance frequency effectively acts for a long time $\sim1/\eta$ and keeps exciting the system resonantly throughout that time. Note also that first-order perturbation theory can be trusted only as long as $\abs{c_n}\ll1$, so at the very center of the resonance we need $\abs{B_{n0}}\ll\hbar\eta$—this contains the caution that the limit $\eta\to0^+$ at the resonance point must be taken while simultaneously making the amplitude of the perturbation small.

(4) Apply the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula to (3), and explain that the imaginary part gives a real excitation accompanied by the delta function, that is, by energy conservation.

Setting $x=\omega-\omega_{n0}$ and using the formula, we obtain

$$ \lim_{\eta\to0^+}c_n(0) =\frac{B_{n0}}{\hbar}\lim_{\eta\to0^+}\frac{1}{(\omega-\omega_{n0})+\ii\eta} =\frac{B_{n0}}{\hbar}\left[\PV\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{n0}}-\ii\pi\,\delta(\omega-\omega_{n0})\right] $$

The right-hand side is a distribution and acquires meaning only upon integration over $\omega$ (the cases in which the external field has a finite bandwidth, or in which the final states $n$ form a continuous spectrum, are exactly such situations). The roles of the two terms are clearly distinct.

  • Real part (principal-value term): This is the contribution from the nonresonant components with $\omega\ne\omega_{n0}$. The state $\ket{n}$ is merely mixed in virtually, without satisfying energy conservation $\hbar\omega=E_n-E_0$, and there is no net absorption. In terms of the response function, it corresponds to $\mathrm{Re}\,\chi$ (dispersion, elastic polarization).
  • Imaginary part (delta term): This stands only at the point where $\hbar\omega=E_n-E_0$ is satisfied exactly. This is a real excitation (real transition) accompanied by energy conservation, and there is a net flow of energy from the external field into the system. In terms of the response function, this is $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi$ (absorption).

That the delta function gives a “transition” can be seen even more directly by looking at the growth rate of the excited fraction. Since $c_n(t)$ of Section 6.8.2 satisfies $\abs{c_n(t)}^2=\abs{c_n(0)}^2\,\ee^{2\eta t}$, the rate of increase at $t=0$ is

$$ w_{0\to n}\equiv\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\abs{c_n(t)}^2\bigg|_{t=0} =2\eta\,\abs{c_n(0)}^2 =\frac{\abs{B_{n0}}^2}{\hbar^2}\cdot\frac{2\eta}{(\omega-\omega_{n0})^2+\eta^2} $$

The Lorentzian function that appears here is exactly the content of the imaginary part of the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula ($\mathrm{Im}\,\dfrac{1}{x+\ii\eta}=-\dfrac{\eta}{x^2+\eta^2}$), and as $\eta\to0^+$ we have $\dfrac{\eta}{x^2+\eta^2}\to\pi\delta(x)$. Therefore

$$ w_{0\to n}\ \xrightarrow{\ \eta\to0^+\ }\ \frac{2\pi}{\hbar^2}\abs{B_{n0}}^2\,\delta(\omega-\omega_{n0}) =\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\abs{B_{n0}}^2\,\delta\!\left(E_n-E_0-\hbar\omega\right) $$

(the last step used $\delta(\omega-\omega_{n0})=\hbar\,\delta(\hbar\omega-\hbar\omega_{n0})$). This is nothing other than Fermi's golden rule. Transitions to final states that satisfy energy conservation occur at a constant rate per unit time—as the hint says, this imaginary part corresponds to $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi$ (absorption) of Chapter 3, and it reappears as the spectral function in Chapter 8.

Physical Meaning: What $\eta$ leaves behind at the end

If we connect the course of this exercise into a single thread, the role of the adiabatic factor becomes clear. (1) Without $\eta$, the time integral itself cannot even be defined. (2) With $\eta$ inserted, it converges absolutely, and (3) even the resonance is rounded off to a finite value. (4) Finally, when we let $\eta\to0^+$, $\eta$ itself disappears, but it disappears only after engraving in the equations the decomposition into real and imaginary parts—the physical distinction between dispersion and absorption, between virtual and real transitions. To use the wording of Section 6.8.3, “what vanishes as $\eta\to0^+$ is a mathematical tool, and what remains is the physics,” and the principal value and the delta function are precisely the physics that $\eta$ leaves behind. In the Kubo formula of Chapter 7 and in the Lehmann representation and the spectral function of Chapter 8, this structure takes center stage just as it is.

Chapter 7: Derivation of the Kubo Formula

Exercise 7.1: Response function of a two-level system (spin $1/2$)

Solution

(1) Solve the Heisenberg equation under $\Ham_0=\frac{\hbar\omega_0}{2}\hat\sigma_z$ and show that $\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t)=\hat\sigma_x\cos\omega_0t-\hat\sigma_y\sin\omega_0t$.

The commutation relations of the Pauli matrices are

$$ \left[\hat\sigma_x,\hat\sigma_y\right]=2\ii\hat\sigma_z,\qquad \left[\hat\sigma_y,\hat\sigma_z\right]=2\ii\hat\sigma_x,\qquad \left[\hat\sigma_z,\hat\sigma_x\right]=2\ii\hat\sigma_y $$

The Heisenberg picture $\hat\sigma_{i,H}(t)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\hat\sigma_i\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}$ is the original operator sandwiched from both sides by the same unitary, so the commutation relations at equal times hold in exactly the same form as the original ones. For example, $[\hat\sigma_{z,H}(t),\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t)]=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}[\hat\sigma_z,\hat\sigma_x]\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}=2\ii\hat\sigma_{y,H}(t)$. Also, since $\Ham_0$ commutes with $\ee^{\pm\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}$, we may write $[\Ham_0,\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t)]=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}[\Ham_0,\hat\sigma_x]\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}$. Therefore the Heisenberg equation reads

$$ \frac{\dd\hat\sigma_{x,H}}{\dd t} =\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\left[\Ham_0,\hat\sigma_{x,H}\right] =\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\cdot\frac{\hbar\omega_0}{2}\left[\hat\sigma_{z,H},\hat\sigma_{x,H}\right] =\frac{\ii\omega_0}{2}\cdot2\ii\,\hat\sigma_{y,H} =-\omega_0\,\hat\sigma_{y,H} $$

Similarly, for $\hat\sigma_y$ we have

$$ \frac{\dd\hat\sigma_{y,H}}{\dd t} =\frac{\ii\omega_0}{2}\left[\hat\sigma_{z,H},\hat\sigma_{y,H}\right] =\frac{\ii\omega_0}{2}\cdot\left(-2\ii\,\hat\sigma_{x,H}\right) =+\omega_0\,\hat\sigma_{x,H} $$

(here we used $[\hat\sigma_z,\hat\sigma_y]=-[\hat\sigma_y,\hat\sigma_z]=-2\ii\hat\sigma_x$). Combining the two equations gives

$$ \frac{\dd^2\hat\sigma_{x,H}}{\dd t^2} =-\omega_0\frac{\dd\hat\sigma_{y,H}}{\dd t} =-\omega_0^2\,\hat\sigma_{x,H} $$

which is the equation of harmonic oscillation. We write the general solution, with operator coefficients, as $\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t)=\hat C_1\cos\omega_0t+\hat C_2\sin\omega_0t$ and impose the initial conditions. From $\hat\sigma_{x,H}(0)=\hat\sigma_x$ we get $\hat C_1=\hat\sigma_x$, and from $\dot{\hat\sigma}_{x,H}(0)=-\omega_0\hat\sigma_y$ we get $\omega_0\hat C_2=-\omega_0\hat\sigma_y$, that is, $\hat C_2=-\hat\sigma_y$. Therefore

$$ \hat\sigma_{x,H}(t)=\hat\sigma_x\cos\omega_0t-\hat\sigma_y\sin\omega_0t $$

holds. Check. The same equation gives $\hat\sigma_{y,H}(t)=\hat\sigma_y\cos\omega_0t+\hat\sigma_x\sin\omega_0t$, and differentiating the solution above gives $\dot{\hat\sigma}_{x,H}=-\omega_0(\hat\sigma_x\sin\omega_0t+\hat\sigma_y\cos\omega_0t)=-\omega_0\hat\sigma_{y,H}$, which indeed satisfies the first-order equation. Physically this is Larmor precession on the Bloch sphere—under a magnetic field along the $z$ axis, the transverse spin component rotates with angular velocity $\omega_0$.

(2) Compute the commutator $[\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t),\hat\sigma_x(0)]$ and see that it is not a c-number.

We expand it using the bilinearity of the commutator. Since $\cos\omega_0t,\sin\omega_0t$ are just numbers, they can be taken outside, and we get

$$ \left[\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t),\hat\sigma_x\right] =\cos\omega_0t\underbrace{\left[\hat\sigma_x,\hat\sigma_x\right]}_{=0} -\sin\omega_0t\left[\hat\sigma_y,\hat\sigma_x\right] $$

Since $[\hat\sigma_y,\hat\sigma_x]=-[\hat\sigma_x,\hat\sigma_y]=-2\ii\hat\sigma_z$, we find

$$ \left[\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t),\hat\sigma_x\right]=2\ii\sin(\omega_0t)\,\hat\sigma_z $$

The operator $\hat\sigma_z$ remains on the right-hand side. Unlike the harmonic oscillator ($[\hat x_H(t),\hat x]=-\frac{\ii\hbar}{m\omega_0}\sin\omega_0t$, a c-number), the expectation value of the commutator therefore depends on the state in which it is taken. The commutator of two Hermitian operators is anti-Hermitian, so it is correct that it has the form of a purely imaginary coefficient ($2\ii\sin\omega_0t$) times a Hermitian operator ($\hat\sigma_z$).

(3) Take the expectation value in the ground state $\ket{\downarrow}$ and find $\chi^R_{\sigma_x\sigma_x}(t)$.

Since $\hat\sigma_z\ket{\downarrow}=-\ket{\downarrow}$, we have $\bra{\downarrow}\hat\sigma_z\ket{\downarrow}=-1$, so

$$ \braket{\left[\hat\sigma_{x,H}(t),\hat\sigma_x\right]}=2\ii\sin(\omega_0t)\cdot(-1)=-2\ii\sin\omega_0t $$

We insert this into the Kubo formula $\chi^R_{AB}(t)=-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\theta(t)\braket{[\hat A_H(t),\hat B(0)]}$. The coefficient is $\left(-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\right)\times(-2\ii)=\frac{2\ii^2}{\hbar}=-\frac{2}{\hbar}$, so

$$ \chi^R_{\sigma_x\sigma_x}(t)=-\frac{2}{\hbar}\sin(\omega_0t)\,\theta(t) $$

This is indeed a real function, consistent with the general theory in the main text ($\chi^R(t)$ is real when both operators are Hermitian).

(4) Fourier-transform to find $\chi^R_{\sigma_x\sigma_x}(\omega)$, and check the sign of the static susceptibility.

We abbreviate $z\equiv\omega+\ii\eta$ ($\mathrm{Im}\,z=\eta>0$) and use the basic integral from the main text, $\int_0^\infty\ee^{\ii Xt}\dd t=\ii/X$ ($\mathrm{Im}\,X>0$). Expanding $\sin\omega_0t=\frac{\ee^{\ii\omega_0t}-\ee^{-\ii\omega_0t}}{2\ii}$, we obtain

$$ \int_0^\infty\sin(\omega_0t)\,\ee^{\ii zt}\dd t =\frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\frac{\ii}{z+\omega_0}-\frac{\ii}{z-\omega_0}\right] =\frac12\cdot\frac{(z-\omega_0)-(z+\omega_0)}{z^2-\omega_0^2} =\frac{\omega_0}{\omega_0^2-z^2} $$

Therefore

$$ \chi^R_{\sigma_x\sigma_x}(\omega) =-\frac{2}{\hbar}\cdot\frac{\omega_0}{\omega_0^2-z^2} =-\frac{2\omega_0}{\hbar\left[\omega_0^2-(\omega+\ii\eta)^2\right]} $$

which agrees with the given expression. The poles are at $\omega=\pm\omega_0-\ii\eta$, that is, in the lower half-plane, consistent with causality. The static susceptibility is

$$ \chi^R_{\sigma_x\sigma_x}(0)=-\frac{2\omega_0}{\hbar\omega_0^2}=-\frac{2}{\hbar\omega_0}\;<\;0 $$

and is negative. In the convention of this book ($\Ham_{\text{ext}}=+f\hat B$), $f$ is a potential-like quantity, so the negative static response for $\hat A=\hat B$ follows the same logic as $\chi^R_{xx}(0)=-1/m\omega_0^2$ for the harmonic oscillator. Converting to the usual susceptibility to a force or a magnetic field (the convention of $\Ham'=-h\hat\sigma_x$), we get $+2/(\hbar\omega_0)>0$, which is the natural answer: applying a transverse magnetic field tilts the spin toward the direction of the field.

Raising to finite temperature gives the Curie law

Following the hint, we check at finite temperature what it means that $\hat\sigma_z$ remained in the commutator in (2). In thermal equilibrium ($Z=2\cosh(\beta\hbar\omega_0/2)$, levels $\mp\hbar\omega_0/2$),

$$ \braket{\hat\sigma_z}_T =\frac{\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega_0/2}\cdot(+1)+\ee^{+\beta\hbar\omega_0/2}\cdot(-1)}{2\cosh(\beta\hbar\omega_0/2)} =-\tanh\!\left(\frac{\beta\hbar\omega_0}{2}\right) $$

so the calculations of (3) and (4) go through as they are, and we obtain

$$ \chi^R_{\sigma_x\sigma_x}(\omega;T) =-\frac{2\omega_0\tanh(\beta\hbar\omega_0/2)}{\hbar\left[\omega_0^2-(\omega+\ii\eta)^2\right]}, \qquad \chi^R_{\sigma_x\sigma_x}(0;T)=-\frac{2}{\hbar\omega_0}\tanh\!\left(\frac{\beta\hbar\omega_0}{2}\right) $$

At high temperature, $k_BT\gg\hbar\omega_0$, we have $\tanh x\simeq x$, so $\chi^R(0;T)\simeq-\beta=-1/k_BT$; that is, $\abs{\chi}\propto1/T$, the Curie law. At $T=0$, $\tanh\to1$ and we return to the result of (4). Whereas the response function of the harmonic oscillator was independent of temperature (because the commutator is a c-number), in the two-level system the response function itself depends on temperature. The functional form is the same, but the coefficient is determined by the state—this is the physical consequence of “the commutator is not a c-number.”

Exercise 7.2: Response function of a free particle

Solution

(1) Show that $\hat x_H(t)=\hat x+\frac{\hat p}{m}t$ under $\Ham_0=\hat p^2/2m$.

First we construct the commutator we need. From $[\hat x,\hat p]=\ii\hbar$,

$$ \left[\hat p^2,\hat x\right] =\hat p\left[\hat p,\hat x\right]+\left[\hat p,\hat x\right]\hat p =\hat p(-\ii\hbar)+(-\ii\hbar)\hat p =-2\ii\hbar\,\hat p $$

The Heisenberg equations are (by the same reasoning as in Exercise 7.1(1), namely that equal-time commutation relations have the same form as the original ones)

$$ \frac{\dd\hat x_H}{\dd t} =\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\left[\frac{\hat p_H^2}{2m},\hat x_H\right] =\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\cdot\frac{-2\ii\hbar\,\hat p_H}{2m} =\frac{\hat p_H}{m}, \qquad \frac{\dd\hat p_H}{\dd t} =\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\left[\Ham_0,\hat p_H\right]=0 $$

The second equation gives $\hat p_H(t)=\hat p$ (the momentum is a conserved quantity). Inserting this into the first equation, integrating, and imposing the initial condition $\hat x_H(0)=\hat x$, we obtain

$$ \hat x_H(t)=\hat x+\frac{\hat p}{m}\,t $$

This is literally identical in form to classical uniform linear motion.

(2) Compute the commutator $[\hat x_H(t),\hat x(0)]$ and confirm that it is a c-number.

$$ \left[\hat x_H(t),\hat x\right] =\underbrace{\left[\hat x,\hat x\right]}_{=0}+\frac tm\left[\hat p,\hat x\right] =\frac tm\cdot(-\ii\hbar) =-\frac{\ii\hbar t}{m} $$

The right-hand side contains no operator; it is a c-number. The reason is clear: $\hat x_H(t)$ is a linear combination of $\hat x,\hat p$, and the commutator of linear combinations produces only the canonical commutation relation $[\hat x,\hat p]=\ii\hbar$ (a c-number). (This is a consequence of the Hamiltonian being at most quadratic; it is generalized in Exercise 7.5(2).) That the result is purely imaginary is also as the general theory says.

(3) Find $\chi^R_{xx}(t)$ and confirm that it agrees with the $\omega_0\to0$ limit of the harmonic oscillator.

Since the commutator is a c-number, the expectation value has the same value in any state, and inserting it into the Kubo formula gives

$$ \chi^R_{xx}(t) =-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\,\theta(t)\cdot\left(-\frac{\ii\hbar t}{m}\right) =\frac{\ii^2\,t}{m}\,\theta(t) =-\frac{t}{m}\,\theta(t) $$

(the $\hbar$ canceled and disappeared—the response function is a classical quantity). On the other hand, take the response function of the harmonic oscillator derived in the main text, $\chi^R_{xx}(t)=-\frac{\sin\omega_0t}{m\omega_0}\theta(t)$, and let $\omega_0\to0$. For fixed $t$ we have $\frac{\sin\omega_0t}{\omega_0}\to t$, so it becomes $-\frac tm\theta(t)$, in complete agreement. Letting the spring constant $m\omega_0^2\to0$ gives the free particle—a natural correspondence.

(4) Fourier-transform to show that $\chi^R_{xx}(\omega)=\frac{1}{m(\omega+\ii\eta)^2}$, and state the position of the pole and the meaning of the divergence as $\omega\to0$.

With $z=\omega+\ii\eta$, we evaluate the required integral by integration by parts:

$$ \int_0^\infty t\,\ee^{\ii zt}\dd t =\left[t\,\frac{\ee^{\ii zt}}{\ii z}\right]_0^\infty-\frac{1}{\ii z}\int_0^\infty\ee^{\ii zt}\dd t =0-\frac{1}{\ii z}\cdot\frac{\ii}{z} =-\frac{1}{z^2} $$

The boundary term vanishes because $\abs{t\,\ee^{\ii zt}}=t\,\ee^{-\eta t}\to0$ ($t\to\infty$)—a polynomial always loses to exponential decay. Here too, $\eta>0$ rescues the integral. Therefore

$$ \chi^R_{xx}(\omega) =-\frac1m\int_0^\infty t\,\ee^{\ii zt}\dd t =-\frac1m\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{z^2}\right) =\frac{1}{m(\omega+\ii\eta)^2} $$

As a check, if we let $\omega_0\to0$ in the harmonic-oscillator result $\chi^R_{xx}(\omega)=-\frac{1}{m[\omega_0^2-(\omega+\ii\eta)^2]}$, we get $+\frac{1}{m(\omega+\ii\eta)^2}$, which agrees.

Pole positions. The zero of the denominator is at $\omega=-\ii\eta$, where there is a pole of order 2 (a double pole). For the harmonic oscillator the two simple poles lay at $\pm\omega_0-\ii\eta$; this can be read as the two merging just below the origin as $\omega_0\to0$ and becoming a pole of order 2. The pole is in the lower half-plane, and there is no singularity in the upper half-plane—this is causality.

Physics of the divergence as $\omega\to0$. A free particle has no restoring force, so if a constant external field keeps being applied, the displacement does not remain finite. In fact, this can be confirmed in the time domain. If a constant external field $f(t')=f_0\,\theta(t')$ (in terms of force, $F=-f_0$) is applied from $t=0$, we get

$$ \delta\braket{\hat x(t)} =\int_0^t\dd t'\left(-\frac{t-t'}{m}\right)f_0 =-\frac{f_0}{m}\int_0^t s\,\dd s =-\frac{f_0\,t^2}{2m} =\frac{F\,t^2}{2m} $$

This is uniformly accelerated motion exactly as in Newton's second law, and it goes off to infinity as $t\to\infty$. Since there is no stationary displacement however weak the external field is, the static susceptibility cannot be defined (it diverges). Note also that it is meaningful that the divergence is $1/\omega^2$ (a pole of order 2) rather than $1/\omega$. In general, a simple pole at $\omega=0$ corresponds to “the response to a constant external field keeps growing in proportion to $t$,” and a pole of order 2 to “it keeps growing as $t^2$.” The present $1/\omega^2$ is precisely the expression of uniformly accelerated motion. Indeed, since the current (velocity) is the time derivative of the displacement, for the same free particle the conductivity, which is the response of the current, has one fewer power of $\omega$: it has a simple pole, $\sigma\propto\ii/\omega$ (Section 7.8.3). Under a constant electric field the current grows as $t$ and the displacement as $t^2$—this is a restatement of the same physics.

Exercise 7.3: Response to a step-like external field

Solution

(1) We compute by inserting $f(t')=f_0\theta(t')$ and the harmonic-oscillator response function $\chi^R_{xx}(t)=-\frac{\sin\omega_0t}{m\omega_0}\theta(t)$ into the convolution $\delta\braket{\hat x(t)}=\int\dd t'\,\chi^R_{xx}(t-t')f(t')$.

Since the external field starts at $t'=0$, there is no worry about convergence on the $-\infty$ side, and we may set $\ee^{\eta t'}\to1$. Because $\theta(t')$ restricts the lower limit of the integral to $0$ and $\theta(t-t')$ inside $\chi^R$ restricts the upper limit to $t$, for $t>0$ we have

$$ \delta\braket{\hat x(t)} =\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd t'\,\chi^R_{xx}(t-t')\,f_0\,\theta(t') =-\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0}\int_0^t\dd t'\,\sin\omega_0(t-t') $$

As the hint says, we substitute $s=t-t'$. Since $\dd t'=-\dd s$, and $t'=0\Rightarrow s=t$, $t'=t\Rightarrow s=0$, the minus sign is used up in swapping the upper and lower limits of the integral, and we obtain

$$ \delta\braket{\hat x(t)} =-\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0}\int_0^t\sin(\omega_0 s)\,\dd s =-\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0}\left[\frac{-\cos\omega_0s}{\omega_0}\right]_0^t =-\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0^2}\left(1-\cos\omega_0t\right) \qquad(t>0) $$

which proves the given expression. For $t<0$ it vanishes (causality—nothing happens before the external field is applied). We can also check that at $t=0$ the response rises from zero with both its value and its derivative continuous: $\delta\braket{x}\simeq-\frac{f_0}{2m}t^2$ (for small $t$), that is, the particle starts to accelerate from zero initial velocity.

(2) A physical explanation of this motion, and the location of the minimum of the potential including the perturbation.

In the convention of this book the perturbation is $\Ham_{\text{ext}}=f_0\hat x$, that is, adding $+f_0x$ to the potential, so, completing the square, the potential for $t>0$ is

$$ U(x)=\frac12m\omega_0^2x^2+f_0x =\frac12m\omega_0^2\left(x+\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0^2}\right)^2-\frac{f_0^2}{2m\omega_0^2} $$

The location of the minimum is

$$ x_{\min}=-\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0^2} $$

The spring constant is unchanged, and only the equilibrium position shifts to $x_{\min}$. The particle was at rest at the old equilibrium point $x=0$ until $t=0$, but at $t=0$ the bottom of the potential suddenly shifts to $x_{\min}$, so it is as if the particle had been “placed at rest at a distance $\abs{x_{\min}}$ from the new equilibrium position.” Therefore simple harmonic motion about the new equilibrium point

$$ \delta\braket{\hat x(t)}=x_{\min}\left(1-\cos\omega_0t\right) $$

takes place. The displacement oscillates between $0$ and $2x_{\min}$; the center of the oscillation is $x_{\min}$ and the amplitude is $\abs{x_{\min}}=f_0/(m\omega_0^2)$. The expression in (1) has exactly this form.

(3) We take the time average and check the relation to the static susceptibility.

Averaging over one period $T=2\pi/\omega_0$ gives $\overline{\cos\omega_0t}=0$, so

$$ \overline{\delta\braket{\hat x}} =-\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0^2} $$

On the other hand, the static susceptibility obtained in the main text is $\chi^R_{xx}(0)=-\dfrac{1}{m\omega_0^2}$, so

$$ \chi^R_{xx}(0)\,f_0=-\frac{f_0}{m\omega_0^2}=\overline{\delta\braket{\hat x}} $$

and the two indeed agree. The center of the oscillation is the static response. Under sudden application the system keeps oscillating about the new equilibrium value, but its average sits at the same place as “the answer when the field is applied infinitely slowly.”

(4) Why the oscillation continues forever, and the behavior in real systems.

It is because this system has no damping term. $\Ham_0$ does not depend on time, and the energy is conserved. At the instant of the sudden switch, the particle's energy exceeds the bottom of the new potential by the amount

$$ \Delta E=U(0)-U(x_{\min})=\frac{f_0^2}{2m\omega_0^2} $$

and because an isolated system has nowhere to dump this excess energy, it remains forever as oscillation (Section 7.7 of the main text, “An isolated quantum system cannot shed energy”). In a real system there is a finite damping $\gamma$ due to coupling to the environment, the transient oscillation decays as $\ee^{-\gamma t/(2m)}$, and the system settles at the new equilibrium position $x_{\min}$—this is exactly the transient response of the damped oscillator solved in Chapter 2.

And here lies the reason for inserting the adiabatic factor $\ee^{\eta t}$. The step function is the extreme case of “sudden application” and excites the transient oscillation to the maximum. Conversely, if we turn the field on infinitely slowly with $\ee^{\eta t}$, no transient is excited, and the system quietly follows the equilibrium value $\chi^R_{xx}(0)f(t)$ of each instant. Even for the same “external field that finally becomes constant,” the fate of the transient is completely different depending on how it is applied—this is the quantum version of the argument in Chapter 3 on forced oscillation, where we separated the transient solution from the steady-state solution.

Exercise 7.4: Converting between sign conventions

Solution

(1) We find the relation between the paper's $\chi^{\text{paper}}_m$ and this book's $\chi^R_{M_zM_z}$.

Writing the definition of this book in the Fourier representation gives

$$ \chi^R_{M_zM_z}(\omega) =-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\int_0^\infty\dd t\;\ee^{\ii(\omega+\ii\eta)t}\, \braket{\left[\hat M_{z,H}(t),\hat M_z(0)\right]} $$

The paper's definition has the prefactor $+\ii/\hbar$ and is otherwise identical (in the integrand, range of integration, and convergence factor), so

$$ \chi^{\text{paper}}_m(\omega)=-\,\chi^R_{M_zM_z}(\omega) $$

holds. Let us check the consistency. The paper's convention $\Ham'=-h\hat M_z$ corresponds, in the notation of this book $\Ham_{\text{ext}}=f\hat B$ ($\hat B=\hat M_z$), to $f=-h$. Hence from the response formula of this book

$$ \delta\braket{\hat M_z} =\chi^R_{M_zM_z}\cdot f =\chi^R_{M_zM_z}\cdot(-h) =\left(-\chi^R_{M_zM_z}\right)h =\chi^{\text{paper}}_m\,h $$

so the paper's $\chi^{\text{paper}}_m$ is exactly the susceptibility that multiplies the magnetic field $h$ directly. Because the sign of the coupling and the sign of the response function are flipped as a set, the physical quantity $\delta\braket{M_z}$ is the same in either convention. This is precisely the $\chi^K$ column of Table 7.2 in the main text (the $\Ham'=-f\hat B$ convention).

(2) For a paramagnet, what is the sign of this book's $\chi^R_{M_zM_z}(0)$?

Paramagnetism means that when $h>0$ is applied, $\delta\braket{M_z}>0$ (the system magnetizes in the direction of the field). For this to be positive in the relation $\delta\braket{M_z}=\chi^R_{M_zM_z}\cdot(-h)$ of (1), we need

$$ \chi^R_{M_zM_z}(0)\;<\;0 $$

that is, it must be negative. At first sight this looks odd, but the reasoning is exactly the same as for the harmonic oscillator's $\chi^R_{xx}(0)=-1/m\omega_0^2<0$. The $f=-h$ of this book's convention is a “potential-like quantity,” and applying $f>0$ raises the energy of states with large $M_z$, so the system responds by decreasing $M_z$—raise the potential and the system runs away from it. The sign is negative not because the physics has changed but because we have changed how the input is measured.

(3) We translate “absorption is $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^{\text{paper}}_m>0$” into the notation of this book.

By (1), $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^{\text{paper}}_m=-\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^R_{M_zM_z}$, so the same statement

$$ \mathrm{Im}\,\chi^R_{M_zM_z}(\omega)\;<\;0 \qquad\text{that is}\qquad -\chi''(\omega)\;>\;0 \qquad(\omega>0) $$

should be written in this form (with $\chi''\equiv\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^R$). This is exactly the same content as the formula for the absorbed power shown in Derivation 7.11 of the main text—the one-period-averaged power is $-\frac12f_0^2\,\omega\,\chi''(\omega)$, and for absorption this must be positive, that is, $\omega\chi''<0$ is required. In the convention of this book, the quantity representing dissipation is $-\chi''$.

(4) We rewrite the dielectric function formula in terms of the $\chi^0$ of the “paper's convention.”

Substituting $\chi^{0,\text{paper}}=-\chi^0$ into $\epsilon=1-v(q)\chi^0$, we obtain

$$ \epsilon(\qq,\omega)=1+v(q)\,\chi^{0,\text{paper}}(\qq,\omega) $$

As a check, let us look at the static long-wavelength limit. In the convention of this book $\chi^0(\qq\to0,0)=-N(\epsilon_F)<0$, so $\epsilon=1+v(q)N(\epsilon_F)>1$, which is correctly screening. In the paper's convention $\chi^{0,\text{paper}}=+N(\epsilon_F)>0$, and inserting it into the rewritten formula again gives $\epsilon=1+v(q)N(\epsilon_F)$, the same answer. If the conversion is done correctly, the physics does not change.

Common Mistake: “Copying” a formula without checking the convention turns screening into anti-screening

In Exercise 7.4(4), if one neglects to check the convention and plugs the paper's $\chi^{0,\text{paper}}(=+N(\epsilon_F)>0)$ directly into this book's formula $\epsilon=1-v\chi^0$, one obtains $\epsilon=1-v(q)N(\epsilon_F)$, so $\epsilon<1$, and at long wavelengths it even becomes negative. A dielectric function smaller than $1$ means that the field produced by the external charge is not weakened but strengthened (anti-screening), an answer that flatly contradicts the electrostatic screening in metals (Chapter 9). The error is a single sign, yet the physics that comes out is the exact opposite. Before using a formula from the literature, check “how the external field is written in the Hamiltonian ($+f\hat B$ or $-f\hat B$)”—the practice of Section 7.6.5 of the main text exists for this reason.

Exercise 7.5: Verifying the classical limit yourself

Solution

(1) Compute $\{x(t),x(0)\}_{\text{PB}}$ for a free particle, and confirm the correspondence $\frac{1}{\ii\hbar}[\ ,\ ]\to\{\ ,\ \}_{\text{PB}}$ with the commutator of Exercise 7.2(2).

The classical free-particle solution, with the canonical variables $(x,p)$ at $t=0$ as initial conditions, is $x(t)=x+\frac{p}{m}t$. Put $F=x(t)$ and $G=x(0)=x$ into the definition of the Poisson bracket, $\{F,G\}_{\text{PB}}=\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}\frac{\partial G}{\partial p}-\frac{\partial F}{\partial p}\frac{\partial G}{\partial x}$. The partial derivatives we need are

$$ \frac{\partial x(t)}{\partial x}=1,\qquad \frac{\partial x(t)}{\partial p}=\frac tm,\qquad \frac{\partial x}{\partial x}=1,\qquad \frac{\partial x}{\partial p}=0 $$

so that

$$ \{x(t),x(0)\}_{\text{PB}} =1\cdot0-\frac tm\cdot1 =-\frac tm $$

On the other hand, the commutator of Exercise 7.2(2) was $[\hat x_H(t),\hat x]=-\ii\hbar t/m$. Dividing it by $\ii\hbar$ gives

$$ \frac{1}{\ii\hbar}\left[\hat x_H(t),\hat x\right] =\frac{-\ii\hbar t/m}{\ii\hbar} =-\frac tm =\{x(t),x(0)\}_{\text{PB}} $$

so the correspondence holds. Moreover, it is an exact equality without even taking the limit $\hbar\to0$—a privilege of linear systems (Hamiltonians that are at most quadratic), and the same thing happened for the harmonic oscillator. Inserting it into the classical version of the Kubo formula, $\chi^{R,\text{cl}}_{AB}(t)=\theta(t)\braket{\{A(t),B(0)\}_{\text{PB}}}$, gives $\chi^{R,\text{cl}}_{xx}(t)=-\frac tm\theta(t)$, which agrees exactly with the quantum result of Exercise 7.2(3).

(2) When is the commutator a c-number, and what property do the harmonic oscillator and the free particle share?

The key is whether the Heisenberg operator remains a linear combination of the canonical operators. In general, if

$$ \hat A_H(t)=a(t)\,\hat x+b(t)\,\hat p+c(t), \qquad \hat B=a'\hat x+b'\hat p+c' $$

(all the coefficients being c-numbers), then expanding the commutator by bilinearity and dropping $[\hat x,\hat x]=[\hat p,\hat p]=0$ gives

$$ \left[\hat A_H(t),\hat B\right] =\left(a(t)b'-b(t)a'\right)\left[\hat x,\hat p\right] =\ii\hbar\left(a(t)b'-b(t)a'\right) $$

which is always a c-number. So when does $\hat x_H(t)$ remain a linear combination? It does so when the Heisenberg equations are linear in $\hat x,\hat p$, that is, when the Hamiltonian is at most quadratic in $\hat x,\hat p$. The harmonic oscillator ($\hat p^2/2m+\frac12m\omega_0^2\hat x^2$) and the free particle ($\hat p^2/2m$) are both quadratic forms, and this is the property they share. Indeed, $\hat x_H(t)=\hat x\cos\omega_0t+\frac{\hat p}{m\omega_0}\sin\omega_0t$ and $\hat x_H(t)=\hat x+\frac{\hat p}{m}t$ are both linear combinations. On the classical side, this corresponds to the fact that $\{x(t),x(0)\}$ does not depend on the initial conditions $(x,p)$ (this is also confirmed in the examples in the main text).

(3) For the anharmonic potential $U=\frac12m\omega_0^2x^2+\lambda x^4$, is $[\hat x_H(t),\hat x]$ a c-number?

No, it is not. The Heisenberg equation becomes nonlinear,

$$ \frac{\dd^2\hat x_H}{\dd t^2} =-\omega_0^2\,\hat x_H-\frac{4\lambda}{m}\,\hat x_H^3 $$

and $\hat x_H(t)$ cannot be written as a linear combination of $\hat x,\hat p$ (as time evolves, higher-order terms such as $\hat x^3$ and $\hat x^2\hat p$ keep being generated; in the short-time expansion $\hat x_H(t)=\hat x+\frac{\hat p}{m}t-\frac12\left(\omega_0^2\hat x+\frac{4\lambda}{m}\hat x^3\right)t^2+\cdots$ we see that $\hat x^3$ already appears in the $t^2$ term). Therefore an operator remains in $[\hat x_H(t),\hat x]$, and its expectation value depends on the state.

As a consequence, the response function depends on temperature, because the response changes with which states are thermally occupied. Physically, we may also say that in an anharmonic oscillator the level spacing is not uniform (the transition frequency differs from one pair of levels to another), so that when the temperature rises and higher levels become occupied, the position and shape of the resonance change. That the optical absorption lines of solids shift and broaden with temperature, and that thermal expansion occurs, are both manifestations of this anharmonicity. In the words of the summary in the main text, “that the response function has a temperature dependence is a feature of anharmonic systems,” and conversely the temperature independence of the response of the harmonic oscillator is an exceptional circumstance.

(4) Why $\hbar$ does not appear in the classical version of the Kubo formula, $\chi^{R,\text{cl}}_{AB}(t)=\theta(t)\braket{\{A(t),B(0)\}_{\text{PB}}}$, and what would go wrong if it did.

The reason is a cancellation of coefficients. Multiplying the prefactor $-\ii/\hbar$ of the Kubo formula by the $\ii\hbar$ of the correspondence principle $[\hat F,\hat G]\to\ii\hbar\{F,G\}_{\text{PB}}$, we obtain

$$ -\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\times\ii\hbar=-\ii^2=+1 $$

so that both $\hbar$ and $\ii$ disappear cleanly. In other words, the $-\ii/\hbar$ in the Kubo formula is there so that the classical limit remains exactly finite. In the calculation for the harmonic oscillator, the $\ii\hbar$ of the commutator was canceled against the prefactor, and the answer $\chi^R_{xx}(t)=-\frac{\sin\omega_0t}{m\omega_0}\theta(t)$ contained no $\hbar$; this was a concrete example of the same thing.

If $\hbar$ remained on the right-hand side (for example, if $\chi\propto\hbar$ or $\chi\propto1/\hbar$), the response would go to zero or to infinity as $\hbar\to0$. But response coefficients such as the softness of a spring, $1/(m\omega_0^2)$, the DC conductivity, and the dielectric constant are finite quantities that can be measured within classical physics, and their experimental values can be written down without knowing $\hbar$. A theory in which they do not remain finite in the classical limit would contradict these everyday measurements. “A response function is a measurable classical quantity, so it must be finite as $\hbar\to0$, and the $-\ii/\hbar$ is there for that purpose”—this is what the statement in the hint amounts to.

Exercise 7.6: Conductivity and the sum rule

Solution

(1) State the behavior of $\chi^R_{J^pJ^p}(\omega)$ that is required for $\sigma(\omega)\to\ii nq^2/(m\omega)$ as $\abs{\omega}\to\infty$.

The Kubo formula for the conductivity derived in the main text is

$$ \sigma_{xx}(\omega)=\frac{\ii}{\omega}\left[\frac{\chi^R_{J^pJ^p}(\omega)}{V}+\frac{nq^2}{m}\right] $$

For this to approach $\ii nq^2/(m\omega)$, it suffices that the paramagnetic term vanish inside the square brackets, that is,

$$ \frac{\chi^R_{J^pJ^p}(\omega)}{V}\;\xrightarrow[\ \abs{\omega}\to\infty\ ]{}\;0 $$

(as long as it tends to zero, only the diamagnetic term $nq^2/m$ remains). We can in fact check that this does happen. Integrating the Fourier transform by parts (with $z=\omega+\ii\eta$), we find

$$ \chi^R(\omega) =\int_0^\infty\chi^R(t)\,\ee^{\ii zt}\dd t =\left[\chi^R(t)\frac{\ee^{\ii zt}}{\ii z}\right]_0^\infty-\frac{1}{\ii z}\int_0^\infty\dot\chi^R(t)\,\ee^{\ii zt}\dd t =\frac{\ii\,\chi^R(0^+)}{z}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{z^2}\right) $$

so the coefficient of $1/\omega$ is determined by the value of the response function at $t\to0^+$. For the current–current response, however, this value is the equal-time commutator

$$ \chi^R_{J^pJ^p}(0^+) =-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}\braket{\left[\hat J^p_x,\hat J^p_x\right]}=0 $$

(the commutator of an operator with itself is identically zero), so the $1/\omega$ term vanishes and $\chi^R_{J^pJ^p}(\omega)=O(1/\omega^2)$ decays—just as the hint says. Physically, the oscillation is so fast that neither the binding force nor scattering can act within one period, and the electrons respond as mere inertia (mass $m$, density $n$). The diamagnetic term is precisely this term of “free acceleration.”

(2) Find the plasma frequency of copper.

Substituting $n=8.5\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$, $e=1.602\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{C}$, $m=9.109\times10^{-31}\ \mathrm{kg}$, and $\epsilon_0=8.854\times10^{-12}\ \mathrm{F/m}$, we obtain

$$ \omega_p=\sqrt{\frac{ne^2}{\epsilon_0 m}} =\sqrt{\frac{(8.5\times10^{28})(1.602\times10^{-19})^2}{(8.854\times10^{-12})(9.109\times10^{-31})}} =\sqrt{2.705\times10^{32}\ \mathrm{s^{-2}}} =1.645\times10^{16}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}} $$

Converted to energy, this is

$$ \hbar\omega_p=(1.055\times10^{-34})(1.645\times10^{16})=1.735\times10^{-18}\ \mathrm{J} \simeq10.8\ \mathrm{eV} $$

(in agreement with the value given in the hint). The wavelength of the corresponding light is $\lambda_p=2\pi c/\omega_p\simeq115\ \mathrm{nm}$, which lies in the vacuum ultraviolet.

(3) Evaluate the right-hand side of the $f$-sum rule for copper.

$$ \int_0^\infty\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma(\omega)\,\dd\omega =\frac{\pi ne^2}{2m} =\frac{\pi\,(8.5\times10^{28})(1.602\times10^{-19})^2}{2\times(9.109\times10^{-31})} =3.76\times10^{21}\ \mathrm{S\,m^{-1}\,s^{-1}} $$

Check. Using $\omega_p^2=ne^2/(\epsilon_0m)$, we have $\frac{\pi ne^2}{2m}=\frac{\pi}{2}\epsilon_0\omega_p^2=\frac{\pi}{2}(8.854\times10^{-12})(1.645\times10^{16})^2=3.76\times10^{21}$, which agrees. The right-hand side of the sum rule contains only $n$ and $m$: the claim of the $f$-sum rule is that the total area of the spectrum is determined by the number of electrons alone, independently of the details of the scattering (the value of $\tau$) and of the interactions. If we change the horizontal axis to $\hbar\omega$ (in eV), we can also say $\int\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma\,\dd(\hbar\omega)\simeq2.5\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{S\,m^{-1}\,eV}$.

(4) Give two reasons why the measured value comes to only $70\%$ of the value in (3).

Reason 1: Truncation of the range of integration. The left-hand side of the sum rule is an integral from $\omega=0$ to infinity, but the frequency range that can be measured experimentally is finite. In copper, the interband transitions from the $3d$ band to above the Fermi level (which set in at about $2\ \mathrm{eV}$ and are also the reason copper looks reddish gold) and the transitions from still deeper core levels carry large spectral weight on the high-energy side. If the measured range is cut off at, say, a few eV, the result is necessarily short by the weight that remains above the cutoff.

Reason 2: The choice of mass. We took $m$ on the right-hand side to be the free-electron mass, but the response of conduction electrons in a crystal is determined by the effective mass $m^*$ of the band. The saturation value of the partial sum rule, when the measured range is limited to the response within the conduction band (the Drude part), is $\pi ne^2/(2m^*)$, and if $m^*>m$ this is only $m/m^*$ times the value estimated with the free-electron mass. A value of $70\%$ corresponds to $m^*\simeq1.4\,m$ (the missing weight has moved to the interband transitions, and when we integrate over all frequencies the sum rule with the free-electron mass is recovered—the weight has not disappeared but has merely changed its place).

In addition, the Kramers–Kronig analysis used to obtain $\mathrm{Re}\,\sigma$ from the reflectance involves an extrapolation of the spectrum outside the measured range, so the extrapolation error is also a cause of lost weight. In any case, when a sum rule does not match, first suspect the range of integration and the effective mass—this is the lesson of the hint.

What $\hbar\omega_p\simeq10.8\ \mathrm{eV}$ means—metallic luster

Visible light spans $1.6$–$3.3\ \mathrm{eV}$ (wavelengths $750$–$380\ \mathrm{nm}$), far below $\hbar\omega_p\simeq10.8\ \mathrm{eV}$. For $\omega<\omega_p$ the dielectric function of a metal is negative and light cannot enter, so it is almost totally reflected—this is why metals, copper among them, shine brilliantly in visible light (show metallic luster). Conversely, for $\omega>\omega_p$ a metal becomes transparent, and in fact alkali metals transmit ultraviolet light. Note also that copper looks reddish rather than silvery because the interband transitions from the $3d$ band described in (4) absorb light above about $2\ \mathrm{eV}$ (the blue-green side); the plasma edge itself lies deep in the ultraviolet.

Chapter 8: General Properties of Response Functions

Exercise 8.1: Lehmann representation of a two-level system

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to write down $\chi^R_{AA}(\omega)$ of the two-level system from the Lehmann representation and combine the two terms into a single fraction by bringing them to a common denominator. Our starting point is the Lehmann representation derived in Chapter 8,

$$ \chi^R_{AB}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_n\left[ \frac{A_{0n}B_{n0}}{\omega-\omega_{n0}+\ii\eta} -\frac{B_{0n}A_{n0}}{\omega+\omega_{n0}+\ii\eta}\right] $$

into which we insert the setup of this problem. The only intermediate state that contributes is $\ket{1}$ (since $A_{00}=A_{11}=0$, the $n=0$ term does not appear). The excitation frequency follows from $\hbar\omega_{10}=\hbar\omega_0-0$ as $\omega_{10}=\omega_0$, and the matrix element is $A_{01}A_{10}=a\cdot a=a^2$. Hence

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{a^2}{\hbar}\left[\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta}-\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_0+\ii\eta}\right] $$

We bring the two terms to a common denominator. The denominator is

$$ (\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta)(\omega+\omega_0+\ii\eta) = (\omega+\ii\eta)^2-\omega_0^2 $$

and the numerator is

$$ a^2\left[(\omega+\omega_0+\ii\eta)-(\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta)\right] = 2a^2\omega_0 $$

so that

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{2a^2\omega_0/\hbar}{(\omega+\ii\eta)^2-\omega_0^2} $$

which agrees with the formula in the hint.

Check. The harmonic oscillator of Example 8.1 is the special case of this problem with $a^2=\hbar/2m\omega_0$. Substituting this gives

$$ \chi^R_{xx}(\omega) = \frac{2\omega_0}{\hbar}\cdot\frac{\hbar}{2m\omega_0}\cdot\frac{1}{(\omega+\ii\eta)^2-\omega_0^2} = \frac{1}{m\left[(\omega+\ii\eta)^2-\omega_0^2\right]} \;\xrightarrow{\;\eta\to0\;}\;\frac{1}{m(\omega^2-\omega_0^2)} $$

which correctly reproduces the result in the main text.

(2) This problem asks us to write down the dissipative part $\chi''_{AA}(\omega)$ and confirm that it is an odd function. Applying the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula $\dfrac{1}{x+\ii\eta}=\PV\dfrac1x-\ii\pi\delta(x)$ to each of the two fractions in (1) and collecting the delta-function parts, we obtain

$$ \chi''_{AA}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi a^2}{\hbar}\left[\delta(\omega-\omega_0)-\delta(\omega+\omega_0)\right] $$

This is the smallest version of a “comb of delta functions,” with one delta function standing at each of $\omega=\pm\omega_0$. Under $\omega\to-\omega$, since the delta function is an even function, $\delta(-\omega-\omega_0)=\delta(\omega+\omega_0)$ and $\delta(-\omega+\omega_0)=\delta(\omega-\omega_0)$, so the two terms simply swap places:

$$ \chi''_{AA}(-\omega) = -\frac{\pi a^2}{\hbar}\left[\delta(\omega+\omega_0)-\delta(\omega-\omega_0)\right] = -\chi''_{AA}(\omega) $$

It is indeed an odd function. Moreover, $\chi''\le0$ for $\omega>0$ and $\chi''\ge0$ for $\omega<0$, so the condition for energy dissipation in the sign convention of this book, $\omega\chi''_{AA}(\omega)\le0$, is satisfied as well.

(3) This problem asks us to actually carry out the integral on the left-hand side of the $f$-sum rule and show that it equals $2a^2\omega_0/\hbar$. We substitute $\chi''$ from (2) into the left-hand side of the $f$-sum rule derived in Chapter 8:

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega}{\pi}\,\omega\,\bigl(-\chi''_{AA}(\omega)\bigr) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega}{\pi}\,\omega\cdot\frac{\pi a^2}{\hbar}\left[\delta(\omega-\omega_0)-\delta(\omega+\omega_0)\right] $$

The factors of $\pi$ cancel, and the delta functions fix $\omega$ at $+\omega_0$ and $-\omega_0$, respectively:

$$ = \frac{a^2}{\hbar}\left[\omega_0-(-\omega_0)\right] = \frac{2a^2\omega_0}{\hbar} $$

Note that the contributions of the two delta functions add rather than subtract (since $\omega\chi''$ is an even function, both sides contribute with the same sign). It is indeed $2a^2\omega_0/\hbar$.

Check (right-hand side). The right-hand side of the $f$-sum rule was $\dfrac{1}{\hbar^2}\braket{[[\hat A,\Ham_0],\hat A]}$. Applying the identity $\braket{[[\hat A,\Ham_0],\hat A]}=2\sum_n\abs{A_{0n}}^2(E_n-E_0)$ shown in Chapter 8 to the present problem, the sum has only the $n=1$ term, and

$$ \braket{[[\hat A,\Ham_0],\hat A]} = 2a^2(\hbar\omega_0-0) = 2a^2\hbar\omega_0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{1}{\hbar^2}\cdot2a^2\hbar\omega_0 = \frac{2a^2\omega_0}{\hbar} $$

The left- and right-hand sides agree, confirming that the sum rule holds exactly for this system.

(4) This problem asks us to find the oscillator strength $f_{10}=\dfrac{2m\omega_0}{\hbar}a^2$ numerically and to judge its plausibility from the TRK sum rule. First we find the excitation frequency:

$$ \omega_0 = \frac{2\ \mathrm{eV}}{\hbar} = \frac{2\times1.602\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J}}{1.055\times10^{-34}\ \mathrm{J\,s}} = 3.04\times10^{15}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}} $$

Substituting this value together with $m=9.11\times10^{-31}\ \mathrm{kg}$ and $a=1\ \text{Å}=1.0\times10^{-10}\ \mathrm{m}$, we obtain

$$ f_{10} = \frac{2\times9.11\times10^{-31}\times3.04\times10^{15}\times(1.0\times10^{-10})^2}{1.055\times10^{-34}} = 0.52 $$

This agrees with the value in the hint. The TRK sum rule requires $\sum_n f_{n0}=1$ per electron, so $f_{10}=0.52\le1$ is a consistent and reasonable value. The remaining $0.48$ can be interpreted as carried by the higher excited states and the ionization continuum that this model does not include. In fact, it is comparable to the $1s\to2p$ transition of the hydrogen atom ($f=0.4162$) seen in Section 8.7.2 of the main text, and is a natural magnitude for a strong allowed transition in the visible region.

Physical meaning. The TRK sum rule $\sum_nf_{n0}=1$ is an exact equality for a Hamiltonian of the form $\Ham_0=\hat p^2/2m+V(\hat x)$, so in a system in which $\hat x$ connects the ground state effectively to only one excited state, $f_{10}=1$ is forced on that single line (the harmonic oscillator is an example: a single transition used up the entire sum rule). Therefore the setting $f_{10}=0.52$ should be read as “an effective model in which only the single strongest transition has been extracted from a real multilevel system.” If an oscillator strength exceeds $1$, a mistake has been made somewhere in the calculation—this is how the TRK sum rule is used as a check.

Exercise 8.2: Deriving the sum rule from the high-frequency expansion

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to show that, for $\hat A=\hat B$ (Hermitian), the $1/\omega$ term vanishes in the high-frequency expansion. By Hermiticity, $A_{0n}B_{n0}=A_{0n}A_{n0}=\abs{A_{0n}}^2$, so the Lehmann representation is (we may drop $\eta$, because $\abs{\omega}$ is large enough that we are far from the poles on the real axis)

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_n\abs{A_{0n}}^2\left[\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{n0}}-\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_{n0}}\right] $$

We use the given geometric-series expansion for both fractions:

$$ \frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{n0}} = \frac1\omega\left(1+\frac{\omega_{n0}}{\omega}+\frac{\omega_{n0}^2}{\omega^2}+\cdots\right), \qquad \frac{1}{\omega+\omega_{n0}} = \frac1\omega\left(1-\frac{\omega_{n0}}{\omega}+\frac{\omega_{n0}^2}{\omega^2}-\cdots\right) $$

Taking the difference, all the even-order terms in $\omega_{n0}$ cancel, and only the odd-order terms survive, doubled:

$$ \frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{n0}}-\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_{n0}} = \frac{2\omega_{n0}}{\omega^2}+\frac{2\omega_{n0}^3}{\omega^4}+\cdots $$

In particular, the $1/\omega$ term (the leading $1$ of each expansion) vanished as $1-1=0$. Going back to the general case $\hat A\ne\hat B$, the coefficient of $1/\omega$, after collapsing the complete set of states again, is

$$ \frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_n\left(A_{0n}B_{n0}-B_{0n}A_{n0}\right) = \frac{1}{\hbar}\left(\bra{0}\hat A\hat B\ket{0}-\bra{0}\hat B\hat A\ket{0}\right) = \frac{1}{\hbar}\braket{[\hat A,\hat B]} $$

and for $\hat A=\hat B$ the commutator becomes $[\hat A,\hat A]=0$—as stated in the hint. What we are seeing here is the special case, in which the commutator vanishes, of the high-frequency asymptotics $\chi^R_{AB}\to\braket{[\hat A,\hat B]}/(\hbar\omega)$ confirmed in Section 8.3.

(2) This problem asks us to write the coefficient of the leading surviving term, $1/\omega^2$, in terms of a double commutator. Picking out the leading term of the expansion in (1), we have

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) \simeq \frac{1}{\hbar}\sum_n\abs{A_{0n}}^2\cdot\frac{2\omega_{n0}}{\omega^2} = \frac{2}{\hbar\omega^2}\sum_n\abs{A_{0n}}^2\,\omega_{n0} $$

This is the first formula to be shown. Next, we rewrite this sum in the language of operators. As in the derivation of Section 8.7, using $\bra{0}[\hat A,\Ham_0]\ket{n}=A_{0n}(E_n-E_0)$ and collapsing the complete set of states, we have

$$ \braket{[[\hat A,\Ham_0],\hat A]} = \bra{0}[\hat A,\Ham_0]\hat A\ket{0}-\bra{0}\hat A[\hat A,\Ham_0]\ket{0} = 2\sum_n\abs{A_{0n}}^2(E_n-E_0) = 2\hbar\sum_n\abs{A_{0n}}^2\,\omega_{n0} $$

That is, $\displaystyle\sum_n\abs{A_{0n}}^2\omega_{n0}=\frac{1}{2\hbar}\braket{[[\hat A,\Ham_0],\hat A]}$. Substituting this into the leading term above, we find

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) \;\xrightarrow{\;\abs{\omega}\to\infty\;}\; \frac{2}{\hbar\omega^2}\cdot\frac{1}{2\hbar}\braket{[[\hat A,\Ham_0],\hat A]} = \frac{1}{\hbar^2\omega^2}\braket{[[\hat A,\Ham_0],\hat A]} $$

The coefficient is precisely the right-hand side of the $f$-sum rule.

Check: The same coefficient also follows from the Kramers–Kronig (KK) relations. In the KK relation $\chi'_{AA}(\omega)=\dfrac1\pi\PV\displaystyle\int\dd\omega'\,\dfrac{\chi''_{AA}(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega}$, let $\abs{\omega}\to\infty$ and expand $\dfrac{1}{\omega'-\omega}=-\dfrac1\omega\left(1+\dfrac{\omega'}{\omega}+\cdots\right)$. This gives

$$ \chi'_{AA}(\omega) \simeq -\frac{1}{\pi\omega}\int\dd\omega'\,\chi''_{AA}(\omega') -\frac{1}{\pi\omega^2}\int\dd\omega'\,\omega'\,\chi''_{AA}(\omega')-\cdots $$

The first term vanishes because $\chi''$ is an odd function, and the second term is $\dfrac{1}{\omega^2}\displaystyle\int\frac{\dd\omega'}{\pi}\,\omega'\bigl(-\chi''_{AA}(\omega')\bigr)$; that is, the coefficient of $1/\omega^2$ is precisely the integral of the $f$-sum rule. A sum rule is nothing other than a restatement of each order of the high-frequency expansion—the statement in the hint has been confirmed from both the Lehmann-representation side and the KK-relation side.

(3) This problem asks us to confirm that for the density operator the high-frequency limit is $nq^2/(m\omega^2)$. The Fourier component of the density, $\hat n_\qq=\sum_j\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx_j}$, is not Hermitian for finite $\qq$, so, as in Section 8.7.3 of the main text, we consider the pair $(\hat n_\qq,\hat n_\qq^\dagger)$. Exactly the same procedure as in (1) and (2) works here as well. The $1/\omega$ term corresponding to (1) vanishes because $\braket{[\hat n_\qq,\hat n_\qq^\dagger]}=0$ (both operators are functions of the positions only, so they commute), and for the coefficient of $1/\omega^2$ in (2) the weights merely split into $\abs{A_{0n}}^2+\abs{A_{n0}}^2$, so that the form of the conclusion is unchanged (Section 8.7.3 of the main text), and we obtain

$$ \chi^R_{nn}(\qq,\omega) \;\xrightarrow{\;\abs{\omega}\to\infty\;}\; \frac{1}{\hbar^2\omega^2}\braket{[[\hat n_\qq,\Ham_0],\hat n_\qq^\dagger]} $$

The double commutator, as computed in Derivation 10 of the main text, is not even an operator but just a number,

$$ [[\hat n_\qq,\Ham_0],\hat n_\qq^\dagger] = \frac{N\hbar^2q^2}{m} $$

so that, with no need even to take the expectation value,

$$ \chi^R_{nn}(\qq,\omega) \;\xrightarrow{\;\abs{\omega}\to\infty\;}\; \frac{1}{\hbar^2\omega^2}\cdot\frac{N\hbar^2q^2}{m} = \frac{Nq^2}{m\omega^2} \qquad\Bigl(\text{per unit volume}\;\frac{nq^2}{m\omega^2},\quad n=\frac NV\Bigr) $$

Neither $\hbar$, nor the interaction $V$, nor the temperature remains.

Physical meaning. When we multiply this result by the long-wavelength Coulomb interaction $v_q=e^2/(\epsilon_0 q^2)$ (SI), $q$ cancels exactly, and we obtain

$$ v_q\cdot\frac{nq^2}{m\omega^2} = \frac{ne^2}{\epsilon_0 m\omega^2} = \frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}, \qquad \omega_p = \sqrt{\frac{ne^2}{\epsilon_0 m}} $$

This is a dimensionless quantity. In Chapter 11, the high-frequency limit of the dielectric function takes the universal form $\epsilon(\qq,\omega)\to1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$ (independent of the wave number and of the details of the interaction) because the coefficient of $1/\omega^2$ in the density response is fixed by the sum rule—in other words, the calculation in this problem is its basis. We may also put it this way: an electron shaken fast enough has no time to feel the interaction and responds as a free electron.

Exercise 8.3: Both limits of the fluctuation–dissipation theorem

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to explain how the fluctuation–dissipation theorem with the thermal factor returns to the general form at $T=0$ as $T\to0$. The key is the fact that, by detailed balance $S_{BA}(-\omega)=\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}S_{AB}(\omega)$, the form with the thermal factor

$$ \chi''_{AB}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\left(1-\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}\right)S_{AB}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\left[S_{AB}(\omega)-\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}S_{AB}(\omega)\right] = -\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\left[S_{AB}(\omega)-S_{BA}(-\omega)\right] $$

is equivalent to the general form at any temperature. We examine the limit $\beta\to\infty$ of this equivalent form case by case.

The case $\omega>0$. The thermal factor becomes $1-\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}\to1-0=1$. Saying the same thing on the side of the general form, $S_{BA}(-\omega)$ is the weight of the “processes in which the system gives up energy $\hbar\omega$,” and by detailed balance $S_{BA}(-\omega)=\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}S_{AB}(\omega)\to0$—it vanishes because a system at $T=0$ is in its ground state and has no energy to give up. In either view, $\chi''_{AB}(\omega)=-\frac{\pi}{\hbar}S_{AB}(\omega)$.

The case $\omega<0$. At $T=0$ the spectral function is identically zero for $\omega<0$, by the Lehmann representation $S_{AB}(\omega)=\sum_n A_{0n}B_{n0}\,\delta(\omega-\omega_{n0})$ ($\omega_{n0}\ge0$). At first sight the thermal factor $1-\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}$ diverges as $-\ee^{\beta\hbar\abs{\omega}}$ for $\omega<0$, but the factor it multiplies, $S_{AB}(\omega)$, is (at finite temperature) small in proportion to $\ee^{-\beta\hbar\abs{\omega}}$, and the product remains exactly finite:

$$ \left(1-\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}\right)S_{AB}(\omega) = S_{AB}(\omega)-S_{BA}(-\omega) \;\xrightarrow{\;\beta\to\infty\;}\; 0-S_{BA}(-\omega) = -S_{BA}(-\omega) $$

To summarize, as $T\to0$, for either sign of $\omega$,

$$ \chi''_{AB}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\left[S_{AB}(\omega)-S_{BA}(-\omega)\right] $$

survives. This is precisely the general form that we derived in the main text as the “form valid both at $T=0$ and at finite temperature.” We can also read off the structure: only the first term contributes on the $\omega>0$ side (absorption), and only the second term on the $\omega<0$ side (emission—which does not occur at $T=0$ but is written into the response function symmetrically).

(2) This problem asks us to derive the classical limit $\hbar\omega\ll k_BT$. Taylor-expanding the exponential function for $x\equiv\beta\hbar\omega\ll1$, we have

$$ 1-\ee^{-x} = x-\frac{x^2}{2}+\cdots \simeq x = \beta\hbar\omega = \frac{\hbar\omega}{k_BT} $$

Substituting this into the expression with the thermal factor in (1), $\hbar$ cancels and we obtain

$$ \chi''_{AB}(\omega) \simeq -\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\cdot\frac{\hbar\omega}{k_BT}\,S_{AB}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi\omega}{k_BT}\,S_{AB}(\omega) \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad S_{AB}(\omega) = -\frac{k_BT}{\pi\,\omega}\,\chi''_{AB}(\omega) $$

This is the classical fluctuation–dissipation theorem, from which $\hbar$ has disappeared, and it agrees with the form that we took in the main text as the starting point of Nyquist's formula.

(3) This problem asks us to estimate the frequency above which the quantum correction exceeds $10\%$ at room temperature. Since the classical approximation replaces the factor $1-\ee^{-x}$ by $x$, its relative error, using the expansion in the hint $1-\ee^{-x}\simeq x(1-x/2)$, is

$$ \frac{x}{1-\ee^{-x}} \simeq \frac{1}{1-x/2} \simeq 1+\frac{x}{2} $$

that is, the correction is about $x/2$. This reaches $10\%$ when

$$ \frac x2 = 0.1 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad x = \beta\hbar\omega = 0.2 $$

At room temperature, $300\ \mathrm{K}$, we have $k_BT=25.85\ \mathrm{meV}$, so

$$ \hbar\omega = 0.2\times25.85\ \mathrm{meV} = 5.17\ \mathrm{meV}, \qquad \nu = \frac{\hbar\omega}{h} = \frac{5.17\times10^{-3}\times1.602\times10^{-19}}{6.626\times10^{-34}} = 1.25\times10^{12}\ \mathrm{Hz} $$

That is, the quantum correction exceeds $10\%$ at about $1.3\ \mathrm{THz}$ and above (this agrees with the value in the hint; solving $x(1-\ee^{-x})^{-1}=1.1$ numerically without using the expansion gives $x=0.194$ and $\nu=1.21\ \mathrm{THz}$, which is almost the same). From this follows the practical division of territory: electronics in the $\mathrm{MHz}$ to $\mathrm{GHz}$ range lies entirely in the classical regime, whereas in THz spectroscopy and for optical phonons (tens of $\mathrm{meV}$) the thermal factor must be used in its quantum form.

(4) This problem asks us to evaluate the Boltzmann factor for visible light, $\hbar\omega=2\ \mathrm{eV}$, at room temperature and to discuss whether the distinction between fluctuation and dissipation is needed. The exponent is

$$ \beta\hbar\omega = \frac{2\ \mathrm{eV}}{25.85\ \mathrm{meV}} = 77.4 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega} = \ee^{-77.4} \approx 2.5\times10^{-34} $$

This is of the same order of magnitude as the estimate $\ee^{-77}\approx3\times10^{-34}$ in the hint, and is effectively exactly zero. Therefore the thermal factor is $1-\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}=1$ (to $34$ digits of accuracy!), that is,

$$ \chi''_{AB}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\,S_{AB}(\omega) $$

holds, for all practical purposes, exactly. In conclusion, as far as absorption experiments with visible light are concerned, (i) emission processes that require thermal excitation (anti-Stokes type, the $\omega<0$ side) do not show up in observation, (ii) the fluctuation spectrum $S$ and the dissipation spectrum $-\hbar\chi''/\pi$ are one and the same function, and (iii) room-temperature experiments may be analyzed with the $T=0$ formula—in this sense one can say that “there is no need to distinguish fluctuation from dissipation.” However, this means that the thermal factor freezes at $1$; it does not mean that the conceptual distinction between fluctuation (spontaneous fluctuations in the absence of an external force) and dissipation (energy absorption when an external force is applied) disappears. The distinction actually matters for $\hbar\omega\lesssim k_BT$, that is, in low-energy spectroscopy such as the THz range and below, the phonon range, and magnetic excitations.

Exercise 8.4: Checking a numerical calculation with the $f$-sum rule

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to write the right-hand side of the $f$-sum rule for the charge-density operator (per unit volume) and to find $n$ numerically. As derived in the main text, the sum rule for the number-density operator $\hat n_\qq$ is, per unit volume,

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega}{\pi}\,\omega\,\bigl(-\chi''_{nn}(\qq,\omega)\bigr) = \frac{nq^2}{m} $$

Writing it with the charge-density operator $\hat\rho^{\text{ch}}_\qq=e\,\hat n_\qq$, one factor of $e$ attaches to the matrix element on each side of the response function, so that $\chi''_{\rho\rho}=e^2\chi''_{nn}$, and therefore

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega}{\pi}\,\omega\,\bigl(-\chi''_{\rho\rho}(\qq,\omega)\bigr) = \frac{ne^2q^2}{m} $$

The valence-electron density is

$$ n = \frac{Z}{V_{\text{cell}}} = \frac{8}{40\times10^{-30}\ \mathrm{m^3}} = 2.0\times10^{29}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}} $$

A feel for the numbers. The plasma energy corresponding to this $n$ is $\hbar\omega_p=\hbar\sqrt{ne^2/(\epsilon_0m)}=16.6\ \mathrm{eV}$. The values $Z=8$ and $V_{\text{cell}}=40\ \text{Å}^3$ give the same valence-electron density as silicon ($\hbar\omega_p^{\text{exp}}\approx16.7\ \mathrm{eV}$), so the setup is realistic. Apart from $q^2$, the right-hand side of the sum rule is fixed by the constant $ne^2/m=5.64\times10^{21}\ \mathrm{C^2\,kg^{-1}\,m^{-3}}$, which does not depend on the details of the material.

(2) This problem asks us to estimate the effective oscillator strength under the assumption that a single peak uses up the entire sum rule. The TRK sum rule requires, per unit cell,

$$ \sum_n f_{n0} = Z = 8 $$

The assumption that all of the spectral weight is concentrated in a single peak at $\hbar\bar\omega=3.0\ \mathrm{eV}$ means that this one peak carries the entire sum rule, so its effective oscillator strength is

$$ f_{\text{eff}} = Z = 8 \quad(\text{per unit cell}), \qquad\text{per valence electron:}\;f=1 $$

That $f=1$ per valence electron is exactly the same situation as the harmonic oscillator of Example 8.1 (a single transition uses up the sum rule), and it is the reason why the classical model that “approximates the optical response of a semiconductor by a single Lorentz oscillator with a resonance at $\hbar\bar\omega$” works even quantitatively.

Check. Let us convert this $f_{\text{eff}}$ into a dipole matrix element. Assuming $f=2m\bar\omega\abs{x}^2/\hbar$ and solving with $f=8$ and $\bar\omega=3.0\ \mathrm{eV}/\hbar=4.56\times10^{15}\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, we find $\abs{x}=\sqrt{8\hbar/(2m\bar\omega)}=3.2\ \text{Å}$. This is comparable to the edge of the unit cell, $V_{\text{cell}}^{1/3}=3.4\ \text{Å}$, and is consistent with the picture that “the whole valence-electron cloud in the unit cell oscillates together” (the reason it is larger than the roughly $1\ \text{Å}$ of a single atom is that the weight of $8$ electrons has been squeezed into a single peak).

(3) This problem asks us to list the causes for the integral of the computed spectrum stopping at only $85\%$ of the sum rule. There are two representative causes.

Cause 1: Truncation of the range of integration. The weight $\omega(-\chi'')$ of the $f$-sum rule has a long tail on the high-energy side. Transitions from the valence band to high-lying conduction bands and continuum states are weak individually but numerous, and the sum rule is not satisfied unless one integrates up to several tens of eV. If the weight lying outside the computed frequency window (for example $0$–$10\ \mathrm{eV}$) is dropped, the integral falls short by that amount. When the sum rule does not come out right, the first thing to suspect is the range of integration—this is the iron rule in practice (an insufficient number of bands—not taking enough unoccupied bands—is the same kind of truncation).

Cause 2: Inconsistency of the matrix elements. The derivation of the sum rule uses the fact that $[\hat\xx,\Ham_0]$ gives $\ii\hbar\hat\pp/m$ from the kinetic energy alone. In first-principles calculations that use a nonlocal pseudopotential $\hat V_{\text{NL}}$, however, $[\hat\xx,\hat V_{\text{NL}}]\ne0$, and the velocity (transition) matrix element is $\hat\pp/m$ plus a correction term $\frac{\ii}{\hbar}[\hat V_{\text{NL}},\hat\xx]$. If one uses a velocity operator that neglects this correction, or matrix elements evaluated with an incomplete basis set, the spectral weight is systematically missing.

Besides these, dropping the contribution of core (semicore) electrons without making it consistent with the way $Z$ is counted, or insufficient $\kk$-point sampling, also produces the same kind of deficit. In any case, the sum rule works as a checking device that quantifies which approximation lost how much weight.

Exercise 8.5: Deriving the Einstein relation from the fluctuation–dissipation theorem

Solution

This problem is worked with the Kubo-style sign convention specified in the problem statement (the convention in which the force $F$ couples as $-F\hat x$ and the classical fluctuation–dissipation theorem (FDT) reads $\tilde S_{xx}(\omega)=\frac{2k_BT}{\omega}\chi''_{xx}(\omega)$). $\tilde S$ follows the convention in which no prefactor $1/2\pi$ is attached, namely $\tilde S_{AB}(\omega)=\int\dd t\,\ee^{\ii\omega t}\braket{A(t)B(0)}$.

(1) This problem asks us to state that the $\omega\to0$ limit of the velocity response function is the mobility $\mu$. The definition of the response function was $v(\omega)=\chi_{vF}(\omega)F(\omega)$. In the sense of the adiabatic switching of Chapter 6, the limit $\omega\to0$ represents “the steady-state response to an external force that changes infinitely slowly (effectively constant),” so $\chi_{vF}(0)$ is nothing other than the ratio between a constant force $F$ and the terminal velocity $v$ it produces. In the setting of the problem this ratio is $v/F=\mu$. Hence

$$ \chi_{vF}(\omega)\;\xrightarrow{\;\omega\to0\;}\;\mu $$

Check. Transforming the equation of motion $M\dot v+\gamma v=F$ ($\gamma=1/\mu$) with the Fourier convention of this book ($\partial_t\to-\ii\omega$) gives $(-\ii M\omega+\gamma)v(\omega)=F(\omega)$, that is,

$$ \chi_{vF}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\gamma-\ii M\omega} \;\xrightarrow{\;\omega\to0\;}\;\frac1\gamma = \mu $$

which indeed agrees (for $\omega\gg\gamma/M$ we have $\chi_{vF}\to\ii/M\omega$; in other words, the response crosses over to that of a free particle with inertia alone).

(2) This problem asks us to derive $\tilde S_{vv}(\omega\to0)=2\mu k_BT$ from the classical FDT for the position.

Step 1: Finding $\chi_{xx}$. Fourier-transform the equation of motion $M\ddot x+\gamma\dot x=F$ with $\partial_t\to-\ii\omega$:

$$ \left[M(-\ii\omega)^2+\gamma(-\ii\omega)\right]x(\omega) = F(\omega) \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \left(-M\omega^2-\ii\gamma\omega\right)x(\omega) = F(\omega) $$ $$ \chi_{xx}(\omega) = \frac{x(\omega)}{F(\omega)} = -\frac{1}{M\omega^2+\ii\gamma\omega} $$

This is the expression given in the hint. Note that, since there is no binding potential, it diverges as $\omega\to0$ (a free particle is swept along indefinitely by a static force, so no static susceptibility exists).

Step 2: Taking the imaginary part. Multiply by the complex conjugate of the denominator and separate the real and imaginary parts:

$$ \chi_{xx}(\omega) = -\frac{M\omega^2-\ii\gamma\omega}{(M\omega^2)^2+(\gamma\omega)^2} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \chi''_{xx}(\omega) = \frac{\gamma\omega}{(M\omega^2)^2+(\gamma\omega)^2} = \frac{\gamma\omega}{\abs{M\omega^2+\ii\gamma\omega}^2} $$

Because of the Kubo-style sign, $\chi''>0$ for $\omega>0$ (the dissipation is positive). As $\omega\to0$, the denominator satisfies $(M\omega^2)^2\ll(\gamma\omega)^2$ (concretely, $\omega\ll\gamma/M$, a time scale slower than the velocity relaxation), so

$$ \chi''_{xx}(\omega) \simeq \frac{\gamma\omega}{\gamma^2\omega^2} = \frac{1}{\gamma\omega} = \frac{\mu}{\omega} $$

Step 3: Inserting into the classical FDT.

$$ \tilde S_{xx}(\omega) = \frac{2k_BT}{\omega}\,\chi''_{xx}(\omega) \;\xrightarrow{\;\omega\to0\;}\; \frac{2k_BT}{\omega}\cdot\frac{\mu}{\omega} = \frac{2\mu k_BT}{\omega^2} $$

Step 4: Converting to the velocity fluctuation. For a stationary process the correlation function depends only on the time difference, so $\braket{\dot x(t)\dot x(0)}=-\dfrac{\dd^2}{\dd t^2}\braket{x(t)x(0)}$ holds (each time a first derivative is moved to the other factor, one minus sign appears). Under the Fourier transform $-\dd^2/\dd t^2\to\omega^2$, so

$$ \tilde S_{vv}(\omega) = \omega^2\,\tilde S_{xx}(\omega) $$

(Equivalently, one may say that the squared modulus $\abs{-\ii\omega}^2=\omega^2$ of $v(\omega)=-\ii\omega\,x(\omega)$ is multiplied.) Inserting the result of Step 3, the factor $\omega^2$ cancels exactly, and

$$ \tilde S_{vv}(\omega) \;\xrightarrow{\;\omega\to0\;}\; \omega^2\cdot\frac{2\mu k_BT}{\omega^2} = 2\mu k_BT $$

The result stays finite. Finally, setting $\omega=0$ in the definition of $\tilde S_{vv}$ gives $\ee^{\ii\omega t}=1$, so

$$ \tilde S_{vv}(\omega\to0) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd t\,\braket{v(t)v(0)} = 2\mu k_BT $$

has been shown. What is beautiful is that the fluctuation of the position (diverging as $1/\omega^2$, that is, continuing to spread diffusively) and the fluctuation of the velocity (finite, that is, the velocity distribution of thermal equilibrium) are connected by a single factor of $\omega^2$.

(3) This problem asks us to derive the Einstein relation from the Green–Kubo expression. By stationarity, $\braket{v(t)v(0)}=\braket{v(0)v(-t)}$, and since classical quantities commute, $\braket{v(0)v(-t)}=\braket{v(-t)v(0)}$. That is, the velocity correlation function is an even function of $t$, and

$$ D = \int_0^{\infty}\dd t\,\braket{v(t)v(0)} = \frac12\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd t\,\braket{v(t)v(0)} = \frac12\times2\mu k_BT = \mu k_BT $$

This is the Einstein relation $D=\mu k_BT$.

Check (direct calculation). The velocity correlation of the Langevin equation is $\braket{v(t)v(0)}=\braket{v^2}\,\ee^{-\gamma\abs{t}/M}$, and from the equipartition of energy, $\frac12M\braket{v^2}=\frac12k_BT$, we get $\braket{v^2}=k_BT/M$. Therefore

$$ D = \int_0^{\infty}\dd t\,\frac{k_BT}{M}\ee^{-\gamma t/M} = \frac{k_BT}{M}\cdot\frac{M}{\gamma} = \frac{k_BT}{\gamma} = \mu k_BT $$

which gives the same result.

Physical Meaning: $D=\mu k_BT$ was evidence for the reality of atoms

The diffusion coefficient $D$ (the rate at which particles spread by themselves without an external force, that is, fluctuation) and the mobility $\mu$ (how readily particles are carried along when dragged by an external force, that is, dissipation) both originate from the same collisions with the molecules of the medium, so the ratio $D/\mu=k_BT$ does not depend on the details of the medium. Perrin verified this relation with colloidal particles, determined $k_B$ (that is, Avogadro's number), and settled the question of atomism (the 1926 Nobel Prize). Behind the one-line calculation of the exercise lies the first experimental triumph of the fluctuation–dissipation theorem.

Exercise 8.6: Recovering $\chi^R$ from $\chi^T$

Solution

(1) This problem asks us to examine the positions of the poles of the given $\chi^T_{AA}(\omega)$ and to confirm that there is a pole in the upper half-plane. First factor the denominator and expand into partial fractions:
