$$ (\omega+\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega)^2-\omega_0^2 = (\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega)(\omega+\omega_0+\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega) $$ $$ \chi^T_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{C}{2\omega_0}\left[ \frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega} -\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_0+\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega}\right] $$

The denominator of the first term approaches zero near $\omega\simeq+\omega_0>0$, where $\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega=+1$. Hence the denominator becomes $\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta$, and the pole is at

$$ \omega = +\omega_0-\ii\eta \qquad(\text{lower half-plane}) $$

The denominator of the second term approaches zero near $\omega\simeq-\omega_0<0$, where $\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega=-1$. The denominator becomes $\omega+\omega_0-\ii\eta$, and the pole is at

$$ \omega = -\omega_0+\ii\eta \qquad(\text{upper half-plane!}) $$

Drawn on the complex plane, the pole on the positive-frequency side lies just below the real axis and the pole on the negative-frequency side lies just above the real axis, a diagonal arrangement symmetric about the origin—this is exactly Figure 8.4(b) of the main text. Indeed there is one pole in the upper half-plane, and $\chi^T$ is not analytic in the upper half-plane. Therefore the premise of Theorem 8.1 breaks down, and the KK relations cannot be applied to $\chi^T$ as they stand. Note also that, compared with the general form of $\chi^T$ derived in the main text, the function of this problem corresponds to a one-excitation (two-level) system with $A_{01}B_{10}=\hbar C/2\omega_0$, and that $C>0$ is consistent with the positivity of the weights.

(2) This problem asks us to write down $\chi^R_{AA}(\omega)$ using the $T=0$ bridge formula. The relation derived in the main text was

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) = \mathrm{Re}\,\chi^T_{AA}(\omega) + \ii\,\mathrm{sgn}(\omega)\,\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^T_{AA}(\omega) \qquad(T=0) $$

First apply the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula $\dfrac{1}{x\pm\ii\eta}=\PV\dfrac1x\mp\ii\pi\delta(x)$ to the partial-fraction representation in (1) to obtain the real and imaginary parts. Note that the support of the delta function $\delta(\omega-\omega_0)$ is $\omega=+\omega_0$ (where $\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega=+1$, that is, of the $+\ii\eta$ type), and the support of $\delta(\omega+\omega_0)$ is $\omega=-\omega_0$ (where $\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega=-1$, that is, of the $-\ii\eta$ type). Then

$$ \frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega} = \PV\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0}-\ii\pi\,\delta(\omega-\omega_0), \qquad \frac{1}{\omega+\omega_0+\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega} = \PV\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_0}+\ii\pi\,\delta(\omega+\omega_0) $$

Hence

$$ \mathrm{Re}\,\chi^T_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{C}{2\omega_0}\,\PV\!\left[\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0}-\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_0}\right] = C\,\PV\frac{1}{\omega^2-\omega_0^2} $$ $$ \mathrm{Im}\,\chi^T_{AA}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi C}{2\omega_0}\left[\delta(\omega-\omega_0)+\delta(\omega+\omega_0)\right] $$

The imaginary part is a sum of delta functions, that is, an even function of $\omega$ (a reconfirmation of the general property of $\chi^T$ stated in the main text). Multiplying by $\mathrm{sgn}(\omega)$ attaches $+1$ to $\delta(\omega-\omega_0)$ and $-1$ to $\delta(\omega+\omega_0)$, giving

$$ \mathrm{sgn}(\omega)\,\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^T_{AA}(\omega) = -\frac{\pi C}{2\omega_0}\left[\delta(\omega-\omega_0)-\delta(\omega+\omega_0)\right] $$

which is restored to an odd function. Reassembling with the bridge formula gives

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{C}{2\omega_0}\left\{ \left[\PV\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0}-\ii\pi\delta(\omega-\omega_0)\right] -\left[\PV\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_0}-\ii\pi\delta(\omega+\omega_0)\right]\right\} $$

Reading the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula in the reverse direction, both terms inside the curly braces are fractions of the $+\ii\eta$ type:

$$ \chi^R_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{C}{2\omega_0}\left[ \frac{1}{\omega-\omega_0+\ii\eta} -\frac{1}{\omega+\omega_0+\ii\eta}\right] = \frac{C}{(\omega+\ii\eta)^2-\omega_0^2} $$

The last equality involves exactly the same step of bringing the fractions to a common denominator as in Exercise 8.1(1). As the hint says, the result takes the form in which the sign of $\ii\eta$ has simply been fixed to $+$, independently of the sign of $\omega$ (it may be remembered as the replacement rule $\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega\to+\ii\eta$).

(3) This problem asks us to confirm that all the poles of the restored $\chi^R$ lie in the lower half-plane, and to state that the KK relations hold. The denominator in (2) vanishes when $(\omega+\ii\eta)^2=\omega_0^2$, that is, at

$$ \omega = +\omega_0-\ii\eta \qquad\text{and}\qquad \omega = -\omega_0-\ii\eta $$

and both lie below the real axis. The pole on the negative-frequency side, which was in the upper half-plane in (1), has been dragged down into the lower half-plane by the removal of the $\mathrm{sgn}$ function. Hence $\chi^R_{AA}(\omega)$ is analytic in the upper half-plane and satisfies Theorem 8.1 (analyticity in the upper half-plane = causality). Therefore the Kramers–Kronig relation of Chapter 4

$$ \chi'_{AA}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\pi}\,\PV\!\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\dd\omega'\,\frac{\chi''_{AA}(\omega')}{\omega'-\omega} $$

can now be applied as it stands. In fact, $\chi''_{AA}=-\frac{\pi C}{2\omega_0}[\delta(\omega-\omega_0)-\delta(\omega+\omega_0)]$, obtained in (2), is an odd function, and since $C>0$ it also satisfies $\omega\chi''_{AA}\le0$ (the correct sign of the dissipation in the convention of this book); by the same calculation as in Example 8.2, the KK integral correctly reproduces $\chi'_{AA}$. The form is that of the harmonic oscillator of Example 8.1 (with $C=1/m$ it is $\chi^R_{xx}$ itself).

Physical meaning. What Feynman diagrams (Wick's theorem) automatically produce is $\chi^T$, which is easy to calculate but not causal. At $T=0$, merely “flipping by hand the sign of the imaginary part on the negative-frequency side” converts it into the causal $\chi^R$ that experiments measure—this problem is the smallest example of that procedure. At finite temperature this simple conversion rule does not exist, so the imaginary-time Matsubara formalism and analytic continuation (Chapter 13) are needed. What then guarantees the landing point of the analytic continuation is the analyticity in the upper half-plane confirmed in (3).

Common Mistake: Mistaking $\ii\eta\,\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega$ for a complex function

An expression containing $\mathrm{sgn}\,\omega$ is a prescription for boundary values on the real axis, not a single analytic function defined on the whole complex $\omega$ plane (there is no natural way to extend $\mathrm{sgn}$ to the complex plane). What we did when “drawing the poles” in (1) was to read the denominator as an analytic function separately in each region of the real axis ($\omega>0$ and $\omega<0$) and to pick up its poles. That “$\chi^T$ has a pole in the upper half-plane” means, in this sense, that the analytic continuation of the fraction on the negative-frequency side has a pole in the upper half-plane. For the same reason, operations that use contour integrals or Jordan's lemma (the derivation of the KK relations) cannot be applied to $\chi^T$. On the other hand, $\chi^R$ is (the boundary value of) a single analytic function on the whole plane, so all of these tools can be used.

Chapter 9: The Density Response Function and the Dielectric Function

Exercise 9.1: Properties of $\hat\rho_\qq$

Solution

(1) We show that the Hermitian conjugate of $\hat\rho_\qq=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}$ coincides with $\hat\rho_{-\qq}$. Applying the rule for the conjugate of a product, $(\hat A\hat B)^\dagger=\hat B^\dagger\hat A^\dagger$, and $(\hat c^\dagger)^\dagger=\hat c$ to each term, we obtain

$$ \left(\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}\right)^\dagger =\hat c^\dagger_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}\,\hat c_{\kk\sigma} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \hat\rho_\qq^\dagger=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}\,\hat c_{\kk\sigma} $$

Now we replace the summation variable by $\kk'=\kk+\qq$. Since $\kk$ runs over all wave vectors, $\kk'$, shifted by the fixed amount $\qq$, also runs over all wave vectors, and the range of the sum does not change. Substituting $\kk=\kk'-\qq$, we get

$$ \hat\rho_\qq^\dagger=\sum_{\kk'\sigma}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'\sigma}\,\hat c_{\kk'-\qq,\sigma} $$

On the other hand, setting $\qq\to-\qq$ in the definition gives $\hat\rho_{-\qq}=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk-\qq,\sigma}$. The two expressions are identical except for the name of the summation variable, so $\hat\rho_\qq^\dagger=\hat\rho_{-\qq}$ has been shown. As stated in Section 9.1.2, this is the Fourier-space version of the fact that $\hat\rho(\xx)$ is Hermitian (a real observable).

(2) We compute the commutator with the free-electron Hamiltonian $\Ham_0=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\varepsilon_\kk\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk\sigma}$. First we verify the identity of the hint itself from the anticommutation relation. Using $\{\hat c_a,\hat c^\dagger_b\}=\delta_{ab}$ in the forms $\hat c_b\hat c^\dagger_c=\delta_{bc}-\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_b$ and $\hat c_d\hat c^\dagger_a=\delta_{da}-\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_d$, we get

$$ \begin{aligned} [\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_b,\ \hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_d] &=\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_b\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_d-\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_d\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_b\\ &=\hat c^\dagger_a\left(\delta_{bc}-\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_b\right)\hat c_d -\hat c^\dagger_c\left(\delta_{da}-\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_d\right)\hat c_b\\ &=\delta_{bc}\,\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_d-\delta_{da}\,\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_b -\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_b\hat c_d +\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_d\hat c_b \end{aligned} $$

The last two terms cancel each other, because the sign is flipped exactly twice, once by $\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c^\dagger_a=-\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c^\dagger_c$ and once by $\hat c_d\hat c_b=-\hat c_b\hat c_d$. Hence

$$ [\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_b,\ \hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_d]=\delta_{bc}\,\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_d-\delta_{da}\,\hat c^\dagger_c\hat c_b $$

Setting $a=b$ and rewriting $(c,d)$ as $(b,c)$ gives the form of the hint, $[\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_a,\hat c^\dagger_b\hat c_c]=\delta_{ab}\hat c^\dagger_a\hat c_c-\delta_{ac}\hat c^\dagger_b\hat c_a$. We apply this to the indices $a=(\kk\sigma)$, $b=(\kk'\sigma')$, $c=(\kk'+\qq,\sigma')$ (here $\delta$ contains both the wave vector and the spin):

$$ [\Ham_0,\hat\rho_\qq] =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\sum_{\kk'\sigma'}\varepsilon_\kk\, [\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk\sigma},\ \hat c^\dagger_{\kk'\sigma'}\hat c_{\kk'+\qq,\sigma'}] =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\sum_{\kk'\sigma'}\varepsilon_\kk \left(\delta_{\kk\kk'}\delta_{\sigma\sigma'}\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk'+\qq,\sigma'} -\delta_{\kk,\kk'+\qq}\delta_{\sigma\sigma'}\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'\sigma'}\hat c_{\kk\sigma}\right) $$

In the first term the sum collapses at $\kk'=\kk$, $\sigma'=\sigma$ and becomes $\sum_{\kk\sigma}\varepsilon_\kk\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}$. The second term collapses at $\kk=\kk'+\qq$ and becomes $\sum_{\kk'\sigma'}\varepsilon_{\kk'+\qq}\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'\sigma'}\hat c_{\kk'+\qq,\sigma'}$, so, after renaming the variable back, $\kk'\to\kk$, the two sums are the same operator $\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}$ multiplied by the coefficients $\varepsilon_\kk$ and $\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}$, respectively. Altogether,

$$ [\Ham_0,\hat\rho_\qq]=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\varepsilon_\kk-\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}\right)\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma} $$

has been shown.

Check. For $\qq=\bm 0$ the coefficient is $\varepsilon_\kk-\varepsilon_\kk=0$, that is, $[\Ham_0,\hat N]=0$, which is consistent with the fact in Section 9.1.2(b) that “the particle number is a conserved quantity.”

(3) We show that the Heisenberg-picture $\hat\rho_\qq(t)$ has a phase factor for each term. We abbreviate $\hat A_{\kk\sigma}\equiv\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}$ and $\lambda_\kk\equiv\varepsilon_\kk-\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}$. Since the calculation in (2) merely collapsed the double sum term by term, for the term of each $\kk$ separately we have

$$ [\Ham_0,\hat A_{\kk\sigma}]=\lambda_\kk\,\hat A_{\kk\sigma} $$

($\hat A_{\kk\sigma}$ is an “eigenoperator” with respect to commutation with $\Ham_0$). We therefore use the operator identity

$$ \ee^{\hat X}\hat A\,\ee^{-\hat X} =\hat A+[\hat X,\hat A]+\frac{1}{2!}\,[\hat X,[\hat X,\hat A]]+\frac{1}{3!}\,[\hat X,[\hat X,[\hat X,\hat A]]]+\cdots $$

with $\hat X=\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar$ and $\hat A=\hat A_{\kk\sigma}$. The single commutator is

$$ [\hat X,\hat A_{\kk\sigma}]=\frac{\ii t}{\hbar}\,[\Ham_0,\hat A_{\kk\sigma}]=\frac{\ii\lambda_\kk t}{\hbar}\,\hat A_{\kk\sigma} $$

and since each commutation merely multiplies the operator by the same number $\ii\lambda_\kk t/\hbar$, the $n$-fold nested commutator is $\left(\ii\lambda_\kk t/\hbar\right)^n\hat A_{\kk\sigma}$. The series sums up directly into an exponential function:

$$ \hat A_{\kk\sigma}(t)=\ee^{\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar}\,\hat A_{\kk\sigma}\,\ee^{-\ii\Ham_0t/\hbar} =\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\frac{\ii\lambda_\kk t}{\hbar}\right)^n\hat A_{\kk\sigma} =\ee^{\ii(\varepsilon_\kk-\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq})t/\hbar}\,\hat A_{\kk\sigma} $$

Since the sum is linear,

$$ \hat\rho_\qq(t)=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\ee^{\ii(\varepsilon_\kk-\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq})t/\hbar}\, \hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma} $$

has been shown.

Physical meaning. Taking the conjugate gives $\hat\rho_{-\qq}(t)=\hat\rho_\qq(t)^\dagger=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\ee^{\ii(\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\varepsilon_\kk)t/\hbar}\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}\hat c_{\kk\sigma}$, and the oscillation frequency of the phase, $(\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\varepsilon_\kk)/\hbar$, is exactly the excitation energy $\hbar\omega_{\kk\qq}=\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\varepsilon_\kk$ of the particle–hole pair counted in Section 9.1.3. Substituting this result into the Kubo formula of this chapter, $\chi_{\rho\rho}(\qq,\omega)=-\frac{\ii}{\hbar V}\int_0^\infty\dd\tau\,\ee^{\ii\omega\tau}\braket{[\hat\rho_\qq(\tau),\hat\rho_{-\qq}(0)]}_0$, we find that the $\tau$ integral produces, term by term, a factor of the form $\int_0^\infty\dd\tau\,\ee^{\ii(\omega+\lambda_\kk/\hbar+\ii\eta)\tau}=\ii/(\omega+\lambda_\kk/\hbar+\ii\eta)$, and the denominator $\hbar\omega+\varepsilon_\kk-\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\ii\eta$—exactly the denominator of the Lindhard function previewed in Section 9.5—appears. Chapter 10 carries out precisely this calculation.

Exercise 9.2: The Fourier transform of the Coulomb interaction once more

Solution

(1) We verify that the Fourier transform of the Yukawa-type potential $e^2\ee^{-\mu r}/r$ is $4\pi e^2/(q^2+\mu^2)$, following the same route as in Section 9.3.1. This time we do not take $\mu\to0^+$ at the end, but simply carry the calculation through with $\mu$ kept finite.

Step 1 (polar coordinates). Taking $\qq$ along the $z$ axis and setting $u=\cos\theta$, we have $\qq\cdot\bm r=qru$ and $\dd^3r=2\pi r^2\dd r\,\dd u$, so

$$ v_\mu(q)=\int\dd^3r\;\frac{e^2\ee^{-\mu r}}{r}\,\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\bm r} =2\pi e^2\int_0^\infty\dd r\,r\,\ee^{-\mu r}\int_{-1}^{1}\dd u\;\ee^{-\ii qru} $$

Step 2 (angular integral).

$$ \int_{-1}^{1}\dd u\;\ee^{-\ii qru} =\left[\frac{\ee^{-\ii qru}}{-\ii qr}\right]_{u=-1}^{u=1} =\frac{\ee^{-\ii qr}-\ee^{\ii qr}}{-\ii qr} =\frac{2\sin qr}{qr} $$

Step 3 (radial integral). Noting that $r\cdot\frac{2\sin qr}{qr}=\frac{2\sin qr}{q}$, we obtain

$$ v_\mu(q)=\frac{4\pi e^2}{q}\int_0^\infty\dd r\;\ee^{-\mu r}\sin qr $$

Writing $\sin qr=\frac{1}{2\ii}(\ee^{\ii qr}-\ee^{-\ii qr})$, each term converges absolutely thanks to $\mu>0$, and

$$ \int_0^\infty\dd r\;\ee^{-\mu r}\sin qr =\frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\int_0^\infty\dd r\,\ee^{-(\mu-\ii q)r}-\int_0^\infty\dd r\,\ee^{-(\mu+\ii q)r}\right] =\frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\frac{1}{\mu-\ii q}-\frac{1}{\mu+\ii q}\right] =\frac{1}{2\ii}\cdot\frac{2\ii q}{\mu^2+q^2} =\frac{q}{\mu^2+q^2} $$

Step 4 (summary).

$$ v_\mu(q)=\frac{4\pi e^2}{q}\cdot\frac{q}{\mu^2+q^2}=\frac{4\pi e^2}{q^2+\mu^2} $$

has been verified. Unlike in the case of the bare Coulomb potential, there is no need to arrange the convergence at $r\to\infty$ separately, because $\ee^{-\mu r}$ cuts off the tail from the start.

(2) We apply the inverse transform to return to real space. Following the convention of this chapter, $f(\xx)=\int\frac{\dd^3q}{(2\pi)^3}f(\qq)\ee^{\ii\qq\cdot\xx}$, we compute

$$ f(r)=\int\frac{\dd^3q}{(2\pi)^3}\;\frac{4\pi e^2}{q^2+\mu^2}\,\ee^{\ii\qq\cdot\bm r} $$

Step 1 (do the angular integral first). This time we take $\bm r$ as the polar axis. By the same calculation as in Step 2 of (1) (only the sign is reversed), $\int_{-1}^1\dd u\,\ee^{\ii qru}=\frac{2\sin qr}{qr}$, so

$$ f(r)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^3}\int_0^\infty\dd q\;q^2\cdot\frac{4\pi e^2}{q^2+\mu^2}\cdot 2\pi\cdot\frac{2\sin qr}{qr} =\frac{16\pi^2e^2}{(2\pi)^3\,r}\int_0^\infty\dd q\;\frac{q\sin qr}{q^2+\mu^2} =\frac{2e^2}{\pi r}\int_0^\infty\dd q\;\frac{q\sin qr}{q^2+\mu^2} $$

The integrand is even under $q\to-q$ (the product is even, since both $q$ and $\sin qr$ are odd), so we can extend the integration interval to the entire real axis and take half:

$$ f(r)=\frac{e^2}{\pi r}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd q\;\frac{q\sin qr}{q^2+\mu^2} $$

This is the form given in the hint. Step 2 (convert to a complex integral). On the real axis $q/(q^2+\mu^2)$ is real, so we can write $\sin qr=\mathrm{Im}\,\ee^{\ii qr}$ and take the imaginary part outside the integral:

$$ f(r)=\frac{e^2}{\pi r}\,\mathrm{Im}\,J, \qquad J=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd q\;\frac{q\,\ee^{\ii qr}}{q^2+\mu^2} =\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd q\;\frac{q\,\ee^{\ii qr}}{(q-\ii\mu)(q+\ii\mu)} $$

Step 3 (residue). Since $r>0$, we close the contour with a large semicircle in the upper half-plane (the reason is given in (3)). The only pole enclosed is $q=+\ii\mu$, and its residue is

$$ \Res_{q=\ii\mu}\frac{q\,\ee^{\ii qr}}{(q-\ii\mu)(q+\ii\mu)} =\frac{\ii\mu\;\ee^{\ii(\ii\mu)r}}{2\ii\mu} =\frac{\ee^{-\mu r}}{2} $$

Since the contour is counterclockwise, the residue theorem gives

$$ J=2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{\ee^{-\mu r}}{2}=\ii\pi\,\ee^{-\mu r} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \mathrm{Im}\,J=\pi\,\ee^{-\mu r} $$

Step 4 (summary).

$$ f(r)=\frac{e^2}{\pi r}\cdot\pi\,\ee^{-\mu r}=\frac{e^2\,\ee^{-\mu r}}{r} $$

We have indeed returned to the original Yukawa-type form.

Check. Taking $\mu\to0^+$, the forward transform gives $4\pi e^2/q^2$ and the inverse transform gives $e^2/r$, reproducing the pair $v(q)=4\pi e^2/q^2\leftrightarrow e^2/r$ of Section 9.3.1. Also, if we read $\mu\to q_{TF}$, this calculation is, as it stands, the derivation of the Thomas–Fermi screening potential $\phi_{\text{tot}}(r)=-\dfrac{Ze^2}{r}\ee^{-q_{TF}r}$ of Section 9.6.3.

(3) Which semicircle to close the contour with is decided by whether $\ee^{\ii qr}$ decays on the arc. Writing $q=R\ee^{\ii\theta}$ on a semicircle of radius $R$, we have

$$ \abs{\ee^{\ii qr}}=\ee^{-Rr\sin\theta} $$

For $r>0$, in the upper half-plane ($0<\theta<\pi$, $\sin\theta>0$) the exponent is negative and the factor decays, whereas in the lower half-plane it blows up as $\ee^{+Rr\abs{\sin\theta}}$. Jordan's lemma of Chapter 5 guarantees that “for an integral containing $\ee^{\ii qr}$ ($r>0$), the contribution of the arc in the upper half-plane vanishes if the remaining factor tends uniformly to $0$ as $R\to\infty$,” and the remaining factor here satisfies $\abs{q/(q^2+\mu^2)}\sim1/R\to0$, so the condition is met. Therefore it is correct to close in the upper half-plane, and the only pole we pick up is $q=+\ii\mu$. As a result the damping factor $\ee^{-\mu r}$ emerges—the physics of decay at large distances is automatically built into the choice of contour that $r>0$ forces.

Common Mistake: Do not close the contour with $\sin qr$ as it is

Since $\sin qr=\frac{1}{2\ii}(\ee^{\ii qr}-\ee^{-\ii qr})$ has $\ee^{-\ii qr}$ blowing up in the upper half-plane and $\ee^{\ii qr}$ blowing up in the lower half-plane, the arc contribution cannot be made to vanish with either semicircle as long as the $\sin$ is kept. Always rewrite first $\sin qr=\mathrm{Im}\,\ee^{\ii qr}$ (after confirming that the remaining factor is real on the real axis), or split it into two exponentials and close each one on its own semicircle. Closing mechanically in the upper half-plane for $r<0$ (or with the sign of $r$ left undetermined) is also wrong: the direction of closing is always decided by the side on which the exponential decays.

Exercise 9.3: Following the self-consistent equation numerically

Solution

(1) For each $q$ we compute $x=-q_{TF}^2/q^2$ and $\epsilon=1-x$. From $q_{TF}=2.05\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ we have $q_{TF}^2=4.20\ \text{Å}^{-2}$. For example, for $q=0.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ we get $x=-4.20/0.25=-16.8$ and $\epsilon=1+16.8=17.8$. The remaining values are computed in the same way, and we also tabulate $1/\epsilon$, which is used in (4).

Values for the self-consistent equation (Al, $q_{TF}=2.05\ \text{Å}^{-1}$)
$q\ [\text{Å}^{-1}]$$x=-q_{TF}^2/q^2$$\epsilon=1-x$$1/\epsilon$
0.5$-16.8$$17.8$$0.0562$
1.0$-4.20$$5.20$$0.192$
2.0$-1.05$$2.05$$0.488$
4.0$-0.263$$1.26$$0.792$

The row $q=1.0\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ agrees with the values given in the hint, $x=-4.20$, $\epsilon=5.20$, and $1/\epsilon=0.192$.

(2) We rewrite the convergence condition of the geometric series, $\abs{x}<1$, as a condition on $q$. Since $x$ is negative, $\abs{x}=q_{TF}^2/q^2$, and therefore

$$ \abs{x}<1 \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \frac{q_{TF}^2}{q^2}<1 \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad q>q_{TF}=2.05\ \text{Å}^{-1} $$

That is, the series converges only on the shorter-wavelength side of the screening wave number, and on the long-wavelength side ($q<q_{TF}$), where screening is most effective, the series diverges. In the table, the three rows $q=0.5,\ 1.0,\ 2.0$ lie in the divergent region ($q=2.0\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ lies slightly below $q_{TF}=2.05\ \text{Å}^{-1}$, and with $\abs{x}=1.05$ it just barely remains on the divergent side). Only the row $q=4.0$ lies in the convergent region.

(3) We follow the partial sums $S_n=\sum_{m=0}^{n}x^m$ at $q=1.0\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ ($x=-4.20$). Since $x^2=17.6$ and $x^3=-74.1$,

$$ S_0=1,\qquad S_1=1-4.20=-3.20,\qquad S_2=-3.20+17.6=14.4,\qquad S_3=14.4-74.1=-59.6 $$

On the other hand, the correct answer is $\dfrac{1}{1-x}=\dfrac{1}{5.20}=0.192$. The partial sums change sign while their amplitude grows by a factor of about $4.2$ at each step, and there is no sign whatsoever of their approaching $0.192$—since $\abs{x}=4.20>1$, it is divergent as a geometric series. The closed form $1/(1-x)$ is nevertheless correct because in Section 9.3 we obtained this expression not as the sum of a series but by moving terms in the self-consistent equation $\phi_{\text{tot}}=\phi_{\text{ext}}+v\chi^0\phi_{\text{tot}}$ to write $(1-v\chi^0)\phi_{\text{tot}}=\phi_{\text{ext}}$ and solving it algebraically. The series is merely a “way of reading” that is valid only for $\abs{x}<1$, not the basis of the answer (the resummation of Section 9.3.3). Even if each order of perturbation theory (= each partial sum) behaves wildly, the result of recombining the infinite orders into a closed form is mild and correct—these four numbers show concisely why resummation is indispensable in many-body theory.

(4) As the fourth column of the table shows, the screening strength $1/\epsilon$ is $0.0562,\ 0.192,\ 0.488,\ 0.792$ for $q=0.5,\ 1.0,\ 2.0,\ 4.0\ \text{Å}^{-1}$. Since $1/\epsilon$ is the fraction of the external potential that survives inside, the smaller it is, the stronger the screening. The smaller $q$ is (the longer the wavelength), the smaller $1/\epsilon$ is; at $q=0.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ only $5.6\,\%$ of the voice from outside gets through. In general, $1/\epsilon=q^2/(q^2+q_{TF}^2)$ is a monotonically increasing function of $q$; as $q\to0$ it behaves as $q^2/q_{TF}^2\to0$ (long wavelengths are completely screened), and as $q\to\infty$ it goes to $1$ (the electrons cannot follow stripes shorter than the Fermi wavelength, so screening does not work). We have thus confirmed numerically that “the longer the wavelength, the more strongly it is screened.”

Exercise 9.4: Converting between $\chi$ and $\chi^0$

Solution

(1) We solve $\chi=\dfrac{\chi^0}{1-v\chi^0}$, derived in this chapter, for $\chi^0$. Clearing the denominator,

$$ \chi\left(1-v\chi^0\right)=\chi^0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \chi=\chi^0+v\chi\chi^0=\left(1+v\chi\right)\chi^0 $$

Since $1+v\chi=\epsilon^{-1}$ is not identically zero (if it were zero, that would be a special point at which $\epsilon$ diverges, and we can simply exclude it), we can divide, and

$$ \chi^0=\frac{\chi}{1+v\chi} $$

is obtained. Comparing with the original expression, the interchange $\chi\leftrightarrow\chi^0$ is paired with the sign reversal $v\to-v$, which is precisely the symmetry between $\epsilon=1-v\chi^0$ and $\epsilon^{-1}=1+v\chi$.

Check. Substituting the result into the original expression gives $\dfrac{\chi/(1+v\chi)}{1-v\chi/(1+v\chi)}=\dfrac{\chi}{(1+v\chi)-v\chi}=\chi$, so we indeed return to where we started.

(2) We examine how the two kinds of divergence points correspond. First, at $(\qq,\omega)$ where $\chi^0\to\infty$, dividing the numerator and the denominator by $\chi^0$ gives

$$ \chi=\frac{\chi^0}{1-v\chi^0}=\frac{1}{\dfrac{1}{\chi^0}-v} \;\xrightarrow{\ \chi^0\to\infty\ }\; -\frac{1}{v} $$

That is, $\chi$ does not diverge but stays at the finite value $-1/v$ (at such a point $\abs{\epsilon}=\abs{1-v\chi^0}\to\infty$, that is, the response is suppressed). Conversely, $\chi$ diverges when the denominator is zero, that is, when

$$ 1-v\chi^0=0 \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \chi^0=\frac{1}{v}=\frac{q^2}{4\pi e^2} \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \epsilon=0 $$

and this is a point where $\chi^0$ takes the finite value $1/v$. In other words, a pole of $\chi^0$ is not a pole of $\chi$, and a pole of $\chi$ is not a pole of $\chi^0$. The locations of the divergences are completely rearranged by the interaction $v$.

(3) According to the Lehmann representation of Chapter 8, the poles of a response function sit at the energies of the excitations that the response sees. The poles of $\chi^0$ lie at the particle–hole pair excitation energies $\varepsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\varepsilon_\kk$ (in the thermodynamic limit, the continuum of Figure 9.1(b), that is, the branch cut), and they represent the single-particle excitations that “kick one electron up.” On the other hand, the response that the experimenter sees by kicking with $\phi_{\text{ext}}$ is $\chi$, and its poles are the true resonances of the system, including the interaction. The calculation in (2) can be read as follows: even if the frequency of the external field matches the energy of a particle–hole pair exactly ($\chi^0\to\infty$), the oppositely directed potential created by the induced charge caps the response and stops it at $\chi=-1/v$. Instead, a new divergence is born at the place $v\chi^0=1$, which coincides with the energy of no particle–hole pair—there $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0=0$ is possible, that is, it lies outside the continuum. This is the collective excitation in which $10^{23}$ electrons sway together (the plasmon), and it is quite distinct from the poles of $\chi^0$, which were a collection of single-particle excitations. That the plasmon dispersion is drawn in Figure 9.1(b) pushed above the particle–hole continuum is a consequence of this “remaking by the interaction.”

Physical Meaning: The solution of $v\chi^0=1$ indeed gives $\omega_p$

The behavior of $\chi^0$ far above the continuum, at $\omega\to\infty$, follows from the Kramers–Kronig relations (Chapter 4) and the $f$-sum rule (Chapter 8; in Section 9.6.2 we wrote $\int_0^\infty\dd\omega\,\omega(-\mathrm{Im}\,\chi_{\rho\rho})=\pi nq^2/2m$ for $\chi_{\rho\rho}$, but the same form $\int_0^\infty\dd\omega\,\omega(-\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0)=\pi nq^2/2m$ also holds for $\chi^0$, which shares the leading term of the high-frequency expansion with $\chi$), and takes the form

$$ \chi^0(\qq,\omega)\;\simeq\;\frac{1}{\omega^2}\cdot\frac{2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\dd\omega'\,\omega'\left(-\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0(\qq,\omega')\right) =\frac{nq^2}{m\omega^2}\;>\;0 $$

so it is a positive real number. Putting this into the condition for collective excitation, $v\chi^0=1$, we get $\dfrac{4\pi e^2}{q^2}\cdot\dfrac{nq^2}{m\omega^2}=\dfrac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}=1$, that is, $\omega=\omega_p=\sqrt{4\pi ne^2/m}$. The plasma frequency that we derive in Chapter 11 shows up here from the sum rule alone.

Exercise 9.5: The loss function and sum rules

Solution

(1) For $\gamma\ll\omega_p$, we show that the peak position is $\omega\simeq\omega_p$ and the full width at half maximum is $\gamma$. What interests us is the neighborhood of $\omega\simeq\omega_p$, where the first term of the denominator, $(\omega^2-\omega_p^2)^2$, becomes small, so we expand there. In the factorization of the difference, $\omega^2-\omega_p^2=(\omega-\omega_p)(\omega+\omega_p)$, we approximate $\omega+\omega_p\simeq2\omega_p$, and setting all slowly varying factors (the $\omega$ in the numerator and the $\omega$ in $\gamma^2\omega^2$ in the denominator) equal to $\omega_p$, we obtain

$$ L(\omega)=\frac{\gamma\,\omega\,\omega_p^2}{(\omega^2-\omega_p^2)^2+\gamma^2\omega^2} \;\simeq\; \frac{\gamma\,\omega_p^3}{4\omega_p^2(\omega-\omega_p)^2+\gamma^2\omega_p^2} =\frac{\gamma\,\omega_p}{4(\omega-\omega_p)^2+\gamma^2} =\frac{\omega_p}{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\gamma^2}{4(\omega-\omega_p)^2+\gamma^2} $$

This is a Lorentzian centered at $\omega_p$. The maximum is at $\omega=\omega_p$, where $L(\omega_p)=\omega_p/\gamma$, and half of this value, $\omega_p/2\gamma$, is reached when $4(\omega-\omega_p)^2=\gamma^2$, that is, at $\omega=\omega_p\pm\gamma/2$, so the full width at half maximum is indeed $\gamma$.

Check (refining the peak position). Solving $\dd L/\dd\omega=0$ without approximation, we find from the quadratic equation in $y=\omega^2$ that the maximum occurs at $\omega_{\text{peak}}=\omega_p\sqrt{1-\gamma^2/4\omega_p^2}$ (this can also be confirmed numerically). The shift is $\gamma^2/8\omega_p^2$ in relative terms, about $4\times10^{-4}$ for the Al values given below, so for $\gamma\ll\omega_p$ it is fine to say that “the peak is at $\omega_p$.”

(2) We verify, with the same approximation, that the first moment $\int_0^\infty\dd\omega\,\omega L(\omega)$ equals $\frac{\pi}{2}\omega_p^2$. The integrand is

$$ \omega L(\omega)=\frac{\gamma\,\omega^2\,\omega_p^2}{(\omega^2-\omega_p^2)^2+\gamma^2\omega^2} $$

which also has a sharp peak at $\omega_p$. We keep the $\omega$ dependence only in the sharp denominator and set $\omega\simeq\omega_p$ elsewhere; moreover, since the tails of a Lorentzian fall off as $1/(\omega-\omega_p)^2$ outside the width $\gamma$, we may extend the integration interval to $(-\infty,\infty)$ (the error introduced by the extension is $O(\gamma/\omega_p)$):

$$ \int_0^\infty\dd\omega\,\omega L(\omega) \;\simeq\; \gamma\omega_p^2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\omega_p^2\,\dd\omega}{4\omega_p^2(\omega-\omega_p)^2+\gamma^2\omega_p^2} =\gamma\omega_p^2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega}{4(\omega-\omega_p)^2+\gamma^2} $$

For the remaining integral we let $s=\omega-\omega_p$ and use $\frac{\dd}{\dd s}\arctan\frac{2s}{\gamma}=\frac{2/\gamma}{1+4s^2/\gamma^2}$ to obtain

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd s}{4s^2+\gamma^2} =\left[\frac{1}{2\gamma}\arctan\frac{2s}{\gamma}\right]_{-\infty}^{\infty} =\frac{1}{2\gamma}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right) =\frac{\pi}{2\gamma} $$

Therefore

$$ \int_0^\infty\dd\omega\,\omega L(\omega)\;\simeq\;\gamma\omega_p^2\cdot\frac{\pi}{2\gamma}=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\omega_p^2 $$

and the sum rule of Section 9.6.2, “the first moment of the loss function is $\frac{\pi}{2}\omega_p^2$,” has been reproduced in the Drude model. This also agrees with the formula given in the hint.

Check. In fact, in the Drude model this integral is exactly $\frac{\pi}{2}\omega_p^2$ with no approximation (numerical integration gives a ratio of $1.000000$ for any value in the range $\gamma/\omega_p=0.05$–$0.3$). This is because the Drude form is a causal response function and therefore satisfies the $f$-sum rule of Chapter 8 exactly. The calculation above has thus confirmed this by a saddle-point-type evaluation for $\gamma\ll\omega_p$.

(3) We compute the peak height for Al numerically. Since $L(\omega_p)=\omega_p/\gamma$ is a ratio, it can be computed as a ratio of quantities multiplied by $\hbar$:

$$ L(\omega_p)=\frac{\omega_p}{\gamma}=\frac{\hbar\omega_p}{\hbar\gamma}=\frac{15.0\ \mathrm{eV}}{0.8\ \mathrm{eV}}=18.8 $$

(dimensionless). The loss function has a sharp peak exceeding $1$, and this number shows why the plasmon peak dominates the other structures in the spectrum of electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS).

Exercise 9.6: From the continuity equation to the longitudinal conductivity

Solution

(1) We Fourier-transform the continuity equation with the convention of this chapter. The convention is

$$ f(\xx,t)=\int\frac{\dd^3q}{(2\pi)^3}\int\frac{\dd\omega}{2\pi}\,f(\qq,\omega)\,\ee^{\ii\qq\cdot\xx-\ii\omega t} $$

so the derivatives act component by component as $\partial_t\to-\ii\omega$ and $\nabla\to\ii\qq$. Writing $\rho$ and $\bm j$ explicitly in this representation and substituting them into $\dfrac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot\bm j=0$, we obtain

$$ \int\frac{\dd^3q}{(2\pi)^3}\int\frac{\dd\omega}{2\pi} \left[-\ii\omega\,\delta\rho(\qq,\omega)+\ii\qq\cdot\bm j(\qq,\omega)\right]\ee^{\ii\qq\cdot\xx-\ii\omega t}=0 $$

This holds for all $(\xx,t)$. Since the Fourier representation is unique (Chapter 1), the square brackets must vanish component by component:

$$ -\ii\omega\,\delta\rho(\qq,\omega)+\ii\qq\cdot\bm j(\qq,\omega)=0 $$

We have thus derived the relation. Note also that the uniform background part of the density does not vary in time and carries no current, so only the fluctuation $\delta\rho$ contributes to the $\qq\neq\bm 0$ components.

(2) Dividing (1) by $\omega$ gives $\delta\rho=\qq\cdot\bm j/\omega$. Splitting the current into a longitudinal component parallel to $\qq$ and a transverse component perpendicular to it, and writing $\bm j=j_L\hat\qq+\bm j_T$ ($\hat\qq=\qq/q$), we get $\qq\cdot\bm j=q\,j_L$, so the transverse component has no effect on the density at all:

$$ \delta\rho(\qq,\omega)=\frac{q\,j_L(\qq,\omega)}{\omega} $$

In other words, the density fluctuation and the longitudinal current are tied one-to-one by the continuity equation, and if we know the response of one at the same $(\qq,\omega)$, the other is determined automatically. Let us put this into a formula. In what follows we write everything in terms of charge (the charge-density fluctuation is $\rho_c=-e\,\delta\rho_n$, where $\delta\rho_n$ is the number-density fluctuation). The longitudinal conductivity is defined as “the response of the charge current to the total (screened) electric field,” $j_L=\sigma_L E_L$. The electric field produced by the total electrostatic potential $\phi_{\text{el}}$ is $\bm E=-\nabla\phi_{\text{el}}\to E_L=-\ii q\,\phi_{\text{el}}$ (the $\hat\qq$ component), and the potential energy felt by an electron is $\phi_{\text{tot}}=(-e)\phi_{\text{el}}$. Rewriting the definition of the irreducible response, $\delta\rho_n=\chi^0\phi_{\text{tot}}$, in terms of the charge density gives

$$ \rho_c=-e\,\delta\rho_n=-e\,\chi^0\,(-e)\phi_{\text{el}}=e^2\chi^0\,\phi_{\text{el}} $$

On the other hand, from the continuity equation and the definition of $\sigma_L$ we have

$$ \rho_c=\frac{q\,j_L}{\omega}=\frac{q\,\sigma_L E_L}{\omega}=\frac{q\,\sigma_L}{\omega}\,(-\ii q\,\phi_{\text{el}}) =-\frac{\ii q^2\sigma_L}{\omega}\,\phi_{\text{el}} $$

Since $\phi_{\text{el}}$ is arbitrary, comparing coefficients gives

$$ e^2\chi^0=-\frac{\ii q^2\sigma_L}{\omega} \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \sigma_L(\qq,\omega)=\frac{\ii\omega e^2}{q^2}\,\chi^0(\qq,\omega)=\frac{\ii\omega}{4\pi}\,v(q)\,\chi^0(\qq,\omega) $$

That is, $\sigma_L$ and $\chi^0$ carry the same information, each being a constant multiple ($\ii\omega e^2/q^2$) of the other, and $\chi^0$ and $\chi_{\rho\rho}$ can be converted into each other by the relation $\chi=\chi^0/(1-v\chi^0)$ of Exercise 9.4, so in the end $\chi_{\rho\rho}$ and $\sigma_L$ also carry the same information. Let us also check the consistency: substituting into $\epsilon_L=1+\dfrac{4\pi\ii}{\omega}\sigma_L$ from the problem statement, we get

$$ \epsilon_L=1+\frac{4\pi\ii}{\omega}\cdot\frac{\ii\omega}{4\pi}\,v\chi^0=1+\ii^2\,v\chi^0=1-v(q)\,\chi^0(\qq,\omega) $$

which reproduces exactly the dielectric function $\epsilon=1-v\chi^0$ of this chapter. From the continuity equation alone, we see that the language of the density response ($\chi^0$) and the language of the current response ($\sigma_L$) are two notations for the same physics.

(3) We substitute the Drude form $\sigma_L=\dfrac{ne^2/m}{\gamma-\ii\omega}$ into $\epsilon_L=1+\dfrac{4\pi\ii}{\omega}\sigma_L$ and check that the Drude dielectric function of Example 9.1 comes out. First, substituting, we get

$$ \epsilon_L=1+\frac{4\pi\ii}{\omega}\cdot\frac{ne^2/m}{\gamma-\ii\omega} =1+\frac{4\pi ne^2}{m}\cdot\frac{1}{\omega}\cdot\frac{\ii}{\gamma-\ii\omega} $$

Following the hint, we multiply the numerator and the denominator of the last factor by the same $\ii$. The denominator becomes $(\gamma-\ii\omega)\times\ii=\ii\gamma-\ii^2\omega=\omega+\ii\gamma$, so

$$ \frac{\ii}{\gamma-\ii\omega} =\frac{\ii\times\ii}{(\gamma-\ii\omega)\times\ii} =\frac{-1}{\omega+\ii\gamma} $$

Therefore

$$ \epsilon_L =1+\frac{4\pi ne^2/m}{\omega}\cdot\frac{-1}{\omega+\ii\gamma} =1-\frac{4\pi ne^2/m}{\omega(\omega+\ii\gamma)} =1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega(\omega+\ii\gamma)} $$

In the last step we used $\omega_p^2=4\pi ne^2/m$. The Drude dielectric function of Example 9.1 has indeed been reproduced.

Check and physical meaning. Setting $\gamma\to0$ gives $\epsilon_L=1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$, which returns us to the dielectric function of the collisionless plasma in Section 9.7. Also, as $\omega\to0$ we have $\sigma_L\to ne^2/m\gamma$, which is the Drude dc conductivity itself. “The conductivity we learn in electromagnetism” and “the dielectric function of this chapter” are two faces of the same response, joined by $\epsilon_L=1+4\pi\ii\sigma_L/\omega$ with the continuity equation as the hinge—the only difference is that the language of $\sigma$ is convenient when we want to look at the side close to dc, and the language of $\epsilon$ when we want to look at resonance and screening.

Common Mistake: To “which field” is $\sigma_L$ a response?

If the electric field that defines the conductivity is the total (screened) electric field, we get the correspondence above, $\sigma_L\propto\chi^0$ (the irreducible response); if we define it as the response to the external electric field, we get $\sigma_L\propto\chi$ (the full response). The $\sigma$ of the Drude model and of Ohm's law is the response to the actual electric field in the material, that is, the former. The distinction between $\chi$ and $\chi^0$ in Table 9.1 is carried over directly to the conductivity, so when comparing formulas in the literature, first check “the response to which field,” just as in the case of the dielectric function (the warning about conventions in Section 9.4).

Chapter 10: Screening — Thomas–Fermi and the Lindhard Function

Exercise 10.1: Both limits of the static Lindhard function, and the order of limits

Solution

(1) We show that the dimensionless form $F(x)=\dfrac12+\dfrac{1-x^2}{4x}\ln\abs{\dfrac{1+x}{1-x}}$ becomes $1-x^2/3+O(x^4)$ as $x\to0$. For $0<x<1$ the argument of the logarithm is positive, so the absolute value can be dropped, and we use the basic expansion given in the hint

$$ \ln\frac{1+x}{1-x}=\ln(1+x)-\ln(1-x) =2\left(x+\frac{x^3}{3}+\frac{x^5}{5}+\cdots\right) $$

Substituting into the second term of $F$, the denominator $4x$ combines with the leading $2x$ of the expansion to give

$$ \frac{1-x^2}{4x}\cdot2\left(x+\frac{x^3}{3}+\frac{x^5}{5}+\cdots\right) =\frac{1-x^2}{2}\left(1+\frac{x^2}{3}+\frac{x^4}{5}+\cdots\right) $$

Multiplying out the two brackets and keeping terms through order $x^4$, we obtain

$$ \left(1+\frac{x^2}{3}+\frac{x^4}{5}\right)\left(1-x^2\right) =1+\frac{x^2}{3}+\frac{x^4}{5}-x^2-\frac{x^4}{3}+O(x^6) =1-\frac{2x^2}{3}-\frac{2x^4}{15}+O(x^6) $$

Multiplying this by $1/2$ and adding the first term $1/2$ of $F$, we get

$$ F(x)=\frac12+\frac12-\frac{x^2}{3}-\frac{x^4}{15}+O(x^6) =1-\frac{x^2}{3}+O(x^4) $$

(If we carry the calculation through the coefficient of $x^4$, we find that this coefficient is $-1/15$.) The value $F(0)=1$ gives $\chi^0(0,0)=-N(\epsilon_F)$, that is, it reproduces the Thomas–Fermi approximation, and $-x^2/3$ gives its first correction.

(2) Next we show that $F(x)=\dfrac{1}{3x^2}+O(x^{-4})$ as $x\to\infty$. Following the hint, put $t\equiv1/x<1$. For $x>1$ we have $\abs{\dfrac{1+x}{1-x}}=\dfrac{x+1}{x-1}$, and dividing the numerator and denominator by $x$ lets us use the same expansion as in (1):

$$ \ln\abs{\frac{1+x}{1-x}}=\ln\frac{1+t}{1-t}=2\left(t+\frac{t^3}{3}+\frac{t^5}{5}+\cdots\right) $$

We also rewrite the prefactor in terms of $t$. Noting that $t/x=t^2$ and $xt=1$, we find

$$ \frac{1-x^2}{4x}\cdot2t=\frac{(1-x^2)\,t}{2x}=\frac{t^2-1}{2} $$

and therefore

$$ \frac{1-x^2}{4x}\ln\frac{1+t}{1-t} =\frac{t^2-1}{2}\left(1+\frac{t^2}{3}+\frac{t^4}{5}+\cdots\right) =-\frac12+\left(\frac12-\frac16\right)t^2+\left(\frac16-\frac{1}{10}\right)t^4+O(t^6) $$ $$ =-\frac12+\frac{t^2}{3}+\frac{t^4}{15}+O(t^6) $$

This cancels exactly against the first term $1/2$ of $F$, leaving

$$ F(x)=\frac{1}{3x^2}+\frac{1}{15x^4}+O(x^{-6})=\frac{1}{3x^2}+O(x^{-4}) $$

This agrees with the result of item (ii) in Section 10.3.4 of the main text.

(3) We insert (2) into $\chi^0(q,0)=-N(\epsilon_F)\,F(x)$, $x=q/2k_F$, to obtain the asymptotic form for $q\gg2k_F$. Since $\dfrac{1}{3x^2}=\dfrac{4k_F^2}{3q^2}$, we have

$$ \chi^0(q,0)\simeq-N(\epsilon_F)\,\frac{4k_F^2}{3q^2} =-\frac{3n}{2\epsilon_F}\cdot\frac{4k_F^2}{3q^2} =-\frac{2nk_F^2}{\epsilon_F\,q^2} $$

Here we used $N(\epsilon_F)=3n/2\epsilon_F$. Finally, inserting $\epsilon_F=\hbar^2k_F^2/2m$, the factors of $k_F^2$ cancel cleanly, and

$$ \chi^0(q,0)\simeq-\frac{2nk_F^2}{q^2}\cdot\frac{2m}{\hbar^2k_F^2}=-\frac{4nm}{\hbar^2q^2} $$

gives the value stated in the hint. The information on Fermi statistics ($k_F$) disappears, and only the density $n$ and the mass $m$ remain. This feature closely resembles the high-frequency limit $\chi^0\to nq^2/m\omega^2$, but the sign is opposite. This is the subject of (4) below.

(4) If we formally put $\omega\to0$ into the high-frequency-limit formula $\chi^0\simeq nq^2/m\omega^2$, it diverges to $+\infty$, which disagrees with the finite negative value in (3). We explain why this “contradiction” is not a contradiction.

The key is that the two asymptotic forms can be used only in separate regions of the $(q,\omega)$ plane. In the high-frequency expansion (Section 10.6.1 of the main text) we assumed $\hbar\omega\gg\abs{\epsilon_{\kk\pm\qq}-\epsilon_\kk}$ for all $\kk$ inside the Fermi sphere. The maximum of this energy difference is $\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}(q^2+2k_Fq)$, that is, the upper end of the particle–hole continuum. In other words, $nq^2/m\omega^2$ can be used only in the region above the continuum in Figure 10.6. If we fix $q$ and let $\omega\to0$, this premise breaks down immediately.

On the other hand, the static limit $\omega=0$ lies on the $q$ axis, and for $q>2k_F$ it belongs to the region below the lower end $\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}(q^2-2k_Fq)>0$ of the continuum. The two regions are separated by the band of $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0\neq0$ (the continuum), where $\chi^0$ is not analytic. Therefore it is not permissible to extrapolate an asymptotic expansion obtained in one region across the continuum into the other. Even for the same “far region,” the two procedures

$$ \underbrace{\omega\to0\ \text{taken first, then}\ q\ \text{made large}}_{\text{path below the continuum:}\ \chi^0\to-4nm/\hbar^2q^2\ <\ 0} \qquad\text{and}\qquad \underbrace{\hbar\omega\gg\tfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}(q^2+2k_Fq)\ \text{kept while going far away}}_{\text{path above the continuum:}\ \chi^0\to+nq^2/m\omega^2\ >\ 0} $$

take different paths and reach different answers. The order of the limits cannot be interchanged.

Physically, too, the two are different things. On the static side (below the continuum) the electrons follow the potential quasistatically and settle into the equilibrium distribution, and since they flee from a repulsive force, $\delta n$ and $U_{\rm ext}$ have opposite signs, $\chi^0<0$ (screening). On the high-frequency side (above the continuum) inertia dominates, and, just as for a forced oscillator driven above resonance, the response is in antiphase with the drive and $\chi^0>0$ (anti-screening). The signs even differ because the mechanisms of the response are entirely different. Always watch the order when taking limits of the Lindhard function—this is the lesson of this exercise.

Exercise 10.2: The one-dimensional Lindhard function and the Peierls instability

Solution

(1) We find the one-dimensional static Lindhard function as an integral, starting from the given expression $\chi^0(q,0)=\dfrac{4}{L}\sum_k\dfrac{n_k}{\epsilon_k-\epsilon_{k+q}}$. The energy difference has the same form as in three dimensions and is given by

$$ \epsilon_k-\epsilon_{k+q}=\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(k^2-(k+q)^2\right)=-\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(2kq+q^2\right) $$

Replacing $\dfrac1L\sum_k\to\displaystyle\int\frac{\dd k}{2\pi}$ and restricting the integration range to $-k_F<k<k_F$ by means of $n_k$, we obtain

$$ \chi^0_{\rm 1D}(q,0)=4\int_{-k_F}^{k_F}\frac{\dd k}{2\pi}\left(-\frac{2m}{\hbar^2}\right)\frac{1}{2kq+q^2} =-\frac{4m}{\pi\hbar^2}\int_{-k_F}^{k_F}\frac{\dd k}{2kq+q^2} $$

The remaining integral is elementary. Note, however, that for $q<2k_F$ the denominator has a zero at $k=-q/2$ on the path of integration, so, as the hint says and as in Section 10.3.2 of the main text, it must be read as a principal value (this is the price paid for combining into a single term what was integrable in the original form $n_k-n_{k+q}$; the logarithm with an absolute value gives the value of that principal value):

$$ \int_{-k_F}^{k_F}\frac{\dd k}{2kq+q^2} =\frac{1}{2q}\Bigl[\ln\abs{2kq+q^2}\Bigr]_{-k_F}^{k_F} =\frac{1}{2q}\ln\abs{\frac{2k_Fq+q^2}{-2k_Fq+q^2}} =\frac{1}{2q}\ln\abs{\frac{q+2k_F}{q-2k_F}} $$

(In the last step we divided the numerator and denominator by $q>0$.) Putting these together,

$$ \chi^0_{\rm 1D}(q,0)=-\frac{4m}{\pi\hbar^2}\cdot\frac{1}{2q}\ln\abs{\frac{q+2k_F}{q-2k_F}} =-\frac{2m}{\pi\hbar^2 q}\ln\abs{\frac{q+2k_F}{q-2k_F}} $$

This is the expression we were to show.

Check. For $q\to0$, $\ln\dfrac{2k_F+q}{2k_F-q}\simeq\dfrac{q}{k_F}$ gives $\chi^0_{\rm 1D}\to-\dfrac{2m}{\pi\hbar^2k_F}$. This is the one-dimensional Fermi-level density of states (including spin, per unit length) $N_{\rm 1D}(\epsilon_F)=\dfrac{2}{\pi}\left(\dfrac{\dd\epsilon}{\dd k}\right)^{-1}_{k=k_F}=\dfrac{2m}{\pi\hbar^2k_F}$ with a minus sign attached, so the general rule $\chi^0(0,0)=-N(\epsilon_F)$ holds in one dimension as well.

(2) We confirm that $\chi^0_{\rm 1D}$ itself diverges logarithmically as $q\to2k_F$, and explain the difference from three dimensions in terms of the dimensionality of the integral. Putting $q=2k_F+\delta q$ ($\abs{\delta q}\ll k_F$), the prefactor is smooth and the numerator of the logarithm is $q+2k_F\simeq4k_F$, so

$$ \chi^0_{\rm 1D}(q,0)\simeq-\frac{m}{\pi\hbar^2k_F}\,\ln\frac{4k_F}{\abs{\delta q}} \;\xrightarrow{\;\delta q\to0\;}\;-\infty $$

that is, the value of the function itself diverges logarithmically. This contrasts with three dimensions, where $F(1)=1/2$ is finite (only the derivative diverges).

The origin of the difference lies in the structure of the integral. Even in three dimensions, exactly the same logarithm $\dfrac{1}{2kq}\ln\abs{\dfrac{2k+q}{2k-q}}$ appeared at the stage of the angular integral (Section 10.3.2 of the main text). In three dimensions, however, the radial integral $\int_0^{k_F}k\,\dd k$ remains afterward. Since the logarithmic singularity (at $k=q/2$) is integrable, integrating over $k$ smooths out the divergence into a finite value, and the singularity survives only in the one-step-weaker form of a logarithmic divergence of $\dd\chi^0/\dd q$. In one dimension there is no such “smoothing” integral to begin with, and at $q=2k_F$ the endpoint $k=-k_F$ of the integral coincides exactly with the zero $k=-q/2$ of the denominator, so the logarithm shows up directly in $\chi^0$.

In geometric language, the one-dimensional Fermi “surface” consists of only the two points $\pm k_F$, and $q=2k_F$ carries the whole of it (each point onto the other) without changing the energy—perfect nesting. In three dimensions, the only pairs that can be shifted by $2k_F$ without changing the energy are antipodal points on the Fermi sphere, a set of measure zero. Because the fraction of states contributing to the singularity decreases as the dimension increases, the singularity weakens correspondingly (Table 10.2).

(3) We insert the result of (2) into the phonon renormalization formula $\widetilde\Omega_q^{\,2}=\Omega_q^2+C\abs{g_q}^2\chi^0(q,0)$ ($C>0$) and show that the lattice becomes unstable even for weak coupling. In the convention of this book $\chi^0(q,0)<0$, so the second term is negative, and we can write

$$ \widetilde\Omega_q^{\,2}=\Omega_q^2-C\abs{g_q}^2\abs{\chi^0_{\rm 1D}(q,0)} $$

Under the assumptions of an ideal one-dimensional system, perfect nesting, mean field and $T\to0$, $\abs{\chi^0_{\rm 1D}(q,0)}$ diverges logarithmically as $q\to2k_F$ by (2). Hence, however small $C\abs{g_{2k_F}}^2$ may be, the second term grows without bound as $q\to2k_F$, and $\widetilde\Omega_q^{\,2}<0$ necessarily holds in some window of wave numbers containing $q=2k_F$. Written out explicitly, the instability condition is

$$ C\abs{g}^2\,\frac{m}{\pi\hbar^2k_F}\ln\frac{4k_F}{\abs{q-2k_F}}\;>\;\Omega^2 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \abs{q-2k_F}\;<\;4k_F\,\exp\!\left(-\frac{\pi\hbar^2k_F\,\Omega^2}{C\abs{g}^2m}\right) $$

Here the smooth quantities $g_q,\ \Omega_q$ are represented by their values $g,\ \Omega$ at $q=2k_F$. The weaker the coupling, the exponentially narrower the window becomes, but it never shrinks to zero. $\widetilde\Omega^2<0$ means that the frequency is purely imaginary, that is, that a small lattice displacement grows exponentially without oscillating. The system falls into a static lattice distortion of wave number $2k_F$ (real-space period $\pi/k_F$), a gap opens at the Fermi surface, and the energy of the electron system is lowered. This is the Peierls transition, and the resulting periodic charge modulation is the charge-density wave (CDW). Note also that at finite temperature the logarithm saturates at about $\ln(\epsilon_F/k_BT)$, so that the transition temperature becomes exponentially small, $k_BT_P\sim\epsilon_F\,\ee^{-1/\lambda}$, and that fluctuations and interchain coupling modify the mean-field conclusion, as cautioned in Section 10.4 of the main text.

Finally, we confirm that this instability is a different matter from a zero of the static dielectric function. Since $v(q)>0$ and $\chi^0(q,0)<0$, we have

$$ \epsilon(q,0)=1-v(q)\chi^0(q,0)=1+v(q)\abs{\chi^0(q,0)}\;>\;1 $$

and when $\chi^0_{\rm 1D}(2k_F,0)$ diverges to negative infinity, $\epsilon(2k_F,0)$ diverges to $+\infty$. Far from vanishing, the static external field is simply screened completely. The electron system itself is stable; what becomes unstable is the lattice, which softens through the electron–phonon coupling—we must not jump to the conclusion that “$\epsilon=0$ means instability.”

Exercise 10.3: Numerical values of the screening length, and the total screening charge

Solution

(1) For Na and Al we compute $k_F,\ \epsilon_F,\ q_{TF},\ \lambda_{TF}$ and reproduce Table 10.1. We use the same constants as in Example 10.1, $e^2=14.40\ \mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}}$ and $\hbar^2/2m=3.810\ \mathrm{eV\cdot\mathring{A}^2}$, and follow the same procedure: $k_F=(3\pi^2n)^{1/3}\to\epsilon_F=\dfrac{\hbar^2k_F^2}{2m}\to N(\epsilon_F)=\dfrac{3n}{2\epsilon_F}\to q_{TF}^2=4\pi e^2N(\epsilon_F)$.

Na ($n=2.65\times10^{22}\ \mathrm{cm^{-3}}=0.0265\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-3}}$):

$$ k_F=(29.61\times0.0265)^{1/3}=(0.7846)^{1/3}=0.922\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}, \qquad \epsilon_F=3.810\times(0.922)^2=3.24\ \mathrm{eV} $$ $$ N(\epsilon_F)=\frac{3\times0.0265}{2\times3.24}=0.01226\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-3}\,eV^{-1}}, \qquad q_{TF}^2=4\pi\times14.40\times0.01226=2.219\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-2}} $$ $$ q_{TF}=1.49\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}},\qquad \lambda_{TF}=\frac{1}{q_{TF}}=0.671\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}\simeq0.67\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}},\qquad \frac{q_{TF}}{2k_F}=\frac{1.490}{1.844}=0.81 $$

Al ($n=18.1\times10^{22}\ \mathrm{cm^{-3}}=0.181\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-3}}$):

$$ k_F=(29.61\times0.181)^{1/3}=(5.359)^{1/3}=1.750\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}, \qquad \epsilon_F=3.810\times(1.750)^2=11.7\ \mathrm{eV} $$ $$ N(\epsilon_F)=\frac{3\times0.181}{2\times11.67}=0.02327\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-3}\,eV^{-1}}, \qquad q_{TF}^2=4\pi\times14.40\times0.02327=4.211\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-2}} $$ $$ q_{TF}=2.05\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}},\qquad \lambda_{TF}=0.487\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}\simeq0.49\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}},\qquad \frac{q_{TF}}{2k_F}=\frac{2.052}{3.500}=0.59 $$

Na: $k_F=0.922,\ \epsilon_F=3.24,\ q_{TF}=1.49,\ \lambda_{TF}=0.67$; Al: $k_F=1.750,\ \epsilon_F=11.7,\ q_{TF}=2.05,\ \lambda_{TF}=0.49$—all of these reproduce the values in Table 10.1 exactly.

There is one thing we would like you to notice. The densities differ by a factor of $18.1/2.65=6.8$, yet the screening lengths differ by only a factor of $0.67/0.49=1.4$. Since $q_{TF}^2=6\pi ne^2/\epsilon_F\propto n/n^{2/3}=n^{1/3}$, we have $\lambda_{TF}\propto n^{-1/6}$, and indeed $6.8^{1/6}=1.38$. The screening length is extremely insensitive to the density, and that is precisely why $\lambda_{TF}$ falls within the narrow range of $0.5$–$0.8\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}$ for every metal in Table 10.1.

(2) At the position $R$ of the nearest-neighbor atom, we compute the suppression factor $\ee^{-q_{TF}R}$, which tells us by what factor the potential has dropped, and compare the two metals.

$$ \text{Na:}\quad q_{TF}R=1.490\times3.66=5.45, \qquad \ee^{-5.45}=4.3\times10^{-3}\simeq\frac{1}{230} $$ $$ \text{Al:}\quad q_{TF}R=2.052\times2.86=5.87, \qquad \ee^{-5.87}=2.8\times10^{-3}\simeq\frac{1}{350} $$

Whether we look at the screening length itself (Al $0.49\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}$ versus Na $0.67\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}$) or at the suppression factor at the nearest-neighbor position ($1/350$ versus $1/230$), Al is screened more strongly. This is because, although the nearest-neighbor distance of Al is shorter ($2.86\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}<3.66\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}}$), the increase in $q_{TF}$ more than compensates for it (the dimensionless product $q_{TF}R$: $5.87>5.45$). Even so, in both metals the potential is “down to one part in a few hundred by the time it reaches the neighboring atom,” and, taken together with copper in Example 10.1 ($\ee^{-4.62}\simeq1/100$), the conclusion that in simple metals the extra charge of an impurity is almost completely hidden before it reaches the nearest-neighbor atoms is universal.

(3) We show that the total charge induced around the screened point charge $+e$ is exactly $-e$ (perfect screening).

Strategy. The total charge density $\rho_{\rm tot}$, the external and induced charges combined, is related to the total potential $\phi$ by the Poisson equation $\nabla^2\phi=-4\pi\rho_{\rm tot}$. Since $\phi$ is the screened Coulomb potential obtained in Section 10.2.4, it suffices to work backward from it to $\rho_{\rm tot}$.

Step 1: The Poisson equation in Fourier space. Since $\nabla^2\to-q^2$, we have $q^2\phi(\qq)=4\pi\rho_{\rm tot}(\qq)$. Substituting $\phi(\qq)=\dfrac{4\pi e}{q^2+q_{TF}^2}$, which we derived in Chapter 10, gives

$$ \rho_{\rm tot}(\qq)=\frac{q^2}{4\pi}\,\phi(\qq)=\frac{e\,q^2}{q^2+q_{TF}^2} $$

Step 2: Extracting the induced charge alone. The external charge is the point charge $e\,\delta^3(\xx)$, whose Fourier component is the constant $e$. Subtracting it, we obtain

$$ \delta\rho(\qq)=\rho_{\rm tot}(\qq)-e =e\left(\frac{q^2}{q^2+q_{TF}^2}-1\right) =-\,\frac{e\,q_{TF}^2}{q^2+q_{TF}^2} $$

Step 3: The total charge is the $\qq\to0$ component. Setting $\qq=0$ in the definition of the Fourier transform, $\delta\rho(\qq)=\int\dd^3r\;\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}\delta\rho(\xx)$, the exponential becomes $1$ and the left-hand side is just the spatial integral itself:

$$ \int\dd^3r\;\delta\rho(\xx)=\delta\rho(\qq\to0)=-\,\frac{e\,q_{TF}^2}{0+q_{TF}^2}=-e $$

The total induced charge is exactly $-e$, and it completely cancels the original charge. This is perfect screening. This is the same thing as the way the hint puts it ($\rho_{\rm tot}(\qq\to0)=0$, that is, the total charge is zero).

Check (in real space). The function $\delta\rho(\qq)$ has the same form as the pair $\dfrac{4\pi e}{q^2+q_{TF}^2}\ \leftrightarrow\ \dfrac{e}{r}\ee^{-q_{TF}r}$ whose inverse transform we took in Section 10.2.4, so we obtain the real-space distribution just by matching the coefficients:

$$ \delta\rho(r)=-\frac{q_{TF}^2}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{e}{r}\,\ee^{-q_{TF}r} $$

Dividing space into spherical shells and integrating, we get

$$ \int\dd^3r\;\delta\rho(r) =-\frac{e\,q_{TF}^2}{4\pi}\cdot4\pi\int_0^\infty r^2\,\frac{\ee^{-q_{TF}r}}{r}\,\dd r =-e\,q_{TF}^2\int_0^\infty r\,\ee^{-q_{TF}r}\,\dd r =-e\,q_{TF}^2\cdot\frac{1}{q_{TF}^2}=-e $$

The two results indeed agree. The cloud of induced charge wraps around the point charge with a thickness of $\sim1/q_{TF}$.

Perfect screening is the response-theoretic expression of “being a metal”

That the total came out to be exactly $-e$ is no accident. $\rho_{\rm tot}(\qq\to0)=0$ holds because $\epsilon(q,0)=1+q_{TF}^2/q^2$ diverges as $q\to0$, and that in turn is a consequence of $\chi^0(0,0)=-N(\epsilon_F)\neq0$—there are states at the Fermi level. In other words, the ability to cancel a static, long-wavelength external field completely serves as the response-theoretic definition of a metal. In an insulator $N(\epsilon_F)=0$, so $\epsilon(0,0)$ remains finite, and the induced charge is $-e\,(1-1/\epsilon)$, which hides the charge only partially. Note also that the spatial distribution of the screening charge is not the Yukawa-type form of the Thomas–Fermi approximation but in reality is accompanied by Friedel oscillations (Section 10.4)—but the total, $-e$, is an exact requirement that does not depend on the approximation, and in the phase-shift method it appears as the Friedel sum rule.

Exercise 10.4: Practice with the residue theorem — another face of screening

Solution

(1) We show that the integral $I_0(r)\equiv\displaystyle\int_0^\infty\dd q\;\frac{\sin qr}{q\,(q^2+\kappa^2)}$ equals $\dfrac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\left(1-\ee^{-\kappa r}\right)$ ($r>0$). We start from the partial-fraction decomposition in the hint:

$$ \frac{1}{q\,(q^2+\kappa^2)}=\frac{1}{\kappa^2}\left(\frac1q-\frac{q}{q^2+\kappa^2}\right) $$

(Bringing the right-hand side to a common denominator gives $\dfrac{1}{\kappa^2}\cdot\dfrac{(q^2+\kappa^2)-q^2}{q(q^2+\kappa^2)}$, which returns us to the left-hand side.) Therefore

$$ I_0(r)=\frac{1}{\kappa^2}\Biggl[\underbrace{\int_0^\infty\frac{\sin qr}{q}\,\dd q}_{\textstyle D} -\underbrace{\int_0^\infty\frac{q\sin qr}{q^2+\kappa^2}\,\dd q}_{\textstyle I}\Biggr] $$

The second integral $I$ is exactly the integral computed in Steps 4–7 of Section 10.2.4 of the main text (we only need to read $q_{TF}\to\kappa$): we extend it to the whole real axis by making the integrand even, write $\sin qr=\mathrm{Im}\,\ee^{\ii qr}$, close the contour in the upper half-plane, and pick up the residue $\ee^{-\kappa r}/2$ at the pole $q=+\ii\kappa$, which gives

$$ I=\frac12\,\mathrm{Im}\left(2\pi\ii\cdot\frac{\ee^{-\kappa r}}{2}\right)=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\ee^{-\kappa r} $$

The first integral $D$ (the Dirichlet integral) can also be obtained with the tools of Chapter 5. Since the integrand is an even function of $q$, it equals half of the integral over the whole real axis, which we can write as

$$ D=\frac12\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\sin qr}{q}\,\dd q =\frac12\,\mathrm{Im}\,\PV\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\ee^{\ii qr}}{q}\,\dd q $$

Since $\sin qr/q$ is finite at $q=0$ (its value is $r$), $q=0$ is not a genuine singularity, but once we replace it with $\ee^{\ii qr}/q$ it becomes a pole on the real axis—we therefore treat it with the principal value plus a small semicircle (Chapter 5). Since $r>0$, the large semicircle in the upper half-plane drops out by Jordan's lemma. Since there is no pole in the interior of the upper half-plane, the contour integral is zero, and the contribution from the small semicircle bulging upward that avoids the pole $q=0$ on the real axis (it goes halfway around the pole in the negative direction, so it contributes $-\ii\pi\times$ the residue) balances the principal value. That is, in accordance with the general rule of Chapter 5 that a simple pole on the real axis contributes only half of its residue, we have

$$ \PV\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\ee^{\ii qr}}{q}\,\dd q=\ii\pi\,\Res_{q=0}\frac{\ee^{\ii qr}}{q}=\ii\pi $$

Hence $D=\dfrac12\,\mathrm{Im}(\ii\pi)=\dfrac{\pi}{2}$. Combining the two, we obtain

$$ I_0(r)=\frac{1}{\kappa^2}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}\,\ee^{-\kappa r}\right) =\frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\left(1-\ee^{-\kappa r}\right) $$

as claimed.

(2) We find the potential that a total charge $Q$ distributed uniformly in a sphere of radius $R$ produces outside the sphere ($r>R$) in a screening medium.

Strategy. Since screening is linear, it suffices to divide by the dielectric function for each Fourier component: under the Thomas–Fermi approximation $\epsilon(q,0)=1+\kappa^2/q^2$,

$$ \phi(\qq)=\frac{\phi_{\rm ext}(\qq)}{\epsilon(q,0)} =\frac{4\pi\rho_{\rm ext}(\qq)/q^2}{1+\kappa^2/q^2} =\frac{4\pi\rho_{\rm ext}(\qq)}{q^2+\kappa^2} $$

What we need is the Fourier transform of the sphere and the integral that appears in the inverse transform—the latter can be written with the result of (1).

Step 1: Taking the Fourier transform of the uniform sphere. For $\rho_{\rm ext}(\xx)=\rho_0\equiv\dfrac{3Q}{4\pi R^3}$ ($r<R$), using the same angular integral $\int\dd\Omega\,\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}=\dfrac{4\pi\sin qr}{qr}$ as in Section 10.2.4, we obtain

$$ \rho_{\rm ext}(\qq)=\rho_0\,\frac{4\pi}{q}\int_0^R r\sin qr\;\dd r =\rho_0\,\frac{4\pi}{q^3}\left(\sin qR-qR\cos qR\right) =Q\,\frac{3\left(\sin qR-qR\cos qR\right)}{(qR)^3} $$

(The integral $\int_0^R r\sin qr\,\dd r=\dfrac{\sin qR-qR\cos qR}{q^2}$ takes one integration by parts. Check: as $q\to0$, $\sin y-y\cos y=\dfrac{y^3}{3}+O(y^5)$, so $\rho_{\rm ext}(\qq)\to Q$, and the total charge comes out correctly.)

Step 2: Writing down the inverse transform. Substituting into the formula $\phi(r)=\dfrac{1}{2\pi^2r}\displaystyle\int_0^\infty q\sin(qr)\,\phi(q)\,\dd q$ of Section 10.2.4, we obtain

$$ \phi(r)=\frac{6Q}{\pi R^3 r}\int_0^\infty\dd q\; \frac{\sin qr\left(\sin qR-qR\cos qR\right)}{q^2\,(q^2+\kappa^2)} $$

Step 3: Converting products to sums and using (1). We name the result of (1) $G(s)\equiv\displaystyle\int_0^\infty\frac{\sin qs}{q(q^2+\kappa^2)}\,\dd q=\frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\left(1-\ee^{-\kappa s}\right)$ ($s>0$). We split the integrand into two parts. For the first part (the $\sin qR$ term), we use the identity $\dfrac{\sin qR}{q}=\displaystyle\int_0^R\cos qa\;\dd a$ and the product-to-sum formula $\sin qr\cos qa=\dfrac12\left[\sin q(r+a)+\sin q(r-a)\right]$; since $r>R\ge a$, we have $r-a>0$, so $G$ can be used as it is, and

$$ P_1\equiv\int_0^\infty\frac{\sin qr\,\sin qR}{q^2(q^2+\kappa^2)}\,\dd q =\int_0^R\dd a\;\frac{G(r+a)+G(r-a)}{2} =\frac{\pi}{4\kappa^2}\int_0^R\dd a\left[2-\ee^{-\kappa(r+a)}-\ee^{-\kappa(r-a)}\right] $$ $$ =\frac{\pi}{4\kappa^2}\left[2R-\ee^{-\kappa r}\int_0^R\left(\ee^{-\kappa a}+\ee^{\kappa a}\right)\dd a\right] =\frac{\pi}{4\kappa^2}\left[2R-\ee^{-\kappa r}\,\frac{2\sinh\kappa R}{\kappa}\right] =\frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\left[R-\frac{\sinh\kappa R}{\kappa}\,\ee^{-\kappa r}\right] $$

In the second part (the $qR\cos qR$ term), one factor of $q$ cancels, and from the product-to-sum formula $\sin qr\cos qR=\dfrac12\left[\sin q(r+R)+\sin q(r-R)\right]$ we directly obtain

$$ P_2\equiv R\int_0^\infty\frac{\sin qr\,\cos qR}{q\,(q^2+\kappa^2)}\,\dd q =\frac{R}{2}\left[G(r+R)+G(r-R)\right] =\frac{\pi R}{2\kappa^2}\left[1-\ee^{-\kappa r}\cosh\kappa R\right] $$

Step 4: Assembling the result. On subtracting, the constant terms $R$, which have nothing to do with screening, cancel against each other, and only the $\ee^{-\kappa r}$ terms remain:

$$ P_1-P_2=\frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\,\ee^{-\kappa r}\left[R\cosh\kappa R-\frac{\sinh\kappa R}{\kappa}\right] $$ $$ \phi(r)=\frac{6Q}{\pi R^3 r}\left(P_1-P_2\right) =Q\,\frac{3\left(\kappa R\cosh\kappa R-\sinh\kappa R\right)}{(\kappa R)^3}\cdot\frac{\ee^{-\kappa r}}{r} $$

That is, outside the sphere,

$$ \phi(r)=Q_{\rm eff}\,\frac{\ee^{-\kappa r}}{r}, \qquad Q_{\rm eff}=Q\,\frac{3\left(\kappa R\cosh\kappa R-\sinh\kappa R\right)}{(\kappa R)^3} $$

The form of the potential is exactly the same Yukawa-type form as for a point charge, the decay length remains $1/\kappa$, and only the effective charge is replaced, $Q\to Q_{\rm eff}$. The effect of the finite size appears only in the amplitude.

Check. Setting $y\equiv\kappa R$, from $\cosh y=1+\dfrac{y^2}{2}+\dfrac{y^4}{24}+\cdots$ and $\sinh y=y+\dfrac{y^3}{6}+\dfrac{y^5}{120}+\cdots$ we find $y\cosh y-\sinh y=\dfrac{y^3}{3}+\dfrac{y^5}{30}+\cdots$, and therefore

$$ \frac{Q_{\rm eff}}{Q}=1+\frac{(\kappa R)^2}{10}+O\!\left((\kappa R)^4\right) $$

For $\kappa R\to0$ (a sphere smaller than the screening length, or the point-charge limit), $Q_{\rm eff}\to Q$ and we return to the Yukawa-type potential of Section 10.2.4. Furthermore, for $\kappa\to0$ we get $\phi\to Q/r$—this also reproduces the theorem of electrostatics that “the field outside a uniformly charged sphere is the same as that of a point charge with the entire charge concentrated at the center.”

Physical meaning. Since the second term of the expansion is positive, $Q_{\rm eff}>Q$, and for $\kappa R\gg1$ it becomes exponentially large, $Q_{\rm eff}\simeq\dfrac{3Q}{2}\,\dfrac{\ee^{\kappa R}}{(\kappa R)^2}\gg Q$. This is because, seen from an observation point outside, the charge near the surface of the sphere (on the side closer to the observer) is “seen better” than the charge at the center by a factor of $\ee^{+\kappa R}$, since the distance through the screening medium is shorter. In vacuum, the spread of a charge distribution affects only the higher-order terms of the multipole expansion, but in a screening medium it changes the apparent size of the monopole itself.

(3) We describe the behavior of the right-hand side for $\kappa r\ll1$ and $\kappa r\gg1$, and its physical meaning.

$$ \kappa r\ll1:\qquad \frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\left(1-\ee^{-\kappa r}\right) \simeq\frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\left(\kappa r-\frac{(\kappa r)^2}{2}+\cdots\right) \simeq\frac{\pi r}{2\kappa} $$

The value of the integral keeps growing in proportion to $r$—this is the region where screening has not yet taken effect.

$$ \kappa r\gg1:\qquad \frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2}\left(1-\ee^{-\kappa r}\right)\;\to\;\frac{\pi}{2\kappa^2} $$

It saturates at a constant value—this is the region where screening has fully taken effect. For $\kappa\to0$ this saturation value diverges, reflecting the long-range nature of the bare Coulomb potential.

These two limits admit a neat physical reading. Let us construct the induced potential of a point charge $e$ (the potential produced by the screening cloud alone). We have

$$ \phi_{\rm ind}(\qq)=\phi(\qq)-\phi_{\rm ext}(\qq) =\frac{4\pi e}{q^2+\kappa^2}-\frac{4\pi e}{q^2} =-\frac{4\pi e\,\kappa^2}{q^2\,(q^2+\kappa^2)} $$

and, with the same inverse-transform formula as in Step 2 of (2), we get

$$ \phi_{\rm ind}(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi^2r}\int_0^\infty q\sin(qr)\,\phi_{\rm ind}(q)\,\dd q =-\frac{2e\kappa^2}{\pi r}\,I_0(r) =-\frac{e}{r}\left(1-\ee^{-\kappa r}\right) $$

Thus the integral in (1) turned out to be nothing other than the potential produced by the cloud of screening charge (the “another face of screening” in the title). In fact, $\dfrac{e}{r}+\phi_{\rm ind}=\dfrac{e}{r}\ee^{-\kappa r}$, so the screened Coulomb potential is correctly reproduced. Restating the two limits in these terms: for $\kappa r\ll1$, $\phi_{\rm ind}\simeq-e\kappa+O(r)$; that is, very close to the charge the cloud merely gives a constant potential shift, and the bare $e/r$ dominates. For $\kappa r\gg1$, $\phi_{\rm ind}\to-e/r$, and the cloud completely cancels the point charge—this is a restatement, in terms of the potential, of the perfect screening of Exercise 10.3(3) (induced charge $-e$).

Exercise 10.5: Checking the $f$-sum rule with the explicit $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0$

Solution

Fix $q<2k_F$. Integrating the closed form derived in the main text

$$ \mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0(q,\omega)=-\frac{m}{4\pi\hbar^2q}\Bigl[k_F^2-\max(k_1,k_2)^2\Bigr]_{+}, \qquad k_1^2=k_F^2-\frac{2m\omega}{\hbar},\quad k_2=\abs{\frac{m\omega}{\hbar q}-\frac q2} $$

over $\omega$, we show that the right-hand side $nq^2/m$ of the $f$-sum rule comes out.

Step 1: Changing variables. Following the hint, we set $y\equiv\dfrac{m\omega}{\hbar q}$. Since $\omega=\dfrac{\hbar q}{m}\,y$, we have

$$ \omega\,\dd\omega=\frac{\hbar^2q^2}{m^2}\,y\,\dd y, \qquad k_1^2=k_F^2-\frac{2m\omega}{\hbar}=k_F^2-2qy, \qquad k_2=\abs{y-\frac q2} $$

Step 2: Sorting out the regions (review of Section 10.5.2). We have $k_1>k_2$ when $\dfrac{m\omega}{\hbar q}+\dfrac q2<k_F$, that is, for $0<y<k_F-\dfrac q2$ (Region I). Since $q<2k_F$, we have $k_F-q/2>0$, so this region does exist. There, from $k_F^2-k_1^2=2qy$ we get

$$ \mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0=-\frac{m}{4\pi\hbar^2q}\cdot 2qy $$

For $k_F-\dfrac q2<y<k_F+\dfrac q2$, $k_2$ comes into play (Region II), giving

$$ \mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0=-\frac{m}{4\pi\hbar^2q}\left[k_F^2-\left(y-\frac q2\right)^2\right] $$

For $y>k_F+\dfrac q2$ we have $k_2>k_F$, so $[\;\cdot\;]_+$ vanishes—this is outside the continuum. The integral is thus automatically cut off on a finite interval.

Step 3: Factoring out the common factor.

$$ -\frac{2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\dd\omega\;\omega\,\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0 =\frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{\hbar^2q^2}{m^2}\cdot\frac{m}{4\pi\hbar^2q}\,\bigl(I_1+I_2\bigr) =\frac{q}{2\pi^2m}\,\bigl(I_1+I_2\bigr) $$ $$ I_1\equiv\int_0^{k_F-q/2}2qy\cdot y\,\dd y, \qquad I_2\equiv\int_{k_F-q/2}^{k_F+q/2}y\left[k_F^2-\left(y-\frac q2\right)^2\right]\dd y $$

Step 4: Computing $I_1$. By elementary integration,

$$ I_1=2q\int_0^{k_F-q/2}y^2\,\dd y=\frac{2q}{3}\left(k_F-\frac q2\right)^3 =\frac{2}{3}k_F^3q-k_F^2q^2+\frac{1}{2}k_Fq^3-\frac{q^4}{12} $$

(We expanded $\left(k_F-\frac q2\right)^3=k_F^3-\frac{3}{2}k_F^2q+\frac{3}{4}k_Fq^2-\frac{q^3}{8}$.)

Step 5: Computing $I_2$. As in the hint, we put $z=y-\dfrac q2$. The range of integration is $z:k_F-q\to k_F$, and with $y=z+\dfrac q2$ we have

$$ I_2=\int_{k_F-q}^{k_F}\left(z+\frac q2\right)\left(k_F^2-z^2\right)\dd z =\int_{k_F-q}^{k_F}\left(k_F^2z-z^3\right)\dd z +\frac q2\int_{k_F-q}^{k_F}\left(k_F^2-z^2\right)\dd z $$

The first integral:

$$ \left[\frac{k_F^2z^2}{2}-\frac{z^4}{4}\right]_{k_F-q}^{k_F} =\frac{k_F^2}{2}\left(2k_Fq-q^2\right)-\frac{k_F^4-(k_F-q)^4}{4} $$

Substituting $(k_F-q)^4=k_F^4-4k_F^3q+6k_F^2q^2-4k_Fq^3+q^4$, we obtain

$$ =\left(k_F^3q-\frac{k_F^2q^2}{2}\right)-\left(k_F^3q-\frac{3k_F^2q^2}{2}+k_Fq^3-\frac{q^4}{4}\right) =k_F^2q^2-k_Fq^3+\frac{q^4}{4} $$

The second integral:

$$ \frac q2\left[k_F^2z-\frac{z^3}{3}\right]_{k_F-q}^{k_F} =\frac q2\left[k_F^2q-\frac{3k_F^2q-3k_Fq^2+q^3}{3}\right] =\frac q2\left(k_Fq^2-\frac{q^3}{3}\right) =\frac{k_Fq^3}{2}-\frac{q^4}{6} $$

Adding them,

$$ I_2=k_F^2q^2-k_Fq^3+\frac{q^4}{4}+\frac{k_Fq^3}{2}-\frac{q^4}{6} =k_F^2q^2-\frac{k_Fq^3}{2}+\frac{q^4}{12} $$

Step 6: Taking the sum. The terms in $q^2$, $q^3$ and $q^4$ all cancel, leaving

$$ I_1+I_2 =\frac{2}{3}k_F^3q +\left(-k_F^2q^2+k_F^2q^2\right) +\left(\frac{k_Fq^3}{2}-\frac{k_Fq^3}{2}\right) +\left(-\frac{q^4}{12}+\frac{q^4}{12}\right) =\frac{2k_F^3q}{3} $$

This is exactly the value given in the hint. Following the hint's recommendation, we also check numerically with $k_F=q=1$: $I_1=\dfrac{2}{3}\left(\dfrac12\right)^3=\dfrac{1}{12}$, $I_2=1-\dfrac12+\dfrac1{12}=\dfrac{7}{12}$, and the sum is $\dfrac{8}{12}=\dfrac23=\dfrac{2k_F^3q}{3}$, in agreement (even if we integrate the closed form directly numerically with $\hbar=m=k_F=q=1$, the left-hand side comes out as $0.033774=1/3\pi^2$, in agreement with the exact value).

Step 7: Finishing up. Recalling that $n=k_F^3/3\pi^2$, we have

$$ -\frac{2}{\pi}\int_0^\infty\dd\omega\;\omega\,\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0 =\frac{q}{2\pi^2m}\cdot\frac{2k_F^3q}{3} =\frac{k_F^3q^2}{3\pi^2m} =\frac{nq^2}{m} $$

We have confirmed that the $f$-sum rule also holds exactly when we start from the explicit expression for $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0$.

Physical meaning. Check (ii) in Section 10.6.2 of the main text showed the sum rule at the stage of the $\kk$ sum (with the delta function left in place); here we have reached the same answer by actually integrating over $\omega$ the cross section of the continuum in the $(q,\omega)$ plane. Had the complete cancellation in Step 6 not occurred, the right-hand side of the sum rule would have acquired a $q$-dependence other than $q^2$, and this cancellation itself serves as a powerful check of the closed forms of Regions I and II (and of the position of the boundary between them). If even a single coefficient is wrong, the sum rule is bound to fail—this is a practical exercise in the habit of “checking with a sum rule.”

Exercise 10.6: Drawing the map of the particle–hole continuum yourself

Solution

(1) We rewrite the three boundary lines of the continuum in terms of the dimensionless variables $x=q/2k_F$ and $\nu=\hbar\omega/4\epsilon_F$. Since $q=2k_Fx$, we have $2k_Fq=4k_F^2x$ and $q^2=4k_F^2x^2$, and the relation in the hint

$$ \frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(2k_Fq\pm q^2\right) =\underbrace{\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\cdot4k_F^2}_{=\,4\epsilon_F}\left(x\pm x^2\right) =4\epsilon_F\left(x\pm x^2\right) $$

is verified ($\epsilon_F=\hbar^2k_F^2/2m$). Dividing both sides by $4\epsilon_F$, each boundary becomes

  • the upper end of the continuum $\hbar\omega=\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(2k_Fq+q^2\right)$ → $\nu=x+x^2$,
  • the lower end of the continuum $\hbar\omega=\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(q^2-2k_Fq\right)$ (positive only for $q>2k_F$) → $\nu=x^2-x$ ($x>1$),
  • the boundary between regions I and II $\hbar\omega=\dfrac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(2k_Fq-q^2\right)$ (positive only for $q<2k_F$) → $\nu=x-x^2$ ($x<1$)

All three are exactly the curves drawn in Figure 10.6.

(2) We rewrite $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0$ in regions I and II in the same dimensionless variables. First we build the conversion tools. Since $\epsilon_F=\hbar^2k_F^2/2m$, we have $\dfrac{m\epsilon_F}{\hbar^2}=\dfrac{k_F^2}{2}$, so the two relations of the hint

$$ \frac{m\omega}{\hbar q} =\frac{m}{\hbar q}\cdot\frac{4\epsilon_F\nu}{\hbar} =\frac{4\nu}{2k_Fx}\cdot\frac{m\epsilon_F}{\hbar^2} =\frac{4\nu}{2k_Fx}\cdot\frac{k_F^2}{2} =\frac{k_F\nu}{x}, \qquad \frac q2=k_Fx $$

follow.

Region I. We use the result of the main text, $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0=-\dfrac{\pi}{2}N(\epsilon_F)\dfrac{\omega}{qv_F}$. Substituting $\omega=\dfrac{4\epsilon_F\nu}{\hbar}=\dfrac{2\hbar k_F^2}{m}\,\nu$ and $qv_F=2k_Fx\cdot\dfrac{\hbar k_F}{m}=\dfrac{2\hbar k_F^2}{m}\,x$, the common factor $2\hbar k_F^2/m$ cancels, and

$$ \frac{\omega}{qv_F}=\frac{\nu}{x} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0=-\frac{\pi}{2}\,N(\epsilon_F)\,\frac{\nu}{x} $$

Region II. We substitute the above relations into the closed form $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0=-\dfrac{m}{4\pi\hbar^2q}\left[k_F^2-\left(\dfrac{m\omega}{\hbar q}-\dfrac q2\right)^2\right]$. The square bracket becomes

$$ k_F^2-\left(\frac{k_F\nu}{x}-k_Fx\right)^2 =k_F^2\left[1-\left(\frac{\nu}{x}-x\right)^2\right] $$

For the prefactor we use $q=2k_Fx$ and $N(\epsilon_F)=\dfrac{mk_F}{\pi^2\hbar^2}$, that is, $mk_F=\pi^2\hbar^2N(\epsilon_F)$:

$$ \frac{m}{4\pi\hbar^2\cdot2k_Fx}\cdot k_F^2 =\frac{mk_F}{8\pi\hbar^2x} =\frac{\pi N(\epsilon_F)}{8x} $$

Putting these together,

$$ \mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0=-\frac{\pi N(\epsilon_F)}{8x}\left[1-\left(\frac{\nu}{x}-x\right)^2\right] $$

We have obtained the two given expressions. Check. That both expressions become $-\dfrac{\pi}{2}N(\epsilon_F)(1-x)$ at the boundary $\nu=x-x^2$ between regions I and II has already been confirmed in the box at the end of Section 10.5.2 of the main text. Furthermore, the expression for region II gives $\dfrac{\nu}{x}-x=1$ at the upper end $\nu=x+x^2$ and $\dfrac{\nu}{x}-x=-1$ at the lower end $\nu=x^2-x$, so the square bracket becomes $1-(\pm1)^2=0$—we can also read off that $\mathrm{Im}\,\chi^0$ closes continuously to zero at the edges of the continuum.

(3) For copper we find $\nu_p$, and then find numerically the $x$ at which the plasmon dispersion meets the upper end of the continuum. First,

$$ \nu_p=\frac{\hbar\omega_p}{4\epsilon_F}=\frac{10.8}{4\times7.04}=\frac{10.8}{28.16}=0.3835 $$

The condition for the intersection is $\sqrt{\nu_p^2+\dfrac35x^2}=x+x^2$. Both sides are positive, so we may square them, and we need only solve

$$ f(x)\equiv\left(x+x^2\right)^2-\left(0.1471+0.6\,x^2\right)=0 $$

(where $\nu_p^2=0.1471$). Since $f$ starts from a negative value before turning monotonically increasing for $x>0$, we search for the root by bracketing:

$x$$(x+x^2)^2$$0.1471+0.6x^2$$f(x)$
$0.30$$0.1521$$0.2011$$-0.0490$
$0.34$$0.2076$$0.2165$$-0.0089$
$0.35$$0.2233$$0.2206$$+0.0027$
$0.348$$0.2201$$0.2198$$+0.0003$

Narrowing down the bisection gives $x_c=0.348$ (three significant digits), which agrees with the hint's $x\simeq0.35$. Converting back to the wave number,

$$ q_c=2k_F\,x_c=0.696\,k_F\simeq0.70\,k_F \qquad\left(\text{For Cu, }q_c=0.696\times1.359=0.946\ \mathrm{\mathring{A}^{-1}}\right) $$

The energy at the intersection is $\nu_c=x_c+x_c^2=0.469$, that is, $\hbar\omega_c=4\epsilon_F\nu_c=28.16\times0.469=13.2\ \mathrm{eV}$; we also see that the plasmon, having climbed up along its dispersion from the plasma energy of $10.8\ \mathrm{eV}$, runs into the continuum at this point. This agrees with the point at which the red plasmon curve in Figure 10.6 enters the continuum ($q\simeq0.7k_F$). For $q>q_c$ the plasmon can decay into a single particle–hole pair while conserving momentum and energy (Landau damping, Chapter 11), and in electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS) the plasmon peak rapidly broadens and disappears beyond this wave number—$q_c$ is a quantity that can be measured experimentally.

Limitations of this estimate of $q_c$

The plasmon dispersion $\nu=\sqrt{\nu_p^2+\frac35x^2}$ used here is the lowest-order correction of the high-frequency expansion (Section 10.6.1 of the main text) extrapolated out to the wave number of the intersection. If the random-phase approximation (RPA) dispersion equation $\epsilon(\qq,\omega)=0$ is solved exactly, the position of the intersection shifts somewhat, and in real metals interband transitions and the screening by core electrons also modify the dispersion. The correct way to read the result is as a rule of thumb: “Landau damping sets in at around $q_c\sim0.7k_F$.”

Chapter 11: Plasma Oscillations and Zero Sound

Exercise 11.1: Resonance wavelength of a silver nanoparticle

Solution
