(1) From the lattice constant we find the electron density $n$, and then obtain the free-electron plasmon energy from the practical formula. Ag is fcc, so a unit cell (volume $a^3$) contains four atoms; with one valence electron per atom, that is four per unit cell.

$$ a^3=(4.09\times10^{-10})^3=6.842\times10^{-29}\ \mathrm{m^3}, \qquad n=\frac{4}{a^3}=\frac{4}{6.842\times10^{-29}}=5.85\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}} $$

Substituting into the practical formula $\hbar\omega_p\,[\mathrm{eV}]=3.714\sqrt{n/10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}}$ built in Example 11.1 gives

$$ \hbar\omega_p=3.714\times\sqrt{5.85}=3.714\times2.418=8.98\ \mathrm{eV}\simeq9.0\ \mathrm{eV} $$

We have reproduced the value $9.0\ \mathrm{eV}$ in Table 11.1. Note that the hint's $n=5.86\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$ is the number corresponding to the standard literature lattice constant $a=4.086\ \mathrm{Å}$; from the $a=4.09\ \mathrm{Å}$ of this problem we obtain $5.85\times10^{28}$. The difference is within the rounding of the last digit and has no effect on $\hbar\omega_p$.

(2) We find the resonance energy of a spherical particle in water ($\epsilon_m=1.77$), with background polarization $\epsilon_\infty=3.7$, from $\hbar\omega_{\text{LSPR}}=\hbar\omega_p/\sqrt{\epsilon_\infty+2\epsilon_m}$, derived in Section 11.6. First the denominator is

$$ \sqrt{\epsilon_\infty+2\epsilon_m}=\sqrt{3.7+2\times1.77}=\sqrt{7.24}=2.691 $$

so

$$ \hbar\omega_{\text{LSPR}}=\frac{9.0}{2.691}=3.34\ \mathrm{eV}, \qquad \lambda=\frac{1239.8}{3.34}=371\ \mathrm{nm} $$

This agrees with the hint's $3.34\ \mathrm{eV}\to371\ \mathrm{nm}$ (using the unrounded value $8.98\ \mathrm{eV}$ from (1) still gives $\lambda=371\ \mathrm{nm}$, unchanged). The absorption peak of real spherical Ag nanoparticles (in water, particle diameter about 20 nm) is around $400\ \mathrm{nm}$, so a model with only two constants, $n$ and $\epsilon_\infty$, is accurate to within just under 10%. The main causes of the discrepancy are, as noted in the problem statement, that $\epsilon_\infty$ actually depends on $\omega$, and corrections for particle size and shape.

(3) The question is in which direction the resonance shifts when the medium is replaced by glass ($\epsilon_m=2.25$). With the same formula,

$$ \hbar\omega_{\text{LSPR}}=\frac{9.0}{\sqrt{3.7+2\times2.25}}=\frac{9.0}{\sqrt{8.2}}=\frac{9.0}{2.8636}=3.143\ \mathrm{eV}\simeq3.14\ \mathrm{eV}, \qquad \lambda=\frac{1239.8}{3.143}=394\ \mathrm{nm} $$

As $\epsilon_m$ increases, the denominator $\sqrt{\epsilon_\infty+2\epsilon_m}$ increases and the resonance energy decreases. That is, the resonance shifts to longer wavelengths (toward the red). From water to glass, $371\to394\ \mathrm{nm}$ is a red shift of about $23\ \mathrm{nm}$.

Physical meaning. The larger the dielectric constant of the surroundings, the more the polarization charge induced on the particle surface is partially canceled by the polarization of the medium, and the restoring force weakens. If the spring becomes softer, the frequency drops—this is the mechanical reading of the red shift. In terms of refractive index, water is $\sqrt{1.77}=1.33$ and glass is $\sqrt{2.25}=1.50$, so the sensitivity of this model is about

$$ \frac{\Delta\lambda}{\Delta n_{\text{medium}}}\simeq\frac{394-371}{1.50-1.33}=\frac{23}{0.17}\simeq1.4\times10^{2}\ \mathrm{nm/RIU} $$

Here RIU stands for refractive index unit. If molecules adsorb on the particle surface and the surrounding refractive index changes slightly, the position of the absorption peak—that is, the color—shifts on the scale of nanometers. This is the principle of the LSPR biosensor mentioned in Section 11.6, and the fact that the resonance condition $\epsilon+2\epsilon_m=0$ contains the environment's $\epsilon_m$ is itself the sensor function.

Exercise 11.2: Collective oscillation of a spherical sample (depolarizing field)

Solution

(1) We set up the equation of motion for the case where all the electrons in the sphere are displaced by $u$, and show that the natural frequency is $\omega_p/\sqrt3$. As given in the problem statement, for a displacement $u$ a uniform electric field $E=\frac{4\pi}{3}neu$ arises inside the sphere, in the same direction as the displacement. The force on one electron (charge $-e$) is

$$ F=(-e)E=-\frac{4\pi ne^2}{3}\,u $$

opposite to the displacement—indeed a restoring force. Compared with the slab in Section 11.3.2, the quantity corresponding to the spring constant has decreased from $4\pi ne^2$ to $\frac{4\pi ne^2}{3}$, by exactly a factor of $1/3$. The equation of motion is

$$ m\frac{\dd^2u}{\dd t^2}=-\frac{4\pi ne^2}{3}u \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \omega^2=\frac{4\pi ne^2}{3m}=\frac{\omega_p^2}{3} $$

Therefore the natural frequency is

$$ \omega=\frac{\omega_p}{\sqrt3} $$

as claimed.

(2) We check that this result agrees with the formula $\omega_{\text{LSPR}}=\omega_p/\sqrt{\epsilon_\infty+2\epsilon_m}$ of Section 11.6 in the case $\epsilon_\infty=\epsilon_m=1$. Substituting immediately gives

$$ \omega_{\text{LSPR}}=\frac{\omega_p}{\sqrt{1+2\times1}}=\frac{\omega_p}{\sqrt3} $$

so the two agree. We can also check from the side of the resonance condition. Putting $\epsilon_m=1$ and $\epsilon(\omega)=1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$ into the resonance condition of the sphere, $\epsilon(\omega)+2\epsilon_m=0$, gives

$$ 1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}+2=0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}=3 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \omega=\frac{\omega_p}{\sqrt3} $$

The mechanical calculation in (1) is a translation of the response-function statement “the denominator of the polarizability, $\epsilon+2\epsilon_m$, is zero” into Newton's equation of motion. In either language the answer is the same.

(3) We find the natural frequency for the case where all the electrons in an infinite cylinder are displaced by $u$ perpendicular to the axis. The internal electric field is given as $E=2\pi neu$ (in the same direction as the displacement), so, exactly as in (1),

$$ m\frac{\dd^2u}{\dd t^2}=-2\pi ne^2u \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \omega^2=\frac{2\pi ne^2}{m}=\frac{\omega_p^2}{2}, \qquad \omega=\frac{\omega_p}{\sqrt2} $$

For a displacement parallel to the axis, as the note in the problem statement says, no depolarizing field arises, so the restoring force is zero and $\omega=0$ (mere translation).

Check. The resonance condition of the cylinder (perpendicular to the axis) is $\epsilon(\omega)+\epsilon_m=0$, from the two-dimensional electrostatic problem, and for $\epsilon_m=1$ we have $1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2=-1$, that is, $\omega=\omega_p/\sqrt2$, in agreement with the above. This is the same value as the surface plasmon of a flat surface, $\omega_s=\omega_p/\sqrt2$, but this is not a coincidence: in both cases the resonance condition reduces to $\epsilon=-1$.

Physical Meaning: Shape determines frequency—the depolarization factor

Putting the three results side by side, and using the depolarization factor $L$ for the direction of displacement, we can summarize them in one line: the internal electric field is $E=4\pi L\,neu$, and the natural frequency is $\omega=\sqrt{L}\,\omega_p$:

Shape (direction of displacement)$L$Natural frequency
Slab (normal direction)$1$$\omega_p$
Cylinder (perpendicular to the axis)$1/2$$\omega_p/\sqrt2$
Sphere$1/3$$\omega_p/\sqrt3$
Slab (in-plane) and cylinder (axial direction)$0$$0$ (no restoring force)

The sum of $L$ over the three orthogonal directions is always $1$ (slab: $1+0+0$, cylinder: $\frac12+\frac12+0$, sphere: $\frac13+\frac13+\frac13$). For an ellipsoid, $L$ takes different values along the three axes, varying continuously, so a nanorod has two resonances, along the long and short axes, and the resonance wavelength can be designed simply by changing the aspect ratio. “If the shape of the sample changes, the resonance frequency changes”—the reason the color can be controlled by the shape of a nanoparticle is entirely contained in this table.

Exercise 11.3: The critical wave number of sodium

Solution

(1) From $n=2.53\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$, we compute the Fermi wave number, the Fermi velocity, and the Fermi energy in turn.

$$ k_F=(3\pi^2n)^{1/3}=(29.61\times2.53\times10^{28})^{1/3}=(7.491\times10^{29})^{1/3} =9.08\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}=0.91\ \mathrm{Å^{-1}} $$ $$ v_F=\frac{\hbar k_F}{m}=\frac{1.0546\times10^{-34}\times9.08\times10^{9}}{9.109\times10^{-31}}=1.051\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{m/s}\simeq1.05\times10^{6}\ \mathrm{m/s} $$ $$ \epsilon_F=\frac12mv_F^2=\frac12\times9.109\times10^{-31}\times(1.051\times10^{6})^2 =5.03\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J}=3.14\ \mathrm{eV} $$

These agree with the values given in the hint, $k_F=0.91\ \mathrm{Å^{-1}}$, $v_F=1.05\times10^6\ \mathrm{m/s}$, and $\epsilon_F=3.1\ \mathrm{eV}$. Compared with Al ($k_F=1.75\ \mathrm{Å^{-1}}$, $\epsilon_F=11.7\ \mathrm{eV}$), the electron gas of Na, which is monovalent and also has a larger lattice, is much more dilute.

(2) We find the dimensionless ratio $\alpha$.

$$ \alpha=\frac{\hbar\omega_p}{\epsilon_F}=\frac{5.9}{3.14}=1.88 $$

This is larger than $\alpha=1.35$ for Al. Since $\hbar\omega_p\propto\sqrt{n}$ and $\epsilon_F\propto n^{2/3}$, we have $\alpha\propto n^{-1/6}$, so the more dilute the metal, the larger $\alpha$ becomes.

(3) We obtain the critical wave number and the shortest wavelength from the rough estimate $q_c/k_F=\alpha/2$ (the intersection obtained by taking the plasmon dispersion to be constant, $\omega\simeq\omega_p$, and the upper edge of the continuum to be $\omega\simeq v_Fq$).

$$ \frac{q_c}{k_F}=\frac{1.88}{2}=0.94, \qquad q_c=0.94\times0.908=0.85\ \mathrm{Å^{-1}} $$ $$ \lambda_{\min}=\frac{2\pi}{q_c}=\frac{6.283}{0.85}=7.4\ \mathrm{Å} $$

In Na, a plasmon with a wavelength shorter than about $7.4\ \mathrm{Å}$ cannot exist. This is longer than $\lambda_c\simeq5\ \mathrm{Å}$ for Al.

(4) For $\alpha=1.88$ of Na, we solve the condition $\alpha+\dfrac65\dfrac{x^2}{\alpha}=2x+x^2$ ($x=q_c/k_F$) of Section 11.5, which includes the $q^2$ correction to the dispersion and the recoil. Moving terms and rearranging gives

$$ \left(1-\frac{6}{5\alpha}\right)x^2+2x-\alpha=0 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad 0.36\,x^2+2x-1.88=0 $$

($6/(5\times1.88)=0.638$, $1-0.638=0.36$.) Taking the positive root with the quadratic formula, we obtain

$$ x=\frac{-2+\sqrt{4+4\times0.36\times1.88}}{2\times0.36} =\frac{-2+\sqrt{6.71}}{0.72} =\frac{-2+2.590}{0.72}=0.82 $$

That is, $q_c=0.82\times0.908=0.74\ \mathrm{Å^{-1}}$ and $\lambda_{\min}=2\pi/0.74=8.5\ \mathrm{Å}$. This agrees with $x\simeq0.82$ in the hint.

Comparison with (3) and physical meaning. The $q_c$ from the careful calculation is smaller than the rough estimate by a little more than 10%. This is because the effect of the upper edge of the continuum rising earlier by the amount of the recoil term $x^2$ outweighs the effect of the plasmon side being slightly lifted by the $q^2$ dispersion; in Al too the value shifted in the same direction ($0.68\to0.65$). Also, $q_c/k_F$ is larger for Na ($0.82$) than for Al ($0.65$). This is because the larger $\alpha=\hbar\omega_p/\epsilon_F$ is, the larger the wave number the upper edge of the continuum needs in order to reach the plasmon height $\hbar\omega_p$. In absolute terms, however, $k_F$ itself is smaller, so $q_c=0.74\ \mathrm{Å^{-1}}$ is smaller than $1.1\ \mathrm{Å^{-1}}$ for Al, and the shortest wavelength that can exist is longer for Na.

Exercise 11.4: The lifetime of a plasmon

Solution

(1) We expand the Drude-type $\epsilon(\omega)=1-\dfrac{\omega_p^2}{\omega(\omega+\ii/\tau)}$ to first order in $1/\omega\tau$ and find the real and imaginary parts. Rearranging the denominator gives

$$ \omega\left(\omega+\frac{\ii}{\tau}\right)=\omega^2\left(1+\frac{\ii}{\omega\tau}\right) $$

Using $\dfrac{1}{1+z}\simeq1-z$ for $\abs{z}\ll1$ with $z=\ii/\omega\tau$, we obtain

$$ \epsilon(\omega)=1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}\cdot\frac{1}{1+\ii/\omega\tau} \simeq1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}\left(1-\frac{\ii}{\omega\tau}\right) =1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}+\ii\,\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^3\tau} $$

Therefore

$$ \mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon=1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}, \qquad \mathrm{Im}\,\epsilon=\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^3\tau} $$

The correction to $\mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon$ starts at second order, $1/(\omega\tau)^2$, so to first order the same form as in the dissipationless case remains. Also, $\mathrm{Im}\,\epsilon>0$ is the sign that represents absorption, consistent with the conventions of Chapters 3 and 8.

(2) We evaluate the $\omega$ derivative of $\mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon$ at $\omega=\omega_p$.

$$ \frac{\partial\,\mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon}{\partial\omega} =\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(1-\frac{\omega_p^2}{\omega^2}\right) =+\frac{2\omega_p^2}{\omega^3}, \qquad \left.\frac{\partial\,\mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon}{\partial\omega}\right|_{\omega=\omega_p}=\frac{2}{\omega_p} $$

(3) We insert the results of (1) and (2) into the damping-rate formula $\Gamma_q=2\,\mathrm{Im}\,\epsilon(\qq,\omega_q)\bigg/\left.\dfrac{\partial\,\mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon}{\partial\omega}\right|_{\omega_q}$ derived in Section 11.2. The zero in the present case is $\omega_q=\omega_p$ ($\mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon(\omega_p)=0$), and the imaginary part there is $\mathrm{Im}\,\epsilon(\omega_p)=\dfrac{\omega_p^2}{\omega_p^3\tau}=\dfrac{1}{\omega_p\tau}$, so

$$ \Gamma=\frac{2\times\dfrac{1}{\omega_p\tau}}{\dfrac{2}{\omega_p}}=\frac{1}{\tau} $$

That is, the damping rate of the plasmon is the electron collision rate itself. Since all the electrons are oscillating in phase, the rate $1/\tau$ at which collisions disrupt the phase of an individual electron becomes directly the damping rate of the collective mode, a straightforward result.

(4) For Al, with $\tau=8\times10^{-15}\ \mathrm{s}$, we evaluate the peak width $\hbar\Gamma=\hbar/\tau$ numerically.

$$ \hbar\Gamma=\frac{\hbar}{\tau}=\frac{1.0546\times10^{-34}}{8\times10^{-15}}=1.32\times10^{-20}\ \mathrm{J}=0.082\ \mathrm{eV}\simeq0.08\ \mathrm{eV} $$

On the other hand, the width measured by electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS) is already about $0.5\ \mathrm{eV}$ at $q\simeq0$, more than six times broader than the prediction from Drude collisions alone. Therefore the main cause of the measured plasmon width is not collisions of the conduction electrons. The width that remains at $q\simeq0$ comes from decay into interband excitations through the potential of the crystal lattice (in the random-phase approximation, or RPA, for the free-electron model, a plasmon at $q\simeq0$ cannot decay into particle–hole pairs, but when a lattice is present, the constraint of momentum conservation is relaxed and the decay becomes allowed). The sharp broadening of the width to several eV as $q$ is increased further occurs because the decay into particle–hole pairs seen in Section 11.5 (Landau damping) begins to take effect as $q\to q_c$. The Drude $1/\tau$ is only the smallest of these contributions.

Common Mistake: Mixing up the factor of 2

The $\Gamma_q$ of this chapter is defined as the damping rate of the intensity (the square of the amplitude), and it corresponds to the full width at half maximum of the resonance peak (Section 11.2). If it were defined as the damping rate of the amplitude, it would be half as large, $1/2\tau$. The factor $2$ in the numerator of the formula in (3) is a consequence of this definition, and if it is dropped, one obtains the “only half right” answer $\Gamma=1/2\tau$. When converting to an energy width as well, $\hbar\Gamma=\hbar/\tau$ is the full width at half maximum, and the half width at half maximum is half of that.

Exercise 11.5: Solving the dispersion equation of zero sound

Solution

(1) For $F_0=2$, the dispersion equation of zero sound $1=\dfrac{F_0}{2}\left[s\ln\dfrac{s+1}{s-1}-2\right]$ becomes $g(s)=1$ if we set $g(s)\equiv s\ln\dfrac{s+1}{s-1}-2$. We solve this by bracketing. Evaluating $g$ at several values of $s$ gives the following (for example, for $s=1.16$, $\ln\frac{2.16}{0.16}=\ln 13.50=2.603$ and $1.16\times2.603-2=1.019$).

$s$$g(s)$Sign of $g(s)-1$
1.101.349$+$
1.200.877$-$
1.151.062$+$
1.161.019$+$
1.170.980$-$

The root lies between $1.16$ and $1.17$. Linear interpolation gives

$$ s\simeq1.16+0.01\times\frac{1.019-1}{1.019-0.980}=1.16+0.01\times\frac{0.019}{0.039}\simeq1.165 $$

and the actual value is slightly below this (solving numerically gives $s=1.1647$). Therefore, to two decimal places,

$$ s\simeq1.16 $$

This agrees with the value in the hint and is also consistent with the adjacent rows of Table 11.2 ($s=1.050$ for $F_0=1.05$, and $s=1.200$ for $F_0=2.28$). The zero-sound speed is $c_0=1.16\,v_F$.

(2) We compare with the strong-coupling approximation $s\simeq\sqrt{F_0/3}$. For $F_0=2$,

$$ \sqrt{\frac{F_0}{3}}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}=0.816 $$

and the exact solution $1.16$ is much larger. Moreover, since $0.816<1$, the approximate value does not even satisfy the condition $s>1$ (outside the particle–hole continuum) in the first place. There are two reasons. First, this approximation keeps only the lowest order of the expansion for $s\gg1$, $g(s)=\dfrac{2}{3s^2}+\dfrac{2}{5s^4}+\cdots$, and the discarded correction term $\frac{3}{5s^2}$ is not small at $s\sim1$—$F_0=2$ is not $F_0\gg1$. Second, since the exact $g(s)$ diverges logarithmically as $s\to1^+$, the solution is always pushed to the $s>1$ side for any $F_0>0$, but the lowest-order approximate expression knows nothing of this “logarithmic wall,” and for $F_0<3$ it casually returns $s<1$. The strong-coupling approximation can be used only for $F_0\gg1$ (in Table 11.2 as well, it was far off, with the approximate value $0.872$ for $F_0=2.28$).

(3) We compute $g'(s)$ and show that $g$ is monotonically decreasing for $s>1$ (that is, that the solution is unique). By the product rule and $\dfrac{\dd}{\dd s}\ln(s\pm1)=\dfrac{1}{s\pm1}$,

$$ g'(s)=\ln\frac{s+1}{s-1}+s\left(\frac{1}{s+1}-\frac{1}{s-1}\right) =\ln\frac{s+1}{s-1}-\frac{2s}{s^2-1} $$

To examine the sign, it is quickest to use the $1/s$ expansion, which converges for $s>1$. We use the expansion $\ln\dfrac{s+1}{s-1}=2\left(\dfrac{1}{s}+\dfrac{1}{3s^3}+\dfrac{1}{5s^5}+\cdots\right)$, which was also used in the supplement of Section 11.4, and the expansion $\dfrac{2s}{s^2-1}=\dfrac{2}{s}\cdot\dfrac{1}{1-1/s^2}=2\left(\dfrac{1}{s}+\dfrac{1}{s^3}+\dfrac{1}{s^5}+\cdots\right)$ obtained from the geometric series, and subtract the latter from the former term by term. The $1/s$ terms cancel and we obtain

$$ g'(s)=2\left(\frac13-1\right)\frac{1}{s^3}+2\left(\frac15-1\right)\frac{1}{s^5}+\cdots =-\frac{4}{3s^3}-\frac{8}{5s^5}-\cdots $$

The coefficient $2\left(\frac{1}{2j+1}-1\right)$ is negative for every term with $j\ge1$, so $g'(s)<0$ throughout $s>1$ (the leading term $-\frac{4}{3s^3}$ is as in the hint). As another way of checking, differentiating once more gives

$$ g''(s)=-\frac{2}{s^2-1}+\frac{2(s^2+1)}{(s^2-1)^2}=\frac{-2(s^2-1)+2(s^2+1)}{(s^2-1)^2}=\frac{4}{(s^2-1)^2}>0 $$

That is, $g'$ is an increasing function, and since its limit as $s\to\infty$ is $0$, we have $g'(s)<0$ all along the way—the same conclusion. To summarize, $g$ starts from $+\infty$ at $s\to1^+$ and decreases monotonically to $0$ as $s\to\infty$. Therefore, for any $F_0>0$, the equation $g(s)=2/F_0$ has exactly one solution, and the zero-sound mode is uniquely determined.

(4) We compare with the first-sound speed for the same $F_0=2$.

$$ \frac{c_1}{v_F}=\sqrt{\frac{1+F_0}{3}}=\sqrt{\frac{3}{3}}=1 $$

$F_0=2$ is exactly the coupling strength at which the first-sound speed equals the Fermi velocity. On the other hand, since $c_0/v_F=s=1.16$,

$$ \frac{c_0}{c_1}=\frac{1.16}{1}=1.16 $$

Zero sound is 16% faster. This is a concrete example of the general rule $c_0>c_1$ of Section 11.8, and this case, $F_0=2$ and $c_0/c_1=1.16$, fits neatly into the trend of Table 11.2 (as $F_0$ increases, $c_0/c_1$ approaches $1$, going $1.49\to1.02$; it is $1.15$ at $F_0=2.28$).

Chapter 12: Inelastic Scattering and the Dynamic Structure Factor

Exercise 12.1: The Bragg condition is a restatement of kinematics

Solution

(1) We are to derive the Bragg condition $2d\sin\theta_B=\lambda$ by substituting $q=2\pi/d$, $k=2\pi/\lambda$ and $\theta=2\theta_B$ into the kinematics of elastic scattering, $q=2k\sin(\theta/2)$.

As derived in Section 12.1, for elastic scattering ($k'=k$) the magnitude of the momentum transfer is

$$ q = 2k\sin\frac{\theta}{2} $$

Setting $\theta=2\theta_B$ here, we have $\sin(\theta/2)=\sin\theta_B$, so

$$ q = 2k\sin\theta_B $$

The condition for diffraction to occur in a crystal is that the momentum transfer coincide with the reciprocal-lattice vector corresponding to the family of planes with spacing $d$, that is, $q=2\pi/d$. Substituting this together with $k=2\pi/\lambda$ gives

$$ \frac{2\pi}{d} = 2\cdot\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}\,\sin\theta_B \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{1}{d} = \frac{2}{\lambda}\sin\theta_B $$

Multiplying both sides by $\lambda d$ and rearranging, we obtain

$$ 2d\sin\theta_B = \lambda $$

which is the Bragg condition.

Physical meaning. Bragg's law is not an independent law; it is merely a restatement of the “kinematics of elastic scattering” together with the “periodicity of the crystal (scattering interferes constructively only when $\qq$ is a reciprocal-lattice vector).” The relation $\theta=2\theta_B$ holds because the Bragg angle $\theta_B$ is the angle measured from the lattice planes, whereas the scattering angle $\theta$ is the angle between $\kk$ and $\kk'$ (the beam is bent by $\theta_B$ at both incidence and reflection, $2\theta_B$ in total). The experimentalists' habit of calling the position of a diffraction peak “$2\theta$” comes from this.

(2) We find the scattering angle at which the reflection from the Al (111) planes ($d=2.34\ \text{Å}$) appears for Cu K$\alpha$ radiation ($\lambda=1.54\ \text{Å}$). From the result of (1),

$$ \sin\theta_B = \frac{\lambda}{2d} = \frac{1.54}{2\times2.34} = \frac{1.54}{4.68} = 0.329 $$

Therefore

$$ \theta_B = \arcsin(0.329) = 19.2^\circ, \qquad \theta = 2\theta_B = 38.4^\circ $$

(3) Now we view the same planes with thermal neutrons ($\lambda=1.8\ \text{Å}$).

$$ \sin\theta_B = \frac{1.8}{4.68} = 0.385, \qquad \theta_B = 22.6^\circ, \qquad \theta = 2\theta_B = 45.2^\circ $$

Why the choice of wavelength matters can be seen from the constraint $\sin\theta_B=\lambda/2d\le1$. Two things can be read off from it.

  • A wavelength that is too long makes the reflection disappear. If $\lambda > 2d$, then $\sin\theta_B > 1$, and the reflection is in principle unobservable however the angle is chosen. In the present example $2d=4.68\ \text{Å}$, so Al (111) cannot be seen with cold neutrons (for example $\lambda=5\ \text{Å}$). Put the other way around, the plane spacings visible with a probe of wavelength $\lambda$ are limited to $d\ge\lambda/2$. This is what the hint in the main text means by “one cannot see fine structure unless the wavelength is made shorter,” and it is the Bragg version of the statement “for elastic scattering $q\le2k$” (the upper limit of the momentum transfer) in Section 12.1.2.
  • A wavelength that is too short squeezes the angles together. Conversely, if $\lambda\ll d$, then $\theta_B\simeq\lambda/2d$ is very small, and all the reflections are pushed into a narrow range of forward angles, which makes them hard to resolve. For example, for the $100\ \mathrm{keV}$ electrons of Example 12.4 ($\lambda=0.037\ \text{Å}$), the same reflection appears at $\theta\simeq0.9^\circ$.

That is, when $\lambda$ is chosen comparable to $d$ (but no larger than $2d$), the diffraction angles open up into the region of several tens of degrees, which is easy to measure. It is precisely because both X-ray Cu K$\alpha$ and thermal neutrons have wavelengths of exactly the same order as the interatomic distance (a few Å) that they are the leading players in structure analysis.

Exercise 12.2: Measuring the sample temperature with detailed balance

Solution

(1) From the integrated-intensity ratio $I_{\text{gain}}/I_{\text{loss}}=0.31$ of a phonon pair at $\hbar\omega=\pm12.0\ \mathrm{meV}$, we find the sample temperature.

We use the detailed balance derived in Section 12.4,

$$ S(\qq,-\omega) = \ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}\,S(\qq,\omega) $$

Since the integrated intensity of the peak on the energy-gain side ($-\omega$) is proportional to $S(\qq,-\omega)$ and that on the loss side to $S(\qq,\omega)$, we have

$$ \frac{I_{\text{gain}}}{I_{\text{loss}}} = \ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega} = 0.31 $$

Taking the logarithm of both sides,

$$ \beta\hbar\omega = -\ln 0.31 = 1.171 $$

Since $\hbar\omega=12.0\ \mathrm{meV}$,

$$ k_BT = \frac{\hbar\omega}{1.171} = \frac{12.0}{1.171} = 10.25\ \mathrm{meV} $$

Dividing by $k_B=0.08617\ \mathrm{meV/K}$,

$$ T = \frac{10.25}{0.08617} = 119\ \mathrm{K} $$

Check. $k_BT=10.25\ \mathrm{meV}$ is of the same order as $\hbar\omega=12.0\ \mathrm{meV}$. For $\beta\hbar\omega\simeq1$ the gain side should be about $\ee^{-1}\simeq0.37$ of the loss side, which is consistent with the observed value $0.31$. It is precisely because this is a temperature range in which the thermal energy and the phonon energy are comparable that the gain side is clearly visible and can be used as a thermometer.

(2) What happens if we take the ratio with the same constant background $B>0$ still superimposed on both sides? The apparent ratio is

$$ R' = \frac{I_{\text{gain}}+B}{I_{\text{loss}}+B} $$

Taking the difference from the true ratio $R=I_{\text{gain}}/I_{\text{loss}}<1$, we get

$$ R' - R = \frac{I_{\text{gain}}+B}{I_{\text{loss}}+B}-\frac{I_{\text{gain}}}{I_{\text{loss}}} = \frac{B\left(I_{\text{loss}}-I_{\text{gain}}\right)}{I_{\text{loss}}\left(I_{\text{loss}}+B\right)} \;>\;0 $$

Since the numerator and the denominator are both positive, $R'>R$. In other words, the weaker side (the gain side) is relatively inflated, and the ratio moves closer to $1$. The apparent $\beta\hbar\omega=-\ln R'$ is smaller than the true value, and $T=\hbar\omega/(k_B\beta\hbar\omega)$ comes out too high.

Let us confirm this numerically. Normalizing the loss-side intensity to $1$ and taking $B$ to be $10\%$ of the loss side ($B=0.10$), we have

$$ R' = \frac{0.31+0.10}{1+0.10} = 0.373, \qquad \beta\hbar\omega = -\ln 0.373 = 0.987, \qquad T' = \frac{12.0}{0.987\times0.08617} = 141\ \mathrm{K} $$

Merely forgetting to subtract a background of only $10\%$ produces an overestimate of as much as $22\ \mathrm{K}$ (about $19\%$). The accuracy of this thermometer is determined by the accuracy of the weak-side peak.

(3) For the same sample ($k_BT=10.25\ \mathrm{meV}$), we predict the gain-side intensity ratio for the peak at $\hbar\omega=+40\ \mathrm{meV}$.

$$ \frac{I_{\text{gain}}}{I_{\text{loss}}} = \ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega} = \exp\left(-\frac{40}{10.25}\right) = \ee^{-3.90} = 0.020 $$

The gain side is only $2.0\%$ of the loss side. Let us consider whether such a measurement is realistic. The error in counting statistics is determined by $\sqrt{N}$ for a count $N$, so obtaining the same relative precision on the gain side as on the loss side requires about $50$ times the measuring time. Moreover, as we saw in (2), that faint peak is easily buried under the error in estimating the background. That is, high-energy modes are effectively invisible on the gain side. If detailed balance is to be used as a thermometer, one should choose a low-energy mode with $\beta\hbar\omega\lesssim1$ (for this sample, around $10\ \mathrm{meV}$).

Common Mistake: Forgetting the correction before taking the ratio

Detailed balance is strictly a relation for $S(\qq,\omega)$. The measured counts are multiplied by the prefactor $k'/k$ of the double-differential cross section, and $k'$ is smaller on the loss side and larger on the gain side. In addition, the detector efficiency and the energy resolution also depend on $\omega$. In a precise temperature determination, one corrects for these, converts back to $S$, and only then takes the intensity ratio. If one measures at an incident energy with $\hbar\omega\ll E_i$, then $k'/k\simeq1$ and this correction is small (it cannot be neglected, for example, when measuring $\hbar\omega=12\ \mathrm{meV}$ with thermal neutrons of $E_i\simeq25\ \mathrm{meV}$), but the list in Section 12.4.3 of things to suspect—“if the ratio deviates greatly from $\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}$, first suspect nonequilibrium or an analysis error”—should also include this forgotten correction.

Exercise 12.3: The $f$-sum rule for the loss function and the plasma frequency

Solution

(1) We substitute the relation between $S$ and the loss function into the left-hand side of the $f$-sum rule. The $f$-sum rule of Section 12.6 was

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd\omega\;\omega\,S(\qq,\omega) = \frac{\hbar q^2}{2m} $$

For the electron gas at $T=0$, $S(\qq,\omega)$ takes nonzero values only for $\omega>0$ (Section 12.4: at $T=0$ no spectrum arises on the energy-gain side), so the range of integration may be narrowed to the interval from $0$ to $\infty$:

$$ \int_{0}^{\infty}\dd\omega\;\omega\,S(\qq,\omega) = \frac{\hbar q^2}{2m} $$

Substituting here the exact rewriting derived in Section 12.5,

$$ S(\qq,\omega) = \frac{\hbar\,q^2}{4\pi^2 n e^2}\,\mathcal{L}(\qq,\omega), \qquad \mathcal{L}(\qq,\omega)\equiv-\mathrm{Im}\,\frac{1}{\epsilon(\qq,\omega)} $$

the coefficient comes out of the $\omega$ integral, and we obtain

$$ \frac{\hbar q^2}{4\pi^2 ne^2}\int_0^{\infty}\dd\omega\;\omega\,\mathcal{L}(\qq,\omega) = \frac{\hbar q^2}{2m} $$

(2) Canceling the common factor $\hbar q^2$ on both sides, $q$ disappears completely:

$$ \int_0^{\infty}\dd\omega\;\omega\,\mathcal{L}(\qq,\omega) = \frac{4\pi^2 ne^2}{2m} = \frac{\pi}{2}\cdot\frac{4\pi ne^2}{m} = \frac{\pi}{2}\,\omega_p^2 $$

In the last step we used the definition of the plasma frequency $\omega_p^2=4\pi ne^2/m$ (Gaussian units, Chapter 11). This is the sum rule we were asked to find.

The meaning of the disappearance of $q$. The first moment of the loss function takes the same value $\frac{\pi}{2}\omega_p^2$ whatever wave number it is measured at. The “shape” of the spectrum changes dramatically with $q$—for $q<q_c$ it is almost a single plasmon peak, and for $q>q_c$ a gentle hump spread over the particle–hole continuum (Figure 12.3)—but the total quantity weighted by $\omega$ is invariant. This is the loss-function version of the statement that the $f$-sum rule says “interactions can change only how the energy is distributed, not its total” (Section 12.6.2). The total capacity of the electron gas to absorb energy from an external field, at long wavelengths or at short, is determined solely by the density and the mass.

(3) We assume the single-mode approximation $\mathcal{L}(\qq,\omega)=A\,\delta(\omega-\omega_p)$ and determine the weight $A$. Substituting into the sum rule, the delta function picks out $\omega=\omega_p$, and

$$ \int_0^{\infty}\dd\omega\;\omega\,A\,\delta(\omega-\omega_p) = A\,\omega_p = \frac{\pi}{2}\,\omega_p^2 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad A = \frac{\pi}{2}\,\omega_p $$

That is, the loss function for $q\to0$ is

$$ \mathcal{L}(\qq,\omega) = \frac{\pi}{2}\,\omega_p\,\delta(\omega-\omega_p) $$

Check. This coefficient can be compared independently with the derivation of Section 12.5 (in the limit $\epsilon''\to0^+$, $\mathcal{L}=\pi\,\delta(\omega-\omega_p)/\abs{\partial\epsilon'/\partial\omega|_{\omega_p}}$). In the random-phase approximation (RPA) for $q\to0$ (Drude form), $\epsilon'(\omega)=1-\omega_p^2/\omega^2$, so

$$ \left.\frac{\partial\epsilon'}{\partial\omega}\right|_{\omega=\omega_p} = \left.\frac{2\omega_p^2}{\omega^3}\right|_{\omega=\omega_p} = \frac{2}{\omega_p} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \mathcal{L} = \frac{\pi}{2/\omega_p}\,\delta(\omega-\omega_p) = \frac{\pi}{2}\,\omega_p\,\delta(\omega-\omega_p) $$

The two agree perfectly. This also shows that for $q\to0$ a single plasmon uses up this sum rule entirely (no weight is left to allocate to other excitations). It is a quantitative expression of what was stated in Chapter 11, that “the long-wavelength plasmon almost monopolizes the oscillator strength.”

(4) Its use in the absolute calibration of electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS) spectra. The raw EELS data are detector counts, and the intensity is obtained only in arbitrary units. However, suppose that, after completing the angular integration and the removal of multiple scattering (Section 12.2, the Fourier-log method of Example 12.6, and so on), we have obtained a spectrum $I(\omega)=C\,\mathcal{L}(\omega)$ proportional to the loss function. Then the unknown calibration constant $C$ is fixed by the sum rule:

$$ \int_0^{\infty}\dd\omega\;\omega\,I(\omega) = C\cdot\frac{\pi}{2}\,\omega_p^2 \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad C = \frac{2}{\pi\omega_p^2}\int_0^{\infty}\dd\omega\;\omega\,I(\omega) $$

This is because $\omega_p^2=4\pi ne^2/m$ on the right-hand side can be calculated from the valence-electron density $n$ alone (known from the crystal structure and the valence). Once $\mathcal{L}$ is thereby fixed in absolute units, we can reconstruct $\mathrm{Re}\,\epsilon^{-1}$ by the Kramers–Kronig transform (Chapter 4), and even $\epsilon(\omega)$ itself. Conversely, if the measured first moment does not match $\frac{\pi}{2}\omega_p^2$, we should suspect one of two things: (i) the range of integration is insufficient (weight such as core excitations remains on the high-energy side), or (ii) the removal of multiple scattering is inadequate. The sum rule is both a tool for calibration and a device for checking the data processing.

Exercise 12.4: Comparing the electron gas and liquid He with Feynman's formula

Solution

(1) We substitute $S(q)=\hbar q/2mc$ for $q\to0$ of liquid $^4$He into Feynman's formula and show that the sound-wave dispersion results. Feynman's formula of Section 12.6 was

$$ \hbar\,\omega(q) = \frac{\hbar^2q^2}{2m\,S(q)} $$

Substituting $S(q)=\hbar q/2mc$, we obtain

$$ \hbar\,\omega(q) = \frac{\hbar^2q^2}{2m}\cdot\frac{2mc}{\hbar q} = \hbar c q \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \omega(q) = cq $$

The dispersion of a sound wave (phonon) with sound speed $c$ has come out correctly. As stated in Section 12.6.3, in this limit the weight of the excitations is concentrated in a single phonon, so the single-mode approximation becomes exact and Feynman's formula agrees with experiment.

(2) We read off the behavior of $S(q)$ in reverse from the fact that for the electron gas $\omega\to\omega_p$ (a finite gap) as $q\to0$. The intermediate result of the single-mode approximation (Step 2 of Derivation 16) was, from the two sum rules,

$$ \omega(q)\,S(q) = \frac{\hbar q^2}{2m} $$

Since $\omega(q)\to\omega_p$ (a constant independent of $q$) as $q\to0$,

$$ S(q) = \frac{\hbar q^2}{2m\,\omega_p} \;\propto\; q^2 \qquad(q\to0) $$

As we saw in Exercise 12.3(3), for $q\to0$ a single plasmon uses up the sum rule, so the single-mode approximation is justified in this limit, and this $q^2$ law is an exact property of the electron gas.

(3) We explain the difference between $S\propto q$ (He) and $S\propto q^2$ (electron gas) in terms of the range of the interaction. The chain of reasoning goes as follows.

  • Neutral $^4$He: The interatomic force is short-range. The cost of creating a long-wavelength density fluctuation is determined by the bulk modulus alone and goes to zero as $q\to0$—a gapless sound wave $\omega=cq$ is the lowest excitation. Because the excitation is “cheap,” long-wavelength density fluctuations can exist to a fair extent as zero-point oscillations, and $S(q)=\hbar q/2mc$ vanishes only linearly in $q$.
  • Electron gas: The Coulomb force is long-range, and $v(q)=4\pi e^2/q^2$ diverges as $q\to0$. A long-wavelength density fluctuation means a macroscopic charge imbalance, and no matter how far we stretch the wavelength it costs a finite energy $\hbar\omega_p$ (Chapter 11: the plasma gap). Because the excitation is “expensive,” long-wavelength fluctuations are strongly suppressed, and $S(q)=\hbar q^2/2m\omega_p$ goes to zero quadratically in $q$, faster than in He.

In other words, the power with which $S(q)$ vanishes reflects whether the lowest excitation has a gap ($\omega\to\omega_p$) or not ($\omega=cq$), and the presence or absence of that gap is in turn a consequence of the range of the interaction (whether or not $v(q)$ diverges as $q\to0$). Feynman's formula shows us this correspondence in the single formula $S(q)=\hbar q^2/2m\omega(q)$.

(4) We evaluate $S(q)$ numerically for Al ($\hbar\omega_p=15.8\ \mathrm{eV}$) at $q=0.3\ \text{Å}^{-1}$. Writing the formula of (2) in terms of the recoil energy gives

$$ S(q) = \frac{\hbar^2q^2/2m_e}{\hbar\omega_p} $$

The recoil energy in the numerator, with $q=0.3\ \text{Å}^{-1}=3\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$, is

$$ \frac{\hbar^2q^2}{2m_e} = \frac{(1.0546\times10^{-34})^2\times(3\times10^{9})^2}{2\times9.109\times10^{-31}} = \frac{1.1122\times10^{-68}\times9\times10^{18}}{1.8219\times10^{-30}} = 5.49\times10^{-20}\ \mathrm{J} = 0.343\ \mathrm{eV} $$

Therefore

$$ S(q) = \frac{0.343}{15.8} = 0.022 $$

Physical meaning. Recall that $S=1$ was the reference value for “uncorrelated, independent particles” (Section 12.6.1). The value $0.022$ is then about $1$ part in $50$ of that. Density fluctuations with a wavelength of $2\pi/q\simeq21\ \text{Å}$ are hardly allowed to exist in an electron gas. This is because the long-range Coulomb force thoroughly suppresses long-wavelength charge fluctuations; in this sense the electron gas is extremely “stiff” against long wavelengths. This contrasts with the fact that, even in the same limit $q\to0$, the $S(q)$ of liquid He vanishes only linearly in $q$.

Exercise 12.5: Choosing a probe

Solution

For each measurement we choose the probe on two criteria: (i) what the probe must couple to, and (ii) whether the kinematics (Figure 12.2: the accessible region of the scan, determined by the probe's $M$ and $E_i$) and the resolution reach the $(q,\omega)$ of the quantity to be measured.

(1) The acoustic phonon dispersion of single-crystal Si out to the zone boundary ($q\simeq1\ \text{Å}^{-1}$, $\hbar\omega\simeq50\ \mathrm{meV}$)—neutrons (inelastic neutron scattering, triple-axis spectrometer).

Phonons are collective motions of the atomic nuclei, so neutrons, which couple to the nuclei, are the natural choice. (X-rays are also possible in principle, since the electron density follows the nuclei, but one would need an instrument with a relative resolution of $10^{-7}$ to cut $\mathrm{meV}$ out of $10\ \mathrm{keV}$.) Let us check the kinematics. As in Example 12.1, neutrons have $E_i=2.07\,k^2\ \mathrm{meV}$ ($k$ in units of $\text{Å}^{-1}$), so putting $q=1\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ and $\hbar\omega=50\ \mathrm{meV}$ into the upper limit of the accessible region, $\hbar\omega\le E_i\,\frac{q}{k}\left(2-\frac{q}{k}\right)=2.07\left(2qk-q^2\right)\ \mathrm{meV}$, requires $k\ge12.6\ \text{Å}^{-1}$, that is, $E_i\ge327\ \mathrm{meV}$—thermal neutrons ($\sim25\ \mathrm{meV}$) cannot deliver $50\ \mathrm{meV}$ at $q=1\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ in the first zone. In a crystal, however, the condition for one-phonon scattering is $\qq=\bm G+\kk_{\text{reduced}}$ (Section 12.8.3), so we can simply measure in a higher Brillouin zone. For example, for $q=3\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ we need only $k\ge5.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$, $E_i\ge63\ \mathrm{meV}$, which thermal-to-epithermal neutrons from a reactor easily reach. In actual measurement planning, one exploits the fact that the upper-limit parabola in Figure 12.2 is maximal at $q=k$ and escapes to a zone with large $q$. Raman/infrared sees only $q\simeq0$ and so cannot measure the dispersion, and EELS has marginal resolution for $\mathrm{meV}$-scale phonons.

(2) The localized surface plasmon resonance ($\hbar\omega\simeq2.4\ \mathrm{eV}$) of a single gold nanoparticle of diameter $20\ \mathrm{nm}$, resolved by position within the particle—electron-beam EELS (STEM-EELS).

The deciding factor is spatial resolution. As Table 12.1 shows, the only probe with $\mathrm{nm}$ spatial resolution is a focused electron beam, which can map not just a single particle but where within the particle (the tip or the center) the resonance is strong. Visible light ($\lambda\simeq500\ \mathrm{nm}$) cannot single out a $20\ \mathrm{nm}$ particle because of the diffraction limit. In terms of energy too, $2.4\ \mathrm{eV}$ is a minuscule loss for a $100\ \mathrm{keV}$ electron, so the kinematic constraint poses no problem, and it can be resolved well by instruments equipped with a monochromator (resolution $5$–$100\ \mathrm{meV}$). Note also that the quantity measured is not $S$ but the loss function (for nanoparticles, the electromagnetic response including the boundary conditions) (Section 12.5), and that, because this is a surface mode of the particle, a localized mode at $2$–$3\ \mathrm{eV}$ appears rather than the volume plasmon $\omega_p$ (a relative of $\omega_s$ in Section 12.8).

(3) The magnon dispersion of an antiferromagnet—neutrons.

Magnons are collective precessions of the electron spins and hardly show up in the density. The probe that couples directly to the spin (magnetic moment) through the dipole–dipole interaction is the neutron (Table 12.1: for magnetic scattering it measures $S_{\text{spin}}$). The kinematics is the same as for phonons: the $(q,\omega)$ window of thermal neutrons overlaps neatly with both the magnon energies ($\mathrm{meV}$ to a few tens of $\mathrm{meV}$) and the Brillouin zone ($\sim\text{Å}^{-1}$). Resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS) can also see magnetic excitations, but for quantitative measurements at the $\mathrm{meV}$ level neutrons are the standard (as the note to Table 12.1 says). In an antiferromagnet, magnon branches rise from the magnetic Bragg points, so we scan around them with a triple-axis spectrometer.

(4) The acoustic mode of a liquid under high pressure in a diamond anvil cell (sample diameter $50\ \mu\mathrm{m}$)—inelastic X-ray scattering (IXS).

This is an example in which the process of elimination works clearly. Because the neutron prefactor $b^2$ is small, neutrons require a sample volume of the $\mathrm{cm^3}$ class (Table 12.1), and with a $50\ \mu\mathrm{m}$ sample the counts are nowhere near sufficient. Electron beams can be used only for thin-film samples in vacuum and cannot pass through diamond. Hard X-rays ($\sim10\ \mathrm{keV}$) pass through the diamond anvil, and synchrotron radiation can be focused to the $\mu\mathrm{m}$ scale, so $S(\qq,\omega)$ can be measured even for a small sample. The acoustic mode of a liquid lies at $q\sim0.1$–$1\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ with $\hbar\omega\sim$ a few $\mathrm{meV}$ to a few tens of $\mathrm{meV}$, so we use the lower end of the IXS resolution range ($1$–$100\ \mathrm{meV}$). This is precisely a measurement where the entry in Table 12.1, “small samples suffice (even in a high-pressure cell),” pays off.

(5) The shape of the Fermi surface and the $\kk$ dependence of the superconducting gap in a high-temperature superconductor—angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES).

The Fermi surface is “where in $\kk$ space the occupied one-electron states terminate,” and the superconducting gap $\Delta(\kk)$ is the shift of the edge of the single-particle excitation spectrum. Both are information contained in the single-particle spectral function $A(\kk,\omega)$ and do not appear directly in the density–density correlation $S(\qq,\omega)$. Only ARPES, which kicks one electron out of the system and measures its $\kk$ and $\omega$, directly shows us $A(\kk,\omega)$ resolved in $\kk$ (a resolution of $1$–$10\ \mathrm{meV}$ is ample for a gap of several tens of $\mathrm{meV}$). However, as the warning in Section 12.7 says, this is a measurement of a different kind from $S(\qq,\omega)$, the protagonist of this chapter—“$S$ shakes the system without destroying it. ARPES pulls out one electron.” Also, since ARPES is surface-sensitive, a clean surface cleaved in ultrahigh vacuum is required.

To summarize, the results are as in the following table.

Quantity to be measuredProbe chosenDeciding factor
(1) Phonon dispersion of Si (out to the zone boundary)NeutronsThe orders of magnitude of $q,\omega$ match; the kinematic constraint is avoided in a higher zone
(2) Plasmon of a single gold nanoparticleSTEM-EELSOnly electron beams give $\mathrm{nm}$ spatial resolution
(3) Magnon dispersionNeutronsCouple directly to spin, and cover the whole zone with $\mathrm{meV}$ precision
(4) Acoustic mode of a liquid in a high-pressure cellX-rays (IXS)Pass through the cell, and a $\mu\mathrm{m}$ sample suffices
(5) Fermi surface and superconducting gap $\Delta(\kk)$ARPESBecause they are information in the single-particle spectral function $A(\kk,\omega)$

The common lesson is this. “What we want to measure” translates into two things—“the correlation of which operator” and “where in the $(q,\omega)$ plane”—and once these two are fixed, the probe is almost uniquely determined. The kinematics of this chapter (Figure 12.2) and Table 12.1 are the translation table for this.

Chapter 13: Matsubara Green's Functions and Analytic Continuation

Exercise 13.1: Deriving $\mathcal{G}^0$ for bosons

Solution

(1) We show that the annihilation operator evolved in imaginary time, $b_\kk(\tau)=\ee^{\hat K\tau/\hbar}b_\kk\ee^{-\hat K\tau/\hbar}$, becomes $b_\kk\,\ee^{-\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar}$. The starting point is the imaginary-time equation of motion derived in Section 13.2 (read with $\Ham\to\hat K$):

$$ \hbar\frac{\dd b_\kk(\tau)}{\dd\tau} = \left[\hat K,\,b_\kk(\tau)\right] $$

First we compute the commutator $[\hat K,b_\kk]$. Since $\hat K=\sum_l\xi_l\,b^\dagger_l b_l$,

$$ [\hat K,\,b_\kk] = \sum_l\xi_l\left[b^\dagger_l b_l,\,b_\kk\right] $$

For boson operators, instead of the anticommutator identity used in the Fermi case, we use an identity that can be written with commutators alone:

$$ [\hat X\hat Y,\hat Z] = \hat X[\hat Y,\hat Z]+[\hat X,\hat Z]\hat Y $$

(This is verified immediately by expanding both sides.) Taking $\hat X=b^\dagger_l$, $\hat Y=b_l$ and $\hat Z=b_\kk$, and using the commutation relations $[b_l,b_\kk]=0$ and $[b_\kk,b^\dagger_l]=\delta_{kl}$ (and hence $[b^\dagger_l,b_\kk]=-\delta_{lk}$), we obtain

$$ \left[b^\dagger_l b_l,\,b_\kk\right] = b^\dagger_l\underbrace{[b_l,b_\kk]}_{=0}+\underbrace{[b^\dagger_l,b_\kk]}_{=-\delta_{lk}}b_l = -\delta_{lk}\,b_l $$

Hence $[\hat K,b_\kk]=-\xi_\kk b_\kk$. The annihilation operator is an “eigenoperator” of $\hat K$: taking the commutator returns $-\xi_\kk$ times the operator itself—this structure is exactly the same as in the Fermi case. Sandwiching this relation between $\ee^{\hat K\tau/\hbar}(\cdots)\ee^{-\hat K\tau/\hbar}$ gives $[\hat K,b_\kk(\tau)]=-\xi_\kk b_\kk(\tau)$, so the equation of motion can be solved as

$$ \hbar\frac{\dd b_\kk(\tau)}{\dd\tau} = -\xi_\kk\,b_\kk(\tau) \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad b_\kk(\tau) = b_\kk\,\ee^{-\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar} $$

(with the initial condition $b_\kk(0)=b_\kk$). Similarly, since $[\hat K,b^\dagger_\kk]=+\xi_\kk b^\dagger_\kk$, we have

$$ \bar b_\kk(\tau) \equiv \ee^{\hat K\tau/\hbar}\,b^\dagger_\kk\,\ee^{-\hat K\tau/\hbar} = b^\dagger_\kk\,\ee^{+\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar} $$

The statistics (commutation or anticommutation) matter only in the intermediate step of evaluating the commutator; the final differential equation and its solution take the same form as in the Fermi case.

(2) We write out $\mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau)=-\braket{T_\tau\,b_\kk(\tau)b^\dagger_\kk(0)}$ separately for positive and negative $\tau$. Since $b_\kk$ contains no fermion operators, $\zeta=+1$, and the definition of Section 13.3 written out with $\theta$ functions reads

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau) = -\theta(\tau)\braket{b_\kk(\tau)\,b^\dagger_\kk} -\theta(-\tau)\braket{b^\dagger_\kk\,b_\kk(\tau)} $$

(Since $\zeta=+1$, the sign of the second term remains minus.)

The case $0<\tau<\beta\hbar$. Substituting the result of (1), we obtain

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau) = -\ee^{-\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar}\braket{b_\kk\,b^\dagger_\kk} $$

By the commutation relation, $b_\kk b^\dagger_\kk = 1+b^\dagger_\kk b_\kk$, and the occupation number of free bosons is $\braket{b^\dagger_\kk b_\kk}=n_B(\xi_\kk)\equiv\left(\ee^{\beta\xi_\kk}-1\right)^{-1}$, so $\braket{b_\kk b^\dagger_\kk}=1+n_B(\xi_\kk)$. Therefore

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau) = -\left[1+n_B(\xi_\kk)\right]\ee^{-\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar} \qquad(0<\tau<\beta\hbar) $$

The case $-\beta\hbar<\tau<0$. The second term survives, giving

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau) = -\braket{b^\dagger_\kk\,b_\kk(\tau)} = -\,n_B(\xi_\kk)\,\ee^{-\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar} \qquad(-\beta\hbar<\tau<0) $$

Both agree with the expressions in the hint. The difference from the Fermi case is the following: there, $\zeta=-1$ changed the sign on the $\tau<0$ side to plus ($+n_F\,\ee^{-\xi\tau/\hbar}$), whereas for bosons the sign remains minus in both regions. Note also that the assumption $\xi_\kk>0$ (the chemical potential lies below the bottom of the band) keeps $n_B(\xi_\kk)$ positive and finite. As $\xi_\kk\to0^+$, $n_B$ diverges. This is a sign of Bose–Einstein condensation, where this treatment of free particles itself must be modified.

(3) We verify the periodicity $\mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau+\beta\hbar)=+\mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau)$ for $-\beta\hbar<\tau<0$ directly from the expressions in (2). Since $\tau+\beta\hbar$ lies in $0<\tau+\beta\hbar<\beta\hbar$, we use the expression of (2) for $\tau>0$:

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau+\beta\hbar) = -\left[1+n_B\right]\ee^{-\xi_\kk(\tau+\beta\hbar)/\hbar} = -\left[1+n_B\right]\ee^{-\beta\xi_\kk}\;\ee^{-\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar} $$

Here we verify an identity for the Bose distribution:

$$ n_B\,\ee^{\beta\xi} = \frac{\ee^{\beta\xi}}{\ee^{\beta\xi}-1} = \frac{\left(\ee^{\beta\xi}-1\right)+1}{\ee^{\beta\xi}-1} = 1+n_B $$

That is, $\left[1+n_B\right]\ee^{-\beta\xi}=n_B$. Substituting this, we find

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau+\beta\hbar) = -\,n_B\,\ee^{-\xi_\kk\tau/\hbar} = +\,\mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\tau) $$

so the function is indeed periodic. The corresponding identity in the Fermi case is $n_F\,\ee^{\beta\xi}=1-n_F$, whose right-hand side has “$1-$” instead of “$1+$”. This one-character difference was the true origin of the antiperiodicity (the sign reversal). The (anti)periodicity of Theorem 13.1 may be said to be this identity for the distribution function translated into the language of imaginary time.

(4) We Fourier transform with the bosonic Matsubara frequencies $\omega_n=2n\pi/\beta\hbar$ and check that the result has the same form as in the Fermi case. In the transform of Section 13.5, $\mathcal{G}(\ii\omega_n)=\int_0^{\beta\hbar}\dd\tau\,\ee^{\ii\omega_n\tau}\mathcal{G}(\tau)$, we substitute the expression of (2) for $\tau>0$:

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\ii\omega_n) = -\left[1+n_B\right]\int_0^{\beta\hbar}\dd\tau\;\ee^{\left(\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar\right)\tau} $$

With $a\equiv\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar$, the exponential integral is

$$ \int_0^{\beta\hbar}\dd\tau\;\ee^{a\tau} = \frac{\ee^{a\beta\hbar}-1}{a}, \qquad \ee^{a\beta\hbar} = \underbrace{\ee^{\ii\omega_n\beta\hbar}}_{=\,+1\ \text{(bosonic)}}\cdot\;\ee^{-\beta\xi_\kk} = +\,\ee^{-\beta\xi_\kk} $$

The factor $\ee^{\ii\omega_n\beta\hbar}$, which was the crux of the Fermi calculation, is plus one for the bosonic frequencies. Therefore

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\ii\omega_n) = -\left[1+n_B\right]\frac{\ee^{-\beta\xi_\kk}-1}{\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar} $$

and the intermediate form given in the hint has appeared. What remains is to collapse the numerator. Since $1+n_B=\dfrac{\ee^{\beta\xi}}{\ee^{\beta\xi}-1}=\dfrac{1}{1-\ee^{-\beta\xi}}$, we have

$$ \left[1+n_B\right]\left(\ee^{-\beta\xi}-1\right) = \frac{\ee^{-\beta\xi}-1}{1-\ee^{-\beta\xi}} = -1 $$

This cancels against the overall minus sign, and the numerator becomes exactly $1$:

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\ii\omega_n) = \frac{1}{\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar} $$

This has exactly the same form as in the Fermi case.

Check. The inverse transform at $\tau=0^-$ should give the bosonic Matsubara sum listed in the second row of Table 13.2 (read with $\omega_0=\xi_\kk/\hbar$):

$$ \frac{1}{\beta\hbar}\sum_n\frac{\ee^{\ii\omega_n\eta}}{\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar} = -\,n_B(\xi_\kk) $$

In fact, this agrees with $\mathcal{G}^0(\kk,0^-)=-n_B(\xi_\kk)$ obtained in (2), which confirms that the $\tau$ representation, the $\ii\omega_n$ representation and the Matsubara sum are consistent (note also that the sign differs from $\mathcal{G}^0(\kk,0^-)=+n_F(\xi_\kk)$ in the Fermi case).

Where does the difference in statistics remain?

The functional form of the propagator, $\left(\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar\right)^{-1}$, is exactly the same for bosons and fermions. The occupation numbers $n_B$ and $n_F$ always cancel and disappear in the end. The difference in statistics survives only in where the argument $\ii\omega_n$ stands on the imaginary axis—whether it is of the even type (including $0$) or the odd type (not including $0$). That is precisely why the statistics return when we carry out a Matsubara sum, in the form of “which distribution function to multiply by” and “whether $\ee^{\ii\omega_n\beta\hbar}$ is $+1$ or $-1$” (this actually matters in Exercise 13.3). A distinctive feature of the Matsubara formalism is that the statistics reside not in the functional form but in the domain.

Exercise 13.2: Carrying out a Matsubara sum with the residue theorem

Solution

(1) We find the poles and residues of $f(z)=\left[(z-\xi_1/\hbar)(z-\xi_2/\hbar)\right]^{-1}$ and explain why no convergence factor is needed. The poles are the zeros of the denominator

$$ z = \frac{\xi_1}{\hbar}, \qquad z = \frac{\xi_2}{\hbar} $$

There are two of them, and since $\xi_1\neq\xi_2$ both are simple poles. The residue at a simple pole is obtained by “removing the factor that gives the pole and evaluating the rest at that point”:

$$ \Res_{z=\xi_1/\hbar}f = \frac{1}{\xi_1/\hbar-\xi_2/\hbar} = \frac{\hbar}{\xi_1-\xi_2}, \qquad \Res_{z=\xi_2/\hbar}f = \frac{\hbar}{\xi_2-\xi_1} $$

The two residues have opposite signs and their sum is zero (a manifestation of the general rule that the sum of residues vanishes for a function that, as $\abs{z}\to\infty$, behaves as $f\sim1/z^2$).

Why no convergence factor is needed. The function $f(z)=\left(z-\xi/\hbar\right)^{-1}$ used when we derived $n_F$ in Section 13.7 decays only as $1/\abs{z}$, and the sum was conditionally convergent, so a convergence factor $\ee^{\ii\omega_n0^+}$ (which specifies the ordering $\tau=0^-$) was needed. The present $f$ decays as $\abs{f}\sim1/\abs{z}^2$ as $\abs{z}\to\infty$, faster than $1/\abs{z}$. The hypotheses of Theorem 13.2 are satisfied as they stand, and the contribution of the large circle $C_R$ vanishes automatically as $R\to\infty$, so there is no need to multiply by a convergence factor. The sum itself is also absolutely convergent, since each term behaves as $\sim1/\omega_n^2$ as $n\to\pm\infty$.

(2) We apply Theorem 13.2 (fermionic type) to put $S$ in closed form. The poles of $f$ lie on the real axis and do not coincide with the Matsubara frequencies on the imaginary axis, so all the hypotheses of the theorem are satisfied:

$$ S = \frac{1}{\beta\hbar}\sum_n f(\ii\omega_n) = \sum_{j}\Res_{z=z_j}\left[n_F(z)\,f(z)\right] = n_F\!\left(\frac{\xi_1}{\hbar}\right)\frac{\hbar}{\xi_1-\xi_2} + n_F\!\left(\frac{\xi_2}{\hbar}\right)\frac{\hbar}{\xi_2-\xi_1} $$

Here $n_F(z)=\left(\ee^{\beta\hbar z}+1\right)^{-1}$ is a function of the frequency variable, so its value at $z=\xi_1/\hbar$ is

$$ n_F\!\left(\frac{\xi_1}{\hbar}\right) = \frac{1}{\ee^{\beta\hbar\cdot\xi_1/\hbar}+1} = \frac{1}{\ee^{\beta\xi_1}+1} = n_F(\xi_1) $$

which returns directly to the Fermi distribution in the energy variable (and likewise for $\xi_2$). Factoring out $\xi_1-\xi_2$ from the two terms, we obtain

$$ S = \frac{\hbar\left[n_F(\xi_1)-n_F(\xi_2)\right]}{\xi_1-\xi_2} $$

which agrees with the formula stated in the problem. Check. The original sum is invariant under the interchange $\xi_1\leftrightarrow\xi_2$, and the result obtained is also invariant (the numerator and denominator change sign simultaneously). Also, as $T\to\infty$, $n_F\to1/2$, so the numerator vanishes and $S\to0$. At high temperature the spacing $2\pi/\beta\hbar$ of the Matsubara frequencies becomes large and every $\ii\omega_n$ moves away from the poles, so it is reasonable that the sum becomes small.

(3) We take the limit $\xi_2\to\xi_1$. The result of (2) has the form of a difference quotient, so the limit is just the definition of the derivative:

$$ S = \hbar\;\frac{n_F(\xi_1)-n_F(\xi_2)}{\xi_1-\xi_2} \;\xrightarrow[\;\xi_2\to\xi_1\;]{}\; \hbar\,\frac{\partial n_F(\xi_1)}{\partial\xi_1} $$

This is the formula in the fourth row of Table 13.2. Writing out the derivative,

$$ \frac{\partial n_F}{\partial\xi} = \frac{\partial}{\partial\xi}\frac{1}{\ee^{\beta\xi}+1} = -\frac{\beta\,\ee^{\beta\xi}}{\left(\ee^{\beta\xi}+1\right)^2} = -\frac{\beta}{4\cosh^2(\beta\xi/2)} $$

The derivative is always negative, forming a valley of width $\sim k_BT$ centered on $\xi=0$ (the Fermi surface).

Check—direct calculation as a pole of order 2. We may also apply Theorem 13.2 to $f(z)=\left(z-\xi_1/\hbar\right)^{-2}$, obtained by setting $\xi_2=\xi_1$. By the residue formula at a pole of order 2, $\Res_{z=z_0}\left[g(z)(z-z_0)^{-2}\right]=g'(z_0)$, we have

$$ S = \Res_{z=\xi_1/\hbar}\left[\frac{n_F(z)}{(z-\xi_1/\hbar)^2}\right] = \left.\frac{\dd n_F(z)}{\dd z}\right|_{z=\xi_1/\hbar} = -\frac{\beta\hbar\,\ee^{\beta\xi_1}}{\left(\ee^{\beta\xi_1}+1\right)^2} = \hbar\,\frac{\partial n_F(\xi_1)}{\partial\xi_1} $$

which agrees with the result of the limiting procedure. This confirms that interchanging the order of the limit and the residue calculation does not change the answer.

(4) We describe the behavior as $T\to0$ and its physical interpretation. The derivative of (3), $-\partial n_F/\partial\xi=\beta/\left[4\cosh^2(\beta\xi/2)\right]$, is a peak of height $\beta/4$ and width $\sim k_BT$, and its area, independent of temperature, is

$$ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd\xi\;\frac{\beta}{4\cosh^2(\beta\xi/2)} = \left[\frac{1}{2}\tanh\frac{\beta\xi}{2}\right]_{-\infty}^{\infty} = 1 $$

As $T\to0$ ($\beta\to\infty$), the peak becomes infinitely high and sharp and, keeping its area equal to 1, concentrates at $\xi=0$, so we obtain

$$ \frac{\partial n_F}{\partial\xi} \;\longrightarrow\; -\,\delta(\xi), \qquad S \;\longrightarrow\; -\,\hbar\,\delta(\xi_1) $$

Physical meaning. $S$ is a weight expressing “how much a one-particle state of energy $\xi_1$ can respond to a static, uniform perturbation” (it corresponds to the Matsubara sum of Exercise 13.3 with $\qq\to0$ and $\Omega_m=0$ set). At $T=0$, the states deeper than the Fermi surface have their destinations blocked by the Pauli principle, and the states above the Fermi surface are empty to begin with. The only states that can respond are those exactly on the Fermi surface ($\xi_1=0$), and that is the meaning of $\delta(\xi_1)$. Multiplying by the density of states $g(\epsilon)$ and integrating over the energy gives

$$ \int\dd\epsilon\;g(\epsilon)\,\frac{\partial n_F}{\partial\epsilon} \;\xrightarrow[\;T\to0\;]{}\; -\,g(\epsilon_F) = -N(\epsilon_F) $$

This connects to the $T=0$ version of the static long-wavelength limit $\chi^0(\qq\to0,0)=-\partial n/\partial\mu$ of Section 13.9, that is, to the reason why $q_{TF}^2\propto N(\epsilon_F)$ in Thomas–Fermi screening (Chapter 10).

Exercise 13.3: Deriving the finite-temperature Lindhard function in the Matsubara formalism

Solution

(1) We carry out the Matsubara sum inside the bubble diagram by the same residue method as in Exercise 13.2. Substituting $\mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\ii\omega_n)=\left(\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar\right)^{-1}$, derived in Section 13.6, the sum to be carried out is

$$ S_\kk(\ii\Omega_m) \equiv \frac{1}{\beta\hbar}\sum_n \frac{1}{\left(\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar\right)\left(\ii\omega_n+\ii\Omega_m-\xi_{\kk+\qq}/\hbar\right)} $$

(here $\omega_n$ is the fermionic summation variable and $\Omega_m$ is the bosonic frequency given from outside). Letting $f(z)=\left[(z-z_1)(z-z_2)\right]^{-1}$, the poles are

$$ z_1 = \frac{\xi_\kk}{\hbar}, \qquad z_2 = \frac{\xi_{\kk+\qq}}{\hbar}-\ii\Omega_m $$

These are two simple poles. Since $\abs{f}\sim1/\abs{z}^2$, no convergence factor is needed, for the same reason as in Exercise 13.2 (1). The assumption of Theorem 13.2, “$f$ has no singularities on the Matsubara frequencies,” is also satisfied. This is obvious for $z_1$, which lies on the real axis, and it holds for $z_2$ because its imaginary part $-\Omega_m$ is an even multiple of $\pi/\beta\hbar$ whereas the imaginary part of a fermionic Matsubara frequency is an odd multiple of $\pi/\beta\hbar$, so the two never coincide (this is the first place where the bosonic nature of the external frequency takes effect). The residues are

$$ \Res_{z=z_1}f = \frac{1}{z_1-z_2} = \frac{1}{\ii\Omega_m+\left(\xi_\kk-\xi_{\kk+\qq}\right)/\hbar}, \qquad \Res_{z=z_2}f = \frac{1}{z_2-z_1} = -\,\frac{1}{\ii\Omega_m+\left(\xi_\kk-\xi_{\kk+\qq}\right)/\hbar} $$

By Theorem 13.2 (fermionic) we have

$$ S_\kk = n_F(z_1)\Res_{z_1}f + n_F(z_2)\Res_{z_2}f $$

As in Exercise 13.2, $n_F(z_1)$ reduces to $n_F(\xi_\kk)$. The problem is $n_F(z_2)$, whose argument is complex:

$$ n_F\!\left(\frac{\xi_{\kk+\qq}}{\hbar}-\ii\Omega_m\right) = \frac{1}{\ee^{\beta\hbar\left(\xi_{\kk+\qq}/\hbar-\ii\Omega_m\right)}+1} = \frac{1}{\ee^{\beta\xi_{\kk+\qq}}\,\ee^{-\ii\beta\hbar\Omega_m}+1} $$

This is the second—and the essential—place where the bosonic nature of the external frequency is decisive. Since $\Omega_m$ is bosonic, $\ee^{\ii\Omega_m\beta\hbar}=+1$, and hence its inverse is also $\ee^{-\ii\beta\hbar\Omega_m}=+1$, so

$$ n_F\!\left(\frac{\xi_{\kk+\qq}}{\hbar}-\ii\Omega_m\right) = \frac{1}{\ee^{\beta\xi_{\kk+\qq}}+1} = n_F(\xi_{\kk+\qq}) $$

and we return to the ordinary Fermi distribution. To summarize,

$$ S_\kk(\ii\Omega_m) = \frac{n_F(\xi_\kk)-n_F(\xi_{\kk+\qq})}{\ii\Omega_m+\left(\xi_\kk-\xi_{\kk+\qq}\right)/\hbar} $$

(this is exactly the third row of Table 13.2).

What if we compute while taking $\Omega_m$ to be fermionic?

Suppose we mistakenly set $\ee^{-\ii\beta\hbar\Omega_m}=-1$ (the fermionic case). Then the distribution function above becomes

$$ \frac{1}{-\ee^{\beta\xi_{\kk+\qq}}+1} = -\,\frac{1}{\ee^{\beta\xi_{\kk+\qq}}-1} = -\,n_B(\xi_{\kk+\qq}) $$

so it turns into a Bose distribution (with a minus sign, at that) and gives a completely different answer. This one line of the residue calculation supports the rule for adding frequencies, “fermionic $+$ bosonic $=$ fermionic” (stated in Section 13.10 as a vertex rule for Feynman diagrams). The density operator $c^\dagger c$ is a product of two fermion operators and is therefore bosonic, so the external frequency of the density response is always bosonic—if this consistency is broken, the whole calculation collapses. Note also that there are situations in which a sum of the same form is needed with a fermionic external frequency (for example, the self-energy of the electron–phonon system). There one does obtain a result in which $n_F$ and $n_B$ are mixed.

Solution (continued)

(2) Putting the result of (1) back into the full expression, we derive the form stated in the problem.

$$ \chi^0(\qq,\ii\Omega_m) = \frac{2}{\hbar V}\sum_\kk S_\kk(\ii\Omega_m) = \frac{2}{\hbar V}\sum_\kk \frac{n_F(\xi_\kk)-n_F(\xi_{\kk+\qq})}{\ii\Omega_m+\left(\xi_\kk-\xi_{\kk+\qq}\right)/\hbar} $$

Multiplying the numerator and denominator by $\hbar$ to absorb the prefactor $1/\hbar$, we obtain

$$ \chi^0(\qq,\ii\Omega_m) = \frac{2}{V}\sum_\kk \frac{n_F(\xi_\kk)-n_F(\xi_{\kk+\qq})}{\ii\hbar\Omega_m+\xi_\kk-\xi_{\kk+\qq}} = \frac{2}{V}\sum_\kk \frac{n_F(\xi_\kk)-n_F(\xi_{\kk+\qq})}{\ii\hbar\Omega_m+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}} $$

In the last equality we used $\xi_\kk-\xi_{\kk+\qq}=(\epsilon_\kk-\mu)-(\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\mu)=\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}$, that is, the chemical potential cancels within the difference ($\mu$ remains in the arguments of the distribution functions). This is the expression required in the problem.

(3) We check that analytic continuation reproduces the Lindhard function of Chapter 10. Before continuing, we examine the conditions of Theorem 13.4. Extending $\ii\Omega_m$ to a complex variable $z$ (frequency) gives

$$ \chi^0(\qq,z) = \frac{2}{V}\sum_\kk \frac{n_F(\xi_\kk)-n_F(\xi_{\kk+\qq})}{\hbar z+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}} $$

Each term of this sum has singularities only on the real axis, at $z=\left(\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk\right)/\hbar$ (the $n_F$ in the numerator is now a function of the real energy, a constant independent of $z$), is analytic in the upper half-plane, decays as $1/\abs{z}$ for $\abs{z}\to\infty$, and contains neither $\abs{\;}$ nor $\mathrm{sgn}$. Hence we may mechanically replace $\ii\hbar\Omega_m\to\hbar\omega+\ii\eta$, which gives

$$ \chi^0(\qq,\omega) = \frac{2}{V}\sum_\kk \frac{n_F(\xi_\kk)-n_F(\xi_{\kk+\qq})}{\hbar\omega+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\ii\eta} $$

This is the Lindhard function of Chapter 10, $\chi^0(\qq,\omega)=\dfrac{2}{V}\displaystyle\sum_\kk\dfrac{n_\kk-n_{\kk+\qq}}{\hbar\omega+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\ii\eta}$, with the occupation number replaced by the thermal average, $n_\kk\to n_F(\xi_\kk)$; that is, it is exactly the finite-temperature Lindhard function obtained in Section 13.9 by way of the Kubo formula. Through an independent route—the Matsubara formalism (bubble diagram + Matsubara sum + analytic continuation)—we have arrived at the same expression.

(4) We explain why it must be “Matsubara sum first, analytic continuation afterward.” The continuation variable $z$ is the extension of $\ii\Omega_m$. Viewing each term before the sum

$$ \mathcal{G}^0(\kk,\ii\omega_n)\,\mathcal{G}^0(\kk+\qq,\ii\omega_n+z) = \frac{1}{\ii\omega_n-\xi_\kk/\hbar}\cdot\frac{1}{z-\left(\xi_{\kk+\qq}/\hbar-\ii\omega_n\right)} $$

as a function of $z$, we see that it has a pole at $z=\xi_{\kk+\qq}/\hbar-\ii\omega_n$. Its imaginary part is $-\omega_n$, so for the terms with $\omega_n<0$ the pole lies in the upper half-plane. In other words, term by term, analyticity in the upper half-plane (Condition 1 of Theorem 13.4) is violated, and the operation “continue uniquely as a function in the upper half-plane” cannot be justified term by term. Only after the sum is taken over all $n$ do these poles disappear and the singularities gather on the real axis alone (as we confirmed in (3)), giving a function that satisfies the assumptions of Theorem 13.4. What we may continue is only the quantity after the sum has been taken.

Put in terms of the feel of the calculation, the same point reads as follows. The crux of (1) was the identity peculiar to discrete values, $\ee^{-\ii\beta\hbar\Omega_m}=1$. Written in terms of the continuation variable, this factor is the value of $\ee^{-\beta\hbar z}$ at $z=\ii\Omega_m$; if we first set $z=\omega+\ii\eta$, it turns into $\ee^{-\beta\hbar\omega}$, a quantity that is not $\pm1$, so this identity cannot be used and the whole framework of the residue calculation breaks down. We first do everything that must be done in the discrete world (the Matsubara sum) and only then pass to the continuous world (real frequencies)—this is the meaning of “sum first, continuation afterward.”

Exercise 13.4: When is analytic continuation not unique?

Solution

(1) We show that $h(z)=\ee^{\beta\hbar z}+1$ vanishes at every fermionic Matsubara frequency. Substituting $z=\ii\omega_n$, $\omega_n=(2n+1)\pi/\beta\hbar$, the exponent is $\beta\hbar\cdot\ii\omega_n=\ii(2n+1)\pi$, so

$$ h(\ii\omega_n) = \ee^{\ii(2n+1)\pi}+1 = \left(\ee^{\ii\pi}\right)^{2n+1}+1 = (-1)^{2n+1}+1 = -1+1 = 0 $$

Since $2n+1$ is odd whether $n$ is positive or negative, it is zero for every $n$. Note also that $h(z)$ is itself the denominator of the Fermi distribution; this is the flip side of the fact that the poles of $n_F(z)=1/h(z)$ sit at the fermionic Matsubara frequencies (Section 13.7).

(2) We explain why the analytic continuation is unique even though the data cannot distinguish $\chi$ from $\chi+c\,h$. First, note that $h(z)$ is analytic in the whole plane (an entire function). Hence Condition 1 of Theorem 13.4 (analyticity in the upper half-plane) alone cannot select between $\chi(z)$ and $\chi(z)+c\,h(z)$. What separates the two is Condition 2 (the decay condition). For a point $z=x+\ii y$ ($y>0$) in the upper half-plane, letting $x\to+\infty$, the reverse triangle inequality gives

$$ \abs{h(z)} = \abs{\ee^{\beta\hbar z}+1} \;\ge\; \ee^{\beta\hbar x}-1 \;\longrightarrow\; \infty $$

so $h$ diverges exponentially within the upper half-plane. On the other hand, the physical response function, by the spectral representation of Section 13.8,

$$ \chi(z) = \frac{1}{\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega'}{2\pi}\,\frac{\mathcal{A}(\omega')}{z-\omega'} \;\simeq\;\frac{\text{const}}{z} \qquad(\abs{z}\to\infty) $$

decays as $1/\abs{z}$ and satisfies Condition 2. Then $\chi+c\,h$ with $c\neq0$ violates Condition 2, so it cannot even get into the arena of “functions that satisfy Conditions 1 and 2 and take the given values on the Matsubara frequencies.” What guarantees that at most one function can get into the arena is Theorem 13.4 (Baym–Mermin): if there were two, their difference $\Delta$ would satisfy the conditions while vanishing at all Matsubara frequencies, and Carlson's theorem would give $\Delta\equiv0$. In short, uniqueness does not follow from the “data” alone. The triple “data + analyticity in the upper half-plane + $1/\abs{z}$ decay” is what makes the continuation unique. The decay condition is guaranteed by the physics (the existence of the spectral function), so in practice we may continue with confidence—but only after checking the conditions.

(3) We give the function that corresponds to $h$ in the bosonic case. The bosonic Matsubara frequencies are $\omega_n=2n\pi/\beta\hbar$, so the function that plays the same role is

$$ h_B(z) = \ee^{\beta\hbar z}-1, \qquad h_B(\ii\omega_n) = \ee^{2\ii n\pi}-1 = 1-1 = 0 $$

(it vanishes also at the point $z=0$, that is, $n=0$). This is the denominator of the Bose distribution $n_B(z)=1/h_B(z)$, and, exactly as in (2), it diverges exponentially as $\mathrm{Re}\,z\to+\infty$, so it is excluded by Condition 2.

(4) The replacement $\ii\omega_n\to\omega+\ii\eta$ may be applied as it stands only to (a). We explain the reasons in turn.

Why (a) can be continued. The right-hand side is already written as the value at $z=\ii\omega_n$ of the complex function $a/(z-\omega_1)$. This function is analytic in the upper half-plane because its only pole $z=\omega_1$ lies on the real axis (Condition 1), decays as $\abs{a}/\abs{z}$ for $\abs{z}\to\infty$ (Condition 2), and contains neither $\abs{\;}$ nor $\mathrm{sgn}$. Since the conditions of Theorem 13.4 are all met, we may set

$$ \chi^R(\omega) = \frac{a}{\omega-\omega_1+\ii\eta} $$

by mechanical replacement.

Why (b) cannot be continued. $\mathrm{sgn}(\omega_n)$ is not the value on the imaginary axis of any single analytic function of $z$. If we try to promote it to a function of $z$, we have no choice but $\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}\,z)$, but this is discontinuous across the real axis and is not an analytic function (indeed, “$\mathrm{sgn}(z)$” has no meaning). If we mechanically set $\ii\omega_n\to\omega+\ii\eta$, we cannot decide what to put in the argument of $\mathrm{sgn}$, and writing $\mathrm{sgn}(\omega)$ would be an error (in the upper half-plane $\mathrm{sgn}(\omega_n)=+1$ is constant, so there is no room for $\mathrm{sgn}(\omega)$ to appear in the continued result).

The correct procedure, as the warning in Section 13.8 says, is to separate the upper and lower half-planes, bring each into an analytic form, and only then continue. The retarded function is determined only by the points in the upper half-plane ($\omega_n>0$), so restricting to them we have $\mathrm{sgn}(\omega_n)=+1$ and

$$ \chi(\ii\omega_n) = \frac{a}{\ii\omega_n-\omega_1} \qquad(\omega_n>0) $$

This has the same form as (a), so the retarded function is uniquely determined as $\chi^R(\omega)=a/(\omega-\omega_1+\ii\eta)$. On the other hand, the points with $\omega_n<0$ lie in the lower half-plane, where $\mathrm{sgn}(\omega_n)=-1$, so the data are $-a/(\ii\omega_n-\omega_1)$ and the continuation target is the advanced function $\chi^A(\omega)=-a/(\omega-\omega_1-\ii\eta)$. The upper and lower half-planes are continued to separate functions across the cut—this is the true nature of an expression containing $\mathrm{sgn}$. The same holds for expressions containing $\abs{\omega_n}=\omega_n\,\mathrm{sgn}(\omega_n)$: whenever such a form appears at an intermediate stage of a Matsubara sum, it must always be reorganized half-plane by half-plane before the continuation.

Exercise 13.5: Numerical comparison of Thomas–Fermi and Debye screening

Solution

(1) We find the Thomas–Fermi screening length of copper, $\lambda_{TF}=1/q_{TF}$, from $q_{TF}^2=4\pi e^2N(\epsilon_F)$ and the density of states of the free-electron gas, $N(\epsilon_F)=3n/2\epsilon_F$. It is quickest to express the numbers with energy in eV and length in Å. In Gaussian units $e^2$ has the dimensions of energy $\times$ length, and

$$ e^2 = 14.40\ \mathrm{eV\,\text{Å}} $$

(this value follows from $e^2/a_B=27.2\ \mathrm{eV}$ and $a_B=0.529\ \text{Å}$). The electron density is $n=8.5\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}=8.5\times10^{-2}\ \text{Å}^{-3}$, so the density of states is

$$ N(\epsilon_F) = \frac{3n}{2\epsilon_F} = \frac{3\times8.5\times10^{-2}}{2\times7.0}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}\,\text{Å}^{-3}} = 1.82\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}\,\text{Å}^{-3}} \;\left(=1.82\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}\,m^{-3}}\right) $$

which agrees with the value $\approx1.8\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}m^{-3}}$ given in the hint. The square of the screening wave number is

$$ q_{TF}^2 = 4\pi e^2N(\epsilon_F) = 4\pi\times14.40\times1.82\times10^{-2} = 3.30\ \text{Å}^{-2} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad q_{TF} = 1.82\ \text{Å}^{-1} $$

Therefore

$$ \lambda_{TF} = \frac{1}{q_{TF}} = 0.551\ \text{Å} \approx 0.55\ \text{Å} $$

which agrees with the value in the hint. This is far shorter than the nearest-neighbor interatomic distance of copper, $2.56\ \text{Å}$. A point charge placed in a metal is covered up, effectively within a single atom, before it reaches the neighboring atoms.

(2) We insert the same electron density into the classical-limit formula $q_D^2=4\pi ne^2/k_BT$ ($T=300\ \mathrm{K}$) and compare with (1). First, $k_BT=8.617\times10^{-5}\ \mathrm{eV/K}\times300\ \mathrm{K}=2.585\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{eV}=25.9\ \mathrm{meV}$. Substituting, we obtain

$$ q_D^2 = \frac{4\pi\times8.5\times10^{-2}\times14.40}{2.585\times10^{-2}} = 595\ \text{Å}^{-2} \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \lambda_D = \frac{1}{\sqrt{595}} = 0.041\ \text{Å} $$

which gives a screening length more than an order of magnitude shorter than that of (1). The ratio of the two can be written analytically as

$$ \frac{q_D^2}{q_{TF}^2} = \frac{n/k_BT}{3n/2\epsilon_F} = \frac{2\epsilon_F}{3k_BT} = \frac{2\times7.0}{3\times2.585\times10^{-2}} = 180 $$

That is, the classical formula overestimates $\partial n/\partial\mu$ by a factor of 180 and underestimates the screening length by a factor of $\sqrt{180}=13.4$. Which one is appropriate? The criterion is the degree of degeneracy, $k_BT/\epsilon_F$. Here $k_BT=25.9\ \mathrm{meV}\ll\epsilon_F=7.0\ \mathrm{eV}$ (a ratio of about $1/270$), so the electron gas of copper is completely degenerate even at room temperature, and the correct answer is (1), the degenerate limit. The classical formula $\partial n/\partial\mu=n/k_BT$ counts “all $n$ electrons as able to respond thermally,” but in fact, because of the Pauli principle, only the electrons in a thin shell of width $\sim k_BT$ near the Fermi surface can respond. Because it counts too many electrons as able to respond, the classical formula overestimates the screening.

(3) We find the Debye length of a $1\ \mathrm{mM}$ aqueous solution of a monovalent electrolyte from $q_D^2=4\pi n_{\text{ion}}e^2/(\varepsilon_r k_BT)$. A monovalent salt at a concentration of $1\ \mathrm{mM}=10^{-3}\ \mathrm{mol/L}$ releases equal numbers of cations and anions, so the total ion number density is

$$ n_{\text{ion}} = 2\times10^{-3}\ \mathrm{mol/L}\times6.022\times10^{23}\ \mathrm{mol^{-1}}\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{L/m^{3}} = 1.20\times10^{24}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}} = 1.20\times10^{-6}\ \text{Å}^{-3} $$

Inserting $\varepsilon_r=78$ and $k_BT=2.585\times10^{-2}\ \mathrm{eV}$, we get

$$ q_D^2 = \frac{4\pi\times1.20\times10^{-6}\times14.40}{78\times2.585\times10^{-2}} = 1.08\times10^{-4}\ \text{Å}^{-2} $$ $$ \lambda_D = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1.08\times10^{-4}}} = 96.2\ \text{Å} = 9.62\ \mathrm{nm} \approx 9.6\ \mathrm{nm} $$

which agrees with the value in the hint. Here $k_BT$ ($25.9\ \mathrm{meV}$) is overwhelmingly larger than the degeneracy energy of the ions (which, at this density, does not even reach $\mathrm{\mu eV}$), so the system is in the classical limit, and using the Debye–Hückel formula is correct.

(4) We explain why the screening lengths of copper and the electrolyte differ by a factor of about 200 in terms of the relation of Section 13.9, $\chi^0(\qq\to0,0)=-\partial n/\partial\mu$, that is, $q_s^2=4\pi e^2\,\partial n/\partial\mu$. What determines the strength of screening is, in both systems, the same quantity $\partial n/\partial\mu$ (how much the density changes when the chemical potential is shifted slightly). Listing the numbers,

$$ \left(\frac{\partial n}{\partial\mu}\right)_{\text{Cu}} = N(\epsilon_F) = 1.82\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}m^{-3}}, \qquad \left(\frac{\partial n}{\partial\mu}\right)_{\text{electrolyte}} = \frac{n_{\text{ion}}}{k_BT} = \frac{1.20\times10^{24}}{2.585\times10^{-2}} = 4.66\times10^{25}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}m^{-3}} $$

and the ratio is $1.82\times10^{28}/4.66\times10^{25}=391\approx400$. Even after paying the degeneracy penalty that “only a thin shell on the Fermi surface can respond,” copper has a carrier density itself (of order $10^{29}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$) that is five orders of magnitude larger than that of the electrolyte (of order $10^{24}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$), so it wins by a factor of about 400 in $\partial n/\partial\mu$. In addition, in the electrolyte the relative permittivity of the medium, $\varepsilon_r=78$, weakens the Coulomb interaction and acts in the direction of lengthening the screening length. Since the screening length depends on these quantities only through their square root,

$$ \frac{\lambda_D}{\lambda_{TF}} = \left[\varepsilon_r\;\frac{(\partial n/\partial\mu)_{\text{Cu}}}{(\partial n/\partial\mu)_{\text{electrolyte}}}\right]^{1/2} = \sqrt{391\times78} = 175 $$

so that, indeed, $0.551\ \text{Å}\times175=96\ \text{Å}=9.6\ \mathrm{nm}$, and the answers of (1) and (3) are tied together exactly. We summarize the results in a single table.

Summary of Exercise 13.5—two faces of the same formula $q_s^2=4\pi e^2(\partial n/\partial\mu)/\varepsilon_r$
Copper (degenerate limit)$1\ \mathrm{mM}$ electrolyte (classical limit)
Carrier density$8.5\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$$1.20\times10^{24}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$
Form of $\partial n/\partial\mu$$N(\epsilon_F)=3n/2\epsilon_F$$n_{\text{ion}}/k_BT$
Value of $\partial n/\partial\mu$$1.82\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}m^{-3}}$$4.66\times10^{25}\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}m^{-3}}$
Relative permittivity $\varepsilon_r$$1$$78$
Screening length $\lambda$$0.55\ \text{Å}$$9.6\ \mathrm{nm}$

Physical meaning. The screening of a degenerate system hardly depends on temperature ($N(\epsilon_F)$ does not contain $T$), whereas the screening of a classical system has $\lambda_D\propto T^{1/2}$ and weakens at higher temperatures.
