Moreover, the Debye length of an electrolyte, $\sim10\ \mathrm{nm}$, is exactly the scale of colloidal stability and of the electric double layer near biological membranes, and the numbers of this exercise, too, give us a direct sense that solid-state physics and electrochemistry share the same single line, the formula $q_s^2=4\pi e^2\,\partial n/\partial\mu$.

Chapter 14: The Quasiparticle Self-Consistent GW Method (QSGW)

Exercise 14.1: Correspondence with the Lindhard function

Solution

(1) We substitute the plane waves $\psi_i=V^{-1/2}\ee^{\ii\kk\cdot\rr}$, $\epsilon_i=\epsilon_\kk$ into the polarization $\Pi^0(\rr,\rr',\omega)$ for a general band structure (Section 14.2.3), show that it becomes a function of $\rr-\rr'$ only, and then Fourier transform it to reproduce the Lindhard function of Chapter 10.

Write the indices as $i=(\kk,\sigma)$ (occupied: $n_\kk=1$) and $j=(\kk',\sigma')$ (unoccupied: $n_{\kk'}=0$). Here $\epsilon_\kk=k^2/2m$. By the orthogonality of the spin functions, the product $\psi_i^*\psi_j$ carries a factor $\delta_{\sigma\sigma'}$ (only particle–hole pairs of the same spin contribute to the polarization). Computing the products of the wave functions gives

$$ \psi_i^*(\rr)\psi_j(\rr)\,\psi_j^*(\rr')\psi_i(\rr') =\frac{1}{V^2}\,\ee^{\ii(\kk'-\kk)\cdot(\rr-\rr')}\,\delta_{\sigma\sigma'},\qquad \psi_i(\rr)\psi_j^*(\rr)\,\psi_j(\rr')\psi_i^*(\rr') =\frac{1}{V^2}\,\ee^{-\ii(\kk'-\kk)\cdot(\rr-\rr')}\,\delta_{\sigma\sigma'} $$

Both depend not on $\rr$ and $\rr'$ separately but only on the difference $\xx\equiv\rr-\rr'$. Therefore the polarization of the main text

$$ \Pi^0(\rr,\rr',\omega)=\sum_{i}^{\occ}\sum_{j}^{\unocc} \left[\frac{\psi_i^*(\rr)\psi_j(\rr)\,\psi_j^*(\rr')\psi_i(\rr')}{\omega-(\epsilon_j-\epsilon_i)+\ii\delta} -\frac{\psi_i(\rr)\psi_j^*(\rr)\,\psi_j(\rr')\psi_i^*(\rr')}{\omega+(\epsilon_j-\epsilon_i)-\ii\delta}\right] $$

becomes $\Pi^0(\xx,\omega)$. This is a manifestation of the translational symmetry of a uniform system. If we rewrite the restrictions to occupied and unoccupied states in terms of occupation-number factors, the sums become free sums over all $\kk,\kk'$:

$$ \Pi^0(\xx,\omega)=\frac{1}{V^2}\sum_{\kk\kk'\sigma}n_\kk(1-n_{\kk'}) \left[\frac{\ee^{\ii(\kk'-\kk)\cdot\xx}}{\omega-(\epsilon_{\kk'}-\epsilon_\kk)+\ii\delta} -\frac{\ee^{-\ii(\kk'-\kk)\cdot\xx}}{\omega+(\epsilon_{\kk'}-\epsilon_\kk)-\ii\delta}\right]. $$

We carry out the Fourier transform $\Pi^0(\qq,\omega)=\int\dd^3x\,\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}\,\Pi^0(\xx,\omega)$. By $\int_V\dd^3x\,\ee^{\ii(\kk'-\kk-\qq)\cdot\xx}=V\,\delta_{\kk',\kk+\qq}$, only $\kk'=\kk+\qq$ survives in the first term and only $\kk'=\kk-\qq$ in the second:

$$ \Pi^0(\qq,\omega)=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left[ \frac{n_\kk(1-n_{\kk+\qq})}{\omega+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\ii\delta} -\frac{n_\kk(1-n_{\kk-\qq})}{\omega+\epsilon_{\kk-\qq}-\epsilon_\kk-\ii\delta}\right]. $$

In the second term, relabel the dummy index as $\kk\to\kk+\qq$ (the same operation by which the denominators were aligned in Chapter 10, Section 10.1.6):

$$ (\text{second term})=-\frac{1}{V}\sum_{\kk\sigma} \frac{n_{\kk+\qq}(1-n_\kk)}{\omega+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\ii\delta}. $$

Now, as the hint says, the numerator of the first term is $n_\kk(1-n_{\kk+\qq})$ and that of the second term is $n_{\kk+\qq}(1-n_\kk)$. The two cannot survive at the same time for the same $\kk$—the former is nonzero only when $n_\kk=1,\ n_{\kk+\qq}=0$, the latter only when $n_\kk=0,\ n_{\kk+\qq}=1$. Therefore, for each $\kk$ only one of them contributes, and with the same cancellation as in Chapter 10, Section 10.1.7,

$$ n_\kk(1-n_{\kk+\qq})-n_{\kk+\qq}(1-n_\kk)=n_\kk-n_{\kk+\qq} $$

the two can be combined into one:

$$ \Pi^0(\qq,\omega)=\frac{1}{V}\sum_{\kk\sigma} \frac{n_\kk-n_{\kk+\qq}}{\omega+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\ii\delta_\kk}, \qquad \delta_\kk\equiv\delta\;\mathrm{sgn}(n_\kk-n_{\kk+\qq}). $$

Noting that $\hbar=1$ (the convention of this chapter), this is the $\chi^0(\qq,\omega)=\frac1V\sum_{\kk\sigma}\frac{n_\kk-n_{\kk+\qq}}{\omega+\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\ii\delta}$ of Chapter 10. We have traced the derivation of Chapter 10 (Lehmann representation → squared matrix element $n_\kk(1-n_{\kk+\qq})$ → relabeling of indices → cancellation of the numerators) in exactly the reverse direction.

Common Mistake: Saying “they agree” without checking what became of $\pm\ii\delta$

The infinitesimal in the combined expression carries the factor $\mathrm{sgn}(n_\kk-n_{\kk+\qq})$. The pole of the term with $n_\kk=1,\ n_{\kk+\qq}=0$ lies at $\omega=\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk>0$ and comes with $+\ii\delta$ (the pole is in the lower half-plane), while the pole of the term with $n_\kk=0,\ n_{\kk+\qq}=1$ lies at $\omega<0$ and comes with $-\ii\delta$ (the pole is in the upper half-plane)—this is the analytic structure of the time-ordered product, with the poles lined up in the fourth and second quadrants (the same arrangement as in Appendix C, Section C.1, and Figure 14.4). On the other hand, the $\chi^0$ of Chapter 10 was derived as a retarded response with $+\ii\eta$ in every term. The two have identical real parts, and the imaginary parts agree for $\omega>0$ and differ only in sign for $\omega<0$ (the relation between the time-ordered product and the retarded function described in Chapter 8). As long as $\epsilon(\qq,\omega)$ and $W$ are evaluated at $\omega>0$, where absorption occurs, no distinction appears, so the statement “they agree” in the main text should be read in this sense.

(2) We move the screening equation $W=v+v\,\Pol\,W$ to $\qq$ space and reproduce $W=v/\epsilon$, $\epsilon=1-v\chi^0$ of Chapter 9.

As we saw in (1), in a uniform system all two-point functions depend only on $\rr-\rr'$. A product of operators is a convolution $\int\dd^3 r''$, so after Fourier transformation it becomes an ordinary product (the convolution theorem). Therefore $W=v+v\Pol W$ (in the random-phase approximation, or RPA, $\Pol\simeq\Pi^0=\chi^0$) becomes a scalar equation that is closed at each $(\qq,\omega)$:

$$ W(\qq,\omega)=v(q)+v(q)\,\chi^0(\qq,\omega)\,W(\qq,\omega),\qquad v(q)=\frac{4\pi e^2}{q^2} $$

Solving for $W$ gives

$$ W(\qq,\omega)=\frac{v(q)}{1-v(q)\chi^0(\qq,\omega)}=\frac{v(q)}{\epsilon(\qq,\omega)}, \qquad \epsilon(\qq,\omega)=1-v(q)\chi^0(\qq,\omega). $$

This is exactly the expression for the dielectric function and the screened interaction of Chapter 9. Expanding it in a geometric series gives $W=v+v\chi^0v+v\chi^0v\chi^0v+\cdots$; that is, it is the closed-form solution of the self-consistent screening of Chapter 9 (the summation of RPA bubbles): “the bare $v$ creates a polarization $\chi^0$, and the potential of its induced charge creates a further polarization.”

Physical meaning. The bare potential $v$ of a test charge is covered by the induced charge produced by the linear response of the electron gas, so that only a net $v/\epsilon$ gets through. To say that the expression $\Pi^0$ is “the Lindhard function for a general band structure” means that this method of computing screening (Chapters 9 and 10) can be carried over as it is to real materials simply by replacing plane waves with Bloch functions. What goes around in the QSGW cycle (② of Figure 14.2) is precisely this calculation.

Exercise 14.2: Two derivations of the fundamental equation

Solution

(1) We reproduce Derivation 14.2 (the Hermitian part of $R$) and confirm that the diagonal elements of mode A and mode B coincide.

Let $A(\omega)\equiv\Re[\Sig(\omega)]$. Since $\Re X\equiv(X+X^\dagger)/2$ means the Hermitian part, $A(\omega)^\dagger=A(\omega)$ at each $\omega$, and in terms of matrix elements $\big(A(\omega)_{ji}\big)^*=A(\omega)_{ij}$. The starting point is the operator that assigns to each $\ket{\psi_i}$ “the $\Sig$ evaluated at that state's own energy”:

$$ R=\sum_{j,i}\ket{\psi_j}\,A(\epsilon_i)_{ji}\,\bra{\psi_i}. $$

First take the conjugate. Since $\big(\ket{\psi_j}A_{ji}\bra{\psi_i}\big)^\dagger=\ket{\psi_i}A_{ji}^*\bra{\psi_j}$,

$$ R^\dagger=\sum_{j,i}\ket{\psi_i}\,\big(A(\epsilon_i)_{ji}\big)^*\,\bra{\psi_j} =\sum_{j,i}\ket{\psi_i}\,A(\epsilon_i)_{ij}\,\bra{\psi_j}. $$

In the second equality we used the Hermiticity of $A(\epsilon_i)$. The energy $\epsilon_i$ at which $\Sig$ is evaluated is now that of the ket-side state. On the other hand, $R$ itself, after renaming the dummy indices $i\leftrightarrow j$, can be written as

$$ R=\sum_{i,j}\ket{\psi_i}\,A(\epsilon_j)_{ij}\,\bra{\psi_j} $$

(this one is evaluated at the bra-side energy). Averaging the two gives

$$ \Re R=\frac{R+R^\dagger}{2} =\frac12\sum_{i,j}\ket{\psi_i}\Big\{A(\epsilon_i)_{ij}+A(\epsilon_j)_{ij}\Big\}\bra{\psi_j}, $$

that is, we obtain the fundamental equation of the main text, $\Vxc_{\rm QSGW}=\frac12\sum_{i,j}\ket{\psi_i}\{\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]_{ij}+\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_j)]_{ij}\}\bra{\psi_j}$ (mode A).

Setting $i=j$ for the diagonal elements gives $\frac12\{A(\epsilon_i)_{ii}+A(\epsilon_i)_{ii}\}=A(\epsilon_i)_{ii}=\bra{\psi_i}\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]\ket{\psi_i}$. The diagonal part of mode B is exactly the first term of the definition ($\Vxc_{\rm mode\,B}=\sum_i\ket{\psi_i}\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]_{ii}\bra{\psi_i}+\sum_{i\neq j}\ket{\psi_i}\Re[\Sig(E_F)]_{ij}\bra{\psi_j}$ in the main text), which again is $\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]_{ii}$. The diagonal elements of the two coincide exactly. This is also the correction term of one-shot GW at $Z=1$, and the difference between the modes appears only in the off-diagonal elements (= the re-mixing of the orbitals).

(2) We show that when the Hermiticity constraint is dropped and the norm of Derivation 14.3 is minimized, a non-Hermitian $R$ emerges as the minimum point.

In the intermediate equations of Derivation 14.3, the norm could be written in terms of matrix elements as

$$ M=\sum_{i,j}\abs{A(\epsilon_j)_{ij}-X_{ij}}^2 $$

Unless the Hermiticity constraint $X_{ji}=X_{ij}^*$ is imposed, the $X_{ij}$ are mutually independent complex variables, so the minimization can be done term by term. From $\partial M/\partial X_{ij}^*=-(A(\epsilon_j)_{ij}-X_{ij})=0$,

$$ X_{ij}=A(\epsilon_j)_{ij}\quad(\text{for all }i,j),\qquad M=0. $$

Since $M\geq0$, this is the absolute minimum. The resulting operator is

$$ X=\sum_{i,j}\ket{\psi_i}\,A(\epsilon_j)_{ij}\,\bra{\psi_j}=R, \qquad X\ket{\psi_j}=A(\epsilon_j)\ket{\psi_j}=\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_j)]\ket{\psi_j}, $$

that is, nothing other than the $R$ of (1) (the formula of Section 14.3.2 with the indices relabeled). The reason $R$ is not Hermitian is, as we saw in (1), that $\Sig$ is evaluated at the different energies $\epsilon_i$ and $\epsilon_j$ in $\bra{\psi_j}R\ket{\psi_i}$ and $\bra{\psi_i}R\ket{\psi_j}^*$.

Check—the cost of the constraint. When Hermiticity is imposed, then, as in Derivation 14.3, the problem becomes, for each pair $(i,j)$, the minimization of $\abs{a-x}^2+\abs{b-x}^2$ (with $a=A(\epsilon_j)_{ij}$, $b=A(\epsilon_i)_{ij}$), and the value at the minimum point $x=(a+b)/2$ is $\abs{a-b}^2/2$. Therefore

$$ M_{\min}=\frac12\sum_{i<j}\Big|\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]_{ij}-\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_j)]_{ij}\Big|^2\;>\;0 . $$

The size of the perturbation that remains even in QSGW and cannot be eliminated is determined by how strongly $\Sig(\omega)$ depends on frequency between $\epsilon_i$ and $\epsilon_j$—this is a quantitative restatement, in terms of the norm, of the statement of Theorem 14.1 that “the approximation of QSGW is concentrated in that single point.” For a static $\Sig$, $M_{\min}=0$ and the result is exact (Exercise 14.3).

(3) We carry out the minimization with the weight changed to $\rho=\delta(\omega-E_F)\,\hat 1$, and compare with mode B.

Since $\rho$ is a $c$-number times the identity operator, the $\omega$ integral is disposed of first:

$$ M=\int\dd\omega\,\delta(\omega-E_F)\,\Tr\Big\{\Re[\Delta V(\omega)]^2\Big\} =\Tr\Big\{\Re[\Delta V(E_F)]^2\Big\} =\sum_{i,j}\abs{A(E_F)_{ij}-X_{ij}}^2 . $$

This time the energy at which $\Sig$ is evaluated is the same $E_F$ for all matrix elements, so the minimum point

$$ \Vxc=\Re[\Sig(E_F)] $$

is automatically Hermitian (since $A(E_F)$ is Hermitian), and $M=0$ is attained without any constraint. It is a static potential with all elements evaluated at the Fermi energy.

Compared with mode B: the off-diagonal elements coincide exactly with those of mode B ($\Re[\Sig(E_F)]_{ij}$), and only the diagonal elements differ (mode B evaluates them at the state's own energy $\epsilon_i$, whereas here it is $E_F$). In other words, mode B can be read as a prescription corresponding to a mixed weight: “$\rho=\delta(\omega-H^0)$ for the diagonal elements and $\rho=\delta(\omega-E_F)$ for the off-diagonal elements.” There is a practical reason not to evaluate the diagonal elements at $E_F$: for deep levels such as the Zn $3d$ (around $-8\,\mathrm{eV}$ relative to the valence-band maximum, Table 14.5), $\Sig(\omega)$ changes greatly between $\epsilon_i$ and $E_F$, so the diagonal elements, which fix the positions of the levels, go badly wrong unless they are evaluated at the state's own energy. On the other hand, the off-diagonal elements, which determine the hybridization of the orbitals, are insensitive to the evaluation point, which is precisely why the results of mode A and mode B are so similar (the Si gap of $1.25$ versus $1.28\,\mathrm{eV}$). Note also that for states near $E_F$ we have $\epsilon_i\simeq\epsilon_j\simeq E_F$ and all three prescriptions coincide—the only place where the QSGW requirement to “make the bare and dressed quasiparticles coincide near $E_F$” leaves room for ambiguity in the prescription is the part away from $E_F$.

Exercise 14.3: The Hartree–Fock limit and the static self-energy

Solution

(1) We show that when the self-energy is an arbitrary static Hermitian operator $S$, $\Vxc=S$ holds exactly at the QSGW fixed point.

Suppose $\Sig(\omega)=S$ (independent of $\omega$). First, since $S$ is Hermitian, taking the Hermitian part does nothing: $\Re[\Sig(\omega)]=S$. Next, the value does not change whether the energy at which we evaluate it is $\epsilon_i$ or $\epsilon_j$:

$$ \Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]_{ij}=\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_j)]_{ij}=S_{ij}. $$

Substituting this into the fundamental equation, the averaging operation becomes trivial, and

$$ \Vxc=\frac12\sum_{i,j}\ket{\psi_i}\{S_{ij}+S_{ij}\}\bra{\psi_j} =\sum_{i,j}\ket{\psi_i}\bra{\psi_i}\,S\,\ket{\psi_j}\bra{\psi_j} =\hat 1\,S\,\hat 1=S . $$

In the last step we used the completeness of $\{\psi_i\}$, $\sum_i\ket{\psi_i}\bra{\psi_i}=\hat 1$. No approximation has entered at all. Therefore the QSGW cycle coincides exactly with the self-consistent (SCF) iteration of $\Veff=\Vext+\VH+S[\{\psi\}]$, and the fixed point is none other than the SCF solution for the static self-energy $S$. If $S=\Sigma_x$ ($W\to v$, Derivation 14.1), QSGW reproduces Hartree–Fock (Theorem 14.1) as it is, and if $S=\Sigma^{\rm SEX}+\Sigma^{\rm COH}$, it reproduces self-consistent COHSEX. QSGW makes an “approximation” only when $\Sig$ depends on the frequency.

(2) We evaluate the fundamental equation for a linear frequency dependence $\Sig(\omega)=S_0+(\omega-\mu)S_1$ ($S_0,S_1$ Hermitian).

For real $\omega$, $S_0+(\omega-\mu)S_1$ is a linear combination of Hermitian operators with real coefficients and is therefore Hermitian, so $\Re[\Sig(\omega)]=\Sig(\omega)$. Hence

$$ \Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]_{ij}=(S_0)_{ij}+(\epsilon_i-\mu)(S_1)_{ij},\qquad \Re[\Sig(\epsilon_j)]_{ij}=(S_0)_{ij}+(\epsilon_j-\mu)(S_1)_{ij}. $$

Taking the average in the fundamental equation gives

$$ \Vxc_{ij}=\frac12\Big\{\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]_{ij}+\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_j)]_{ij}\Big\} =(S_0)_{ij}+\frac12\,(\epsilon_i+\epsilon_j-2\mu)\,(S_1)_{ij}. $$

The contribution of $S_0$ enters in full, as in (1), and the contribution of the frequency-dependent part $(\omega-\mu)S_1$ is $\tfrac12(\epsilon_i+\epsilon_j-2\mu)(S_1)_{ij}$—the expression we were asked to obtain. On the diagonal it becomes $(\epsilon_i-\mu)(S_1)_{ii}$, that is, precisely the value evaluated at the state's own energy.

This result can be summarized in one line:

$$ \Vxc_{ij}=\bra{\psi_i}\Sig\!\left(\frac{\epsilon_i+\epsilon_j}{2}\right)\ket{\psi_j}. $$

The frequency dependence of $\Sig$ is not discarded; it is incorporated into the static potential in the form “evaluate each matrix element at the arithmetic mean of the energies of the two states involved”. Within the linear range this replacement is exact, and all that QSGW loses is the curvature of $\Sig(\omega)$ (terms of order $\omega^2$ and higher). Moreover, if we take $\mu$ to be $E_F$, then for pairs of states near $E_F$ we have $\epsilon_i+\epsilon_j-2\mu\simeq0$, so the contribution of $S_1$ vanishes—consistent with the design of QSGW based on the Landau quasiparticle picture, in which the static approximation $\Vxc$ works best near the Fermi energy.

Exercise 14.4: Estimating the effect of the $Z$ factor

Solution

(1) We compute $Z_i$ from the linearization $\Sig(\omega)\simeq\Sigma_0+(\omega-\epsilon_i)\Sigma_1$, $\Sigma_1=-a$. We start from the definition of one-shot GW (Section 14.1.1):

$$ Z_i=\Big[1-\bra{\psi_i}\frac{\partial\Sig}{\partial\omega}\Big|_{\epsilon_i}\ket{\psi_i}\Big]^{-1} $$

Substituting $\partial\Sig/\partial\omega=\Sigma_1=-a$ into this gives

$$ Z_i=\frac{1}{1-(-a)}=\frac{1}{1+a}. $$

For $a>0$ ($\Sig$ decreases with $\omega$, the usual case), $Z_i<1$: part of the quasiparticle weight is transferred to the satellites (Figure 14.1).

(2) We find the difference in the correction for $a=0.3$ and $\Delta=\bra{\psi_i}\Sigma_0-\Vxc_{\rm LDA}\ket{\psi_i}=1.0\,\mathrm{eV}$.

$$ Z=\frac{1}{1.3}=0.769,\qquad Z\Delta=0.769\,\mathrm{eV},\qquad \Delta=1.0\,\mathrm{eV}, $$

The difference is $(1-Z)\Delta=0.231\,\mathrm{eV}$. For a material in which the correction from the local density approximation (LDA) is of order $1\,\mathrm{eV}$, whether or not we multiply by $Z$ makes a difference of order $0.2\,\mathrm{eV}$—the same order as the systematic error of the one-shot GW gap ($0.3\,\mathrm{eV}$ for Si, Table 14.1). This is why the “treatment of $Z$” cannot be ignored as an ambiguity of the prescription.

(3) We back out the effective $Z$ from the gaps in Table 14.4 for one-shot (with $Z$) and $Z=1$ (NZ).

The gap changes by the difference between the corrections at the conduction-band edge and at the valence-band edge. Approximating the effective $Z$ at the two edges by a common $\bar Z$, we obtain

$$ E_g^{Z}=E_g^{\rm LDA}+\bar Z\,(\Delta_c-\Delta_v),\qquad E_g^{Z=1}=E_g^{\rm LDA}+(\Delta_c-\Delta_v) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \bar Z=\frac{E_g^{Z}-E_g^{\rm LDA}}{E_g^{Z=1}-E_g^{\rm LDA}} . $$

Using the LDA values in Table 14.4 (Si $0.46$, GaAs $0.34\,\mathrm{eV}$) gives

Material$E_g^{\rm LDA}$one-shot ($Z$)one-shot ($Z=1$)$\bar Z$$a=1/\bar Z-1$
Si0.460.981.13$0.52/0.67=0.776$0.29
GaAs0.341.441.75$1.10/1.41=0.780$0.28

In both cases $\bar Z\simeq0.78$ and $a\simeq0.28$–$0.29$. The value $a=0.3$ ($Z=0.77$) assumed in (2) agrees well with the actual values at the band edges of $sp$ semiconductors (and is also consistent with the statement in Section 14.1.3 that $Z\simeq0.7$–$1$). The reason $\bar Z$ is almost the same regardless of the material and band is that the main source of $\partial\Sig/\partial\omega$, which determines $Z$, is the frequency dependence of the screening by plasmons, and this changes little between the valence-band and conduction-band edges.

(4) At the QSGW fixed point, by item 1 of Section 14.3.4, $\bra{\psi_i}\Re[\Sig(\epsilon_i)]-\Vxc\ket{\psi_i}=0$ holds identically, so the first-order correction itself, to which $Z_i$ would be applied, is zero, and the question of “whether to multiply or not” loses its object and disappears.

Exercise 14.5: The chain of gap, effective mass, and dielectric constant

Solution

(1) Using the two-band $\kk\cdot\pp$ model $m_0/m_c\simeq1+E_P/E_g$, we estimate the effect on $m_c$ of a $16\%$ overestimate of $E_g$.

Taking the reciprocal gives $m_c/m_0=1/(1+E_P/E_g)$. Inserting the experimental values for GaAs (corrections included) $E_g=1.69\,\mathrm{eV}$ and $E_P=25\,\mathrm{eV}$ gives

$$ \frac{m_c}{m_0}=\frac{1}{1+25/1.69}=0.0633, $$

which reproduces the experimental value $0.065$ well (evidence that the two-band model is effective for the $\Gamma$ valley of GaAs). Increasing $E_g$ by $16\%$ to $E_g=1.16\times1.69=1.96\,\mathrm{eV}$ (almost equal to the QSGW value of $1.97\,\mathrm{eV}$), we obtain

$$ \frac{m_c}{m_0}=\frac{1}{1+25/1.96}=0.0727,\qquad \frac{0.0727}{0.0633}=1.15 . $$

That is, $m_c$ is overestimated by about $15\%$. In general, for $E_P\gg E_g$ we have $m_c\simeq(E_g/E_P)m_0$, so $m_c$ is nearly proportional to $E_g$, and the sensitivity is

$$ \frac{\dd\ln m_c}{\dd\ln E_g}=\frac{E_P}{E_P+E_g}=\frac{25}{26.7}=0.94 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 16\%\times0.94\simeq15\% . $$

The actual ratio in Table 14.5 is $0.077/0.065=1.18$ ($+18\%$), and, as stated in the main text (“the ratio of $E_g$ is $1.16$ and the ratio of $m_c$ is $1.18$”), it agrees well with the $\kk\cdot\pp$ estimate of $+15\%$ (the remaining few $\%$ come from the higher bands that the two-band model omits). An error in the gap drags along an error in the effective mass, almost proportionally—the first link of the chain of errors.

(2) Using the Penn model $\epsilon_\infty-1\simeq(\omega_p/E_g^{\rm avg})^2$, we determine what $\%$ error in the average gap is equivalent to a $-20\%$ error in $\epsilon_\infty$.

Since $\omega_p$ (determined by the valence-electron density) is common, $E_g^{\rm avg}\propto(\epsilon_\infty-1)^{-1/2}$. Letting the calculated value be $\epsilon_\infty'=0.8\,\epsilon_\infty$,

$$ \frac{E_g^{\rm avg\prime}}{E_g^{\rm avg}} =\sqrt{\frac{\epsilon_\infty-1}{0.8\,\epsilon_\infty-1}} \;\xrightarrow{\ \epsilon_\infty\gg1\ }\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{0.8}}=1.12 . $$

Evaluating for GaAs ($\epsilon_\infty=10.8$, $0.8\epsilon_\infty=8.64$—almost equal to the QSGW value $8.4$ in Table 14.5) gives

$$ \frac{E_g^{\rm avg\prime}}{E_g^{\rm avg}}=\sqrt{\frac{9.8}{7.64}}=1.13 . $$

That is, a $-20\%$ error in $\epsilon_\infty$ corresponds to a $+12$–$13\%$ overestimate of the average gap. This is exactly the same size as the observed QSGW gap error of $+10$–$20\%$ (Section 14.5.1). The overestimated gap and the underestimated $\epsilon_\infty$ are not two independent errors; through the Penn model they are restatements of each other—the second link of the chain.

(3) We discuss the direction and magnitude of the effect on the gap when the long-wavelength, static screened interaction $W(q\to0,0)=v(q)/\epsilon_\infty$ is too strong by $20\%$.

Direction. What opens the gap is the screened exchange term $\ii G^0W$ (in the static limit, $\Sigma^{\rm SEX}\propto -\sum^{\occ}\psi\psi^*\,W$), and its magnitude is nearly proportional to $W$. If $W$ is too strong, the opening is also too large—the error always appears in the direction of gap overestimation. This is why the sign of the error is the same for all materials (Figure 14.3).

Order of magnitude. If we approximate the many-body correction to the gap, $E_g^{\rm QSGW}-E_g^{\rm LDA}$, as proportional to $W$, then $20\%$ of it is the overestimate:

MaterialCorrection $E_g^{\rm QSGW}-E_g^{\rm LDA}$$20\%$ of itActual overestimate
GaAs$1.97-0.34=1.63\,\mathrm{eV}$$0.33\,\mathrm{eV}$$1.97-1.69=0.28\,\mathrm{eV}$
Si$1.25-0.46=0.79\,\mathrm{eV}$$0.16\,\mathrm{eV}$For $\Gamma$–$\Gamma$, $\sim0.25$; for $\Gamma$–$X$, $\sim0.1\,\mathrm{eV}$

Both the direction and the order of magnitude agree with observation. However, this estimate is close to an upper bound: the error in $W$ (the absence of the electron–hole interaction in the RPA) is largest in the excitonic region of $q\to0$ and $\omega=0$ and is small at large $q$ and high $\omega$, so the average overestimate of $W$ that matters for $\Sig$ is smaller than $20\%$. Even so, the conclusion—an error of the order of a few hundred meV, in the positive direction—does not change.

Physical Meaning: The three errors are one chain

Reading (1)–(3) straight through, we find that the claim of Section 14.5.3 has been confirmed with numbers. The $\Pol$ of the RPA has no electron–hole attraction → the excitation energies of the particle–hole pairs are too high, so $\epsilon_\infty$ is $-20\%$ → $W=v/\epsilon$ is $+20$–$25\%$ → the gap is $+10$–$20\%$ (a few hundred meV) → through $\kk\cdot\pp$, $m_c$ is also $+15$–$18\%$. Every link is a proportional relation, so fixing one place fixes all of them. That the errors in the gap and in $\epsilon_\infty$ vanish simultaneously in QSG$\hat W$ (Section 14.5.4), in which ladder diagrams are put into $\Pol$, is a direct consequence of the structure of this chain.

Exercise 14.6: Deformation of the integration contour

Solution

(1) We rotate the $\omega'$ integral on the real axis onto the imaginary axis and derive the two-term form of $X_{\rm img}$. The starting point is

$$ X=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\ii\,\dd\omega'}{2\pi}\,\frac{W^c(\omega')}{\omega_\epsilon-\omega'\pm\ii\delta}, \qquad \omega_\epsilon\equiv\omega-\epsilon_{\qq-\kk\,n'} $$ First we check that the contribution of the distant arc vanishes. As $|\omega'|\to\infty$, $\epsilon\to1$, so $W^c=W-v=v(\epsilon^{-1}-1)=\mathcal O(1/\omega'^2)$; the integrand is $\mathcal O(1/\omega'^3)$ and the length of the arc is $\mathcal O(R)$, so the contribution of the quarter-circle arcs is $\mathcal O(1/R^2)\to0$. Therefore the contour on the real axis may be moved directly onto the imaginary axis (provided the pole contributions are counted separately).

On the imaginary axis we substitute $\omega'=\ii u$ ($u:-\infty\to+\infty$), $\dd\omega'=\ii\,\dd u$. The denominator does not vanish on the imaginary axis (when $\omega_\epsilon\neq0$), so $\pm\ii\delta$ may be dropped, and we have

$$ X_{\rm img} =\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\ii\cdot\ii\,\dd u}{2\pi}\,\frac{W^c(\ii u)}{\omega_\epsilon-\ii u} =-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\dd u\,\frac{W^c(\ii u)\,(\omega_\epsilon+\ii u)}{u^2+\omega_\epsilon^2} $$ The last equality was obtained by rationalizing, that is, multiplying the numerator and denominator by $\omega_\epsilon+\ii u$. If we split the integration interval into $u>0$ and $u<0$ and replace $u\to-u$ in the latter, the expression becomes

$$ X_{\rm img} =-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\dd u\, \frac{\omega_\epsilon\left[W^c(\ii u)+W^c(-\ii u)\right]+\ii u\left[W^c(\ii u)-W^c(-\ii u)\right]}{u^2+\omega_\epsilon^2} $$ after collecting terms. Here, with the definitions $W^S(u)=[W^c(\ii u)+W^c(-\ii u)]/2$ and $W^A(u)=[W^c(\ii u)-W^c(-\ii u)]/2\ii$ of the main text, the square brackets are $2W^S(u)$ and $2\ii W^A(u)$, respectively, so we obtain

$$ X_{\rm img} =-\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty\dd u\;W^S(u)\,\frac{\omega_\epsilon}{u^2+\omega_\epsilon^2} \;+\;\frac1\pi\int_0^\infty\dd u\;W^A(u)\,\frac{u}{u^2+\omega_\epsilon^2} $$ (note that $\ii u\cdot2\ii W^A=-2uW^A$); rewriting the integration variable as $u\to\omega'$, we obtain an expression that coincides with $X_{\rm img}$ of the main text. If time-reversal symmetry holds, $W^c(\ii u)$ is real and an even function of $u$, so $W^A=0$ and the second term vanishes.

(2) We count the poles crossed in the course of the rotation. When the real axis ($-\infty\to+\infty$) is rotated counterclockwise by $90^\circ$ onto the imaginary axis ($-\ii\infty\to+\ii\infty$), the regions swept out are the first and third quadrants (the light-blue regions in Figure 14.4). Sorting the poles by the quadrant in which they lie:

  • The poles of $W^c(\omega')$ are $+\Omega_n-\ii\delta$ in the fourth quadrant and $-\Omega_n+\ii\delta$ in the second quadrant. Neither lies in the swept region, so the rotation never crosses them. It is precisely because the poles of $W^c$ do not contribute that the smooth $W^c(\ii u)$ on the imaginary axis alone suffices.
  • The unoccupied pole of $G^0(\omega-\omega')$, $\omega'=\omega-\epsilon_j+\ii\delta$ ($\epsilon_j>E_F$), lies in the upper half-plane. The condition for it to lie in the first quadrant is $\Re\,\omega'=\omega-\epsilon_j>0$, that is, $\epsilon_j<\omega$. Taken together, $E_F<\epsilon_j<\omega$; in other words, only the unoccupied states with $0<\omega_\epsilon<\omega-E_F$, where $\omega_\epsilon=\omega-\epsilon_j$, are crossed in the first quadrant.
  • The occupied pole of $G^0$, $\omega'=\omega-\epsilon_i-\ii\delta$ ($\epsilon_i<E_F$), lies in the lower half-plane. The condition for it to lie in the third quadrant is $\omega-\epsilon_i<0$, that is, $\omega<\epsilon_i<E_F$. This happens only for $\omega<E_F$ (holes), and corresponds to $\omega-E_F<\omega_\epsilon<0$.

We also check the sign of the residue. The first quadrant is enclosed counterclockwise by the closed contour “real axis $0\to R$ + quarter-circle arc + imaginary axis $\ii R\to0$”, so $\oint=+2\pi\ii\times$ (residue). The residue of the integrand $\dfrac{\ii}{2\pi}\dfrac{W^c(\omega')}{\omega_\epsilon-\omega'+\ii\delta}$ at $\omega'=\omega_\epsilon+\ii\delta$ is $\dfrac{\ii}{2\pi}\times(-1)\times W^c(\omega_\epsilon)$, so the contribution is $2\pi\ii\cdot\left(-\dfrac{\ii}{2\pi}\right)W^c=+W^c(\omega_\epsilon)$. With the same construction, the closed contour in the third quadrant runs clockwise, so the sign is reversed, and the contribution of an occupied pole is $-W^c(\omega_\epsilon)$. This is the case distinction for $X_{\rm real}$ in the main text ($+$ for unoccupied, $-$ for occupied, and $0$ otherwise).

(3) Why the lifetime is exactly infinite in an insulator if the electron's energy, measured from the conduction-band minimum $\epsilon_c$, is less than $E_g$. As stated in the main text, $\Im\Sig$, which determines the quasiparticle lifetime, comes entirely from $X_{\rm real}$ ($X_{\rm img}$ is an integral of the real functions $W^S,W^A$ on the imaginary axis, and contributes only to the Hermitian part of $\Sig$). The imaginary part of $X_{\rm real}$ is $\Im W^c(\omega_\epsilon)$, and this is nonzero only when the medium can actually absorb the energy $|\omega_\epsilon|$—that is, only when $|\omega_\epsilon|$ is at or above the excitation threshold of an electron–hole pair. In an insulator $\Im\epsilon(\omega')=0$ ($|\omega'|<E_g$), so

$$ \Im W^c(\omega')=0 \qquad (|\omega'|<E_g) $$ holds. On the other hand, as we saw in (2), the poles crossed at $\omega=\epsilon_i$ (an electron in the conduction band, $\epsilon_c\le\epsilon_i$) are limited to the unoccupied states $\epsilon_j\ge\epsilon_c$ with $0<\omega_\epsilon=\epsilon_i-\epsilon_j<\epsilon_i-E_F$, so the argument of $W^c$ appearing in $X_{\rm real}$ is at most

$$ \omega_\epsilon=\epsilon_i-\epsilon_j\;\le\;\epsilon_i-\epsilon_c\;<\;E_g $$ and it does not go beyond that. Therefore $\Im W^c(\omega_\epsilon)=0$ at every pole in the window $0<\omega_\epsilon<\omega-E_F$, so that $\Im\bra{\psi_i}\Sig(\epsilon_i)\ket{\psi_i}=0$ and the lifetime is exactly infinite.

Physical meaning. When an electron drops from $\epsilon_i$ to an unoccupied state $\epsilon_j$ below it, the energy it can release is only $\epsilon_i-\epsilon_j<E_g$. But the smallest unit of energy that an insulating medium can accept is $E_g$, that of an electron–hole pair. With no partner to balance the energy books, no decay occurs—the threshold for impact ionization ($E_g$ above the conduction-band minimum) can be read off directly as the overlap between the “window of $X_{\rm real}$” and the “onset of $\Im W^c$” in the contour figure.

Appendix B: Essentials of Second Quantization

Exercise B.1: Basic check of the commutation relations

Solution

(1) The problem is to derive $[\hat n_i,\hat c_j^\dagger]=\delta_{ij}\hat c_j^\dagger$ and $[\hat n_i,\hat c_j]=-\delta_{ij}\hat c_j$ for the fermion number operator $\hat n_i=\hat c_i^\dagger\hat c_i$ from the anticommutation relations alone. The tool is the identity prepared in Derivation 6 of Section B.3.3,

$$ [\hat A\hat B,\hat C]=\hat A\{\hat B,\hat C\}-\{\hat A,\hat C\}\hat B $$

(the key point was that one commutator can be written with two anticommutators). Following the hint, set $\hat A=\hat c_i^\dagger$, $\hat B=\hat c_i$, and $\hat C=\hat c_j^\dagger$, and insert the anticommutation relations $\{\hat c_i,\hat c_j^\dagger\}=\delta_{ij}$ and $\{\hat c_i^\dagger,\hat c_j^\dagger\}=0$. This gives

$$ [\hat n_i,\hat c_j^\dagger] =\hat c_i^\dagger\underbrace{\{\hat c_i,\hat c_j^\dagger\}}_{=\delta_{ij}} -\underbrace{\{\hat c_i^\dagger,\hat c_j^\dagger\}}_{=0}\,\hat c_i =\delta_{ij}\,\hat c_i^\dagger =\delta_{ij}\,\hat c_j^\dagger $$

In the last equality we used the fact that $\delta_{ij}$ survives only for $i=j$, in which case we may rewrite $\hat c_i^\dagger=\hat c_j^\dagger$. Similarly, if we take $\hat C=\hat c_j$, then from $\{\hat c_i,\hat c_j\}=0$ and $\{\hat c_i^\dagger,\hat c_j\}=\delta_{ij}$ we obtain

$$ [\hat n_i,\hat c_j] =\hat c_i^\dagger\underbrace{\{\hat c_i,\hat c_j\}}_{=0} -\underbrace{\{\hat c_i^\dagger,\hat c_j\}}_{=\delta_{ij}}\,\hat c_i =-\delta_{ij}\,\hat c_i =-\delta_{ij}\,\hat c_j $$

This completes the proof.

Physical meaning. These two equations are the operator expression of the statement that “if we create, the occupation number reads one higher; if we annihilate, it reads one lower.” Indeed, for an eigenstate $\ket{n}$ of $\hat n_i$ (eigenvalue $n_i$), rewriting the first equation in terms of its action gives $\hat n_i\left(\hat c_j^\dagger\ket{n}\right)=\hat c_j^\dagger\left(\hat n_i+\delta_{ij}\right)\ket{n}=(n_i+\delta_{ij})\,\hat c_j^\dagger\ket{n}$, from which we read off that $\hat c_j^\dagger\ket{n}$ is an eigenstate in which only the $j$th occupation number has increased by one.

(2) We show that the same relations $[\hat n_i,\hat a_j^\dagger]=\delta_{ij}\hat a_j^\dagger$ and $[\hat n_i,\hat a_j]=-\delta_{ij}\hat a_j$ hold for bosons as well. This time we use the commutator version of the identity,

$$ [\hat A\hat B,\hat C]=\hat A[\hat B,\hat C]+[\hat A,\hat C]\hat B $$

(expanding the right-hand side gives $\hat A\hat B\hat C-\hat A\hat C\hat B+\hat A\hat C\hat B-\hat C\hat A\hat B=[\hat A\hat B,\hat C]$, so it indeed holds). Substituting the commutation relations $[\hat a_i,\hat a_j^\dagger]=\delta_{ij}$ and $[\hat a_i^\dagger,\hat a_j^\dagger]=[\hat a_i,\hat a_j]=0$, together with $[\hat a_i^\dagger,\hat a_j]=-[\hat a_j,\hat a_i^\dagger]=-\delta_{ij}$, which follows from the Hermitian conjugate of the first relation, we get

$$ [\hat n_i,\hat a_j^\dagger] =\hat a_i^\dagger\underbrace{[\hat a_i,\hat a_j^\dagger]}_{=\delta_{ij}} +\underbrace{[\hat a_i^\dagger,\hat a_j^\dagger]}_{=0}\,\hat a_i =\delta_{ij}\,\hat a_j^\dagger, \qquad [\hat n_i,\hat a_j] =\hat a_i^\dagger\underbrace{[\hat a_i,\hat a_j]}_{=0} +\underbrace{[\hat a_i^\dagger,\hat a_j]}_{=-\delta_{ij}}\,\hat a_i =-\delta_{ij}\,\hat a_j $$

which has exactly the same form as for fermions. The commutation relations with the number operator carry no content beyond “counting the number of particles,” so it is natural that they do not depend on the statistics (the same circumstance as Lemma B.1 taking the same form for bosons and fermions).

(3) We show that $\hat N=\sum_i\hat n_i$ commutes with the one-body operator $\hat T=\sum_{ij}t_{ij}\hat a_i^\dagger\hat a_j$. Lemma B.1 of Section B.3.3,

$$ \left[\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b,\;\hat a_c^\dagger\hat a_d\right] =\delta_{bc}\,\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_d-\delta_{ad}\,\hat a_c^\dagger\hat a_b $$

with $c=d$ gives $[\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b,\hat n_c]=(\delta_{bc}-\delta_{ac})\,\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b$. Summing this over $c$, as the hint says, we obtain

$$ \left[\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b,\;\hat N\right] =\sum_c\left(\delta_{bc}-\delta_{ac}\right)\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b =(1-1)\,\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b=0 $$

Here $\sum_c\delta_{bc}=1$ ($c$ runs over all states, so it passes through $c=b$ exactly once) and $\sum_c\delta_{ac}=1$ are, respectively, the contributions with which the annihilation operator $\hat a_b$ and the creation operator $\hat a_a^\dagger$ touch the bookkeeping of $\hat N$, and they show that the tally “$+1$ for creation, $-1$ for annihilation” cancels out to zero. Multiplying by $t_{ab}$ and summing over $a,b$ gives

$$ \left[\hat T,\hat N\right]=\sum_{ab}t_{ab}\left[\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b,\hat N\right]=0 \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \left[\hat N,\hat T\right]=0 $$

Since $\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b$ is the operation “annihilate one particle and create one,” no term changes the particle number—and we have now confirmed this by means of equations. $[\hat N,\hat T]=0$ means that “the eigenvalue of the particle number is preserved under time evolution by $\hat T$,” that is, that a one-body operator does not change the particle number.

(4) We show $[\hat N,\hat V]=0$ for the two-body operator $\hat V=\frac12\sum_{ijkl}\bra{ij}v\ket{kl}\,\hat a_i^\dagger\hat a_j^\dagger\hat a_l\hat a_k$ (Theorem B.2) as well. First, summing the results of (1) and (2) over $i$, we obtain

$$ \left[\hat N,\hat a_j^\dagger\right]=\sum_i\delta_{ij}\,\hat a_j^\dagger=+\,\hat a_j^\dagger, \qquad \left[\hat N,\hat a_j\right]=-\,\hat a_j $$

(common to bosons and fermions). Since $\hat N$ is a sum of creation–annihilation pairs $\hat a_i^\dagger\hat a_i$—that is, it is built from an even number of fermion operators—the ordinary Leibniz rule $[\hat N,\hat A\hat B]=[\hat N,\hat A]\hat B+\hat A[\hat N,\hat B]$ can be used as it is even for fermions (rule of thumb 1 of Section B.2.4). Applying it successively to the product of four operators gives

$$ \left[\hat N,\;\hat a_i^\dagger\hat a_j^\dagger\hat a_l\hat a_k\right] =\underbrace{[\hat N,\hat a_i^\dagger]}_{+\hat a_i^\dagger}\hat a_j^\dagger\hat a_l\hat a_k +\hat a_i^\dagger\underbrace{[\hat N,\hat a_j^\dagger]}_{+\hat a_j^\dagger}\hat a_l\hat a_k +\hat a_i^\dagger\hat a_j^\dagger\underbrace{[\hat N,\hat a_l]}_{-\hat a_l}\hat a_k +\hat a_i^\dagger\hat a_j^\dagger\hat a_l\underbrace{[\hat N,\hat a_k]}_{-\hat a_k} $$ $$ =\left(+1+1-1-1\right)\hat a_i^\dagger\hat a_j^\dagger\hat a_l\hat a_k=0 $$

Since each term vanishes, multiplying by the matrix elements and summing still gives $[\hat N,\hat V]=0$. A two-body operator “annihilates two particles and creates two,” so it too does not change the particle number.

If creation and annihilation operators are equal in number, the particle number is conserved

Looking at the calculations in (3) and (4), the reason for the commutation has nothing to do with the details of the matrix elements: it comes down to the fact that each $\hat a^\dagger$ counts $+1$ and each $\hat a$ counts $-1$, and the total is zero. In general, a product $\hat O$ of $p$ creation operators and $q$ annihilation operators satisfies $[\hat N,\hat O]=(p-q)\hat O$ (the same Leibniz-rule calculation). If $p=q$, the particle number is conserved; if $p\ne q$, $\hat O$ is an operator that changes the particle number by $p-q$. Rule of thumb 3 of Section B.2.4, “if the numbers of $\hat c$ and $\hat c^\dagger$ inside $\braket{\cdots}$ do not match, the result is automatically zero,” is nothing other than a consequence of this equation. The kinetic energy, the external field, and the Coulomb interaction are all of the form $p=q$, so the electron number is exactly conserved at the level of the Hamiltonian.

Exercise B.2: Tracking the Fermi signs by hand

Solution

Let us first organize the rules we will use. When the occupation-number states are defined as the product in numerical order $\ket{n_1n_2n_3n_4}=(\hat c_1^\dagger)^{n_1}(\hat c_2^\dagger)^{n_2}(\hat c_3^\dagger)^{n_3}(\hat c_4^\dagger)^{n_4}\ket{0}$, the action rules of Section B.2.4 are

$$ \hat c_i^\dagger\ket{\dots,n_i,\dots}=(-1)^{S_i}\,(1-n_i)\,\ket{\dots,n_i+1,\dots}, \qquad \hat c_i\ket{\dots,n_i,\dots}=(-1)^{S_i}\,n_i\,\ket{\dots,n_i-1,\dots}, \qquad S_i=\sum_{j<i}n_j $$

Here $S_i$ is “the number of particles to the left of the position where the operator acts,” that is, the number of operators that $\hat c_i^{(\dagger)}$ has to pass through to reach its proper position.

(1) We find $\hat c_2^\dagger\ket{1,0,1,0}$. The neighbor to the left has $n_1=1$, so $S_2=1$, and the target is empty ($n_2=0$), so the state does not vanish, and

$$ \hat c_2^\dagger\ket{1,0,1,0}=(-1)^1\ket{1,1,1,0}=-\ket{1,1,1,0} $$

Check. Let us do it directly with the string of operators. Since $\ket{1,0,1,0}=\hat c_1^\dagger\hat c_3^\dagger\ket{0}$,

$$ \hat c_2^\dagger\,\hat c_1^\dagger\hat c_3^\dagger\ket{0} =-\,\hat c_1^\dagger\,\hat c_2^\dagger\,\hat c_3^\dagger\ket{0} =-\ket{1,1,1,0} $$

Moving $\hat c_2^\dagger$ to its proper position (to the right of $\hat c_1^\dagger$) requires passing one operator, so the sign is $(-1)^1$. The two results agree.

(2) We find $\hat c_3\ket{1,1,1,0}$. Since $S_3=n_1+n_2=2$ and the target is occupied ($n_3=1$),

$$ \hat c_3\ket{1,1,1,0}=(-1)^2\ket{1,1,0,0}=+\ket{1,1,0,0} $$

Two operators ($\hat c_1^\dagger$ and $\hat c_2^\dagger$) are passed, so the sign returns to $+$.

(3) We evaluate $\hat c_4^\dagger\hat c_1\ket{1,1,0,0}$, that is, the operation “move the electron in state 1 to state 4,” executing the operators in order from the right.

  • First $\hat c_1$: $S_1=0$ (nobody is to the left) and $n_1=1$, so $\hat c_1\ket{1,1,0,0}=+\ket{0,1,0,0}$.
  • Next $\hat c_4^\dagger$: in the present state $S_4=n_1+n_2+n_3=0+1+0=1$ and $n_4=0$, so $\hat c_4^\dagger\ket{0,1,0,0}=-\ket{0,1,0,1}$.

Combining the two steps,

$$ \hat c_4^\dagger\hat c_1\ket{1,1,0,0}=-\ket{0,1,0,1} $$

Note that at the moment $S_4$ is counted, the electron in state 1 has already disappeared (if this count is done in the original state, the sign comes out wrong).

(4) We execute the reverse order $\hat c_1\hat c_4^\dagger\ket{1,1,0,0}$ in the same way.

  • First, $\hat c_4^\dagger$: $S_4=n_1+n_2+n_3=1+1+0=2$ and $n_4=0$, so $\hat c_4^\dagger\ket{1,1,0,0}=(+1)\ket{1,1,0,1}$. This time the electron in state 1 is still present, so there are two overtakings.
  • Next, $\hat c_1$: $S_1=0$ and $n_1=1$, so $\hat c_1\ket{1,1,0,1}=+\ket{0,1,0,1}$.

Hence

$$ \hat c_1\hat c_4^\dagger\ket{1,1,0,0}=+\ket{0,1,0,1} $$

and the sign is opposite to that of (3). That the two do not agree is as it should be. The reason is the anticommutation relation itself. Since $1\ne4$, $\{\hat c_1,\hat c_4^\dagger\}=\delta_{14}=0$; that is, as an operator identity

$$ \hat c_4^\dagger\hat c_1=-\,\hat c_1\hat c_4^\dagger $$

holds. Acting on any state, the two give results that differ only by a sign, and (3) and (4) confirm this in a concrete example. In terms of occupation numbers, the mechanism is the following: in (3) the electron in state 1 has already been removed before $\hat c_4^\dagger$ acts, so there is one fewer overtaking, and the sign is shifted by one flip accordingly—a direct manifestation of the rule “the sign flips each time we overtake one companion on the left.”

Physical meaning. The sign $-$ in (3) by itself has no physical meaning. The phase of an occupation-number state depends on the convention “in order of number, from the left,” and changes if the convention is changed. What carries physical content is the relative sign of (3) and (4), that is, the anticommutation relation. And as long as the operation that moves a particle is used in the fixed form of the “pair” $\hat c_4^\dagger\hat c_1$, the intermediate factors $(-1)^{S}$ necessarily come in pairs and cancel in the calculation of observables—this is the payoff of rule of thumb 1 in Section B.2.4, “Treat the pair $\hat c^\dagger\hat c$ as a unit.”

Exercise B.3: Field operators and the current density

Solution

(1) We show that $\hat N=\int\dd^3x\,\hat\psi^\dagger\hat\psi$ becomes $\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hat n_{\kk\sigma}$ in the plane-wave basis. Substituting the plane-wave expansion (Section B.4.3)

$$ \hat\psi_\sigma(\xx)=\frac{1}{\sqrt V}\sum_{\kk}\ee^{\ii\kk\cdot\xx}\,\hat c_{\kk\sigma}, \qquad \hat\psi_\sigma^\dagger(\xx)=\frac{1}{\sqrt V}\sum_{\kk}\ee^{-\ii\kk\cdot\xx}\,\hat c_{\kk\sigma}^\dagger $$

(writing out the spin sum as well) gives:

$$ \hat N=\sum_\sigma\int_V\dd^3x\;\hat\psi_\sigma^\dagger(\xx)\hat\psi_\sigma(\xx) =\sum_\sigma\sum_{\kk\kk'}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk'\sigma}\; \underbrace{\frac1V\int_V\dd^3x\;\ee^{\ii(\kk'-\kk)\cdot\xx}}_{=\,\delta_{\kk\kk'}} =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk\sigma} =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hat n_{\kk\sigma} $$

The orthogonality of plane waves (Section B.4.3) simply collapses the sum over $\kk'$. “Collecting the density over all of space gives the total particle number” becomes, in the language of plane waves, “add up the occupation numbers of all $\kk$.”

(2) We express the current-density operator $\hat{\bm j}(\xx)=\frac{\hbar}{2m\ii}\left(\hat\psi^\dagger\nabla\hat\psi-(\nabla\hat\psi^\dagger)\hat\psi\right)$ in terms of plane waves and find its Fourier component. Let us carry out the differentiation first:

$$ \nabla\hat\psi_\sigma(\xx)=\frac{1}{\sqrt V}\sum_{\kk'}(\ii\kk')\,\ee^{\ii\kk'\cdot\xx}\,\hat c_{\kk'\sigma}, \qquad \nabla\hat\psi_\sigma^\dagger(\xx)=\frac{1}{\sqrt V}\sum_{\kk}(-\ii\kk)\,\ee^{-\ii\kk\cdot\xx}\,\hat c_{\kk\sigma}^\dagger $$

Putting these together, the two terms are proportional to the same operator $\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk'\sigma}$ and merge into one:

$$ \hat{\bm j}(\xx) =\frac{\hbar}{2m\ii}\cdot\frac1V\sum_{\kk\kk'\sigma} \left[\ii\kk'-(-\ii\kk)\right]\ee^{\ii(\kk'-\kk)\cdot\xx}\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk'\sigma} =\frac{\hbar}{2m}\cdot\frac1V\sum_{\kk\kk'\sigma} \left(\kk+\kk'\right)\ee^{\ii(\kk'-\kk)\cdot\xx}\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk'\sigma} $$

On the way we used $\frac{1}{2\ii}\left[\ii\kk'+\ii\kk\right]=\frac{\kk+\kk'}{2}$ (this is the calculation in the hint). Inserting this into the definition of the Fourier component, $\hat{\bm j}_\qq=\int\dd^3x\,\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}\hat{\bm j}(\xx)$, the spatial integral becomes

$$ \int_V\dd^3x\;\ee^{\ii(\kk'-\kk-\qq)\cdot\xx}=V\,\delta_{\kk',\,\kk+\qq} $$

so the factor $V$ cancels and the sum over $\kk'$ collapses onto $\kk'=\kk+\qq$:

$$ \hat{\bm j}_\qq =\frac{\hbar}{2m}\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\kk+(\kk+\qq)\right)\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma} =\frac{\hbar}{m}\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\kk+\frac{\qq}{2}\right)\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma} $$

This is the expression we were to show. It has the same structure as the density $\hat\rho_\qq=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}$ (Derivation 15)—“annihilate $\kk+\qq$ and create $\kk$”—multiplied by a velocity weight $\frac{\hbar}{m}(\kk+\qq/2)$, the velocity corresponding to the average wave vector of the initial state $\kk+\qq$ and the final state $\kk$.

(3) Setting $\qq\to0$, the weight becomes $\frac{\hbar\kk}{m}$ and the operator becomes diagonal:

$$ \hat{\bm j}_0=\frac{\hbar}{m}\sum_{\kk\sigma}\kk\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk\sigma} =\frac1m\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hbar\kk\,\hat n_{\kk\sigma} =\frac{\hat{\bm P}}{m} $$

The last equality uses the total momentum $\hat{\bm P}=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\hbar\kk\,\hat n_{\kk\sigma}$ derived in Section B.5.3. The uniform ($\qq=0$) component of the current is “the total momentum divided by the mass,” that is, the sum of the velocities of the whole system, and it has the same content as $\sum_\alpha\bm v_\alpha=\bm P_{\text{tot}}/m$ of classical mechanics.

(4) We derive the continuity equation $\dot{\hat\rho}_\qq+\ii\qq\cdot\hat{\bm j}_\qq=0$ from the Heisenberg equation of motion. The commutator obtained in Derivation 17 of Section B.7.4 is

$$ \left[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0\right] =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk\right) \hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma} $$

Computing the energy difference, as in the hint, we find

$$ \epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk =\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left[(\kk+\qq)^2-k^2\right] =\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(2\kk\cdot\qq+q^2\right) =\frac{\hbar^2}{m}\,\qq\cdot\left(\kk+\frac{\qq}{2}\right) $$

and exactly the same combination $\kk+\qq/2$ as in the weight of $\hat{\bm j}_\qq$ in (2) appears. Substituting this gives

$$ \left[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0\right] =\hbar\,\qq\cdot\left[\frac{\hbar}{m}\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\kk+\frac{\qq}{2}\right)\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}\right] =\hbar\,\qq\cdot\hat{\bm j}_\qq $$

Inserting this into the Heisenberg equation of motion $\ii\hbar\,\dot{\hat\rho}_\qq=[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0]$ and dividing by $\ii\hbar$, we obtain

$$ \dot{\hat\rho}_\qq=\frac{1}{\ii\hbar}\cdot\hbar\,\qq\cdot\hat{\bm j}_\qq =-\ii\,\qq\cdot\hat{\bm j}_\qq \qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad \dot{\hat\rho}_\qq+\ii\qq\cdot\hat{\bm j}_\qq=0 $$

This is the continuity equation we were to show.

Check (consistency with real space). Multiplying the real-space continuity equation $\partial_t\hat\rho(\xx)+\nabla\cdot\hat{\bm j}(\xx)=0$ by $\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}$, integrating, and integrating the second term by parts (the boundary term vanishes by the periodic boundary conditions), we obtain

$$ \int\dd^3x\;\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}\,\nabla\cdot\hat{\bm j} =-\int\dd^3x\;\left(\nabla\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}\right)\cdot\hat{\bm j} =+\ii\qq\cdot\hat{\bm j}_\qq $$

so that we indeed arrive at the form $\dot{\hat\rho}_\qq+\ii\qq\cdot\hat{\bm j}_\qq=0$. This agrees, even in sign, with the Fourier convention of this book, $\hat\rho_\qq=\int\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}\hat\rho(\xx)\,\dd^3x$ (Derivation 15).

Physical meaning. This confirms that charge conservation (particle-number conservation) holds not at the level of expectation values but at the level of operators. Moreover, it is not broken even when interactions are included. As we saw in Section B.7.3, the Coulomb interaction is built from the density alone, so $[\hat\rho_\qq,\hat V]=0$; hence even for $\Ham=\Ham_0+\hat V$ we have $[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham]=[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0]$, and the continuity equation above holds unchanged. When we connect the longitudinal dielectric function and the density response in Chapter 9, and again when we use the $f$-sum rule (Exercise B.4), this operator identity serves as the foundation.

Exercise B.4: Commutators of the density operator and the $f$-sum rule

Solution

(1) We show that two density operators without a $\dagger$ commute, $[\hat\rho_\qq,\hat\rho_{\qq'}]=0$, by means of Lemma B.1 (the calculation parallels the case with a $\dagger$ in Derivation 16). The spin index does not affect the result, so we omit it. From the expression in Derivation 15, we have

$$ \hat\rho_\qq=\sum_\kk\hat c^\dagger_\kk\hat c_{\kk+\qq}, \qquad \hat\rho_{\qq'}=\sum_{\kk'}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'}\hat c_{\kk'+\qq'} $$

In Lemma B.1, $[\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_b,\hat a_c^\dagger\hat a_d]=\delta_{bc}\hat a_a^\dagger\hat a_d-\delta_{ad}\hat a_c^\dagger\hat a_b$, we set $a=\kk$, $b=\kk+\qq$, $c=\kk'$, and $d=\kk'+\qq'$, which gives

$$ \left[\hat c^\dagger_\kk\hat c_{\kk+\qq},\;\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'}\hat c_{\kk'+\qq'}\right] =\delta_{\kk+\qq,\,\kk'}\;\hat c^\dagger_\kk\hat c_{\kk'+\qq'} -\delta_{\kk,\,\kk'+\qq'}\;\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'}\hat c_{\kk+\qq} $$

First term: The delta requires $\kk'=\kk+\qq$, so the sum over $\kk'$ collapses and we get

$$ \sum_\kk\hat c^\dagger_\kk\,\hat c_{\kk+\qq+\qq'}=\hat\rho_{\qq+\qq'} $$

Second term: The delta requires $\kk'=\kk-\qq'$, so we get

$$ -\sum_\kk\hat c^\dagger_{\kk-\qq'}\hat c_{\kk+\qq} $$

Now relabeling the dummy index as $\kk\to\kk+\qq'$, we find $-\sum_\kk\hat c^\dagger_{\kk}\hat c_{\kk+\qq+\qq'}=-\hat\rho_{\qq+\qq'}$. Adding the two terms, we obtain

$$ \left[\hat\rho_\qq,\;\hat\rho_{\qq'}\right]=\hat\rho_{\qq+\qq'}-\hat\rho_{\qq+\qq'}=0 $$

Note also that, if we recall $\hat\rho_{\qq'}^\dagger=\hat\rho_{-\qq'}$ from Derivation 15, this result has the same content as $[\hat\rho_\qq,\hat\rho_{\qq'}^\dagger]=0$ of Derivation 16 with $\qq'\to-\qq'$; the two are consistent.

(2) Since the interaction is built from the density alone, we argue that $[\hat\rho_\qq,\hat V]=0$. The starting point is the field-operator representation in Derivation 12

$$ \hat V=\frac12\int\dd^3x\int\dd^3x'\; v(\xx,\xx')\;\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx)\,\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx')\,\hat\psi(\xx')\,\hat\psi(\xx) $$

together with the rearrangement identity derived there, $\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx)\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx')\hat\psi(\xx')\hat\psi(\xx)=\hat\rho(\xx)\hat\rho(\xx')-\delta(\xx-\xx')\hat\rho(\xx)$. Substituting this, we obtain

$$ \hat V=\frac12\int\dd^3x\int\dd^3x'\;v(\xx,\xx')\,\hat\rho(\xx)\hat\rho(\xx') \;-\;\frac12\int\dd^3x\;v(\xx,\xx)\,\hat\rho(\xx) $$

The second term (the subtraction of the self-interaction) is also, as an operator, merely linear in $\hat\rho(\xx)$: when the $x'$ integral is collapsed with the delta function, the coefficient $v(\xx,\xx)$ just comes out in front as a c-number. In other words, $\hat V$ is built, as a whole, from the density operator $\hat\rho(\xx)$ alone. (For the Coulomb interaction the coefficient $v(\xx,\xx)=v(0)$ diverges as $r\to0$, so, strictly speaking, this is a formal term after regularizing the short-distance side; but the coefficient is, after all, a c-number, and the argument here depends only on the structure of the operators.)

Next we check, in terms of field operators, that the density operators commute with one another. As the continuous version of Lemma B.1, exactly the same calculation as in Derivation 6, which uses the identity of Section B.3.3 twice, gives

$$ \left[\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx)\hat\psi(\xx),\;\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx')\hat\psi(\xx')\right] =\delta(\xx-\xx')\,\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx)\hat\psi(\xx') -\delta(\xx'-\xx)\,\hat\psi^\dagger(\xx')\hat\psi(\xx) $$

(the only change is that $\delta_{bc}$ and $\delta_{ad}$ are replaced by delta functions). Thanks to the delta functions, both terms on the right-hand side contribute only when $\xx=\xx'$, and then the two terms are the same operator, so the difference vanishes identically:

$$ \left[\hat\rho(\xx),\;\hat\rho(\xx')\right]=0 $$

This is, in terms of first quantization, the obvious fact that “functions of position alone commute with one another.” Since $\hat\rho_\qq=\int\dd^3x\,\ee^{-\ii\qq\cdot\xx}\hat\rho(\xx)$ is a linear combination of the $\hat\rho(\xx)$, it commutes with everything that commutes with $\hat\rho(\xx)$. As we saw above, $\hat V$ is a quadratic plus linear expression in $\hat\rho$, so we reach the conclusion

$$ \left[\hat\rho_\qq,\;\hat V\right]=0 $$

With this, we see that the entire time evolution of $\hat\rho_\qq$ is carried by the kinetic-energy term $\Ham_0$. We use this in (3) below.

(3) We reproduce the right-hand side of the $f$-sum rule, $\braket{[[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0],\hat\rho_\qq^\dagger]}=N\hbar^2q^2/m$, following Derivation 18.

Step 1 (compute the double commutator as an operator). By Derivation 17, $[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0]=\sum_{\kk\sigma}\Delta_\kk\,\hat c^\dagger_{\kk\sigma}\hat c_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}$ with $\Delta_\kk\equiv\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk$. We compute its commutator with $\hat\rho_\qq^\dagger=\sum_{\kk'\sigma'}\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'+\qq,\sigma'}\hat c_{\kk'\sigma'}$ by means of Lemma B.1, with $a=\kk$, $b=\kk+\qq$, $c=\kk'+\qq$, and $d=\kk'$:

$$ \left[\hat c^\dagger_\kk\hat c_{\kk+\qq},\;\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'+\qq}\hat c_{\kk'}\right] =\delta_{\kk+\qq,\,\kk'+\qq}\;\hat c^\dagger_\kk\hat c_{\kk'} -\delta_{\kk,\,\kk'}\;\hat c^\dagger_{\kk'+\qq}\hat c_{\kk+\qq} $$

This time both deltas require $\kk'=\kk$, so together they give $\delta_{\kk\kk'}\left(\hat n_\kk-\hat n_{\kk+\qq}\right)$. Multiplying by $\Delta_\kk$ and summing, we obtain

$$ \left[\left[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0\right],\hat\rho_\qq^\dagger\right] =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk\right) \left(\hat n_{\kk\sigma}-\hat n_{\kk+\qq,\sigma}\right) $$

Step 2 (take the expectation value and shift the dummy index). We write $\braket{\hat n_{\kk\sigma}}=n_\kk$. Relabeling $\kk\to\kk-\qq$ in the second term, the coefficient becomes $\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk\to\epsilon_{\kk}-\epsilon_{\kk-\qq}$ and the distribution becomes $n_{\kk+\qq}\to n_{\kk}$, so

$$ \braket{\cdots} =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk\right)n_\kk -\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\epsilon_\kk-\epsilon_{\kk-\qq}\right)n_\kk =\sum_{\kk\sigma}\left(\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\epsilon_{\kk-\qq}-2\epsilon_\kk\right)n_\kk $$

Step 3 (the $\kk$ dependence disappears). Inserting the parabolic dispersion $\epsilon_\kk=\hbar^2k^2/2m$, we get

$$ \epsilon_{\kk+\qq}+\epsilon_{\kk-\qq}-2\epsilon_\kk =\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left[\left(k^2+2\kk\cdot\qq+q^2\right)+\left(k^2-2\kk\cdot\qq+q^2\right)-2k^2\right] =\frac{\hbar^2q^2}{m} $$

The mechanism by which the $\kk$ dependence disappears has two stages. First, the $k^2$ terms vanish because $+1+1-2=0$. Next, the cross terms linear in $\kk$, $2\kk\cdot\qq$, appear as $+2\kk\cdot\qq$ from the $+\qq$ shift and as $-2\kk\cdot\qq$ from the $-\qq$ shift, with opposite signs, and so they cancel (the hint's “the cross terms cancel between the $\pm$ contributions”). In short, the left-hand side is the second difference of a quadratic function, and the second difference of a quadratic function is a constant independent of the variable—the curvature $\hbar^2/m$ of the parabola multiplied by $q^2$. The remaining sum does not depend on the details of the distribution, and we obtain

$$ \braket{\left[\left[\hat\rho_\qq,\Ham_0\right],\hat\rho_\qq^\dagger\right]} =\frac{\hbar^2q^2}{m}\sum_{\kk\sigma}n_\kk=\frac{N\hbar^2q^2}{m} $$

Combined with $[\hat\rho_\qq,\hat V]=0$ from (2), the left-hand side is unchanged even if we replace $\Ham_0\to\Ham_0+\hat V$. Moreover, since Step 3 never used the form of $n_\kk$, the same value results for a distribution distorted by the interaction, and also at finite temperature, as long as $\sum_\kk n_\kk=N$ holds. This is the mathematical heart of the statement “the $f$-sum rule is exact, independent of the interaction.”

(4) We evaluate the value per unit volume, $n\hbar^2q^2/m$, in SI units for Na ($n=2.65\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}$, Table B.2) and $q=0.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}=0.5\times10^{10}\ \mathrm{m^{-1}}$. First, the factor per electron is

$$ \frac{\hbar^2q^2}{m} =\frac{\left(1.0546\times10^{-34}\times0.5\times10^{10}\right)^2}{9.109\times10^{-31}} =3.05\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J} \quad(=1.90\ \mathrm{eV}) $$

(As a check, this is twice $\hbar^2q^2/2m=0.95\ \mathrm{eV}$, which is the same quantity as in Exercise B.5 (2).) Multiplying by the number density gives

$$ n\,\frac{\hbar^2q^2}{m} =2.65\times10^{28}\ \mathrm{m^{-3}}\times3.05\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J} \simeq8.1\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{J\,m^{-3}} $$

The dimension is (energy) $\times$ (inverse volume) = $\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$, so this is the spectral weight of the density response counted per unit volume. About $8\times10^9\ \mathrm{J\,m^{-3}}$—it looks like a large number at first sight, but per electron it is only $1.9\ \mathrm{eV}$, which is of the same order as the electronic energy scales of a metal ($\epsilon_F=3.24\ \mathrm{eV}$, $\hbar\omega_p=6.0\ \mathrm{eV}$). In the inelastic scattering experiments of Chapter 12, when the spectrum is integrated with weight $\omega$, the result must converge to this value. (Note, however, that the “one-sided $f$-sum rule,” in which only the positive-frequency side is integrated, gives half of this, $n\hbar^2q^2/2m$. As noted about the factor of 2 in the box “The power of the sum rule” in Section B.7.4, first check which of the two values you are comparing with.)

Exercise B.5: The particle–hole continuum and numerical values

Solution

(1) We re-derive the boundaries of the continuum, $\hbar\omega_\pm(q)=\hbar^2q^2/2m\pm\hbar qv_F$, and explain in words why the lower edge touches $\omega=0$ for $q<2k_F$. As in Derivation 19, the excitation energy of a particle–hole pair is

$$ \hbar\omega_{\kk\qq}=\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk =\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(q^2+2kq\cos\theta\right), \qquad \theta\equiv\angle(\kk,\qq) $$

Here the allowed $\kk$ are limited to those that satisfy “the starting point is inside the sphere: $k<k_F$” and “the end point is outside the sphere: $\abs{\kk+\qq}>k_F$.”

Upper limit. Since $\hbar\omega$ is an increasing function of $k\cos\theta$, the maximum is attained at $\cos\theta=+1$ ($\kk\parallel\qq$) and $k\to k_F$. Then the end point has $\abs{\kk+\qq}=k_F+q>k_F$, which satisfies the condition, so this value is indeed reachable, and

$$ \hbar\omega_+(q)=\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(q^2+2k_Fq\right) =\frac{\hbar^2q^2}{2m}+\hbar qv_F, \qquad v_F=\frac{\hbar k_F}{m} $$

Lower limit ($q>2k_F$). If we choose $\cos\theta=-1$, then $\abs{\kk+\qq}=q-k>2k_F-k_F=k_F$, so for any starting point the end point necessarily lies outside the sphere. The minimum occurs at $\cos\theta=-1$ and $k\to k_F$, where

$$ \hbar\omega_-(q)=\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}\left(q^2-2k_Fq\right) =\frac{\hbar^2q^2}{2m}-\hbar qv_F\;>\;0 $$

The lower limit ($q<2k_F$) is zero. In terms of formulas, if we choose $\cos\theta=-q/2k$, then $\abs{\kk+\qq}^2=k^2+2kq\cos\theta+q^2=k^2$; that is, the end point returns to the spherical surface of the same radius as the starting point, and $\hbar\omega=0$. The condition for this angle to exist is $\abs{-q/2k}\le1$, which for $k\to k_F$ reads $q\le2k_F$.

In words (Figure B.3): $\hat\rho_\qq^\dagger$ is the operation of picking up an electron inside the Fermi sphere and translating it by $\qq$, so it helps to draw the “original Fermi sphere” and the “Fermi sphere shifted by $\qq$” on top of each other. If $q<2k_F$, the two spherical surfaces intersect. Near the ring where they intersect, an electron just inside the Fermi surface can be moved to a place that, after the shift, is again almost on the Fermi surface. Since both the starting point and the end point have energy of almost $\epsilon_F$, the cost of the excitation can be made arbitrarily small—“on the Fermi surface, excitations can be made at almost no cost.” This is the meaning of the lower limit $0$. When $q>2k_F$, the shifted sphere lies completely outside the original one, the line of intersection disappears, and however we move an electron, we must pay a finite energy $\hbar\omega_-$. This is why, in Figure B.4, the lower edge rises from $q=2k_F$.

(2) We find the upper and lower edges of the continuum at $q=0.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ for Al ($v_F=2.03\times10^6\ \mathrm{m/s}$, $k_F=1.75\ \text{Å}^{-1}$, Table B.2). We evaluate the two terms separately.

$$ \frac{\hbar^2q^2}{2m} =\frac{\left(1.0546\times10^{-34}\times0.5\times10^{10}\right)^2}{2\times9.109\times10^{-31}} =1.53\times10^{-19}\ \mathrm{J}=0.95\ \mathrm{eV} $$ $$ \hbar qv_F=1.0546\times10^{-34}\times0.5\times10^{10}\times2.03\times10^6 =1.07\times10^{-18}\ \mathrm{J}=6.68\ \mathrm{eV} $$

Therefore the upper edge is given by

$$ \hbar\omega_+=0.95+6.68=7.63\ \mathrm{eV}\simeq7.6\ \mathrm{eV} $$

The lower edge is $0$, since $q=0.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ is much smaller than $2k_F=3.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ and hence the case distinction in (1) applies (the formal value $\hbar\omega_-=0.95-6.68=-5.7\ \mathrm{eV}$ is negative and has no meaning as a boundary). That is, at this $q$ the continuum is a band $0\le\hbar\omega\le7.6\ \mathrm{eV}$.

(3) Comparing with the plasmon of Al, $\hbar\omega_p=15.8\ \mathrm{eV}$, we find

$$ 15.8\ \mathrm{eV}\;>\;7.6\ \mathrm{eV}=\hbar\omega_+(q) $$

so at $q=0.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ the plasmon lies outside the continuum (above it). Since no particle–hole pair exists into which it could decay while conserving energy and momentum, Landau damping does not occur, and the plasmon is observed (within this approximation) as a sharp, undamped peak.

(4) Approximating the plasmon dispersion by $\omega\simeq\omega_p$ (a horizontal line independent of $q$), we find the critical wave number $q_c$ at which it meets the upper edge of the continuum, from $\hbar\omega_+(q_c)=\hbar\omega_p$. If $q$ is measured in units of Å$^{-1}$, the numbers of (2) become the coefficients directly: $\hbar^2/2m=3.81\ \mathrm{eV\,\text{Å}^2}$ and $\hbar v_F=13.4\ \mathrm{eV\,\text{Å}}$, so

$$ 3.81\,q_c^2+13.4\,q_c=15.8 \qquad(q_c\ \text{is in Å}^{-1}\ \text{units}) $$

This is a quadratic equation (the same equation as the hint's $0.95(q/0.5)^2+6.7(q/0.5)=15.8$). Taking the positive root, we get

$$ q_c=\frac{-13.4+\sqrt{13.4^2+4\times3.81\times15.8}}{2\times3.81} =\frac{-13.4+20.5}{7.62} \simeq0.93\ \text{Å}^{-1} $$

That is, $q_c\approx0.9\ \text{Å}^{-1}$. This is only a little under 30% of $2k_F=3.5\ \text{Å}^{-1}$:

$$ \frac{q_c}{2k_F}\simeq\frac{0.93}{3.5}\simeq0.27 $$

Physical meaning. The plasmon plunges into the particle–hole continuum long before reaching the edge of the Fermi sphere—at about $q_c\sim k_F/2$. From the moment it enters, a channel opens for decay into a single particle–hole pair (Landau damping), and the peak rapidly broadens. In electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS), the plasmon peak of Al abruptly thickens around $q\sim1\ \text{Å}^{-1}$ and eventually dissolves into the continuum and can no longer be seen, precisely because this $q_c$ has been exceeded (Chapters 11 and 12). Note that the actual plasmon has a positive dispersion ($\omega(q)$ rises with $q$; the purple line in Figure B.4), so the true point of entry is shifted to a slightly larger $q$ than the above estimate, but the order-of-magnitude argument does not change.

Common Mistake: Do not mechanically take $\hbar\omega_-$ as the lower end

If you memorize the boundary formula $\hbar\omega_\pm=\hbar^2q^2/2m\pm\hbar qv_F$, it is a common mistake to write $\hbar\omega_-$ (which is negative in this case) as the “lower end” even for $q<2k_F$. The excitation energy $\epsilon_{\kk+\qq}-\epsilon_\kk$ is always nonnegative under the condition that the final point lies outside the sphere and the initial point inside it, and the negative $\hbar\omega_-$ merely indicates that “the endpoint $\cos\theta=-1$, $k=k_F$ is caught by the constraint $\abs{\kk+\qq}>k_F$ and cannot be realized.” The lower end is $0$ for $q<2k_F$, and becomes $\hbar\omega_-$ only for $q>2k_F$ (as the cautionary note in the summary of Section B.11.1 says). When you draw Figure B.4, be sure to check that the lower-end curve rises from $q=2k_F$.

Appendix C: How to Read Feynman Diagrams

Exercise C.1: Wick's theorem and signs

Solution

The starting point is the string of six operators that appears in the numerator of the first-order perturbation:

$$ \hat\psi^\dagger_\lambda(x_1)\,\hat\psi^\dagger_\mu(x_1')\,\hat\psi_{\mu'}(x_1')\,\hat\psi_{\lambda'}(x_1)\,\hat\psi_\alpha(x)\,\hat\psi^\dagger_\beta(y) $$

Number the positions $1,2,3,4,5,6$ from the left ($1=\hat\psi^\dagger_\lambda$, $2=\hat\psi^\dagger_\mu$, $3=\hat\psi_{\mu'}$, $4=\hat\psi_{\lambda'}$, $5=\hat\psi_\alpha$, $6=\hat\psi^\dagger_\beta$). Inside the $T$-product, the fermion operators may be freely rearranged, producing a factor $-1$ for each transposition of two of them. If we write down which permutation of the original position numbers the rearranged string corresponds to, and count its number of inversions (the number of pairs whose order is reversed), we can determine the parity of the transpositions.

(1) The contraction (C) is $(\hat\psi_\alpha\hat\psi^\dagger_\lambda)(\hat\psi_{\lambda'}\hat\psi^\dagger_\mu)(\hat\psi_{\mu'}\hat\psi^\dagger_\beta)$. The target arrangement, in which the contracted pairs are adjacent, is

$$ \hat\psi_\alpha\,\hat\psi^\dagger_\lambda\;\big|\;\hat\psi_{\lambda'}\,\hat\psi^\dagger_\mu\;\big|\;\hat\psi_{\mu'}\,\hat\psi^\dagger_\beta \qquad\text{(original position numbers: }5,1,4,2,3,6\text{)} $$

Count the inversions of the permutation $(5,1,4,2,3,6)$: the smaller numbers that come after $5$ are $1,4,2,3$, four of them; the smaller numbers after $4$ are $2,3$, two of them; the others contribute zero. The total is $4+2=6$, an even number. Hence the sign is $+1$. Since (C) is a diagram with no loop (an exchange term), this is consistent with “$(-1)^{F}=(-1)^0=+1$.”

(2) The contraction (D) is $(\hat\psi_\alpha\hat\psi^\dagger_\lambda)(\hat\psi_{\lambda'}\hat\psi^\dagger_\beta)(\hat\psi_{\mu'}\hat\psi^\dagger_\mu)$, and the last pair is the density loop at $x_1'$. In terms of the original position numbers, the target arrangement is $(5,1,4,6,3,2)$. Inversions: the smaller numbers after $5$ are $1,4,3,2$, giving four; after $4$ come $3,2$, giving two; after $6$ come $3,2$, giving two; after $3$ comes $2$, giving one. The total is $4+2+2+1=9$, an odd number. Hence the sign is $-1$. The only difference from (C) is that $\hat\psi_{\mu'}$ and $\hat\psi^\dagger_\mu$ (positions $3$ and $2$) are made into a closed pair, and this operation of “closing a line into a loop” flips the parity of the transpositions. This is the origin of the factor $-1$ per loop.

(3) The contraction (A) is $(\hat\psi_\alpha\hat\psi^\dagger_\beta)(\hat\psi_{\lambda'}\hat\psi^\dagger_\lambda)(\hat\psi_{\mu'}\hat\psi^\dagger_\mu)$: a free external line and two density loops, at $x_1$ and $x_1'$. The target arrangement is $(5,6,4,1,3,2)$. Inversions: after $5$ there are the four smaller numbers $4,1,3,2$; after $6$ there are the four smaller numbers $4,1,3,2$; after $4$ there are the three smaller numbers $1,3,2$; after $3$ there is the one smaller number $2$. The total is $4+4+3+1=12$, an even number, so the sign is $+1$. Two loops give $(-1)^2=+1$—exactly consistent with the rule “$-1$ per loop.” ∎

Check. Counting inversions is easy to get wrong by missing a pair, so we recount the three permutations mechanically and obtain $6,\ 9,\ 12$, that is, even, odd, even. This agrees with the hand count.

Exercise C.2: The anharmonic oscillator and the continuum

Solution

(1) We show that $\bra{n}(\hat a+\hat a^\dagger)^4\ket{n}=6n^2+6n+3$. Since $\hat A\equiv\hat a+\hat a^\dagger$ moves the level by $\pm1$, the contributions to the diagonal element come from the $\binom42=6$ paths with two raisings and two lowerings (Figure C.21 in Derivation C.6). The factor of each path (a raising gives $\sqrt{\ell+1}$, a lowering gives $\sqrt{\ell}$, where $\ell$ is the preceding level) is

$$ \begin{aligned} {+}{+}{-}{-}&:\ (n{+}1)(n{+}2), & {+}{-}{+}{-}&:\ (n{+}1)^2, & {+}{-}{-}{+}&:\ n(n{+}1),\\ {-}{+}{+}{-}&:\ n(n{+}1), & {-}{+}{-}{+}&:\ n^2, & {-}{-}{+}{+}&:\ n(n{-}1), \end{aligned} $$

and the sum is

$$ (n{+}1)(n{+}2)+(n{+}1)^2+2n(n{+}1)+n^2+n(n{-}1)=6n^2+6n+3 . $$

The Wick expansion (Section C.9.3) gives the same answer: $\hat A^4=\,:\!\hat A^4\!:+6:\!\hat A^2\!:+3$, where the diagonal element of $:\!\hat A^2\!:$ is $2n$ and that of $:\!\hat A^4\!:$ is $\binom42\bra{\phi_n}\hat a^{\dagger2}\hat a^2\ket{\phi_n}=6n(n{-}1)$, so that $6n(n{-}1)+6\cdot2n+3=6n^2+6n+3$, in agreement. ∎

(2) We take the result $\tilde\omega_1=\omega_0+3\lambda\hbar/(m^2\omega_0^2)$ of Derivation C.1 and make it dimensionless with the oscillator length $x_0\equiv\sqrt{\hbar/m\omega_0}$, as the problem specifies. Since $m\omega_0^2x_0^2=\hbar\omega_0$ is an energy, the natural yardstick for measuring $\lambda$ (dimension: energy/length$^4$) is $m\omega_0^2x_0^2/x_0^4=m^2\omega_0^3/\hbar$. We therefore define

$$ \bar\lambda\equiv\frac{\lambda}{m\omega_0^2x_0^2/x_0^4}=\frac{\lambda x_0^4}{\hbar\omega_0}=\frac{\lambda\hbar}{m^2\omega_0^3} $$

and, since $\tilde\omega_1-\omega_0=\dfrac{3\lambda\hbar}{m^2\omega_0^2}=3\,\dfrac{\lambda\hbar}{m^2\omega_0^3}\,\omega_0=3\bar\lambda\,\omega_0$, we can write

$$ \tilde\omega_1=\omega_0\left(1+3\bar\lambda\right)+O(\bar\lambda^2) $$

The parameter $\bar\lambda$, which measures “how much the energy of the anharmonic term matters over the spread $x_0$ of the zero-point oscillation,” is the only small parameter, and the condition for the validity of perturbation theory can be read off at a glance as $\bar\lambda\ll1$. Note also that if we build the same yardstick $m\omega_0^2x_0'^2/x_0'^4$ with the zero-point width $x_0'=\sqrt{\hbar/2m\omega}$ of Section C.9 (which is $1/\sqrt2$ times the $x_0$ of this problem), we obtain $\bar\lambda'=\bar\lambda/2$ and $\tilde\omega_1=\omega_0(1+6\bar\lambda')$: only the coefficient changes (the physics is the same).

(3) Consider the Fano–Anderson model with $\epsilon_k$ uniformly distributed over $[-D,D]$ (density of states $\rho$) and $V_k=V$ constant. Converting the sum into an integral in the exact self-energy of Derivation C.2, we obtain

$$ \Sigma(E)=\abs{V}^2\rho\int_{-D}^{D}\frac{\dd\epsilon}{E-\epsilon+\ii\eta} =\abs{V}^2\rho\left[\ln\abs{\frac{E+D}{E-D}}-\ii\pi\right]\qquad(\abs{E}<D) $$

(the real part is the principal-value integral $\displaystyle\PV\!\int_{-D}^D\frac{\dd\epsilon}{E-\epsilon}=\ln\abs{\frac{E+D}{E-D}}$, and the imaginary part is the $-\ii\pi\delta$ of the Sokhotski–Plemelj formula picked up at $\epsilon=E$). Therefore

$$ \Delta(E)=\abs{V}^2\rho\,\ln\abs{\frac{E+D}{E-D}},\qquad \Gamma(E)=-2\Im\Sigma=2\pi\abs{V}^2\rho\qquad(\abs{E}<D). $$

For $\abs{E}\ll D$ we have $\ln\abs{(E+D)/(E-D)}\simeq2E/D\to0$, so that $\Delta\simeq0$: if the continuum extends symmetrically above and below $E$, the push-down and the push-up cancel and the shift vanishes. The width is $\Gamma=2\pi\abs{V}^2\rho$, which is Fermi's golden rule itself.

(4) If $D$ is finite, $\Delta(E)$ depends on $E$. We check that this can be reproduced from the Kramers–Kronig (KK) relations. Since $\Sigma(E)$ is a retarded function (all its poles lie just below the real axis), the KK relations of Chapter 4 can be used as they are, and

$$ \Delta(E)=\frac{1}{\pi}\,\PV\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(E')/2}{E-E'}\,\dd E' =\frac{1}{\pi}\,\PV\!\int_{-D}^{D}\frac{\pi\abs{V}^2\rho}{E-E'}\,\dd E' =\abs{V}^2\rho\,\ln\abs{\frac{E+D}{E-D}} . $$

This agrees exactly with the direct calculation in (3). The shift $\Delta$ and the width $\Gamma$ are not independent quantities; they are the real and imaginary parts of a single analytic function, tied together by causality—the lesson of Chapter 4 is replayed, just as it is, on the stage of the self-energy. ∎

Exercise C.3: Practice with the Feynman rules

Solution

(1) We write down (a)–(d) of Figure C.12 using the rules of Definition C.2. Common conventions: a factor $V$ for each wavy line; $(\ii/\hbar)^2$ because the order is $n=2$; a factor $(-g)$ ($g=2s+1$) for each closed loop; integration over the two independent internal four-momenta by $\intk{p}\intk{p'}$; and a factor $\ee^{\ii\omega\eta}$ for a line that closes on itself (or that joins the two ends of the same wavy line).

  • (a) Tadpole containing an exchange. The wavy line leaving the external line carries momentum transfer $0$, so it is $V(0)$. There is one loop ($-g$). Because an exchange blob is inserted on a line of the loop (momentum $p$), there are two $G^0$ of the same momentum $p$—$[G^0(p)]^2$—and the exchange partner gives $V(\pp-\pp')G^0(p')$. The convergence factor $\ee^{\ii\omega_p\eta}\ee^{\ii\omega_{p'}\eta}$ from the rule for closed lines (rule 6 of Definition C.2) is attached. Altogether this agrees with $\Sig_{(2a)}$ in the main text.
  • (b) Tadpole of a tadpole. Both wavy lines carry momentum transfer $0$, giving $[V(0)]^2$, and the two loops give $(-g)^2$. Since the second tadpole is attached to the first loop line, we get $[G^0(p)]^2\,\ee^{\ii\omega_p\eta}$, and the second loop gives $G^0(p')\,\ee^{\ii\omega_{p'}\eta}$. This agrees with $\Sig_{(2b)}$.
  • (c) Rainbow. The outer exchange gives $V(\kk-\pp)$ and the inner exchange gives $V(\pp-\pp')$. Since the inner blob is inserted on the internal line $p$, we get $[G^0(p)]^2$, and the inner partner (the line joining the two ends of the same wavy line) is $G^0(p')\,\ee^{\ii\omega_{p'}\eta}$. There is no loop. This agrees with $\Sig_{(2c)}$.
  • (d) Exchange with a tadpole. The exchange gives $V(\kk-\pp)$, and a tadpole ($V(0)$, with one loop giving $-g$) is attached to the internal line $p$, giving $[G^0(p)]^2\,G^0(p')\,\ee^{\ii\omega_{p'}\eta}$. This agrees with $\Sig_{(2d)}$.

The common lesson: when a first-order blob is inserted on an internal line, two $G^0$ of the same four-momentum stand side by side before and after the insertion point (because, by momentum conservation, $p$ does not change across the insertion). $[G^0(p)]^2$ is its footprint.

(2) We check that (a)–(d) of Figure C.11 are the expansion of $G^0\,\Sig_{(1)}\,G^0\,\Sig_{(1)}\,G^0$. Substituting $\Sig_{(1)}=\Sigma_H+\Sigma_x$ (tadpole + exchange), we obtain

$$ G^0\,\Sig_{(1)}G^0\,\Sig_{(1)}G^0 =G^0\big[\Sigma_H G^0\Sigma_H+\Sigma_x G^0\Sigma_x+\Sigma_H G^0\Sigma_x+\Sigma_x G^0\Sigma_H\big]G^0 $$

The four terms correspond, in order, to Figure C.11 (a) tadpole–tadpole, (b) exchange–exchange, and (c), (d) the two mixed ones. All of them are reducible diagrams that split into two when one solid line is cut, and they do not enter the proper self-energy $\Sig_{(2)}$—they are terms that the Dyson equation generates automatically.

(3) The small-$q$ divergence of Figure C.12(e). Putting $V(\qq)=4\pi e^2/q^2$ into $\Sig_{(2e)}$, the small-$q$ side of the $q$ integral is

$$ \int\dd^3q\,[V(\qq)]^2\times(\text{loop; as }q\to0\text{ it is finite}) \;\sim\;\int_0\dd q\,q^2\cdot\frac{1}{q^4}\times(\text{finite}) =\int_0\frac{\dd q}{q^2} $$

which gives a linear divergence at the lower limit. The polarization loop $\Pi^0(\qq,\omega)$ is finite as $q\to0$ (the Lindhard function of Chapter 10), so the cause of the divergence is solely $[V(\qq)]^2\sim q^{-4}$. If we sum the ring diagrams to infinite order and combine them as $V^2\Pi^0\to V\,[V\Pi^0/(1-V\Pi^0)]$ (the random-phase approximation, RPA), $V/\epsilon$ stays finite as $q\to0$ and the divergence disappears—in the words of Section C.7, screening cures the divergence.

(4) Examples of third-order proper self-energies (only how to draw them). (i) A ring diagram in which two polarization loops are inserted in series into a wavy line (the exchange of $v\Pi^0v\Pi^0v$). (ii) A rainbow nested three deep (an exchange inside an exchange inside an exchange). (iii) The diagram of Figure C.12(e) with a further exchange or tadpole inserted on the particle line or the hole line in the loop. (iv) The crossed exchange of Figure C.12(f) with one more wavy line crossed through it (the second order of the vertex correction). (v) A proper polarization in which two loops are connected “sideways” by a wavy line (Figure C.19, right), inserted into a wavy line. In every case, the diagram is proper if you check that “cutting any single solid line does not split it into two.” ∎

Exercise C.4: The Dyson equation and quasiparticles

Solution

(1) When $\Sig(\kk,\omega)=\Delta_\kk-\ii\gamma_\kk$ (a constant), the solution of the Dyson equation (in momentum space) is

$$ G(\kk,\omega)=\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_\kk-\Delta_\kk+\ii\gamma_\kk} $$

with the pole at $\omega=\omega_\kk+\Delta_\kk-\ii\gamma_\kk$, which lies in the lower half-plane of the complex plane. Returning to time:

$$ G(\kk,t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\dd\omega}{2\pi}\,\ee^{-\ii\omega t}\,G(\kk,\omega) $$

For $t>0$, $\ee^{-\ii\omega t}$ decays in the lower half-plane, so we close the contour there (clockwise, $-2\pi\ii$) and pick up one pole:

$$ G(\kk,t)=(-2\pi\ii)\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\ee^{-\ii(\omega_\kk+\Delta_\kk)t}\,\ee^{-\gamma_\kk t} =-\ii\,\ee^{-\ii(\omega_\kk+\Delta_\kk)t}\,\ee^{-\gamma_\kk t}\qquad(t>0) $$

For $t<0$ we close in the upper half-plane, where there is no pole, so the result is zero. Combining the two cases, we obtain

$$ \ii G(\kk,t)=\ee^{-\ii(\omega_\kk+\Delta_\kk)t}\,\ee^{-\gamma_\kk t}\,\theta(t)\qquad(k>k_F) $$

Thus the same reading as for the classical damped oscillator of Section C.1.1—the shift $\Delta_\kk$ of the real part is the renormalization, and the imaginary part $\gamma_\kk$ is the damping rate—is realized exactly as it stands.

(2) When $\Sig=\Delta+a(\omega-\omega_\kk)$ (linear in $\omega$):

$$ G=\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_\kk-\Delta-a(\omega-\omega_\kk)} =\frac{1}{(1-a)(\omega-\omega_\kk)-\Delta} =\frac{1/(1-a)}{\omega-\omega_\kk-\dfrac{\Delta}{1-a}} $$

That is, the weight of the pole is $Z=1/(1-a)$, and the shift of the pole position is $Z\Delta$. Since $a=\partial\Sig/\partial\omega$, we have $Z=[1-\partial\Sig/\partial\omega]^{-1}$—exactly the same structure as the linearization of the quasiparticle equation $\epsilon^{\rm QP}=\epsilon+Z[\Sigma(\epsilon)-V_{xc}]$, $Z=[1-\partial\Re\Sigma/\partial\omega]^{-1}$ of the main text.
