The stronger the $\omega$ dependence of $\Sig$ (the larger $\abs{a}$), the further $Z$ departs from $1$. For a physical quasiparticle pole, $a=\partial\Re\Sig/\partial\omega<0$, so $Z<1$, and the weight $1-Z$ flows into the satellites.

(3) In a uniform system a convolution becomes a product, so the series for the reducible self-energy can be summed as a geometric series:

$$ \Sigma=\Sig+\Sig G^0\Sig+\Sig G^0\Sig G^0\Sig+\cdots =\Sig\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\big(G^0\Sig\big)^n =\frac{\Sig}{1-G^0\Sig} $$

Substituting this into $G=G^0+G^0\Sigma G^0$ gives

$$ G=G^0+\frac{G^0\,\Sig\,G^0}{1-G^0\Sig} =\frac{G^0\big[(1-G^0\Sig)+G^0\Sig\big]}{1-G^0\Sig} =\frac{G^0}{1-G^0\Sig} =\frac{1}{[G^0]^{-1}-\Sig} =\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_\kk-\Sig(\kk,\omega)} $$

and the solution of the Dyson equation is reproduced. $G^0\Sigma G^0$ (the reducible $\Sigma$, with thin lines at both ends) and $[G^0]^{-1}-\Sig$ (the proper $\Sig$) are two ways of writing the same content. ∎

Exercise C.5: GW and vertex corrections (a discussion problem)

Solution (model answer)

(1) Substituting $W=v+v P^0v+vP^0vP^0v+\cdots$ into $\Sigma=\ii GW$ gives

$$ \Sigma=\underbrace{\ii Gv}_{\text{Fock exchange}} +\underbrace{\ii G\,vP^0v}_{\text{Figure C.12(e)}} +\underbrace{\ii G\,vP^0vP^0v}_{\text{third-order diagram with two rings}}+\cdots $$

Each term is “a diagram in which $0,1,2,\dots$ particle–hole loops (rings) are inserted in series into the wavy line of the exchange term.” The only second-order diagram that appears is Figure C.12(e) (= Figure C.11(i), the polarization-loop insertion), and Figure C.12(f) (crossed exchange), although it is also second order, never appears in GW with $\Gamma=1$. GW is another name for the approximation “sum the (e) series to infinite order and discard the (f) series entirely.”

(2) We insert $\Sigma\simeq\Sigma_x=\ii Gv$ into Hedin's fourth equation (the vertex equation). The functional derivative is

$$ \frac{\delta\Sigma_x(1,2)}{\delta G(4,5)}=\ii\,v(1,2)\,\delta(1,4)\,\delta(2,5) $$

(since $\Sigma_x(1,2)=\ii G(1,2)v(1^+,2)$ is linear in $G(1,2)$). Putting this into the vertex equation and evaluating the $\Gamma$ on the right-hand side with the lowest-order $\delta\delta$, we obtain the first-order vertex correction

$$ \Gamma^{(1)}(1,2;3)=\delta(1,2)\delta(1,3)+\ii\,v(1,2)\,G(1,3)\,G(3,2) $$

The second term is the correction that “draws one interaction line $v$ between the incoming leg and the outgoing leg.” Putting this into $\Sigma=\ii GW\Gamma$ and picking up the lowest order with $W\to v$, the second-order correction to $\Sigma$ is $\ii Gv\times[\ii vGG]$—an external line emits a wavy line, and inside it another wavy line spans the incoming and outgoing legs—and if we draw this on paper, Figure C.12(f), with two crossed wavy lines, appears just as it is.

(3) Putting the same $\Gamma^{(1)}$ into the polarization $P=-\ii GG\Gamma$ gives

$$ P^{(1)}=-\ii GG+(-\ii)GG\cdot\big[\ii v\,GG\big] $$

The second term is the diagram in which “one $v$ is drawn between the particle line and the hole line of the particle–hole loop,” that is, Figure C.19 (right) (the lowest-order vertex correction to the proper polarization). Physically it is the interaction between the particle and the hole (the first rung of the ladder), that is, the seed of the excitonic effect. Adding this up to infinitely many rungs gives the Bethe–Salpeter equation.

(4) By the Ward identity, $\Gamma\simeq1/Z$ near the Fermi surface, so, correctly,

$$ \Sigma=\ii GW\Gamma\simeq\ii\,(ZG_{\rm QP})\,W\,\frac1Z=\ii\,G_{\rm QP}W $$

and the $Z$ cancels. If we ignore the cancellation and compute $\Sigma=\ii(ZG_{\rm QP})W$, the self-energy comes out $Z$ times the correct value, that is, a 30% underestimation for $Z=0.7$. “If you put $Z$ into $G$, put $1/Z$ into $\Gamma$ as well. Rather than putting in only one of them, it is more correct to put in neither (the convention of $G_0W_0$ and QSGW)”—this is the numerical version of the cancellation rule of Reason 2. ∎

Exercise C.6: Constructing the matrix elements yourself

Solution

(1) $N=4$, $k=+2$: we write out the $\binom41=4$ paths with three ups and one down (up: $\sqrt{\ell+1}$, down: $\sqrt{\ell}$).

PathLevel transitionsFactor
$+++-$$n\to n{+}1\to n{+}2\to n{+}3\to n{+}2$$\sqrt{n{+}1}\sqrt{n{+}2}\sqrt{n{+}3}\cdot\sqrt{n{+}3}=(n{+}3)\sqrt{(n{+}1)(n{+}2)}$
$++-+$$n\to n{+}1\to n{+}2\to n{+}1\to n{+}2$$\sqrt{n{+}1}\sqrt{n{+}2}\cdot\sqrt{n{+}2}\cdot\sqrt{n{+}2}=(n{+}2)\sqrt{(n{+}1)(n{+}2)}$
$+-++$$n\to n{+}1\to n\to n{+}1\to n{+}2$$\sqrt{n{+}1}\cdot\sqrt{n{+}1}\cdot\sqrt{n{+}1}\sqrt{n{+}2}=(n{+}1)\sqrt{(n{+}1)(n{+}2)}$
$-+++$$n\to n{-}1\to n\to n{+}1\to n{+}2$$\sqrt{n}\cdot\sqrt{n}\cdot\sqrt{n{+}1}\sqrt{n{+}2}=n\sqrt{(n{+}1)(n{+}2)}$

All four paths have the common factor $\sqrt{(n{+}1)(n{+}2)}$, and the sum of the coefficients is $(n{+}3)+(n{+}2)+(n{+}1)+n=4n+6$. Therefore

$$ \bra{\phi_{n+2}}\hat A^4\ket{\phi_n}=2(2n+3)\sqrt{(n+1)(n+2)} . $$

(2) $k=-2$: there are four paths with one up and three downs. Similarly,

$$ \begin{aligned} {-}{-}{-}{+}&:\ (n{-}2)\sqrt{n(n{-}1)}, & {-}{-}{+}{-}&:\ (n{-}1)\sqrt{n(n{-}1)},\\ {-}{+}{-}{-}&:\ n\sqrt{n(n{-}1)}, & {+}{-}{-}{-}&:\ (n{+}1)\sqrt{n(n{-}1)}, \end{aligned} $$

The sum of the coefficients is $4n-2$, and $\bra{\phi_{n-2}}\hat A^4\ket{\phi_n}=2(2n-1)\sqrt{n(n-1)}$. This agrees with the $N=4$, $k=\pm2$ rows of Table C.8 (one can also see a structure in which reversing the sign of $k$ corresponds, roughly, to $n{+}1\leftrightarrow n$).

(3) For $k=\pm N$ there is only one path: “go up (down) $N$ times in a row.” With only one path to add, there is no room for a polynomial coefficient (the counting of the number of paths), and only the product of successive square roots $\sqrt{\phantom{n}}$

$$ \bra{\phi_{n+N}}\hat A^N\ket{\phi_n}=\sqrt{(n{+}1)(n{+}2)\cdots(n{+}N)} $$

remains. That the coefficient in front of the root is $1$ is a direct manifestation of “there is only one path.”

(4) We organize the eight terms of $\hat A^3=(\hat a+\hat a^\dagger)^3$ using the commutation rules $\hat a f(\hat n)=f(\hat n{+}1)\hat a$, $f(\hat n)\hat a^\dagger=\hat a^\dagger f(\hat n{+}1)$ and $\hat a\hat a^\dagger=\hat n{+}1$, $\hat a^\dagger\hat a=\hat n$. The three terms containing exactly one $\hat a^\dagger$ (a net lowering by one level) are

$$ \hat a\hat a\hat a^\dagger=\hat a(\hat n{+}1)=(\hat n{+}2)\hat a,\qquad \hat a\hat a^\dagger\hat a=(\hat n{+}1)\hat a,\qquad \hat a^\dagger\hat a\hat a=\hat n\hat a, $$

and their sum is $\big[(\hat n{+}2)+(\hat n{+}1)+\hat n\big]\hat a=3(\hat n{+}1)\hat a$. The three terms containing exactly one $\hat a$ (a net raising by one level) are its Hermitian conjugate, $3\hat a^\dagger(\hat n{+}1)$. The remaining terms are $\hat a^3$ and $\hat a^{\dagger3}$. Hence

$$ \hat A^3=\hat a^3+\hat a^{\dagger3}+3(\hat n+1)\hat a+3\hat a^\dagger(\hat n+1) $$

has been derived. Check (following the convention of the Pitfall box in the main text): acting on $\ket{\phi_0}$, the third term vanishes, $3\hat a^\dagger(\hat n{+}1)\ket{\phi_0}=3\ket{\phi_1}$, and $\hat a^{\dagger3}\ket{\phi_0}=\sqrt6\ket{\phi_3}$—in agreement with the $n=0$ case of Derivation C.7. A numerical check using the matrix representation also confirms that the identity holds to machine precision. ∎

Exercise C.7: Checking everything for $N=2$

Solution

(1) $\Ham'=\lambda\hat x^2$ merely replaces the frequency, so it can be solved exactly: $\tilde\omega=\omega\sqrt{1+2\lambda/m\omega^2}$, $E_n=(n+\tfrac12)\hbar\tilde\omega$. Setting $u\equiv2\lambda/m\omega^2$ and using $\sqrt{1+u}=1+\tfrac{u}{2}-\tfrac{u^2}{8}+O(u^3)$, we have

$$ E_n=\Big(n+\tfrac12\Big)\hbar\omega\left[1+\frac{\lambda}{m\omega^2}-\frac{\lambda^2}{2m^2\omega^4}+O(\lambda^3)\right] $$

that is, the first-order term is $+(n+\tfrac12)\dfrac{\hbar\lambda}{m\omega}$ and the second-order term is $-(n+\tfrac12)\dfrac{\hbar\lambda^2}{2m^2\omega^3}$.

(2) We compare with the $N=2$ row of Table C.9. Using $x_0^2=\hbar/2m\omega$,

$$ E_n^{(1)}=\lambda x_0^2\,d_2(n)=\frac{\lambda\hbar}{2m\omega}(2n+1)=\Big(n+\tfrac12\Big)\frac{\hbar\lambda}{m\omega}, \qquad E_n^{(2)}=\frac{\lambda^2x_0^4}{\hbar\omega}\,S_2(n)=-\frac{\lambda^2\hbar}{4m^2\omega^3}(2n+1)=-\Big(n+\tfrac12\Big)\frac{\hbar\lambda^2}{2m^2\omega^3} $$

and the result agrees with the expansion in (1) exactly at both orders. This is the simplest exhaustive check that the perturbation series (Table C.9) has been set up correctly.

(3) We construct the matrix elements of $\hat A^2$ by the path method. There are only four two-step paths:

  • $k=+2$ (the one path $++$): $\sqrt{(n+1)(n+2)}$
  • $k=0$ (the two paths $+-$ and $-+$): $(n+1)+n=2n+1$
  • $k=-2$ (the one path $--$): $\sqrt{n(n-1)}$

Putting these into the master formula for second-order perturbation theory, $S_N(n)=\sum_{k\neq0}\abs{\bra{\phi_{n+k}}\hat A^N\ket{\phi_n}}^2/(-k)$, we obtain

$$ S_2(n)=\frac{(n+1)(n+2)}{-2}+\frac{n(n-1)}{+2} =\frac{n^2-n-(n^2+3n+2)}{2}=\frac{-(4n+2)}{2}=-(2n+1) $$

and the table value used in (2), $S_2=-(2n+1)$, is reproduced. ∎

Exercise C.8: Using the Wick expansion

Solution

(1) Take $N=5$ in the general formula of the Wick expansion: $\hat A^5=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\binom{5}{2k}(2k-1)!!\,:\!\hat A^{5-2k}\!:$. The individual terms are

$$ k=0:\ \binom50\cdot1\cdot:\!\hat A^5\!:,\qquad k=1:\ \binom52\cdot1!!\,:\!\hat A^3\!:=10:\!\hat A^3\!:,\qquad k=2:\ \binom54\cdot3!!\,\hat A=5\cdot3\,\hat A=15\hat A $$

so that $\hat A^5=\,:\!\hat A^5\!:+10:\!\hat A^3\!:+15\hat A$. The meaning of the coefficients: $10=\binom52\times1$ is “the number of ways to choose two of the five $\hat A$'s to contract, $\binom52$” × “the number of ways to pair the two chosen, $1!!=1$,” and $15=\binom54\times3!!$ is “the number of ways to choose the four to contract, $\binom54=5$” × “the number of ways to divide the four into two pairs, $3!!=3$.” The coefficients are the numbers of ways to draw the contractions (= the propagator lines)—the same counting as the enumeration of Feynman diagrams.

(2) Take the general formula for the diagonal elements ($N=2M$)

$$ \bra{\phi_n}\hat A^{2M}\ket{\phi_n} =\sum_{j=0}^{M}\binom{2M}{2M-2j}(2M-2j-1)!!\,\binom{2j}{j}\,\frac{n!}{(n-j)!} $$

and set $M=3$. For each $j$ we have

$$ \begin{aligned} j=0&:\ \binom66\cdot5!!\cdot1=15, & j=1&:\ \binom64\cdot3!!\cdot\binom21\,n=15\cdot3\cdot2n=90n,\\ j=2&:\ \binom62\cdot1!!\cdot\binom42\,n(n{-}1)=15\cdot6\,n(n{-}1)=90n(n{-}1), & j=3&:\ \binom60\cdot\binom63\,n(n{-}1)(n{-}2)=20\,n(n{-}1)(n{-}2) \end{aligned} $$

Summing,

$$ 15+90n+90n(n{-}1)+20n(n{-}1)(n{-}2) =20n^3+30n^2+40n+15 $$

(we collected $90n^2-90n+90n=90n^2$ and $20n^3-60n^2+40n$ carefully). This agrees with the $N=6$ row of Table C.7.

(3) In the expectation value in $\ket{\phi_0}$, all normal-ordered terms $:\!\hat A^{m}\!:$ ($m\ge1$) vanish: in normal order either $\hat a$ comes to the right end or $\hat a^\dagger$ comes to the left end, so either $\hat a\ket{\phi_0}=0$ or $\bra{\phi_0}\hat a^\dagger=0$ always applies. What remains is only the constant term of complete contractions ($k=N/2$ when $N$ is even), and

$$ \bra{\phi_0}\hat A^N\ket{\phi_0}=\binom{N}{N}(N-1)!!=(N-1)!! $$

This is itself “the number of ways to pair up all $N$ of the $\hat A$'s.” The counting of vacuum bubbles in Section C.4.1—expanding $\bra{\Phi_0}\hat S\ket{\Phi_0}$ by the Wick expansion leaves only the completely contracted terms without external lines (vacuum bubbles)—is nothing other than the many-body version of this calculation. In field theory each contraction gives a propagator $\ii G^0$, but with a single degree of freedom, as here, each contraction is $1$, so only the “counting of pairs” is exposed, in pure form.

(4) Taking $M=4$ in the same general formula:

$$ \begin{aligned} j=0&:\ 7!!=105, & j=1&:\ \binom86\cdot5!!\cdot2n=28\cdot15\cdot2n=840n,\\ j=2&:\ \binom84\cdot3!!\cdot\binom42\,n(n{-}1)=70\cdot3\cdot6\,n(n{-}1)=1260\,n(n{-}1), & j=3&:\ \binom82\cdot1\cdot\binom63\,(n)_3=28\cdot20\,(n)_3=560\,(n)_3,\\ j=4&:\ \binom88\cdot\binom84\,(n)_4=70\,(n)_4 && \end{aligned} $$

(where $(n)_j\equiv n(n{-}1)\cdots(n{-}j{+}1)$). Expanding and collecting terms, we get

$$ \bra{\phi_n}\hat A^8\ket{\phi_n}=70n^4+140n^3+350n^2+280n+105 $$

and this agrees with the $N=8$ row of Table C.7 (numerically as well, $n=0,1,2$ give $105,\ 945,\ 4305$, in agreement with the direct calculation in the matrix representation). ∎

Exercise C.9: Second-order perturbation theory and its physics

Solution

(1) We insert the four off-diagonal elements for $N=4$ from Table C.8 into the master formula (the denominator is $-k$):

$$ S_4(n)=\underbrace{-\tfrac14(n{+}1)(n{+}2)(n{+}3)(n{+}4)}_{k=+4} \underbrace{+\tfrac14\,n(n{-}1)(n{-}2)(n{-}3)}_{k=-4} \underbrace{-2(2n{+}3)^2(n{+}1)(n{+}2)}_{k=+2} \underbrace{+2(2n{-}1)^2n(n{-}1)}_{k=-2} $$

We collect the two pairs separately. For $k=\pm4$:

$$ (n{+}1)(n{+}2)(n{+}3)(n{+}4)-n(n{-}1)(n{-}2)(n{-}3) =(n^4{+}10n^3{+}35n^2{+}50n{+}24)-(n^4{-}6n^3{+}11n^2{-}6n) =16n^3+24n^2+56n+24 $$

For $k=\pm2$:

$$ (2n{+}3)^2(n{+}1)(n{+}2)-(2n{-}1)^2n(n{-}1) =(4n^4{+}24n^3{+}53n^2{+}51n{+}18)-(4n^4{-}8n^3{+}5n^2{-}n) =32n^3+48n^2+52n+18 $$

Hence

$$ S_4(n)=-\tfrac14(16n^3{+}24n^2{+}56n{+}24)-2(32n^3{+}48n^2{+}52n{+}18) =-(68n^3+102n^2+118n+42) $$

Factoring gives $-2(2n{+}1)(17n^2{+}17n{+}21)$—the $N=4$ row of Table C.9 has been derived (we have also confirmed by numerical diagonalization that $S_4(0)=-42$, $S_4(1)=-330$, and $S_4(2)=-1230$).

(2) The level spacing for $N=4$. From $E_n=(n+\tfrac12)\hbar\omega+\lambda x_0^4\,d_4(n)+\dfrac{\lambda^2x_0^8}{\hbar\omega}S_4(n)$ we have

$$ E_{n+1}-E_n=\hbar\omega+\lambda x_0^4\big[d_4(n{+}1)-d_4(n)\big]+\frac{\lambda^2x_0^8}{\hbar\omega}\big[S_4(n{+}1)-S_4(n)\big] $$

Computing the differences gives $d_4(n{+}1)-d_4(n)=6(2n{+}1)+6=12(n+1)$ and $S_4(n{+}1)-S_4(n)=-(204n^2{+}408n{+}288)$. If $\lambda$ is small, the first-order term dominates, and

$$ E_{n+1}-E_n\simeq\hbar\omega+12(n+1)\,\lambda x_0^4 $$

—for $\lambda>0$ the spacing widens linearly with $n$. The $x^4$ term makes the walls of the potential steeper the higher up one goes (a hard spring), so the higher the level, the tighter the confinement, and the spacing opens up.

Connection with molecular vibrations. The potential of a real chemical bond is the opposite: the more the bond is stretched, the softer it becomes (heading toward dissociation), and, as the Morse-type levels $E_v=\hbar\omega_e(v{+}\tfrac12)-\hbar\omega_ex_e(v{+}\tfrac12)^2$ show, the spacing narrows with $v$ (the anharmonicity constant is $\omega_ex_e>0$; the overtone absorption lines deviate from equal spacing and crowd toward the red, a basic fact of infrared spectroscopy). This “softening” corresponds to the fact that the cubic term of the expansion $V(x)=\tfrac12m\omega^2x^2+\lambda_3x^3+\lambda_4x^4+\cdots$ always gives a negative contribution in second-order perturbation theory ($S_3=-(30n^2{+}30n{+}11)<0$; its contribution to the difference between adjacent levels is $-60(n{+}1)\,\lambda_3^2x_0^6/\hbar\omega$). In the Morse-type potential the bare quartic term has $\lambda_4>0$ (the sign that widens the spacing), but the second-order contribution of the cubic term outweighs it, and the net result is that the spacing narrows. Conversely, the $\lambda x^4>0$ (hardening) of this problem is realized in cases of “nowhere to go” confinement, such as the vibration of a molecule trapped in a cage or a bending mode with steep walls. The sign of the $v$ dependence of the level spacing directly reports whether the potential opens or closes toward the top—the sign in perturbation theory is tied directly to how we read spectroscopic spectra.

(3) The general argument for $E_0^{(2)}\le0$. Setting $n=0$ in the Rayleigh–Schrödinger second-order formula, the denominator is $E_0^{(0)}-E_m^{(0)}<0$ (the ground state lies below every intermediate state), and the numerator is $\abs{\bra{\phi_m}\Ham'\ket{\phi_0}}^2\ge0$, so every term of the sum is negative (or zero). Therefore, for any perturbation, the second-order correction to the ground state is necessarily negative—“perturbation pushes the ground state down” (a general theorem that is also consistent with the variational principle). That $S_N(0)=-1,-11,-42,-449,-3495$ in Table C.9 are all negative is a manifestation of this.

(4) The rapid growth of $S_N(0)$ ($1\to11\to42\to449\to3495$) and asymptotic series. Since the matrix elements of $\hat A^N$ grow through a “counting of pairs” of the $(N-1)!!$ type, the coefficients grow factorially as the order is raised. The same thing happens to the $p$th-order coefficient of the perturbation series in $\lambda$; for $\lambda\hat x^4$ it is known that the $p$th-order coefficient grows like $p!$ (Bender–Wu). The reason the radius of convergence is zero is physical: for $\lambda<0$ the potential is unbounded below and no ground state exists—since the character of the theory changes discontinuously across $\lambda=0$, the Taylor series about $\lambda=0$ has no way of converging. Even so, the first few low-order terms give a good approximation (the numerical check in Section C.9.5)—an asymptotic series is a tool for “using the first few terms and truncating at the optimal order”, not something that “gets better the more terms you add.” The rapid growth of $S_N(0)$ lets us see, in numbers, the entrance to this way of thinking. ∎
